Catalog
Toh 1659 (Tōhoku catalogue; also cited as D 1659 of the Derge edition) · Derge Tengyur · Tantra commentaries (rgyud 'grel) · Tantra

About this text

An Explanation of the Meaning of the Tantra of the Glorious Union with All Buddhas, the Ḍākinī Māyā Saṃvara, known in Sanskrit as the Śrī Sarvabuddhasamāyoga Ḍākinījālasaṃvara Tantrārthodaraṭīkā and in Tibetan as Dpal sangs rgyas thams cad dang mnyam par sbyor ba mkha' 'gro sgyu ma bde mchog gi rgyud kyi don rnam par bshad pa, is an Indian commentary on the Sarvabuddhasamāyoga, an early tantra centered on Vajrasattva and the many families and maṇḍalas of deities that emanate from him. Working through the root tantra chapter by chapter, it explains the tantra's view, its maṇḍala meditations, dances, mudrās, offerings and rites of accomplishment, and the fruits they are said to bring, reading the whole as the display of a buddha whose six bodies are spontaneously present. Its colophon attributes it to Indranāla, a great scholar and accomplished king of Oḍḍiyāna, and records that it was translated into Tibetan by the Indian preceptor Vidyākarasiṃha and the editor-translator Lhé Rinpoché, and later revised to the new language standard. It is Toh 1659 in the Tengyur, where it belongs to the section of commentaries on the tantras.

Translation

In the language of India: śrī sarba buddha sa ma yo gi ḍā ki nī mā yā śambā ra ta ntrā rtho da ro ṭī kā nā ma In the language of Tibet: An Explanation of the Meaning of the Tantra of the Glorious Union with All Buddhas, the Ḍākinī Māyā Saṃvara.

Homage to glorious Vajrasattva.

Glorious, precious one, embodiment of all the family bodies and wisdoms,
source of all, become a friend, sunlight performing distinct activities:
may the hundred and twenty-eight arisen from nondual Vajradhara,
the countless deities of the families emanated from the maṇḍala, be victorious!

Of the king of tantras, the Union with All Buddhas, the Ḍākinī Māyā,
which illuminates the very nature of things, revealing its intent to followers,
the forty-two chapters that express it, the text of the ten excellent summaries of meaning,
the Later Tantra, the Condensed Chapters and the other collections of speech: may they be victorious!

Lord of Secrets, Vajra, who holds and follows the mind of the victors of the three times,
acting for beings through emanation, opener of the secret door, sun of Jambudvīpa, the king,
the one like the day, indivisible, who unlocked the host of intentions, renowned as a second Buddha:
I bow to the lineage of supreme protectors; may they be victorious over the hosts of enemies!

Heart of the teaching, stainless, king that is the root of all tantras,
jewel arisen from the ocean, endowed with the mode of the five branches,
lighting the lamp of its many facets of words, established by the four seals that mark the Dharma,
I shall explain it thoroughly, as my master taught, for the benefit of beings.

Vajradhara, the embodiment of all the victors,
emanating at the beginning of the Good Eon,
in order to purify the things of the world and its beings, the stable and the moving,
through compassion made the emanated maṇḍala appear.
In order to care for the fortunate followers, the hosts of deities,
in keeping with the intention of the teachers who came before,
intending to teach clearly the meaning of the tantra that is symbolized,
he emanated the maṇḍala on the summit of Mount Meru,
and in due order, the first great tantra and the rest,
the pure yoga and the rest, he taught in sequence.
The Lord of Secrets and the other fortunate ones
divided the essence of the ocean of the intent of the victors' minds
into extensive, medium and condensed versions,
and, in accord with the ritual texts of each intended meaning,
they arranged well-condensed texts of words that express it,
as a means for the victors' intended meaning to remain long.
When the sun of the emanation body had illuminated Jambudvīpa
and tamed Jambudvīpa through the three baskets of scripture,
although he set those of lesser intellect on the noble path,
they did not clearly see their own suchness, the definitive meaning.
In order to liberate those of supreme intellect in the future,
the emanated bodhisattva, the one of supreme intelligence,
by the power of his intention, as a way of taming the deluded,
made the magical wheel of the maṇḍala shine forth.
Training in the footsteps of the protectors,
he set down the Glorious Union with All Buddhas,
clarified the meaning of accomplishing the secret maṇḍala step by step,
and liberated the hosts of pure disciples.
So that the secret vehicle might long endure,
to the emanated bodhisattva, the king called Wrathful Impurity,
he fully unlocked the sacred secret instructions
and gave thorough advice for the sake of yoga.
At a certain later time, after the emanation body had passed away,
the secret word of the Victor dawned in a place in Jambudvīpa.
It was invited to Rājagṛha and worshiped;
with joy, reverence and praise they looked at it well, but could not fathom its symbols.
Through compassion and the force of connection, one renowned as a holder of secret mantra
was taken as the excellent one and was asked to examine the volume.
The text of the empty maṇḍala of the Drop of Magical Display
was explained in a prophecy by the adept's exalted deity.
The various traditions of the Vajra Vehicle, to their very limits,
just as in the instructions of the holy ones,
he gathered well without contradiction and fully unravelled,
and taught many commentaries, summaries and means of accomplishment.
Since all the victors have taught many tantras,
what need is there for this explanation that conforms to them?
The hard-to-understand meaning of the arrangement of the holy traditions,
condensing vast and many meanings in few words
and making them somewhat clearer: in this holy text,
what fault or flaw could there be?
In the age of the five degenerations, for those churned by waves of affliction,
short-lived, zealously attached to their own tenets,
who engage in every kind of activity and yet to no purpose,
in order to care for them, I unravel the secret meaning of the Word.
Whatever lacks the branches of purpose and connection
is not well composed.
Whatever has impaired the facets of the branches of explanation
cannot clearly teach the meaning of the tantra.
Whoever is confused about view, meditation and conduct
will not see the genuine true meaning.
Whatever lacks the branches of practicing the two paths
will not accomplish the fruit of the excellent teaching.
The subject matter abides equal to the limits of space:
glorious Vajrasattva of the Good Eon,
the body of great bliss of skillful means and wisdom,
the Union of Buddhas, the Ḍākinī Māyā Saṃvara,
the mode of nonduality that teaches the meaning of the tantra,
abiding as the essence of the chapters on the nondual three bodies.
The means of expression is the collections of texts and words,
which condense the meanings of the great root tantra,
consisting of ten chapters that teach it clearly
through the Later of the Later, the Later, and the rest.
The four tantras in extent run from ten thousand to more than a hundred thousand,
and the explanatory tantras and the rest are known as collections of a hundred thousand.
Although it has been taught thus, in what is to be done
a person will not engage without a purpose.
Whatever activity is to be condensed and accomplished,
in treatises and in the explanatory tantras,
as long as the reason for its purpose has not been stated,
for so long what person will engage in it?
The purpose is that oneself and others enter the maṇḍala,
enter that very path, and accomplish worldly activities.
The ultimate purpose is the accomplishment of the Great Seal (Mahāmudrā),
the result that arises from the two accumulations, skillful means and wisdom.

As for the connection: first, the relation of what is symbolized and what symbolizes;
second, the relation of cause and effect, of means and what arises from means.
In particular, the purpose is like a churned ocean:
by completing the branches of the means of accomplishment the nectar is drawn out,
placed well in a fine precious vessel and spread among beings,
fully dispelling many diseases and piercing pains and setting them in bliss.
As for the branches: examples and meanings, clearing away faults and flaws,
and establishing through scripture, instruction and reasoning in agreement.
The proofs by example, divided by occasion, are seven;
the branches for explaining the collected meanings are of five kinds;
the faults and flaws are taught as eight kinds.
The scriptures of tantra that follow the Ḍākinī Māyā
are unlocked through symbols that bring out the three hidden instructions,
and well established through special reasoning and valid cognition;
like a precious jewel that is extremely hard to assess,
they are to be determined by way of all the branches.
Those things that illuminate the appearance of the meaning to yogins
are to be unravelled through the words of yoga;
the meaning of the symbols hidden and secret from non-Buddhists and others
is not known through grammar, epistemology and logic.
Thus the Ādibuddha Tantra too says:
"The meaning of the secret mantra tantras, extremely hard to realize,
is to be established through the explanatory tantras, the concordant tantras and the rest;
by seeking well the instructions of that very lineage,
one should give birth to an expanse of certainty, decide, and realize it.
For that purpose, intelligent beings of the future,
with means of accomplishment, summaries, commentaries and the like, through reasoning,
having composed them well and condensed my word,
hold it with reverence so that it may long endure."
Since it says this, what contradiction is there in clearly explaining what is hard to realize?
Moreover, in order to encourage followers,
drops of words illuminate an ocean of meaning.
Just like words fully applied to that very meaning,
what contradiction is there in an explanation?
Through it, the yogin who enters by that door
accomplishes a great aim with little effort and without hardship.
Like worshiping a precious jewel well,
take it up with great diligence and with clear faith.

The summarized meaning is of two kinds:
in terms of the tantra as what symbolizes and as what is symbolized.
For now, it is to be examined following the words.
So it is said. In this tantra, which condenses one portion,
the first chapter: the setting, the manner of connection, the meaning of the opening,
summarizes the meaning of the tantra by way of its name,
and teaches at medium length the things to be fully known;
it condenses three chapters of the root tantra.
The second: the general characteristics of the view,
the means of realization, the reasoning, the Dharma of the four modes of conduct,
and the benefits of ground, path and fruit, all of them;
it condenses the fourth chapter of the root tantra.
The third: the general characteristics of accomplishment,
its meaning, its means, its particulars and its benefits,
taught as divided in two, the common and the supreme;
it condenses the words and meaning of the fifth.
The fourth: the general characteristics of conduct,
by way of four kinds of person, one's own benefit,
the benefit of others, and what has fruit and benefits;
the conduct of followers is the Dharma of the path,
and it also teaches the result, together with the deeds of the victors who have reached the end;
it condenses the words and meaning of the sixth.
The fifth: through the play of Vajrasattva, Vairocana, Heruka,
the Lord of Dance (Narteśvara), Sūrya and Paramāśva,
by way of breathing out, drawing in and gathering,
in particular the setting, the intention of what occurred before,
and the means by which followers practice samādhi,
it teaches, condensing the words and meaning of six chapters.
The sixth promises to explain the feast gathering:
the characteristics of the deity coming first,
the feast gatherings of merit and wisdom following,
the tasks of the disciple, the enlightened activities and the result summarized;
incidentally, the characteristics of the support and the sacred commitments (samaya);
it condenses the words and meaning of the thirteenth.
The seventh: the images taught in brief, the desired
excellent maṇḍala, the rite, the activity of entering,
the empowerment, the signs of the substances, the respective seals,
and what follows, with incidental digressions: these it condenses.
The eighth: offering, taught in brief, and the extensive way:
offering by way of the mudrās of homage,
and offering by way of the mudrās of offering;
it is the ritual branch of all the chapters.
The ninth: the collection of twenty-seven mudrās,
the branch of every rite to be accomplished.
The first, sealing with the Great Seal,
is the branch of all practice of samādhi.
The second, making firm with the samaya mudrā,
is the branch of samaya, samādhi and enlightened activity.
The third, blessing oneself and the rest,
is the branch of samādhi, samaya and empowerment.
The fourth, the preliminary of the four applications,
is the branch of accomplishing view and samādhi.
The fifth, performing various applications,
is the branch that sets in motion the feast and the enlightened activities.
The sixth, abiding with changes of facial expression,
is the branch of offering, praise, practice and enlightened activity.
The seventh, the seal of each one's posture of entering,
is the branch of the stages of the conduct of offering and praise.
The eighth, the eyes that change into each one's gaze,
is the branch of all practice and enlightened activity.
The ninth, the collection of Dharma heart-essences on the tongue,
is the branch of the rite of sealing and recitation.
The tenth, the varieties of voice in the melodies of song,
are the varieties of the activity of offering and praising.
The eleventh, performing the four activities of each,
is the branch for undertaking offering, praise and enlightened activity.
The twelfth, performing activities through various methods of dance,
is the branch of the practice of the feast gathering (gaṇacakra).
The thirteenth, the varieties of vajra songs,
is the branch of offering, supplicating and exhorting.
The fourteenth, arranging the substances of the five offerings,
is the branch of gathering the accumulations and accomplishing the images.
The fifteenth, pleasing the guests and the activity of pacifying obstacles,
is the branch of the maṇḍala, practice, feast and enlightened activity.
The sixteenth, empowering the deities and the disciples,
is the branch of samādhi and empowerment.
The seventeenth, the activity of making the accomplishments auspicious,
is the branch of practice, feast, one's own benefit and enlightened activity.
The eighteenth, pleasing through the signs of letters,
is the branch of the feast, protection, practice and empowerment.
The nineteenth symbolizes the realization of the meaning of yoga;
it is the conduct of empowerment, samaya, feast and practice.
The twentieth, the means of accomplishing the undefiled higher perceptions,
is the mark and sign of practice, enlightened activity and maṇḍala.
The twenty-first, the omens of the yoga of firmness and blending,
is the branch of practice, enlightened activity and empowerment.
The twenty-second, the activity of protecting oneself and others,
is the branch for undertaking the enlightened activities of practice.
The twenty-third, twenty-fourth, twenty-fifth and twenty-sixth
are branches that purify particular enlightened activities.
The twenty-seventh, gaining mastery over the accomplishments and activities,
is the branch of every feast and practice.

From the fourteenth chapter up to the fortieth,
it condenses twenty-seven chapters.
The tenth: the summarized meaning, the result and the benefits;
having promised to explain, in explaining according to the meaning,
releasing the mudrās of the dance endowed with union,
accomplishing in accordance with the meaning of meditating on the maṇḍala,
together with the benefits, the result, the qualities and the deeds,
the method of offering to oneself and sealing with mudrās,
and delight, rejoicing and praise:
it condenses the last two chapters.

As for the manner of connection: the setting is the cause, and so it is taught first;
the opening is the condition, and so it comes after it.
To make it easy to understand, it is set out briefly and at medium length.
The teaching of the meaning of the tantra is the subject matter in the manner of nonduality;
the view and the benefits are the Dharma of the ground, the knowable,
connected with the particular settings of the fifth.
Accomplishing according to its meaning is the third;
conduct in accord with that is the fourth.
Intending to examine in accord with the Teacher's intention,
the fifth arose as the stages of samādhi.
The preliminaries, the feast, empowerment and firm sacred commitment make the sixth;
that in turn relies on images, the seventh.
The activity of offering, the branch of them all, is the eighth;
the supplement of twenty-seven mudrās is the ninth.
By the power of having practiced the path in this way,
the final result and the rest are taught by the tenth.

The connection of the meaning that is symbolized is of two kinds:
practice for one's own benefit and conduct for the benefit of others.
One who is endowed with excellent preliminaries,
having come to know the view of the real state of knowable things,
in a supreme and excellent place that possesses a basis,
the images, the maṇḍala, the empowerment and the sacred commitments,
the samādhi, the enlightened activities, the varieties of conduct,
and the stages of practicing the feast gathering:
these, by the categories that divide one's own and others' benefit,
should be applied in two stages and understood.
When the two paths have been practiced and made familiar,
the result is the appearance of qualities together with deeds.

The great soaring garuḍa crosses the whole expanse with sweeps of its wings;
the king of geese, never turning aside, alights upon the meaning;
the lion, by leaping, knows how to launch out and gather in at the junctures;
the tortoise, with its able gait, brings the branches down to their kernels;
the blue sheep dwelling on the crag shows how the ill-fitting fits;
the hawk, hunting its prey, seizes the precious intent;
the precious lamp lights up the many facets of the words.
With example and meaning in accord, the divisions of the chapters are determined.

Among the divisions of the profound and vast meanings,
the common and the special, the texts of the vehicle of characteristics and of secret mantra,
the lesser, the assemblages of forms, and the texts of the meaning of secret mantra,
are to be understood as the ultimate meaning of the Dharma.
The faults and flaws are explained in passing:
the scripture of tantra, free of contradiction, concordant and clear, gives rise to certainty;
the instructions bring out the hidden words, meanings and letters;
reasoning, through objection and reply, cuts through superimpositions.

Now, in general, if one condenses all the tantras of the Great Vehicle of secret mantra, there is the meaning that is symbolized and the words that symbolize. The first has the characteristics of continuum, means and result. These are: all beings abiding as the nature of a buddha; practicing the path comprised of the undefiled ripening and liberating continua; and the bodies and wisdom that appear to oneself and others, together with their qualities and deeds.

The words of the second are the kriyā tantras, the caryā tantras, the yoga tantras and the great yoga tantras. These are divided by the three kinds of generation and completion. First, among what is taught as eighteen categories of tantra, this one is the ground and root of all the tantras of body. Since their characteristics and divisions are taught at length in the commentary on the Glorious Sacred Garland, I shall not elaborate on them here.

Now I shall enter into the meaning of the tantra. In explaining it by way of symbolizing the meaning to be symbolized just as it is, first: since without the gathering of excellent causes there is no basis, in order to teach the meaning of the setting, the Teacher and the rest, it says:

The being who is the reality of all the buddhas,
Vajrasattva, supreme bliss,
in the delight of the supreme secret,
the self of all, abides forever.

Others say: The Blessed One said, "Sons of good family, after I have passed into complete nirvāṇa, you should compile the sacred Dharma. At the beginning of every discourse place the extensive setting, 'Thus did I hear at one time,' and so on, and at the end place rejoicing and praise." If one asks why, then, the compiler, the Lord of Secrets Vajrapāṇi, is not mentioned here, there are two reasons for this. The stages of training in the vast intention of the great Vajradhara are taught at length in the root tantra in forty-two chapters; and the great Vajradhara abides forever in the nature of the great nirvāṇa that does not abide in extremes, as is made clear by the words "the great self, permanent." Therefore, in other tantras the setting and the subject matter are taught separately, but here, by way of nonduality, the setting is taught through the meaning of the names of the buddhas of the six families, and that very setting stands as the meaning of the subject matter.

If one objects, "In that case it is no different from the things to be fully known, so it need not be set out separately," then although that is so, it is, moreover, a concordant setting, or else a condensation of the root tantra in the manner of first setting out the cause.

In the manner that corresponds to other scriptures, there are the Teacher, the place, the retinue, the compiler and the time. Therefore the root tantra says, "Thus did I hear at one time: the Blessed One, the buddha of the nondual three bodies, the great Vajradhara, the Tathāgata, the embodiment of all vajras, lotuses, jewels and crossed vajras, who has become the source of all that is sacred," and so on. With these vast words it teaches the characteristics at length: the suchness of the Teacher's nature, the collection of his characteristics, the appearance of his qualities, and his distinction from others by way of his vast enlightened activity; the emanated buddha-field and the place of the teaching; the great retinue, emanated not other than him, yet distinct and independent; the host of tathāgatas who compile the maṇḍala of mind; the compiler of speech, the bodhisattva Lord of Secrets Vajrapāṇi; and the two times, of emanation and of teaching.

The Seven Chapters also says:

In order to establish that it does not contradict other texts,
the common setting is taught
through the distinctions of teacher, place, assembly,
compiler and time.

For the sake of the special meaning of secret mantra, it teaches the meaning of the excellences by way of the names of the six buddhas. The Condensed Chapters says:

"The being, the reality of all the buddhas,"
is Vairocana, since he is perfectly awakened;
Vajrasattva is the great victor,
since he is the supreme holder of the wisdom vajra.
"Secret" is what is called the Blood Drinker (Heruka),
the deity who accomplishes the Great Seal.
"Supreme" is the king, the Lotus Holder,
who brings knowledge of the sacred commitment.
"Delight" is the sacred Sūrya,
through whom generosity and the rest become supremely accomplished.
"The self of all, abiding forever,"
is Vajra Paramāśva, the lord of activity itself.

And the Seven Chapters also says:

Through the names of the six buddhas
the meaning of the special setting is made clear.

Further, as one who illuminates and as the embodiment of clear wisdom, he makes the nondual being, and since he possesses the two excellences through his special activity, he is the very reality of all the buddhas. The distinction in his characteristics is Vajrasattva and what is called "having become the supreme body of great bliss": by way of the two, meaning and sign, Vajrasattva has become the supreme of bliss. The three syllables teach the three doors of liberation, and what symbolizes them is the shape of the secret place, the triangular bhaga. That itself is free of all extremes of attachment, and as the embodiment of great passion, it is free of the stains of conceptual thought and purifies them; therefore it has become supreme. It is the nature of Padmanarteśvara, the Lotus Lord of Dance. Bliss, the characteristic of the mind of awakening, having become the source of all the enjoyments of delight, is the array of qualities: that is Sūrya. Paramāśva is the embodiment of all enlightened activity: he is the self of the whole retinue and of the compiler, and at all times he abides forever in his own characteristic and for the sake of those to be tamed.

The nature of the great wisdom that abides as the nonduality of the moving support of secret mantra and the secret supported is the being. That itself abides as the nature of all the buddhas, the reality of the five winds. Since it performs activity that cannot be destroyed by others, it abides like a vajra; and since the great being who experiences his own suchness expands with the taste of bliss, that is the undefiled path, and so it has become supreme. As for the secret meaning: by way of the aspects of the three mantras arising, entering and abiding, it performs the activity of radiating and gathering; and since it fills the three supreme channels and delights in the samādhi of great bliss, it abides forever as the self of all phenomena. The Seven Chapters says:

The nondual wisdom, the great being,
the self of all the buddhas, is the five movements;
Vajrasattva, the king of great bliss,
expands the supreme undefiled path;
the secret is the three mantras arising, entering and abiding;
the three supreme channels delight in great bliss;
the self of all is suchness,
the source of all phenomena.

In terms of shape: the nature of the being symbolized as the nonduality of skillful means and wisdom abides as the reality of all the buddhas in the form of the wheels of the five elements. The form of the letters that are the sign of Vajrasattva and the supreme of great bliss are nondual and not distinct. When the secret place, the mudrā of Vajradhātvīśvarī, and the blazing jewel are joined, it is urged on by supreme bliss through the conditions of wind and fire; from the four moments it increases into the sixteen joys, and thus delighted and satisfied, it seals all phenomena, whose essence is the nonduality of appearance and emptiness, with great bliss, and abides forever as the self of the buddha of the nondual three bodies. The Seven Chapters says:

The nondual being, the supreme buddha,
abides in the form of the wheels of the elements;
the nondual letters are supreme great bliss,
Vajrasattva, suchness.
Having fully united with the secret mudrā,
it is to be urged on by the condition of supreme bliss;
the four joys unfold into sixteen states,
and all is the body of great bliss.

In terms of the Dharma: the being, the great self-arisen wisdom free of all the stains of conceptual thought, is the very reality of all the buddhas. It abides as the body of great bliss in the manner of the nonduality of object and subject, and since it is accomplished from undefiled wisdom, it is inseparable. It is the body in which the suchness of all phenomena is experienced. Although its meaning abides in all things, it is obscured by the stains of conceptual thought and remains secret to itself; and since those who undergo hardships through the vehicle of characteristics do not realize it, it remains as though its appearance were utterly hidden. Those who travel the path of the Great Vehicle of secret mantra, practicing it, possess the supreme result of the nondual three bodies, and delighting in the taste of great bliss, they abide forever as the self of all phenomena. The Seven Chapters says:

Appearance itself is the reality of emptiness;
that itself, through great bliss,
that itself is the being, the reality of the buddhas,
that itself is the expanse of all phenomena.
Great bliss, Vajrasattva,
abides in the manner of appearance, pervasion and clarity.

These are both the meaning of the subject matter and the cause from which it arises: the excellent setting. Furthermore, without conditions, it does not arise even when the causes are gathered, like the fruit of the pṛ ka ta flower.

Then, in order to condense the second chapter, the opening, by way of the names of two buddhas, it says, "This is the self-arisen Blessed One, the one, supremely great deity." That is: the buddhas of the ten directions, who from the outer and inner things that are spontaneously accomplished by nature have recollected their own suchness as ultimate and conventional phenomena and made its realization manifest, also teach their own suchness, ultimate and conventional phenomena.

Therefore it says: "Then, from the blessing of the buddha of the nondual three bodies, the great Vajradhara, the realms of the great trichiliocosm and the rest were illuminated by the radiance of light rays. He roused the hosts of buddhas and bodhisattvas of the ten directions and gathered all beings whose continua were fully purified, and he emanated and set out before them the various stages of the maṇḍala. From this, through his power, the elements, the stable and the moving that came from the elements, and the buddhas of the ten directions and the hosts of emanations revealed the meaning of the Glorious Union with All Buddhas, the Ḍākinī Māyā Saṃvara, teaching the characteristic of the nonduality of the two truths, and they manifested various magical displays of form and various magical displays of sound. At that time conceptions arose in the assembly. With hosts of offerings arranged in variety and with praises in various modulations they pleased the principal maṇḍala, and circling it many times, they bowed and paid homage. The bodhisattva mahāsattvas, Vajrapāṇi and the others, thirty-two in all, supplicated again and again and raised the subject." So it speaks, in vast words and meanings.

The meaning of these words is this. It is speech of what is near and directly perceived: the things of appearance and existence, the appearances of the senses. They are self-arisen from the unborn expanse, emptiness. The magical displays of qualities, the five great elements, the five destinies, the five aggregates and the rest, by way of holding their individual characteristics and their general aspects, the discordant conceptions of characteristics purified in their own place, appear as the domain of the Blessed Ones, the six buddhas. The great self-arisen wisdom that illuminates them, free of all the stains of duality, the single body of great bliss in the manner of the union of appearance and emptiness, is the characteristic of things: it is the intention of all the buddhas of the three times. The supremely great deities of great compassion who emanate from it abide in excellence, each in the manner of forming the maṇḍala of a family. The Seven Chapters says:

These are emptiness, self-arisen;
the phenomena of appearance and existence are the Blessed One.
From the single nondual wisdom,
the great deities of the various maṇḍalas,
through the sound of blessing and emanation,
raise the very meaning of the tantra.

This teaches the profound and the vast meanings together. In particular: glorious Vajrasattva is the nature of all the buddhas, the source of all phenomena, the one who emanates the various maṇḍalas. Since this one is accomplished from the appearance of great self-arisen wisdom, he has conquered the four māras; and since he possesses the eight masteries, he is the great Blessed One. He abides as the essence of the single nondual self, and he abides as the deity fully differentiated into the aspects of the qualities. The Seven Chapters says:

Vajrasattva, the body of great bliss,
the lord of all, himself the Blessed One,
the reality of the single nondual wisdom,
the supremely great deity of manifold display.

By way of mantra and shape: this, together with the two, the victorious wind and the melting, is the self-arisen Blessed One. Since it conquers the conceptions of the ten kinds of wind and of the thirty-three abodes, and abides as the essence of the single great natural bliss, it is the supremely great deity. The Seven Chapters says:

Through the mudrā of the collection of mantras and of shape,
the mind of awakening, arising, entering and abiding,
is the nondual self-arisen Blessed One,
great bliss, the supremely great deity.

And again:

Relying on that very thing,
the maṇḍalas fully emanated, the hosts of illusion,
through the characteristics of mantra and shape,
in order fully to teach the suchness of the Dharma,
by way of that very meaning
make that very meaning clear;
through symbols he made the condition for the opening.

These are the explanation of the brief teaching of the meaning of the opening.

What thus arises from the gathering of causes and conditions is the symbolizing tantra. What is to be accomplished by it is the Dharma of ground and path, and what arises from that is the six bodies, spontaneously accomplished. Therefore the whole of what is symbolized abides, according to the occasion, in the mode of the result. So, in order to teach in brief the things to be fully known by way of the characteristic of the nonduality of the tantra and the Teacher, and to condense the meaning of the third chapter, it says, "Union with All Buddhas, the Ḍākinī Māyā, supreme bliss." Thus these six words condense the text that follows.

Among them, all the buddhas of nature, who abide in the ten directions and are comprised by the three times, are equally united in the state of the nonduality of phenomena and their nature. The realized victors are the embodiment of all excellences: through great wisdom they abide in the nature of reality, and through great compassion they are equally joined to the welfare of sentient beings. By way of the five kinds of ḍākinī they abide in their respective suchness; through the various maṇḍalas of illusion they emanate in accord with the needs of those to be tamed; and by way of the distinct stages of the vehicles, through the five paths of the common vehicle and the two special paths, they place the realms of sentient beings in the supreme bliss of definitive goodness, the state endowed with the bodies, wisdom, qualities and deeds. The Seven Chapters says:

The reality of all the buddhas of the three times,
the supreme Vajradhara, the body of great bliss,
with all phenomena of the ten directions without exception,
all of them, equally united in the three bodies,
through the maṇḍala of the Ḍākinī Māyā,
by revealing the manifold display of the activities
of various bodies, speech and minds, tames beings:
this is the path together with its result, supreme bliss.

In particular: by way of "buddha," the signless samādhi is taught; by way of "all," the samādhi with signs, the enlightened activities and the feast gathering; by way of "union," practice and the rest; by way of "ḍākinī," the maṇḍala of images; by way of "māyā," the view and the conduct; and by way of "supreme bliss," empowerment. The thirty-fourth chapter of the root tantra says:

The enumeration of names, "buddha" and the rest,
the six words that are taught,
how they abide as the ground,
the collection of the eight branches of the path,
and the result together with the deeds, condensed:
from the single suchness of what is symbolized,
through the particular verbal paths of sound,
the reasoning that ascertains the meaning of the symbols is taught.

In the mantra mode, that very buddha, in whom life-force and exertion are nondual and who is pure of the stain of conceptual thought, is emanated from the five exertions up to twenty-one thousand six hundred, and is gathered from the ten places into four hundred thousand koṭis. Uniting in equipoise with the suchness of all things, through the particular quality of experience, in the form of the mantra of life-force and exertion, it enters the avadhūtī, the nature of the Ḍākinī Māyā, and abides in the supreme undefiled primordial wisdom of great bliss. As the Seven Chapters says:

Life-force and exertion nondual, the body of the Buddha,
stainless, with its retinue, joins with the places;
through the magical display of the Ḍākinī Māyā
it enters great bliss, the avadhūtī.
The supreme undefiled primordial wisdom:
all phenomena are nothing other than that.

In the mode of shape, from the appearance of the innate bodhicitta that dwells in the place of the wheel, it is made to circle through all the petals and letters of the wheel. By the condition of fire and wind, which accomplish means and wisdom, the two are united in equipoise; relative bodhicitta is brought down, held, and turned back upward again, and thereby the stages of joy arise in the places of the wheels. In the forward order and in the reverse order, in the manner of the ḍākinī, the undefiled bodhicitta abides as illusion and supreme bliss. As the Seven Chapters says:

Great bliss, the innate, the place of the wheel;
the Buddha everywhere in the manner of a wheel;
the bodhicitta of the union of means and wisdom;
arising and reversing, the undefiled body of supreme bliss.

In the Dharma mode, the root tantra says:

Nondual primordial wisdom, the great Buddha,
unites in equipoise all things and the rest;
through the magical display of the Ḍākinī Māyā,
phenomena are the expanse of great bliss.

That is the brief teaching of the meaning of the tantra. These points have been taught in common by way of the seven examples, the five branches, and so on.

Now the things to be fully understood are taught at intermediate length. First, in order to teach the suchness of primordial wisdom by way of the single one, the tantra says:

There is no attachment, nor is it free from attachment,
nor is it observed in the middle either.

In terms of mantra, there is the attachment of emanating and gathering in the sequence of going out and coming in, in the form of life-force and exertion; there is the joy and bliss that has wholly abandoned that; and there is the middle that has abandoned all extremes. None of these is observed at all. Having thoroughly purified oneself by way of the three suchnesses, one should behold the suchness of great bliss. As the Seven Chapters says:

In the sequence of going out, coming in and abiding,
attachment, freedom from attachment, and the middle:
fully freed from the extremes of every kind of conduct,
one should behold great bliss.

By way of shape, the innate stream is neither restrained in its own place, nor released into the place of the other, nor partly held and partly released; none of these is observed. At the juncture of Vajradhātvī and the blazing jewel, the kata, one beholds the primordial wisdom of suchness, so that the nature of all phenomena, what is not held, what is held, and the rest, becomes clear; then, drawing it upward again, one should set it in the place of the innate. As the Seven Chapters says:

The stream of the gathered bodhicitta of bliss:
attachment, freedom from attachment, the actual middle;
by passing beyond the extremes of clinging and attachment,
one abides in innate union.

In terms of Dharma, in any of the whole range of aspects of sense appearances, superior, middling and lesser, none of the conceptions of ordinary beings, the conceptions of śrāvakas, or the conceptions of bodhisattvas is observed; in a manner free from the two attachments, one enjoys the two attachments. At that time, for all phenomena, in the appearance of the great self-arisen primordial wisdom, there is no division into the categories of things and of mind. Appearing phenomena abide as emptiness, empty of their own intrinsic nature and essential nature; and because they are sealed with great bliss, the undefiled body can appear and exist as anything at all. As the Seven Chapters says:

Practicing the way of attachment and freedom from attachment,
one becomes bound by the concept of the middle.
In the state of the nondual Great Seal,
within appearance itself is the shape of emptiness,
within emptiness itself is appearance.
Sporting in the objects of the six senses,
phenomena are directly perceived as great bliss.
Form and the rest have no superior, middling or inferior;
sealed with appearance, emptiness and bliss,
one is fully freed from every object of focus.

Now, in order to teach by way of that very object of focus the meaning of the words "this is self-arisen," the tantra says:

The illusory seal of all women:
this is the supreme nondual vehicle.

In the mantra mode, life-force and exertion abide in the manner of the nonduality of means and wisdom, and because all the magical displays of things appear from that, it is like a woman. Because it symbolizes and marks the meaning of everything, it is the seal of illusion. It is free from the dualistic conceptions of apprehended and apprehender, and abides as the supreme vehicle. As the Seven Chapters says:

All things that arise from life-force and exertion nondual
are the shape of suchness.
The woman, the supreme seal of illusion:
this is the Dharma of the vehicle's liberation.

In the mode of shape, because it arises from union with the action seal and reveals the suchness of things, it is the seal of illusion; and because it makes clear the meaning of nonduality, it is the nature of the Great Seal, the supreme vehicle. As the Seven Chapters says:

Through the woman, the seal of illusion,
the illusion of joy and bliss is overcome;
by the power of familiarity with the Dharma seal,
the Great Seal becomes clear.

As Dharma, the seal that is the dharmadhātu, the mother Samantabhadrī, sporting with the seal of bliss in all phenomena whose character is illusion, abides in suchness, nondual in its own character. As the Seven Chapters says:

Phenomena such as form, being nondual,
are the seal of all women.

These are the characteristics of the view: how all phenomena abide, and the means by which the yogin is to realize that. They are set out by way of the profound, not by way of the vast accumulations. This summarizes the power of view of the second chapter, and it is taught through the example of the great garuḍa soaring.

Now, in order to teach conduct by way of illusion, the tantra says:

Of all illusions,
the illusion of woman is especially great;
through the powers of its essential nature
it is, by its very nature, established by that.

This verse teaches the reasoning of the union of view and conduct. Next, the characteristic of the ground: although it divides into two, the actual buddha and the buddha of realization, it should be gathered within illusion.

Here, what establishes all phenomena is direct valid cognition and the inference that follows it. Direct perception appears clearly; inference concerns what is hidden, and is the cognition of mental analysis. Now, the eight examples of illusion appear directly, and following that, one measures them by neither one nor many, by the arising and ceasing of the four alternatives, and so on, and holds that all phenomena are without intrinsic nature: that is the system of the Characteristics Vehicle. By that, one does not come to realize and see the suchness of all phenomena, the body of great bliss that has the character of illusion; and so the ground of the definitive buddha is never traversed.

Here, by the system of this Secret Vehicle, which is distinct from other means of illustration through direct perception, one experiences the joy of the illusory woman by way of the path of liberation, which is means, by way of the path of passion, which is wisdom, and by way of the nondual Great Seal; and thereby the flourishing of the taste of great bliss is especially great. That very thing, in the manner of cause, condition and result, is established in this way through the power of its essential nature and by its intrinsic nature. As the root tantra says:

One's own nature, hard to realize,
liberation and passion nondual, the Great Seal:
by the power of relying on the three women,
one experiences bliss directly.
Its meaning is the sublime object of the view;
it is also what is to be practiced in samādhi;
it is to be accomplished, and the result is in the manner of illusion:
that itself is the body of the definitive Buddha.

The manner of its result is taught in the words:

In these three worlds, through this woman,
they come to pay reverence.

That is, in this world too all good qualities are accomplished, and elsewhere great beings endowed with the whole range of great qualities pay reverence.

According to the distinct situations of four kinds of persons, there is vast conduct, the supreme mode, the teaching of the path as profound for the middling and the lesser, and abiding in the most profound mode. So that the senses may enjoy their objects with delight, it says:

Through gain, pleasure and every joy,
even through base conduct one attains accomplishment.

A beginner should seek whatever seal of a compatible family he finds and engage by way of the sixty-four methods of union. One with slight mastery of primordial wisdom should remain in union until the primordial wisdom of bliss arises, and should hold just that. One with true mastery of primordial wisdom, merely by seeing his own seal and a joyful mind arising, becomes clear as the body of great bliss. For a person who is completely liberated, all the objects of the six senses become the undefiled path. Then one should put it into practice by meditating on the meaning of the nondual view, and as an aid to the two samādhis one should engage in conduct: in food, drink, clothing, dwelling and bed, and in the tasks to be done, accomplishment comes through engaging in actions that are reviled and base in the world. As the root tantra says:

By the distinction of the four persons,
one enjoys gain, pleasure and every joy:
vast, supreme, middling and lesser,
the mode of the profound and the most profound.
Through the innate bliss of the example,
great bliss, the innate bliss, expands.
Therefore, by abiding in conduct,
one will find true primordial wisdom.

If one asks, "Then is one liberated by the very thing that binds?", the tantra says:

Having wholly transformed one's own essence,
it is accomplished by the illusion of all women.
This wondrous seal of illusion
is shown in symbol as "ḍākinī."

For the special purpose, ordinary conceptual thought is reversed by the samādhi of great emptiness and the rest; then, having wholly transformed one's own essence, the body, speech and mind of the deity are accomplished through illusion, together with the assembly of twenty women. Therefore this wondrous seal of illusion is itself the symbol of the ḍākinī. In terms of the profound, those very aspects of good conception are also transformed into their respective essences, and being accomplished through the illusion of the three women, one comes to abide in the suchness of all phenomena. That is why it says "wondrous illusion" and so on. As the Seven Chapters says:

A person seized by poison
is freed by knowing the nature of the poison;
likewise, from being bound by concepts,
one is freed by knowing the nature of concepts.

Of the characteristics of the union of view and conduct, this teaches mainly conduct; it therefore summarizes the fourth chapter, and is taught through the example of the wild sheep that dwells on the crags.

Now, in order to teach the characteristics of the maṇḍala by way of "ḍākinī," the tantra says:

ḍākini means "she who goes in the sky";
this is derived from the verbal root.
Because she accomplishes moving everywhere in space,
she is well known as "ḍākinī."

Possessing the nature of going everywhere, through the two vast doors, by limitless kinds of deeds of great compassion, in order to benefit those to be trained, various emanated maṇḍalas are displayed from that very buddha in whom the three bodies are nondual, and it acts for the welfare of beings with the wheel of primordial wisdom. And in order to care for later followers, there are the stages of meditating on samādhi and the accomplishment of the maṇḍala of images: the ḍākinīs, who have the character of going in the sky, are to be accomplished and made clear from images. The outer and inner elements, and the things derived from the elements, abide as the nature of the six family maṇḍalas; and from that, in order to undertake special actions, they are manifestly set up with colored powders, supports, and the seal of the consort, and all actions that move through the sky are accomplished. Therefore, because she accomplishes in various ways, she is known as ḍākinī. As the root tantra says:

Abiding fully by going everywhere,
she makes clear the maṇḍala that arose before, hard to realize;
the host of tasks for those who follow:
from images, and from the host of outer and inner elements,
three maṇḍalas that closely illustrate it
are fully explained through the symbol of the ḍākinī.

In terms of the profound, through great primordial wisdom one goes into the state of the nature of reality. Because it is derived from the element of the three women, the union of means and wisdom, it is accomplished by way of means, by way of wisdom, and by way of nonduality, by moving everywhere in nondual illusion, great bliss, which is like the sky; and so she is well known as ḍākinī. As the root tantra says:

Through the symbol of the ḍākinī
the profound maṇḍala is distinguished.

And the Seven Chapters also says:

From the element of the three women,
the union of means and wisdom,
nondual illusion, great bliss,
comes to abide like the sky.
Through the seal of the ḍākinī,
relying on images, primordial wisdom becomes clear.

Now, in order to conclude what has been taught in this way by way of the profound and the vast, the tantra says:

Of the seal of all without exception,
of the supreme bliss of all without exception,
the Union with All Buddhas
is well known as ḍākinī.

That is, all phenomena of saṃsāra and nirvāṇa without exception appear as the nature of the two seals, and all of them without exception have the aspect of the nondual body of great bliss. At that time, whatever has signs imputed by the mind, and all phenomena that are things, are nondual and have the form of illusion; and to this the symbolic name "Union with All Buddhas, the Ḍākinī Māyā" is given. As the ninth chapter of this very tantra also says:

ḍi stands for the ḍākinī;
these three realms are to be examined.
The seals that go in the sky
are well known as ḍākinīs.
The seals, various in every way,
all go in the sky;
of the Union with All Buddhas
they are well known as the ḍākinīs.
The accomplished Lord of Dance of illusion
is well known as ārali.
The ḍākinī too is ārali,
the seal of the symbol of the buddhas.

If one asks, "Does an image cause one to obtain something that did not exist before?", the tantra says:

She is "the one who goes in the sky,"
derived from the buddha-element.
She who is accomplished as going everywhere
is the ḍākinī, the very self of all buddhas.

That is, from the essence in which all phenomena are awakened from the very beginning, Supreme Bliss, which is like an illusion, beings are obscured by their own respective conceptions and engage in various dualistic phenomena; yet there is no wavering or adulteration in the nature of suchness, and it abides in its own character. By distinctions of situation, what abides spontaneously present from the very beginning is the cause; focusing on it and engaging with it is the path; and experiencing it clearly is the result, the dharmakāya, which abides always in the manner of the inseparability of object and subject. Going everywhere from that are the two kinds of form body. Their being accomplished as the illusion of great bliss is called the consummate body. Thus she is the ḍākinī, the very self of all buddhas; and what shows this clearly is the maṇḍala of images. As the Seven Chapters says:

All appears as body and primordial wisdom;
that very nature, the entity, is secret.
Derived from the buddha-element,
the manner of accomplishing going everywhere
is experienced through the power of images:
the ḍākinī, the very self of all buddhas.

Among the characteristics of all the maṇḍalas, this is specifically the stage of images; summarizing a portion of the seventh chapter, it is taught through the example of the king of geese in flight.

Now, in order to teach empowerment by way of "bliss," the tantra says:

śam is explained as bliss;
it is the supreme bliss of all buddhas.
Thoroughly uniting with all illusions,
through supreme bliss it is the supreme of bliss.

Just as, in the words śambara abhiṣiñca, the reasoning is applied to the sounds of the Sanskrit, so empowerment washes away the stains of the three doors and generates bliss; it generates special capacity, and in the manner of a single instant it fully ripens the mind-stream, joins one to the path of liberation, and makes one a special vessel, possessing the nature of all qualities and activities.

Then, on the occasion of the first empowerment, one is empowered with the outer vase blessed by all the buddhas, and with the inner vase, which is supreme: the stream melted into light by the great bliss arising from the union of father and mother. The vajra master empowers one with the bliss of union with the illusion of wisdom. Oneself, relying on the action seal, generates the primordial wisdom of supreme bliss according to the guru's instructions. And having generated the supreme of bliss, undefiled primordial wisdom, there is the empowerment of abiding in the suchness of all phenomena. Thus, although the last two empowerments arise in oneself, they come about through the power of the instructions, and so they are conferred empowerments. As the Seven Chapters says:

Through the sound "supreme bliss"
is the path of empowerment that purifies the three doors;
through the methods of conferring the four,
it is the supreme way to accomplish the clarity of great bliss.
"The supreme bliss of all" is the first;
"thorough union with illusion" is the second;
"supreme bliss" is the third;
"the supreme of bliss" is the fourth.

This briefly teaches the meaning of the stages of empowerment; summarizing the sixteen seals of the sixth, seventh and ninth chapters, it is taught through the example of the lion and the wild sheep.

Now, in order to teach signless meditation by way of "buddha," the tantra says:

Everywhere, from every side, all of it,
the self, always, in every aspect,
all things stable and moving, buddhas and the rest:
the self becomes the entity of all.

That is, by way of means, by way of wisdom, and by way of nonduality, in all places and at all times, all sense appearances are the self of the primordial wisdom of awareness, unchanging in every situation; and in the manner of the threefold union, means and wisdom being nondual, one should abide as the self of all things stable and moving, buddhas and the rest. As the Seven Chapters says:

With a mind undiminished in all places and times,
one should meditate on the samādhi of the buddha;
through the seals of mantra, shape and Dharma,
meditate as the entity of all things stable and moving.

The stages of meditating on this are to be learned from the means of accomplishment that the master, the Second Buddha, set out by compiling sections of the tantra. This teaches the completion stage. It is distinguished by way of the profound, summarizes a portion of the fifth chapter and the seal of union of the ninth, and is taught through the example of the hawk.

Now, in order to teach samādhi with signs by way of "all," the tantra says:

The samādhi of mind and of the being,
and the various conduct of awakening itself;
likewise the māras and victory over the māras:
that is awakening, the Buddha.

Here the yogin who meditates on the generation stage first generates the mind directed to supreme awakening. Possessing empowerment and samaya, he abides with the intention of acting for the benefit of all beings. He meditates on emptiness and on the body of the deity; he performs the blessing in order to generate the capacity of the three doors; he joins himself with beings and makes them clear; he visualizes the heroes and gathers the accumulations toward the noble ones; he gathers the accumulations toward sentient beings; with the protection wheel he cuts off the obstructors and then protects again; and having meditated on the samādhi of the ground and the samādhi of the support, he should contemplate the meaning of union. From the body of Vajrasattva, abiding in the manner of delightful magical display, he comes onto the central seat and abides in the absorption of the four doors of liberation; roused from it by the buddhas of the ten directions, his body becomes complete by way of the five manifest awakenings.

First, the sun and moon maṇḍalas, the signs of the profound and vast expanse and primordial wisdom, are "the samādhi of mind and of the being": they are the signs of bodhicitta and great compassion. The sixteen vowels and forty consonants abiding on them radiate and gather, bringing about the marks and minor marks where these are lacking and unclear; and the centers of sun and moon, marked by the garlands to the right and left, are the conduct of awakening that cares for sentient beings. Upon that is a five-pronged vajra, marked at its hub with hūṃ and radiating a mass of five-colored light rays: that is "variety itself." The buddhas of the six families arising from it conquer the four māras and those of their class, are gathered into body, speech and mind, and enter their own place, whereby it becomes the great vajra. Then becoming the body of the causal great Vajradhara: that is the body of the buddha, the nature of the five bodhicittas. As the Seven Chapters says:

The moon is the nature of mind;
the sun is the samādhi of the being.
From the vowels and consonants, the two accumulations;
the conduct of awakening cares for beings.
The vajra, with light of various colors,
radiating and gathering: the māras and victory over the māras.
Endowed with the primordial wisdom of bodhicitta,
the causal protector, the supreme Buddha.

And the Condensed Chapters also says:

The distinction of the action of samādhi:
because the mind is stable, it is the being;
this is meditation on the samādhi of the being.
The being abides in consciousness;
it is the vajra itself; from the vajra
Vajrasattva arises.

The stages are given at length in the root tantra:

On the lotus seat in the center
the glorious Paramādya takes his seat.
All phenomena are empty, free of signs,
wishless; entering the samādhi
whose nature is clear light,
he abides in the nature of cessation.
Urged on by the victors of the ten directions,
he becomes the complete body of the five manifest awakenings.
On the moon maṇḍala of one's own mind
abide the sixteen letters of the A series;
on the sun maṇḍala, which makes great compassion dawn,
are the thirty-four letters of the KA series,
four of the tongue-root class, and two unclear ones:
one should meditate on them as like the forms of stars.
Through their radiating and gathering
the two accumulations, the marks and minor marks, are completed.
This shows the first two primordial wisdoms,
the characteristic of the two bodhicittas.
Third, a five-pronged vajra,
with a clear white and red radiance,
at its hub hūṃ, its marks clear:
the sign of the complete result of the two accumulations.
From the appearance of five colors,
by contemplating the maṇḍalas of the six families,
they are joined to their own places,
and each maṇḍala, with its retinue,
gathers back and enters body, speech and mind,
whereby it becomes the great vajra with its light.
This shows the middle two primordial wisdoms,
the characteristic of the two bodhicittas.
Fifth, from the completion of the rite,
one arises in the body of Vajrasattva;
from the fifth, the primordial wisdom bodhicitta,
the Great Seal is made clear.

Now, in order to generate the buddhas of the families of the result, the tantra says:

Vajra and the Vajra Holder himself,
likewise lotus and the Lotus Holder,
jewel and the Jewel Holder himself:
these too are their families.

That is, Vajrasattva and Heruka, the two arising from the causal Vajrasattva, are the vajra family. The Lord of Dance and Paramāśva are the lotus family. If one asks why they become the lotus family: because the one holds a lotus in his hand, and the other has the neigh of the horse. Vairocana and Sūrya are the jewel family, because they enjoy the body and the assembly of precious qualities. Their families are the goddesses of the retinue; that is, through union, subsequent union, and the great supreme union, one should meditate on any of the maṇḍalas of the six buddha families. These are explained for the special purpose of secret mantra; they are established through the example of the lion, and summarize a portion of the fifth chapter and part of the ninth.

Now, in order to show the characteristics of the buddha spontaneously present by nature appearing in relative form, and to gather activities within all, the tantra says:

That itself is passion and freedom from passion;
it is desire and it is also liberation.
The three times, the three existences, the three supreme ones,
the three worlds and the three realms,
all the various, distinct
abodes are that itself.

Passion is the sentient beings of the six classes. Free from it are those of the lesser type who have entered the path of the lower vehicles. Desire is intoxication with the five pleasures. Liberation is not being attached even to those. It is the nature of all of them, and moreover it abides in emanated forms in order to train each in their own mind-stream. Therefore the great Vajradhara abides as the nature of both existence and peace. As the Condensed Chapters says:

Firmly, in faultless examination,
because all passions are passionately embraced,
that secret one, the Blessed One,
is great passion, great joy.

Further, with regard to one aspect of existence: gathered in the aspect of the three times, it is the nature of all phenomena of existence, cause, condition and result; and it also abides as the essence of Brahmā, Viṣṇu and Śakra (Indra), who have become the supreme ones among the abodes of the world. The three worlds are below the earth, upon the earth, and above the earth; their lords are Madana, Jayakara and Madhukara. The three realms are the aspects comprised by desire, form and the formless.

Therefore all that abides as the things of the world and its inhabitants, various and distinct, which have become the objects of the six senses, is that itself. As the Condensed Chapters says:

Because he is passionately attached to all accomplishments,
the Blessed One is great passion;
the undefiled bliss that accomplishes all
is the body of Vajradhara, great bliss.

And again:

Because he is the boundless lord of the three worlds,
the tamer, the three existences and the three supreme ones;
because he is all, he is the Three Jewels;
the lord of the three worlds is the three realms.

And again:

Far exalted above beings in merit,
the unmoving is the very self of all;
the tamer is the movement of mind,
because he alone is all formations.

And again:

Because through goodness, passion and darkness (sattva, rajas and tamas)
he tames sentient beings,
he is therefore Samantabhadra;
through the conduct of awakening he cares for others.

This is explained in terms of the common vehicle. For the special meaning, the Seven Chapters says:

Because he liberates, it is pacifying;
the accomplishment of the host of desires is enriching;
abiding in the way of passion is the action of magnetizing;
free of passion, he slays through the wrathful.
In the three times the three bodies abide permanently;
the three existences are purified in the mind as the three bodies;
through the three supreme vehicles he tames beings;
through the deeds of the three paths he clears others' mind-streams;
he thoroughly subdues delight in abiding in the three levels;
he performs the host of deeds and magical displays,
and fully engages in the various activities.

And the root tantra also says:

Attached to the aims desired, the magnetizing;
free of passion, he slays through the wrathful;
the action of desire increases the host of qualities;
through pacifying, he fully liberates.

And so on. The secret activity by way of the expanse is given in the Seven Chapters:

Desire has the aspect of joy;
passion is the mode of subsequent joy;
freedom from passion is the reality free of the limits of joy;
liberation is the innate.

And again:

In the three times, bringing down, holding and reversing;
with the letters of the three existences the three channels are filled;
the three supreme ones become clear,
and the world-realms are made clear.
All phenomena abiding in variety
are nothing other than great bliss.

Concluding these, and as the reasoning, the tantra says:

All beings without exception
in every way come to be here.
That and the rest, the infinite, the supreme,
the dharmadhātu, equal and unequal,
the boundless expanse of space:
all things are that.

That is, all phenomena themselves, abiding distinctly by way of the two vast modes and the mode of shape, are the nature of Samantabhadra. As the Condensed Chapters says:

Always pure, supreme of all,
liberated from the very beginning, the Tathāgata,
Samantabhadra, the self of all,
is definitively bodhicitta itself.

This summarizes most of the seals of the ninth chapter, and is taught through the examples of the precious jewel and the lion.

Now, in order to gather the feast gathering by way of the relative, in which conceptions become manifest, and by way of the meaning of union, the tantra says:

Throughout all of space, the glorious
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Union with All Buddhas,
is the supreme bliss of the Ḍākinī Māyā.

Wherever phenomena pervade as things, their nature, the nature of reality, is in all cases glorious Vajrasattva, just as in a pod full of sesame. The reality of glorious Vajrasattva, who abides in this twofold way, has the character of having gone thus. If one asks what it is: it is accomplished through the feast gathering of the glorious Union with All Buddhas, the Ḍākinī Māyā, Supreme Bliss. As the Seven Chapters says:

Throughout all of space, the glorious
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
abides in the maṇḍala of the feast
and generates the bliss of all the goddesses.

This summarizes the sixth chapter, and is taught through the example of the wild sheep and the lion.

Now, in order to teach accomplishment and the rest by way of union, the tantra says:

What all the buddhas did not attain
in countless koṭis of eons
is attained in this very life
through the rites of the seal.

In the system of the Characteristics Vehicle, those who hold that, by practicing austerities and gathering the accumulations for countless eons and completing the five paths, buddhahood is accomplished, in the provisional sense, do not find the definitive result: because they do not know their own suchness, they will not see the buddha. By knowing the meaning of the two kinds of secret seal, through the rites by way of the four branches of approach and accomplishment, one accomplishes all the common siddhis in this very life and is joined to the path of the supreme siddhi; or else, in this life one attains the supreme siddhi, that of a vidyādhara. Therefore the tantra says:

That is to be examined by oneself:
the very self of all buddhas, the supreme of bliss.
From thoroughly accomplishing oneself,
the very self of all buddhas is Supreme Bliss.

It is to be examined by way of body, speech and mind: by accomplishing the aspect of the maṇḍala of the six families, and that very thing, the own-character of the primordial wisdom of great bliss, its aspect becomes clear and is experienced. As the tantra says:

Those who have entered the path of the Characteristics and Lesser Vehicles
will not see the true buddha.
Abiding in the four branches of approach and accomplishment, the meaning,
the awakening of the well-proclaimed vehicle is accomplished.

This summarizes the stages of accomplishment, the third chapter, and is taught through the example of the goose and the lion.

Now, in order to refute viewing oneself and the deity as two, the tantra says:

Those whose minds are not true to yoga,
who focus on the deity,
take yoga to have the nature of an image:
yoga born from cast figures and the like.

That which shows means and wisdom as nondual is yoga. As the Seven Chapters says:

Emanating the maṇḍala of means,
and accomplishing union in the expanse of wisdom,
yoga is clearly illustrated:
it shows the nondual seal itself.

And again:

Arising from the expanse of wisdom,
well established in the four unions,
yoga has the character of space;
at all times one engages in it.

And the glorious Guhyasamāja also says:

The union of means and wisdom
is explained to be called yoga.
Wisdom is without intrinsic nature;
the magical display of signs is means.

The practitioner who abides in that is said to be "joined." But those entrants who lack it, who with untrue minds view and focus on themselves and the deity as two, and who hold that yoga comes about and arises from images that serve as supports, will not attain accomplishment.

This is also found in Restraining the Elephant and in the Net of Magical Display (Māyājāla):

From no world of the ten directions
will a buddha be found;
since mind itself is the perfect Buddha,
do not seek the Buddha elsewhere.

These points are also taught at length elsewhere. That was a digression made in connection with practice.

Now, to teach the samaya by way of union, the text says:

Yogins of the perfect, supreme mind,
those who focus on the deity,
joining their own samaya with themselves,
will accomplish the sublime unchanging.

That is, by realizing the suchness of all phenomena and abiding in its meaning, one joins the common and the special samayas with one's own self in a nondual way, and if one abides in the sublime unchanging, one will attain accomplishment. This is also stated in the Seven Chapters:

Abiding in yoga with the perfect, supreme mind
of the profound and vast dharmas,
by fully joining the two, samaya and deity,
one's own nature will be clearly accomplished.

This sums up the samayas of the sixth and seventh chapters and explains them through the example of the garuḍa and its young.

Now, in connection with that, to show the distinction between the vehicles, the text says:

In the manner of images and the like
yoga will not arise;
if one strives diligently in the awakening mind,
from that the yoga becomes the deity.

That is, above, viewing oneself and the deity as two was refuted; one who focuses inwardly on one's own meditative absorption is distinguished, as the Great Vehicle, from the lower ones. This is also stated in the Condensed Chapters:

The vast and profound beyond thought,
the inexhaustible bliss of all accomplishments,
free from every stain,
is reliance on the awakening mind as one's self.

And again:

Mind itself is Māra;
mind itself is the Tathāgata.
For the sake of taming one's own mind,
oneself is the Victor over Māra.

And again:

Discerning one's own mind individually
is the supreme awakening mind, the vajra.
Through it, that very one becomes a buddha,
for he cultivates the supreme self-blessing.

This distinguishes the higher and lower vehicles, and explains it through the example of the garuḍa and the lesser birds.

Now, to show the greatness of the benefits, the text says:

This awakening mind is a vajra;
it is the very self of all buddhas.
Therefore, by joining all things with oneself,
one will attain all the buddhas.

That is, by way of the profound and the vast, these four kinds of awakening mind are like a vajra and are the very self of all buddhas. Therefore, if one understands and puts into practice the meanings of view, meditative absorption, conduct and the rest, one will attain it through the power of union. This is also stated in the root tantra:

View, conduct, meditative absorption and practice,
maṇḍala, empowerment, samaya and the gathering,
by joining all activities with oneself,
one will attain all the buddhas.

And the Condensed Chapters also say:

For one who practices this supreme chapter,
Māra himself does not draw near, nor does he lean on him;
together with the host of conceptions, I myself destroy him,
and so that the supreme of the supreme may swiftly be accomplished, he does not rely on him.
Whoever constantly accomplishes my heart essence within himself,
the great supreme accomplishment, the chapter of great bliss,
passion, utter joy, stability and increase,
I bring these about myself, without relying even on the great syllables.

This sums up the medicine of the second chapter and the distinctive benefits of the individual chapters, and explains them through the example of the tortoise.

Now, to show by way of union the result together with its enlightened activity, the text says:

Oneself is all the buddhas,
and all the heroes are oneself.
Therefore, by joining oneself with the deity,
one should fully accomplish oneself.

That is, in order to conclude in a connected way, the first two lines teach the characteristic of the ground. Through direct realization, the buddha in its two aspects, the ultimate characteristic and the conventional appearance, and the hero, the awakening mind, the body of great bliss, are the abiding nature of all phenomena; this is the intent of the Victors. Having realized this through the three modes, the next two lines then give the limbs of the path: through meditative absorption and the rest one should join oneself with the deity and accomplish it. The result of growing familiar with this and making it manifest is:

Through that, all the buddhas,
and all the heroes too,
and all the vajra holders
will be accomplished in this very life.

That is, by the power of meditating on and accomplishing, by way of the definitive meaning, the Vajra Vehicle, all the assemblies of the maṇḍala of buddhas, the assemblies of heroes, the sattvas of the retinue who have entered the door of secret mantra, and the vajra holders who abide like the various classes of vidyādharas, in this life the nature of a buddha and the form of the hero, the vajra holder, will become clear. By accomplishing through that very means, the form of a buddha too will become clear through the stages of the vajra holder. This is also stated below: "This one will be a buddha in a later time." And the Seven Chapters say:

In this life one attains the state of a vidyādhara,
and abiding there, through the power of gradual familiarity,
the great Buddha of the definitive meaning,
the nondual three bodies of great bliss, is accomplished.

This sums up the tenth chapter and teaches it through the example of the jewel.

What has been taught in this way is not the compiler's own invention, for the text says: "the trustworthy source, the Blessed One, glorious Vajrasattva, spoke thus." That is, having liberated the mind streams of those to be trained on six occasions through the wheel of miraculous display of the past, then, in order to care for his followers, Vajrasattva, the nondual three bodies, explained the tantra on the summit of Mount Meru.

From the glorious Union with All Buddhas, the Supreme Bliss of the Ḍākinīs' Illusion (Sarvabuddhasamāyoga Ḍākinīmāyāsaṃvara), the first chapter, the chapter of the wisdom mudrā that enters the domain of the sublime illumination of suchness.

On this: for the sake of those to be trained who delight in the extensive, the Teacher spoke the great root tantra in an extensive manner, as his vast intent. Then, for the sake of those who delight in the condensed, and in order to cut off divergent conceptions, he spoke this condensed tantra, which explains through their symbolic meaning vajra words that carry many intentions. Here, that which has the character of making appear and clarifying the meaning of the three suchnesses, of what is to be known, of what is to be practiced, and of the result to be seen, and of clarifying and sealing the means for entering the domain of the sublime buddhas, is what is called a chapter.

This is also stated in the Seven Chapters:

The dharmas of the knowable, the path, practice and result,
the three suchnesses abiding equally in the two truths,
the domain of the sublime ones, appearing and clear:
this is the very nature of the chapter of the mudrā.

And again in the Condensed Chapters:

From the dharmas free of conception,
one fully conceives for the sake of beings;
that is why it is called a chapter,
since conception is completely purified.

This concludes the explanation of the meaning of the first chapter.

Those who set out on the road by the thousands with no goal are unable to assay jewels, and those who do not know the treatises on jewels and their benefits will not undertake the search or come upon them either. In the same way, those who have not realized the general characteristic, the specific characteristic and the intrinsic nature do not see the true meaning, and since they do not know the particular benefits, why would they engage in it? Therefore the second chapter teaches the ground to be known, the distinctions of view, and, in connection with that, the benefits. This is also stated in the root tantra:

Having examined every direction without exception,
being free of intrinsic nature and essence,
one engages in all the coarse stains,
and the subtle view of liberation becomes deluded.
Alas! Not seeing oneself,
what else will one see and know?

Here, first, the general characteristic of the view:

This is the yoga, the Blessed One,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Union with All Buddhas,
the Ḍākinī Māyā, Supreme Bliss.

This body of great bliss, whose characteristic is the nonduality of means and wisdom, has the nature of yoga. It is the Blessed One because by its very nature it has conquered the hosts of māras and the like, the signs of dualistic conception, and because it has mastered every array of excellent qualities, so that there is no other quality beyond it; it abides as naturally spontaneous and as made manifest through realization. Through the two, meaning and sign, Vajrasattva abides without beginning or end. Transcending body, speech and mind, it abides in its own suchness, the vajra body, speech and mind of the Tathāgata, and so it abides in the mode of the Union with All Buddhas, the Ḍākinī Māyā. This is also stated in the Condensed Chapters:

The being without beginning or end,
Vajrasattva, of great joy,
Samantabhadra, the self of all,
the vajra essence, chief of chiefs.
Because primordially it has no beginning,
it is held to be "the first of the sublime";
because it is chief of all the circles,
it is the sublime lord of the sublime.

Thus it abides in the manner of the twofold buddha. From the side of the conventional, the Seven Chapters say:

The supreme body, with the major and minor marks,
its qualities unceasing, appearing clearly,
endowed with maṇḍala, empowerment and the rest,
its speech, mind and activity appear clearly.

And again:

The protectors, free of obscuration in the three times,
abide in the state of great bliss;
the powers of clarity and the host of fearlessnesses,
self and other not two, appear in their variety.

Their ultimate is the expanse of great peace, emptiness abiding by its very nature, free of every sign of objectification. Their general characteristic is the body of the Ḍākinī Māyā, the wisdom of nondual great bliss. Thus, from the standpoint of its phases it is explained as three truths, while in its own characteristic it is not divided in any way.
