Therefore it was said above: "Of all, without exception, of the mudrā, of all, without exception, of Supreme Bliss."

In order to settle that very meaning through the view, practice it through meditation, and accomplish it, the text says:

If one joins oneself with Vajrasattva,
all the descents will be fully accomplished.

That is, if one joins nondually by way of the three mudrās, the unmistaken aspects of the supreme meaning will be accomplished, because they arise from one's own suchness. This is also stated in the root tantra:

All is great bliss, the vajra mind,
the unceasing play, the body of the mudrā,
appearing conventionally yet without entity,
passing beyond the ultimate, the expanse of illusion.
By joining so that one's own nature becomes clear,
all the descents will be fully accomplished.

The means for realizing that is:

The Union with All Buddhas,
the lord of yoga, great bliss.

Here, first, union with all buddhas is the way expanse and wisdom are connected. The ninth chapter says:

All things have the characteristic of space,
and space too has no characteristic.
The three realms without exception
are all like an illusion.
Just as an illusion, in every respect,
can be seen and can be touched,
yet is not something that can be objectified,
so is the way of all beings.

And again:

Not existent and not nonexistent,
not to be objectified even in the middle:
joining with the perfection of wisdom
fully accomplishes the awakening of the buddhas.

Those meanings are the characteristic of sublime yoga. The lord is endowed with the qualities of perfect lordship, the nature of great bliss. This is to be realized by way of means, by way of wisdom, and by way of nonduality. The Seven Chapters say:

By way of means, great bliss
passes beyond the subtle drop
and does not dwell in the place of the winds;
it is free of the focus of secret mantra.
Through the power of relying on the action of wisdom,
the awakening of the circles is brought down and held,
and radiated in its own place or elsewhere;
it passes beyond inferior shapes such as the channels.
In the state of the nondual Great Seal (Mahāmudrā),
appearance itself is emptiness,
and emptiness itself is appearance;
it plays in the objects of the six senses.

Its reason is given:

Therefore, through bliss itself,
all the bliss of the buddhas is attained.

That is, by way of the cause, bliss, the path is practiced as bliss, and the result is accomplished as the body of great bliss. The Seven Chapters say:

Phenomena, directly perceived, are great bliss,
images without highest, middling or lowest;
sealed with the bliss of appearance and emptiness,
one is completely freed from all objectification.

That is, one directly seals all phenomena with the nonduality of bliss and emptiness. This also teaches the qualities of realization.

Now it is taught that there is no need to practice hardships of body and speech:

Through the Union with All Buddhas,
the lord of yoga, great bliss,
here there is no need for ablutions or restraint,
no need to practice austerities and hardships.

There is no need for the conduct of ablutions and dietary observances of brahmins, śrāvakas and others bound by their own conceptions, for the austerities of the twelve limbs of restraint and purification, for attending the five fires, for the triple staff, the youth's austerity, the cow austerity and the like, or for practicing hardships on the five paths through the conduct of a bodhisattva. If one abides in the meaning of nonduality, the two accumulations will be perfected through every mode of conduct. Therefore:

Without austerity or vows,
it is accomplished through bliss and joy.
Therefore, practicing as one wishes,
eating everything, and likewise enjoying all,
one acts in whatever conduct one likes,
and one moves as one likes as well.

Here the stages of conduct that are deliberately pursued in the scriptures of the lower vehicles are not required, and there is no prohibition. Through any conduct whatever that is free of the ways of brahmins, tīrthikas and the like, it is accomplished by way of bliss of mind and joy of body. For this reason it is taught below that even one who has not purified negative deeds will succeed by practicing the meaning of equality. The Seven Chapters also say this:

However one acts toward whatever,
nondual great bliss becomes one's companion.

This should be understood in terms of the person: the beginner, the one with slight mastery of wisdom, the one with perfect mastery, and the one who is utterly liberated. If, acting as one wishes, it serves the benefit of beings, then even engaging in evil deeds is blameless and does not become a fault. Eating everything means that by enjoying the ten kinds of samaya food together with the meaning of nonduality, they become a limb for gaining accomplishment. Likewise, all means the entire collection of the common and special samayas. Likewise, conduct as one likes by way of body and speech, and movement as one likes by way of mind, means that there is neither prohibition nor injunction. This refutes the extremes of the Lesser Vehicle and of ordinary people. The glorious Guhyasamāja also says:

The mantrin roams like a lion.
For him there is nothing not to be done,
and nothing not to be eaten;
he is adorned with every enjoyment of desire.

Thus, although all phenomena abide as the nature of great bliss, certain beings who have not realized this engage, under the power of delusion, in the very causes and results of the three sufferings and the eight sufferings. The Seven Chapters say:

Not realizing their own nature,
beings deluded into something other,
by imputing the six objects as independent,
appear as the various dharmas of duality.
They engage in states of discordant pleasure and pain,
distracted by the signs of the eight worldly concerns,
and through the afflictions with their branches,
the abodes of the six destinies appear distinctly.
Tormented by the three and the eight sufferings,
through the bond of karma they cling to things.

Again, certain persons of wrongly deluded understanding engage in the path of the tīrthikas, the path of the śrāvakas and the path of the bodhisattvas. Fallen from their own suchness, they remain without tasting the flavor of great bliss; they are to be held with great compassion. The Seven Chapters say:

Alas! Some, through wrong conception,
have wrongly entered evil paths:
deluded about the cause, deluded about the path,
and deluded about the reality of the result,
they suffer even in this world.
With the dog and cow austerities, the triple staff,
the five fires and the like, they torment their bodies,
and when life is spent, go to the three lower realms.
Entering the path of the lowly, inferior family,
wishing for bliss, they stray from that very path.
Wishing to know suffering and abandon its origin,
they yet remain in suffering and its origin;
cultivating cessation and the path as something manifest,
they turn their backs on the noble path.
Alas! Apart from the perfect truth,
they imagine four deluded truths.
Wishing for the ground of the arhat who realizes conditions,
they engage in dependent origination and abandon that very thing.
Through afflictions, karma and suffering,
confused about the nature, they engage in it;
that very thing becomes deluded through signs.
All arises from ignorance,
yet from what other cause did that arise?
All is self-arisen wisdom.
Alas! Wishing for the ground of the arhat,
through the hardships of stream-entry, once-returning,
the conduct of non-returning and the rest,
they enter the arhat path but are not liberated.
Without the meaning of the perfect truth,
grasping at duality in what is imputed,
even practicing hardships for many eons,
they will not attain perfect buddhahood.
Alas! Not finding the definitive meaning,
distracted by other paths, their lives are cut short;
like the wrestler's jewel, it is lost to them on every side,
and the result, the three bodies, will not arise.
Alas! How pitiable these beings are!
From the state of great bliss they have come to this.
From what else has this suffering arisen?
When will they see their own suchness?
When will self-arisen wisdom dawn?
When will they be adorned with great bliss?

Then would they not succeed if they entered the path of the Great Vehicle? Whoever completely gives up his own view, takes wisdom and compassion as his basis, and is endowed with the awakening mind may enter it, and will succeed:

Whether standing or sitting,
walking, or however one abides,
whether laughing or speaking much,
wherever and however one abides.

The Seven Chapters say:

People, a stream of coarse apprehended and apprehender,
their conceptions rising up, circle on and on;
going about with the conduct of tīrthikas,
the deluded host of sufferers brings about suffering.
Those who sit blocking the six objects,
of lesser mind, cling to the path that strays.
The pratyekabuddhas abide as they are;
the many who walk in single file are on the arhat path.
By way of laughter comes the chatter of brahmins;
those who speak are the bodhisattvas.
Turning back from these paths that dwell in conception,
by practicing this meaning one will succeed.

Then would they succeed without having gathered the accumulations and without having practiced with images?

Though one has not entered the maṇḍalas,
and though one is possessed of every obscuration,
if one joins oneself with one's own deity,
though one's merit be small, one will succeed.

First one relies on a holy guru, receives empowerment in a maṇḍala of images, and keeps the samaya. If then, through the instructions, one makes manifest the nature of all phenomena, one will succeed, even without relying on a maṇḍala of practice, even having committed negative deeds before, and even with only the accumulations gathered through body and speech, provided one joins with the meaning of nonduality with one's own deity.

Now, to conclude the benefits of realizing the view, the text says:

Since through this very yoga
everything is accomplished,
through any bad food or bad conduct
one does not become faulty at all.

The root tantra says:

The yogin of the six spontaneously present bodies,
who joins in entering the supreme view,
in the vast expanse where the dharmas that have entities are liberated,
fully accomplishes everything.
Bad conduct is the pure Dharma;
the one who accomplishes it does not come to fault.
Like the bright splendor of the peacock's plumage,
it is pure by its own nature and essence.

These teach the characteristic of the pure view. Now, in connection with that, the benefits of the dharmas of path and result are taught. In their respective contexts these are distinctive characteristics, but here they are given in order to sum up the object to be known and to draw one in by way of their qualities. First, by way of accomplishing the supreme:

What is not attained in ten million eons,
all the sublime buddhas,
whoever practices through the rites of the mudrā
will attain entirely.

The supreme accomplishment, that very state of the six spontaneously present bodies, is the sublime buddha of the definitive meaning. Even if the Lesser Vehicle practices for ten million eons, it will not be attained. Whoever does not know the causes and conditions is devoid of the means for accomplishing the result. If, by way of the special means, one practices any maṇḍala of the assembly of mudrās with the four limbs of approach and accomplishment, one will attain the accomplishment of the Great Seal (Mahāmudrā). If one asks from what:

The supreme means of accomplishing oneself:
that one should examine for oneself.

That is, the source of all qualities, the nature of the three or the six bodies, is one's own characteristic. Whenever it is made clear by wisdom, it arises and is accomplished in one's own stream; that is to be examined in one's own stream. The Seven Chapters say:

The meaning of the four limbs of approach and accomplishment
accomplishes the sublime buddha.
Through that very rite of the mudrā
one views oneself as lord of the maṇḍala,
and from the distinctions of the vidyādhara path
the supreme accomplishment will arise.

By way of accomplishing the common accomplishments:

The Union with All Buddhas,
the Ḍākinī Māyā, Supreme Bliss:
joining oneself with Vajrasattva,
by changing one's attire accordingly.

That is, as a limb for joining with the supreme accomplishment, one should accomplish the worldly activities and the common accomplishments. Through the absorption of the ground and the absorption of support and union, one joins oneself with Vajrasattva. As for the mudrā activities, such as the protection rite of the ninth chapter, and the other activities, these are endowed with dispelling the harmful bhūtas, with protection and with blessing; so one changes one's attire accordingly, by way of supports and substances, in keeping with each particular activity. If one asks what this accomplishes:

All, without exception, that has the nature of the mudrā
will be accomplished, though one's merit be small.

That is, the powers and particular activities found among ordinary worldly beings; and, more commonly, the five clairvoyances possessed by the kings of the gods, the kings of the nāgas and others; the seven precious things; walking on water without sinking; climbing into the sky as on a ladder; passing unobstructed through mountains and cliffs; treasures beneath the earth and the elixir that turns things to gold; the excellent vase, the sword, the bell, the eye ointment, the extraction of essences, swift-footedness and the rest, all the various common accomplishments having the nature of the mudrā that are taught in the fifth chapter of the root tantra, will be accomplished even by a formerly sinful person who has not gathered the accumulations and has not trained for long. What need is there to mention others? The Seven Chapters say:

Changing one's attire accordingly,
the many activities and accomplishments,
all of the various mudrās,
should be practiced as aids to the path.

These teach the benefits of the practice of the third chapter. Through the one-pointedness of the accumulation with signs and the meaning of absence of intrinsic nature, that very thing becomes clear:

Merely by accomplishing this seal,
all the buddhas bestow their blessing;
and once thoroughly blessed,
all the buddhas will gather.

That is, by cultivating familiarity by way of the two mudrās, there is the blessing in which the forms of the two mudrās become clear. From that, through the four mudrās, through mantras and the other mudrās, and through the blessing of one's own suchness, one should gather all the buddhas. The Condensed Chapters say:

Whoever fully cultivates the generation
through the four kinds, meditative absorption and the rest,
if he meditates, all things, as his own self,
will be accomplished in this very life.
Though there is also the union of the being's blessing,
this self-blessing is the supreme accomplishment;
whoever accomplishes it day by day
attains Vajrasattva himself.

Its reason is:

Through union with all buddhas,
one becomes equal with all buddhas;
because one is equal with all buddhas,
all the buddhas will be accomplished.

That is, by joining with the essence of all phenomena, just as their characteristic abides, through the wisdom of one's own awareness, in the manner of the two mudrās, one comes to share an equal fortune with all the maṇḍalas of every direction and time. This teaches that body and wisdom become clear and are accomplished in one's own stream. The root tantra says:

From the buddha of great bliss and illusion,
entering through one's own wisdom,
through the union of the two mudrās,
one comes to share an equal fortune with all the maṇḍalas.

This teaches the meaning of the subsequent activities of the fifth chapter and of the stages of meditation of the ninth chapter, such as the Great Seal. As for the abode of images that serves as the basis for entering the path:

Whoever sees this, endowed with these,
and worships it in every way,
by him all the buddhas too
will have been seen and worshipped.

The first word applies universally: when any person whatever sees a maṇḍala of images, the three endowed with the mudrās of body, speech and mind abide here; if one sees it and worships it in every way, by that all the buddhas will have been seen and worshipped.

This is also stated in the Condensed Chapters:

Just as is the lasting merit
of seeing and worshipping the maṇḍala
of Vajrasattva and the other deities,
so it is with the maṇḍala of images.

This illustrates one part of the seventh chapter. The profound and vast aspects of merit and wisdom are accomplished:

Through seeing and touching
this and all the buddhas,
even those who have not entered the maṇḍalas
will come to see the truths.

That is, having gazed with a joyful face upon this maṇḍala of images and upon all that abides on it in the manner of a maṇḍala of the assembly of buddhas of the families, the circle of deities, and having applied the bliss of the tangible, one will be accomplished through the signs of seeing and touching, by way of the particular accumulations of merit and wisdom. Even those who have not entered the maṇḍala of practice will see the truth, the accomplishment of the nature of reality. The Seven Chapters say:

Having seen the maṇḍala of merit,
through touching the maṇḍala of wisdom,
by way of the joy and bliss of the gathering,
the wisdom of bliss will arise.

This illustrates one part of the meaning of the sixth chapter. Since it ripens and purifies the stains of the three continua:

Through seeing and touching,
through hearing and recollecting,
one is freed from all evils
and accomplishes all the accomplishments of the buddhas.

That is, the four empowerments are conferred on the disciple's continuum; they free him from the four groups of evils and stains and cause him to obtain the four kinds of accomplishment. The Seven Chapters say:

The outer ornaments and the rest are shown,
and one is purified with the water of the deity;
through the touch of the inner empowerment
one is firmly placed on the path of ripening.
Having heard the explanation of the secret meaning,
one is joined with the place of the action mudrā;
through the power of recollecting the experience of that meaning,
one's own wisdom is completely liberated.
The four stains of evil are purified,
and the four accomplishments of the buddhas are obtained.

This illustrates part of the sixth, seventh and ninth chapters. No one is to be passed over on account of his state or his entry:

Through the union of all yogas
with this and all the buddhas,
even women will be liberated
and will touch the awakening of the buddhas.

That is, if one joins, by way of the two samayas, the common and the special, with the nature of all things stable and moving that are comprised in appearance and existence, and of all the buddhas, together with their heirs, of all the maṇḍalas without exception, then even women, whose bodies are inferior and whose wisdom and fortitude are weak, will be liberated; what need is there to speak of others? The Seven Chapters say:

Always hold your own samaya;
always worship the deity and the guru.
Through union with all dharmas,
even lowly women will be liberated.

So one should attend to the sense of "even." This illustrates one part of the meaning of the sixth chapter and the others. By accomplishing the excellent path and result, one acts for the benefit of beings:

At all times, in all ways, all of it,
by oneself, constantly, in all things:
the accomplishment of all the buddhas,
its very nature, will be seen.

That is, for the benefit of oneself and others, at all times, or else in all situations, all the activities, namely pacifying, increasing, protecting, healing, suppressing, subjugating, summoning, dividing, wrathful acts, paralyzing, stupefying, withering, expelling, stirring up conflict and the like, should be undertaken in stages endowed with the union of oneself. If one practices constantly, at all times, one will be accomplished by way of the activities. The Seven Chapters say:

The actions practiced at all times and in all ways,
the power of the twenty activities and the rest,
the supreme discipline of play together with union:
one will see the form of all the buddhas.

This sums up the collections of activities from the latter part of the ninth chapter. As a limb of all the rites, accomplishment comes through the act of pleasing:

This one, in whom all selves abide,
with all the great clouds of offerings,
with supreme and boundless arrays,
makes offering to the Tathāgatas.

By this it is said that through thoroughly training in the accumulations of merit and wisdom, one's own suchness becomes clear, and one abides as the nature of all the buddhas of every direction and time and emanates every offering. These do not arise from conditioned substances: all the clouds of outer, inner and secret offerings arising from one's own wisdom, and all the things stable and moving that dwell in the world of the trichiliocosm, are declared to be the supreme and boundless array, and one makes offering by way of the nonduality of the circle of the maṇḍala. Its quality is:

The glory of offering through union with all as oneself,
having become the supreme Vajrasattva,
the nature of all the buddhas,
Supreme Bliss will be attained.

If one joins with all phenomena by way of the four modes, and enjoys and offers all the appearances of the six senses in the complete purity of the three spheres, one will attain the supreme accomplishment of glorious Vajrasattva, the undefiled wisdom, the nature of all the buddhas, the supreme bliss. The Seven Chapters say:

With offerings that arise from oneself
one should please one's own maṇḍala;
through the power of union with all as oneself,
Vajrasattva, great bliss, is accomplished.

The aid for clearly accomplishing the intrinsic nature of the reality of things is conduct in harmony with the mode, which accomplishes that very result:

Yoga, all, and close application are performed,
and the glory will be accomplished fourfold.

This is the brief presentation: yoga, all, close application and glory make four. In the extensive presentation, first, the stages of conduct are summed up under three points:

All yoga is the Blessed One,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata;
its close application
is the three realms, all without exception.

That which, as the nonduality of means and wisdom, is victorious over all discordant factors and displays the various arrays of its own qualities is Vajrasattva, the nature of all the Tathāgatas. That is the great yoga, which by way of two modes is closely applied to all the three realms without exception. Moreover, in terms of four kinds of person, one enters through the two vast conducts and the three profound doors.

In the proper manner, with all enjoyments,
one relies on them as is comfortable;
through the yoga of one's own deity
one should thoroughly make offering to oneself;
one makes offering through subsequent union;
and when one personally experiences everywhere
the bliss of all the yogas,
one will accomplish it through utmost union.

The beginner, by way of stable meditative absorption, engages in enjoyments at certain times and occasions when it becomes beneficial, and the others are to be rejected; with conduct in accord with the proper manner, one should be shown and should train in how to relate comfortably to the enjoyments of desire, which are poison.

One who has slight mastery over wisdom, in order to grasp the wisdom of bliss without being separated from the yoga of one's own deity, should make offering by increasing the bliss of the coemergent, both example and actual, that arises from oneself.

One who has genuine mastery over wisdom, through the offering that subsequently joins with the bliss of yoga, pleases the deities of the maṇḍala, purifies beings, purifies yoga, and so forth, and thereby clearly puts one's own suchness into practice.

One who is utterly liberated personally savors, everywhere, all the appearances of the six faculties, and enjoys them as the deity maṇḍala clearly unfolds in full; at that time, from that very aspect, one makes the body of great bliss clearly manifest.

Through the power of long familiarization, the result becomes manifest. As the Seven Chapters says:

What makes clear the abiding of suchness,
its helper, is the stages of conduct;
by the distinctions of four kinds of person
the rite of entry is taught according to the meaning.
The conduct the beginner should practice
is to abide at ease amid all enjoyments.
After that, through the yoga of the deity,
one practices for the sake of holding self and others.
The third pleases and purifies;
through clarity of yoga, great bliss expands.
Faculties, objects, times: in all of these one practices;
the liberated one's wisdom is free of defilement.

When the capacity of experiencing clarity becomes directly manifest, one comes to abide in the six spontaneously present bodies. This is the meaning of the verse:

Through this bliss, the elixir (rasāyana)
that is the nature of all buddhas,
the glory of Vajrasattva, long life,
youth, freedom from illness, and supreme bliss are accomplished:
the four glories taught in brief come about.

That is, this Vajrasattva, whose nature possesses every assembly of qualities gathered from the ten directions and the three times, is the intrinsic nature of all buddhas; through the power of the mode of union with him, one accomplishes, by the nectar of deathlessness, that which has become the four glories of great bliss. The glory of Vajrasattva, through vast excellence and the excellence of wisdom, comes to serve the aims of self and others; the three bodies abide in a permanent aspect without beginning or end and act for the welfare of beings; his own body possesses the youthfulness of the major and minor marks, the powers, the fearlessnesses, the masteries, the higher knowledges, and so forth; and having abandoned the hosts of afflictions and the suffering of the conceptions of apprehended and apprehender, supreme bliss is accomplished. As the Seven Chapters says:

The glory of Vajrasattva, life,
youth, freedom from illness, and supreme bliss
enter the abode of all offerings
and sport for the aims of self and others.

If one asks whether this abides only occasionally, now and then, the root text says:

At all times, in all circumstances, in every way,
Union with All Buddhas,
the Ḍākinī Māyā, Supreme Bliss,
is constantly accomplished.

At all times, in all circumstances, in every aspect, it is accomplished as the supreme of qualities. As the Seven Chapters says:

Abiding in the mode of all passion,
Vajrasattva, the body of great bliss,
constantly enters my own abode,
and for the welfare of beings is the Tathāgata.
Through the power of the clarity of one's own essence,
the intent appears in the mindstream.

The all-pervading union of glorious Vajrasattva is by way of the intrinsic nature of all phenomena, and of the path and result that accord with it. This concludes the explanation of the meaning of the second chapter.

Those who, out of great compassion, wish to bring the aims of self and others to completion should apply themselves to the activities by means of the two kinds of accomplishment. Insofar as these are limbs of what is supreme, they arise incidentally and need not be set out separately. But those who have not long undergone hardships with respect to their basis must first actually take up the foundation of the common path. As the root tantra says:

Through practice, accomplishments are applied to activities.
Although from accomplishing the supreme mudrā
the fruit of the work
for self and others becomes complete,
what the beginner should practice
is by way of the common accomplishments.
By practicing, qualities increase;
by practicing, one applies them to activities.
Having long pleased the guru,
one begins with the assembled limbs of the rite.
Those of lesser intellect, lacking faith and diligence,
devoid of the awakening mind, compassion, and wisdom,
deceived by doubts and conceptions,
will not succeed even if they practice for long.

Here the general characteristic is this:

This yoga is the Blessed One,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata;
through Union with All Buddhas,
the Ḍākinī Māyā, Supreme Bliss.

This means of entering yoga by way of the four limbs of approach and accomplishment, which abide in close and direct presence, destroys the net of the two conceptions and endows one with the twofold appearance of qualities. It is the sublime means that indicates the subject matter just as it is, and it has the nature of nonduality. What is it like? It has the character of the path, together with its limbs, that joins one to that nature of glorious Vajrasattva's six spontaneously present bodies: it is the Tathāgata. That very thing has the character of Union with All Buddhas, the bliss of the Ḍākinī Māyā. Therefore the root tantra says:

Through the supreme path, the four limbs of approach and accomplishment,
one applies oneself to the activities one desires;
by the power of entering by way of means,
one makes that Blessed One manifest.

If one asks on what result the subject matter focuses in its practice: it joins one to the state of the six bodies, the six wisdoms, and the six families, and through deeds concordant with these, by way of activities regarding the six objects, it joins one to the very nature of purified enlightened activity.

First, by way of the bliss of the lord:

The yogin of Vajrasattva,
the glorious bliss of one's own pledge,
Union with All Buddhas,
the great bliss of the lord of yogins.

That great Vajradhara, the nature of all the families, who arises from the pure body of great bliss, the nature of the wisdom of great emptiness, in the manner of the inseparability of cause and result, is called Vajrasattva: he has the character of the cause. The paths of entering him bear the name "yogin." Being endowed with the pledge mudrā of Śrī, the meaning that arises from the bliss of union with her is the bliss of the pledge. What accomplishes this very thing is the accomplishment of the meaning of Union with All Buddhas, the great bliss of the lord of yogins, by way of particular modes, mantras, shapes, and teachings. In that manner one should apply it extensively to the others as well.

Second, great bliss:

By the very power
of union with glorious Vajrasattva,
everywhere, all the mudrās of great bliss
are thoroughly accomplished, each in its own way.

The Tathāgata Vairocana, who appears from the body of great emptiness, the nature of the wisdom of the dharmadhātu, appears from the great Vajradhara, the nature of the body of all buddhas. He emanates in various arrays of union, and has a disposition that goes everywhere through activities concordant with his name. By the very power that arises from accumulating his own actions, he brings all phenomena to accomplishment in the aspect of the mudrā of great bliss.

By way of devotion to bliss:

By the very power
of the union of glorious Vajrasattva,
everywhere, all the yogic devotions to bliss
are spontaneously accomplished, each in its own way.

From the wisdom body, glorious Heruka, the nature of mirror-like wisdom, made wrathful by the great compassion of all the tathāgatas, appears in the mind of the great Vajradhara. With rays of clear light he spreads undefiled wisdom; by the power of the two kinds, restraint and correction, he pacifies all the hosts of the arrogant; and he accomplishes, each in its own way, the character of the sublime yoga in the mode of devotion to bliss.

By way of the accomplishments of devotion to bliss:

By the very power
of union with glorious Vajrasattva,
everywhere, all the accomplishments of devotion to bliss
are spontaneously accomplished, each in its own way.

Glorious Padmanarteśvara, who, through the purity of the nondual suchness that appears by the wisdom of equality, the nature of the dharmakāya, cuts through the extremes of superimposition and denial, is the nature of the great Vajradhara's speech. He displays his magic in the suchness of means and wisdom, and by the very power of the enlightened activity of the lotus family he makes all beings joyful, bringing them to accomplishment, each in its own way, by way of the accomplishments of devotion to bliss.

The lordship of devotion to bliss:

By the very power
of the union of glorious Vajrasattva,
everywhere, all the lordships of devotion to bliss
are thoroughly accomplished, each in its own way.

The glorious Blessed One Vajrasūrya, the nature of the saṃbhogakāya, who appears from discriminating wisdom, is a magical display of the qualities of the great Vajradhara. Through deeds concordant with his name he spreads, pervading everywhere, and by the power that arises from his array he adorns everything; thus the lordship of devotion to bliss is accomplished.

The boundless expanse of space:

By the very power
of union with glorious Vajrasattva,
everywhere, supreme women,
boundless as the limits of the expanse of space, will be born.

The emanation, the nature of all-accomplishing wisdom, is the play of the Tathāgata Paramāśva. Through the magical display that appears from the power of union, the nature of the great Vajradhara's enlightened activity, in order to purify all phenomena and to tame the hosts of the arrogant, he emanates the goddesses of the maṇḍala, equal in measure to the boundless limits of the expanse of space. As the root tantra says:

By way of the six buddhas,
he teaches the meaning of that very maṇḍala:
the categories of wisdom and body,
and the families and the hosts of deeds and activities.
By the power of union, everywhere,
lordship, bliss, and the rest are accomplished;
he accomplishes them, each in its own way,
and joins them to the abode of their own reality.

If one asks why, within a nondual nature, they are set out separately: with reference to those on the path, they are distinguished by the distinctions of their stages. If one asks why the distinctions of wisdom are taught in a way that does not accord with other systems: it is intended with respect to the bodies, with respect to the wisdoms, with respect to the families, and with respect to the enlightened activities. As for these, the root tantra says:

Vairocana is the body of emptiness;
great wisdom is Heruka;
nondual equality is the Lord of Dance;
the saṃbhogakāya is Sūrya;
by way of emanation, Paramāśva.
The body of great bliss, Vajrasattva,
is the nature of the six buddhas,
the nondual, spontaneously present body.

And again, by way of the families:

The Tathāgata, supreme Vairocana,
teaches in accord with the meaning of the Great Sage.
Heruka, the vajra tamer,
tames the wrathful by the supreme of wrath.
Padmanarteśvara, the purifier,
free of passion, abides in the mode of passion.
Among the six families, the jewel is supreme Sūrya,
who rains a great rain of jewels in the sky.
Performer of all activities is Paramāśva,
who turns the wheel of the crossed vajra.
The nature of all the families
is the sixth, the supreme great Vajradhara.

As for wisdom, the Condensed Chapters says:

The mirror is the king, the Blood Drinker (Heruka);
in equality, the supreme Lord of Dance;
the discriminating is Sūrya;
in activity, Paramāśva;
in the dharmadhātu, the buddha
is the Great Sage Vairocana.
The nature of the five wisdoms
is great passion, great bliss.

As for enlightened activity, the Seven Chapters says:

Those attached to peace, cessation, and nirvāṇa
are tamed by Vairocana, who abides in existence.
To destroy aversion, Rudra and the rest,
there is the fierce Heruka.
For delusion, he frees from the extremes of superimposition and denial:
the pure maṇḍala of the Lotus Lord.
Those attached to existence, engaged in attachment to karma,
are tamed by the maṇḍala of Sūrya.
Laziness, pride, jealousy, and the like
are subdued by the power of glorious Paramāśva.
For those who abide on the path of the afflictions in general,
there is the consoling body of Vajrasattva.

This is beyond doubt.

The means of accomplishment is putting those meanings into practice: one practices relying on a consort (vidyā), endowed with the four unions by way of the four limbs of approach and accomplishment. For beginners, the practice relies on a consort of light:

Her pledge, and what arises from it;
the lovely pledge, following oneself;
utmost union and great union;
the supreme pledge, the nature of all.

The four limbs of union are union, subsequent union, utmost union, and great union. What indicates the five kinds of awakening mind, the nature of the five wisdoms, is the five awakenings: this is the pledge of light. What arises from it is the causal great Vajradhara and the six principal deities that accord with him. Following that, upon the appearance of light, an object that accords with the family of the object of wisdom, one generates them in the way a shadow surrounds a sprout. Having clearly accomplished their bodies, with the particulars of their ornaments, attire, and emblems, one empowers and blesses them: this is the lovely pledge. Sporting with the four mudrās and offering to the maṇḍala with rays of the light of the awakening mind is "following oneself," that is, subsequent union. Then purifying beings with the rays of light, purifying yoga, and generating the maṇḍala of the support and the supported is utmost union. Blessing the six sense sources and the four places of the expanded maṇḍalas, and contemplating the result of empowerment, is great union. Thus to meditate on all things, the vessel and its contents, as the nature of the maṇḍala, and to abide in that, is the pledge. As the Seven Chapters says:

Absorption in wisdom alone
is explained as what is called union.
Offering through play, blessed together with the consort,
and abiding: this is subsequent union.
Having completed the joy-making maṇḍala,
radiating and gathering is utmost union.
Blessing, empowerment, and the completion of activities
is what is called great union.

The mode and the qualities:

Practicing alone, as one;
practicing together; the lovely subsequent practice:
Union with All Buddhas,
the Ḍākinī Māyā, blazes supremely.

Thus, in group practice, mudrā practice, the practice of substances, the practice of medicines, the practice of the higher knowledges, the practice of the vidyādhara, and the practice of the Great Seal (Mahāmudrā), one should enter by way of the four, approach and accomplishment, through the condensed families, the successive maṇḍalas, the single maṇḍala, or the solitary hero. If one asks what that brings about: in Union with All Buddhas, the power and splendor of the ḍākinī blaze supremely. As the Seven Chapters says:

The yogin who is a beginner,
having gathered the accumulations of merit and wisdom,
relying on a consort of light,
accomplishes by being endowed with the four unions.
Approach and close approach,
accomplishment and great accomplishment, should be done properly.
Approach is engaging in the activities;
close approach is practicing alone, as one;
practicing together is the work of accomplishment;
subsequent practice, great accomplishment, is the supreme result.

And again, by way of the four limbs of approach and accomplishment:

One enters the activities through seven kinds of practice:
group practice, mudrā practice,
substances, medicines, the higher knowledges,
the practice of the vidyādhara, and the Great Seal.
One should enter through the paths of the condensed maṇḍala,
or the six maṇḍalas, or the single one,
or from the solitary hero.

The supreme person, in order to accomplish by relying on the union of faculties, objects, and times:

Of all joys without exception,
all the mudrās are great bliss;
permanent, by the queen of the lotus goddesses,
the offering is supreme among all.

One meditates on every aspect of the inner aggregates, elements, and sense sources, and of the outer faculties, objects, and times, as the maṇḍala of the family of father and mother, means and wisdom. In the way the faculties engage the six objects, by performing nondual play there is joy without exception, that is, undefiled great bliss increases; and all the mudrās, too, are of the nature of great bliss. Because that very thing abides without interruption, it is the sublime permanence; and because it pleases all, it is the supreme offering. As the Seven Chapters says:

The six objects, the mudrās, magically displayed,
are all the consort of nondual play.

And the root tantra too says:

The aggregate of form, the great wisdom of the dharmadhātu:
in the eye, the buddha Vairocana;
he sports with the three kinds of form,
magically displaying all things, stable and moving.
Feeling, the great discriminating wisdom:
in the ear is Vajrasūrya;
he sports with the three kinds of sound,
magically displaying all things, stable and moving.
Perception, the great wisdom of equality:
on the tongue, Padmanarteśvara, the king;
he sports with the three kinds of taste,
magically displaying all things, stable and moving.
Formations, the activity of all-accomplishing:
in the nose, the play of Paramāśva;
he sports with the three kinds of smell,
magically displaying all things, stable and moving.
Consciousness, the great mirror-like wisdom:
in the mind, Vajraheruka;
he sports with the three kinds of phenomena,
magically displaying all things, stable and moving.
The great all-pervading wisdom of emptiness:
in the body abides Vajrasattva;
he sports with the three kinds of touch,
magically displaying all things, stable and moving.

Thereby one is joined to the Great Seal, the body of great bliss, whose dynamism of qualities is unceasing.

Those with slight mastery over wisdom and those with genuine mastery, in order to accomplish by relying on a mudrā consort:

Union with Śrī Vajrasattva,
and all gathered as goddesses:
every good fortune without exception,
and bliss, are accomplished, each in its own way.

Having made it clear through approach and close approach, endowed with the union of Vajrasattva, one blesses the mudrā women of the family in the manner of whichever maṇḍala it may be and begins the play. When one enjoys in the stages of accomplishment and great accomplishment, by that very means every good fortune is accomplished, each in its own way, together with all accomplishments without exception. As the Seven Chapters says:

What the middling person should practice:
in the manner of the action seal,
through the clarity of one's own attire,
one brings about the accomplishments, each in its own way.

In the manner of play with the mudrā:

From sealing with this mudrā,
Vajrasattva, the great deity.

By way of the four limbs of approach and accomplishment, one practices in the manner of the mudrā and of play. If one asks how: Vajrasattva and all the goddesses enter into union:

All the goddesses of great bliss,
boundless as the limits of the expanse of space,
are all intoxicated with the bliss of yoga;
by the power of the sinless, into exaltation.

The yogin whose mind is moistened by the water of love and compassion, and who relishes the taste of the awakening mind, summons all the mudrā women into the exaltation of sinful action and enjoys them; relying on defiled bliss, undefiled bliss arises. If one asks whether this is for oneself alone:

Through mutual supreme joy,
one delights in the manner of a man.

By the taste of passion one generates the bliss of great delight, and accomplishes it closely through the example of ordinary beings. As the Seven Chapters says:

Through the absorption of play with the mudrā,
one pleases the maṇḍalas of the ten directions,
and having placed the hosts of goddesses without exception
into the sphere of wisdom,
through the offering of supreme joy and bliss
one enjoys in the manner of a man.

The practice arising from that, the offering with the awakening mind:

With the arrays of vast hosts
of all the great clouds of offering
that arise from all the faculties,
one makes offering to oneself, the nature of all.

The support that pervades the seven bodily constituents is the coemergent: the ten winds, whose nature becomes clear as they abide in the five faculties and engage in all their respective functions, and the wind that moves in a single direction and serves as the support for the abiding of life. They radiate and gather from the three channels out to the ten million and four hundred thousand body hairs. The awakening mind that arises from that and signals it spreads everywhere and makes offering to one's own maṇḍala. As the root tantra says:

What serves as the support of life and exertion
arises from all the faculties.
The stream of bliss, the awakening mind and the rest,
spreads from the places of all the body hairs;
it pleases the maṇḍala of the Victors
and is applied to the welfare of beings.

At the beginning and middle of the four limbs of accomplishment, and at the time of receiving the accomplishments, one should give the empowerment that accords with the activity:

Through all the blisses of yoga,
arising from the great bliss of empowerment,
through all the hosts of jewels,
one is empowered to experience it fully.

The bliss of the yogin is endowed with the bliss of the two signs, the meaning of the nonduality of means and wisdom. By it all the hosts of stains are washed away; and since it is ascertained from the signs of the bhaga and the liṅga, it is endowed with the hosts of jewels. By way of the faculties and of experience at the crown, upon the tongue, at the heart, and so forth, one should confer the empowerment in such a way that it expands and is experienced through the wisdom of bliss. As the root tantra says:

Savoring the taste of undefiled joy and bliss,
one abides in the state of great bliss.
Its sign, arising from the empowerment,
is the river's stream of the two, melting and bliss;
adorned with all the hosts of jewels,
it joins one to the abode of one's own wisdom.
The secret empowerment, unsurpassed,
delights all places and produces hosts of fruits.

By this one should also understand it as a particular form of empowering disciples through an image maṇḍala and a feast gathering.

So that the yogin endowed with the four unions may engage in the enjoyments of the goddesses and so forth:

The enjoyment of all the goddesses
should be relied upon as is comfortable.

By way of the four kinds of person, one should rely, as is comfortable, on the mudrā women, beginning with the consort of light, and on the hosts of enjoyments, according to the time, the particulars of realization, one's constitution, companions, and so forth. As the root tantra says:

In all the enjoyments of yoga,
according to distinctions of time and so forth,
the activity that serves the aims of self and others
should be relied upon as is comfortable.

This is put into practice by making clear the aspects of the four unions. The first union:

Through union with the deity of great nature,
one thoroughly makes offering to oneself by oneself.

Having become the body of the causal great Vajradhara through the five awakenings is the great deity, the nature of all buddhas. One joins that very deity to the mudrās of body, speech, and mind, and through ornaments, attire, empowerment, and blessing one makes offering to oneself by oneself.

Subsequent union:

One makes offering through the subsequent union
of the bliss of all the yogas.

One generates the consort that accords with the family. Just as, for example, at a time when there is the appearance of light a shadow arises for a sprout, so the appearance of wisdom magically displays an image that accords with its cause, the dharmadhātu. Preceded by empowerment and blessing, and urged on by signs and the like, she makes play; abiding in the meditative absorption of bliss for the aim of all the yogas, through the offering of subsequent union one pleases the Victors and purifies beings.

Utmost union:

Since one experiences it in everything,
one will accomplish through utmost union.

Having purified the outer signs through the bliss of light, one generates the maṇḍala of the support and the supported in the secret place.

Great union:

Through the bliss of the elixir (rasāyana)
that is the nature of all buddhas,
the life of Vajrasattva is accomplished,
and youth, freedom from illness, and supreme bliss are accomplished.

Through blessing the six sense sources, blessing the four places, the sign of the crown ornament, and empowering and bathing with the vase of the goddesses, that bliss of the elixir, the stream of the awakening mind, accomplishes the fourfold glory. As the root tantra says:

Through the four limbs of approach and accomplishment
one should put the result into practice.
Through the stages of the four unions
one is thoroughly joined to its path.

By way of the methods of union of body, speech, and mind:

The great body of all buddhas,
the pleasant speech of all buddhas,
the great mind of all buddhas,
the great offering of all buddhas.

According to the distinctions of the support, one should accomplish union in the stages of the profound and the vast. Moreover, as a special point, one should make offering to and accomplish the three maṇḍalas of body, speech, and mind: by way of the body, Vairocana and Vajrasūrya; by way of speech, Padmanarteśvara and the play of Paramāśva.

By way of the mind, Vajradhara and Heruka.

By mantra, one makes clear the suchness of the three syllables, the radiating and gathering of the arising, entering, and abiding of mantra. By shape, one purifies the places of body, speech, and mind by way of the three channels and the four cakras, and accomplishes offering. By phenomena, one indicates the meaning of great bliss, the means of joining to the nondual nature: appearing forms, resounding sounds, and the conceptions of the mind, by way of that very nature, indicate the undefiled path of the wisdom of great bliss. As the root tantra says:

In the maṇḍala of body, speech, and mind
one should enter by practicing that very thing;
through the distinctions of the four, approach and accomplishment,
the bliss that is the result of the path is accomplished.

And again the Seven Chapters says:

Through the radiating and gathering of body, speech, and mind,
he gains mastery over the places of the three winds;
the Blessed One pervades everywhere:
this is one's own wisdom, liberated.

And again:

Through the three channels of body, speech, and mind,
the great bliss and joy of the cakras is generated;
the Protector, having fully risen,
gazes in all directions.

And again the root tantra says:

Protector of appearance and existence, undefiled,
liberated, the body of liberation;
the hosts of conceptions of body, speech, and mind
are sealed with the seal of illusion, spontaneously present.

Through those paths the maṇḍala of the families becomes clear.

By way of the methods of union of the six families:

Great king of all the buddhas,
Vajra Holder, lord of all,
Lord of the World, chief of all,
Lord of Jewels, master of all.

Vairocana is the tathāgata family, the king who holds the body of all buddhas. Vajrasattva himself, in wrathful form, is Heruka, the Vajra Holder. The sixth family, the great Vajradhara, is Vajrasattva, the nature of all the tathāgatas. Padmanarteśvara, since he looks upon all beings with his eyes, is the Lord of the World, having become the nature of all the speech families. Vajrasūrya holds sway over the kingdom's hosts of jewels and has become the nature from which all the hosts of jewels arise. Paramāśva acts as the chief of the great gods of the world, and through the four kinds of activity he abides as the chief of the goddesses.

Those who delight in the expansive practice and closely apply these in an elaborate manner, in the maṇḍala of the condensed families; others practice and apply them by dividing the six maṇḍalas according to the stages of approach and accomplishment; others practice and apply them in terms of a single maṇḍala; and for those who delight in the extremely condensed, they are practiced and applied by way of a single family. Therefore the root tantra says:

In the seven limbs of approach,
having clearly applied it closely,
through the four modes of concordant engagement:
this is called the practice of activity, the close approach.
Having transferred the place from the former,
one should accomplish it with a form concordant with the family.
By applying the result at the end of the path to the activities,
the great accomplishment, the accomplishments appear as they should.

Its benefit:

If one practices joyfully with them,
one goes, comes, and rises at will;
the great accomplishment of all the goddesses,
a wheel-turner, is thoroughly accomplished.

Being endowed with union, through the limbs of action, such as the four excellences that precede the practice, and through the stages of the four, approach and accomplishment, one practices and applies the maṇḍala of the six families. Thereby the result that accords with it arises: going, coming, and rising at will, and becoming a wheel-turner of the maṇḍala, the nature of the great accomplishment of the great goddess, is thoroughly accomplished.

Then the second chapter says:

Union with All Buddhas,
the Ḍākinī Māyā, Supreme Bliss:
joining oneself to Vajrasattva,
by transforming one's attire just as it should be,
the lord of all the various mudrās
will be accomplished even with little merit.

By this, when one delights and enjoys through union in the manner of the two maṇḍalas, worldly accomplishments and the common activities will be accomplished. If one asks whether that result is undertaken as a limb for accomplishing that very thing: relying on it, one attains all the supreme in this very life. As the first chapter says:

All the sublime buddhas,
not attained in ten million eons,
will be attained everywhere
if one practices by whatever rites of mudrā;
that supreme means of accomplishing oneself
I shall set forth.

To show that there is no accomplishment higher than this:

Equal and unequal to the dharmadhātu,
boundless as the limits of the expanse of space,
devotion to the bliss of all accomplishments:
there is nothing else more vast and supreme than that.

Within the boundless limits of the expanse of space, comprising the equal and the unequal, the various things of all phenomena and the aspects of their nature of reality, the lord of accomplishment is the six spontaneously present bodies. Apart from devotion to that very thing in bliss and making it clear, there is no other result so vast.

To show that all of these are accomplished in this life:

Through Union with All Buddhas,
that great bliss of the lord of yogins,
there is no devotion to the bliss of all accomplishments,
nothing sublime and vast,
equal and unequal to the dharmadhātu,
boundless as the limits of the expanse of space,
that is not accomplished in every way
from this, in this very life.
Devotion to bliss through all the lordships.

By way of an excellent cause, excellent conditions, and an excellent path, one attains in this life the result of a vidyādhara, and that gives rise to the supreme accomplishment. In the manner of the vidyādhara of devoted conduct, the vidyādhara applied to activities, the vidyādhara practicing the path, the vidyādhara of the ripened path, the vidyādhara of the Great Seal, and the vidyādhara of completed activity, one practices the conduct of secret mantra. As a buddha, one purifies in order, and attains all at once, the grounds of Universal Light, Lotus Eye, the Great Assembly of the Wheel of Syllables, Vajradhara, Unsurpassed Wisdom, and Great Bliss.

Moreover, the root tantra says:

"By the stages of the four of approach and accomplishment,
one applies oneself fully to the aim one desires;
if one undertakes austerities with these,
one goes, comes and rises up at will.
As a universal monarch of the families,
one accomplishes any lord whatsoever.
In this very life one attains the state of a vidyādhara,
and by that, great bliss is accomplished."

And again:

"Through the seven kinds of vidyādhara,
one joins with the abode of one's own wisdom;
by the distinctions that accord with secret mantra,
one shows what accords with the devoted mind and with the meaning.
Liberation, undefiled buddhahood,
is attained by this, brought to completion.
Universal Light and Lotus Eye,
the Wheel of Syllables, Vajradhara:
having trodden the ground of Unsurpassed Wisdom,
one abides in undefiled, everlasting bliss."

And the Seven Chapters also says:

"In order to accomplish buddhahood in this life,
one should become familiar with the grounds of the three bodies."

To demonstrate this very point decisively as a reason, it is said:

"Union with All Buddhas,
the Ḍākinī Māyā, Supreme Bliss, bliss:
in this, in this very life,
there is nothing whatsoever that is not accomplished in every way."

The chapter called "The Vajra Commitment of the Glorious Fortunate One, Union with All Goddesses": it is union with all the maṇḍalas of accomplishment by way of the four branches of approach and accomplishment; it is endowed with the fortune of the two kinds of accomplishment; and it is called the chapter that abides in the state that makes clear the commitments of the body, speech and mind of glorious Vajrasattva, who is the very nature of that. This concludes the explanation of the meaning of the third chapter.

One who knows the meaning of suchness should make it manifest by putting it into practice, meditating on it and accomplishing it. Its aid is the stages of conduct, and these are to be entered by way of four kinds of individuals. Therefore the root tantra says:

"The means that makes known what abides as the intrinsic nature,
characterized by meditation and accomplishment,
has as its supreme aid the stages of conduct;
it is entered in the manner of the four individuals."

First, the general characteristic of conduct:

"This yoga is the Blessed One himself,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
Union with All Buddhas,
the Ḍākinī Māyā, Supreme Bliss."

All these things, stable and moving, comprised by appearance and existence, appear in the character of self-arisen wisdom. Since the magical display of method and wisdom is endowed with its own suchness, it abides as the Blessed One himself. Furthermore, there is not even an atom of movement away from the essence of the spontaneously present six bodies; since it cannot be shaken by any adverse factor, it is known by various designations such as Vajrasattva and Great Bliss, and it is the very nature of all the tathāgatas. The yogin endowed with that performs every action to be done through the three doors in accordance with the meaning of secret mantra, as union with all buddhas, in the manner of the bliss of the Ḍākinī Māyā. Thus the root tantra says:

"Seeing the suchness of one's own wisdom,
through one's own attire made clearly manifest,
in actions that accord with that very conduct,
the yogin does not act for any purpose.
The wise one who abides in this disposition
is truly the fortunate one."

First, the conduct common to the three individuals, taught together:

"United with all the goddesses,
desiring the supreme fortunate lady,
experiencing the bliss of perfect union,
firm in constant passion."

The beginner, distinguishing them by the division into the six families, unites with all the goddesses by way of the sixty-four of method and wisdom. For the sake of empowerment, the feast gathering, the offering of accomplishment and the holding of the life wind, he enjoys the desire of the supreme fortunate lady. Having experienced the bliss of perfect union, the bliss of letting the stream descend, of holding it, and of turning it back upward again, he enjoys the two passions in a manner free of the two passions, and abides constantly firm.

Those with slight mastery over wisdom and those with full mastery over wisdom rely on the action seal (karmamudrā) in the manner of the higher meaning. Until the wisdom of bliss has arisen, they hold the bliss; by merely seeing and focusing on method and wisdom, they become supreme among the fortunate; they gather the essence born from the melting of those two from all its own places and send it forth everywhere; and they also properly enjoy other sensual pleasures with their own seal. Through experiencing the bliss of perfect union, the body of great bliss becomes constantly firm. Thus the root tantra says:

"The door of joining method and wisdom,
the supreme intelligence of the action of entering,
is well joined through the stages of support,
and abides relying on great passion.
By the first, one finds knowledge of the ways of the phases,
and applies oneself to the work of oneself and of beings;
by the second, one comes to see suchness;
the third gives rise to the limb of mindfulness.
United with all the goddesses,
one abides supremely in the bliss of fortunate union."

The conduct of the fourth, who has become supreme:

"The great forms of all the buddhas,
their melodies, scents and tastes,
the supreme touch of all the buddhas:
these he enjoys as his own enjoyment."

The signs of actual realization in what is enjoyed as the objects of the six senses are the appearance of wisdom. When yogins endowed with nondual understanding enjoy them as the maṇḍala of gods and goddesses, the undefiled wisdom that makes manifest the body of great bliss increases. Thus the root tantra says:

"Whatever sense faculty comes onto the path,
its very essence,
through the yoga of knowing equality,
all appears as the nature of the buddhas.
In the eye is the protector Vairocana;
the ear is the victor Vajrasūrya;
the nose is Paramāśva himself;
the mouth is Padmanarteśvara;
in the mind is Vajraheruka;
the body is Vajrasattva.
Thus one endowed with perfect union,
whose nature is compassion, practices.
With activity just as one wishes,
eating everything and doing everything, one abides thus.
Through a mind free of conceptual thought,
suchness will be accomplished."

Again, the beginner should train by way of what is held in common:

"Buddhahood and the perfections are accomplished,
and the dhāraṇīs and the grounds likewise,
if one practices with delight; so what need is there to speak
of the fortunate women of gods and humans?"

By way of what is held in common with the Characteristics Vehicle, one should gather the accumulations, train in the path, and make the result manifest.

What is that result? It is the taking on of the aspect of the qualities of the buddhas of the provisional sense, who abide in the continuum of the tenth ground. The path is the ten perfections, comprised of generosity, moral discipline, patience, diligence, meditative concentration, wisdom, skillful means, power, aspiration and primordial wisdom. By this the concordant results are accomplished one by one, and through the power of applying oneself with delight, the four doors of dhāraṇī and the ten grounds are accomplished in an instant. What need is there even to mention the women of gods and humans and so forth? Thus the root tantra says:

"The yogin who is a beginner
practices cause, condition, path and result
through the vast common conduct:
the way in which all are perfected at once."

This is not accomplished by practicing austerities for a long time, as in the Characteristics Vehicle; rather, it is perfected in a single instant.

The benefits:

"The accomplishments of the gods, hard to gain,
the awakening of the buddhas, great bliss,
the experience of the bliss of great fortune:
each follows and is accomplished of itself."

They follow and are accomplished by way of conduct. That is, the lesser accomplishments, hard for ordinary worldly people to accomplish, and the supreme accomplishments, the awakening of the buddhas, great bliss, the accomplishment of Vajrasattva, the Fortunate One, follow and are accomplished by way of their respective methods.

The method of practice is this: one focuses on all things as the father and mother of whichever is the lord of the family, and, making them clear as the seals of one's own body, speech and mind, one should accomplish and enjoy them:

"Arising from all accomplishments,
the glory of all the tathāgatas,
all the enjoyments of the Fortunate One:
these he enjoys as his own conduct."

Here the first phrase extends to all the rest. It means enjoying any accomplishments whatsoever that serve as support, cause, path and result. Thus the root tantra says:

"From the power of the union of the Fortunate One,
the sixteen accomplishments, the glory of excellent fruit,
born from the sixteen stations of approach and accomplishment,
arising from all accomplishments,
likewise the supreme enjoyment of the Fortunate One:
by the divisions of the sixteen supports,
according to the stations of the stages of characteristics,
these he enjoys as his own conduct.
Vajrasattva, lord of all the families,
adorned with all the enjoyments of desire,
through the power of practicing on the sixteen grounds,
enjoys all phenomena without exception in bliss."

First, the sixteen supports are distinguished by the division into four individuals. For the beginner, beginning from the intent of meditative absorption, there is clarity up to yoga, clarity up to anuyoga, clarity up to atiyoga, and clarity up to mahāyoga. For one with slight mastery over wisdom, again from the higher station, there is the activity of focusing on the wisdom maṇḍala, the activity of focusing on the maṇḍala in front, the activity of focusing on the nondual maṇḍala, and the activity of focusing on the emanation and gathering of the four seals. For one with full mastery over wisdom, it is in the manner of making clear, in sequence, recitation, the drop, the subtle and the pure, or else the four, namely body, speech, mind, and emanation and gathering. For the fully liberated, all sense faculties, objects and times are made clear in the aspect of the gross maṇḍala, in the aspect of the subtle parts, and as the nonduality of appearance and emptiness, and they are sealed with great bliss.

These sixteen paths, thus divided by way of the supports, also comprise the seven kinds of vidyādhara. Having become clear is the culmination of the five signs. Moreover, some tantras distinguish and teach the four individuals by way of the five signs, but here this is not explained clearly. Thus the Seven Chapters says:

"Through the characteristics of the sixteen supports,
their clarity is the path, sixteen in number;
having found the seven supreme stations of the vidyādhara,
one joins the six fields of the Victors."

And again:

"From the divisions of the four yogas,
one accomplishes the absorption of the beginner;
in the manner of the four focuses,
one finds slight mastery over wisdom;
from the emanation and gathering of the four branches,
the supreme station of the wisdom being becomes clear;
gross, subtle, emptiness, and the seal of bliss:
this is full liberation."

The sixteen of approach and accomplishment are: the four of approach and accomplishment focused on any single deity of a family; the four of approach and accomplishment focused on any single maṇḍala of a family; the four of approach and accomplishment in which the maṇḍalas of the six families are meditated on in sequence; and the four branches of approach and accomplishment in which the maṇḍala of the condensed families is meditated on. Thus the Seven Chapters says:

"Generating the body through the yoga of manifest awakening,
the clarity of the family ornaments is the activity of approach.
Like the shadow of a sprout in the light,
the clarity of the companion is near approach.
Blessing, empowering and urging:
this is called the practice of accomplishment.
The mind of awakening, the source from which all assemblies arise,
is the manner of the great accomplishment of the hosts of activity."

And again the root tantra says:

"Meditating clearly on the maṇḍala of clarity,
through completing the two activities of pleasing and recitation,
one approaches the maṇḍala of the retinue;
approach made clear is near approach.
Endowed with all the branches of enlightened activity,
one applies oneself to the activities that accomplish the desired aims;
through the activities of knowing the result,
accomplishing the accomplishments is the manner of great accomplishment."

And again the Seven Chapters says:

"Vajrasattva and the others,
endowed with great compassion,
the retinues of the five maṇḍalas and so forth,
pleased by peaceful offerings:
for the sake of approach, through them
the root maṇḍala is properly beheld.
Having grown familiar with near approach
through the maṇḍala of the four activities,
one shifts the station from the former one
and contemplates the maṇḍala of accomplishment.
In order to receive the great accomplishment,
like the massing of the fire at the end of the aeon,
when one sees firm signs,
one should undertake the great accomplishment."

And again the root tantra says:

"In the manner of the four branches of approach and accomplishment,
one should accomplish the maṇḍala of the condensed families.
Through the wheel of the accumulation of merit
and the wheel of the accumulation of wisdom,
the most beautiful wheel of the families
is accomplished through perfect activity."

That is, the condensed and the expanded forms comprised in the accumulation of merit are approach and near approach; the condensed and the expanded forms comprised in the accumulation of wisdom are accomplishment and great accomplishment.

The sixteen accomplishments, from the root tantra:

"The sword, the eye ointment, swiftness of foot, force,
the vase, the pill, the elixir that turns things to gold,
nectar, the essence elixir, the vidyādhara:
these are the common accomplishments, supreme among activities.
The excellent auspicious accomplishment is
the all-pervading body, speech, qualities and mind,
radiating and pervading, the basis of glory,
fearless, of supreme power, the manner of a king."

The sixteen grounds, again from the root tantra:

"Through the ways of the vidyādhara kings,
devoted in accord with all the buddhas,
having abandoned the common vehicles,
united with all the buddhas,
treading the sixteen supreme grounds,
one enters the station of one's own great bliss."

Why are these taught at this point? Conduct is a branch that aids view, meditation and accomplishment; therefore they are stated here so as to gather them into a place where the wording is brief.

What arises from that is the glory of all the tathāgatas, Vajrasattva, and he is called the Fortunate One. All the enjoyments that are the appearance of his qualities, the yogin who has entered the family maṇḍala enjoys as his own conduct. Thus, for his own sake, he also applies himself to the other family maṇḍalas, and in order to bring consorts, accomplishments and so forth under his control:

"The supreme accomplished lady of the vajra holder,
the glory of the supreme Lord of the World,
the sublime bliss of the pure buddhas:
having seized them, he enjoys them intimately."

From the vajra family of glorious Heruka he summons the consort who appears in the form of a great wrathful one, the supreme accomplished Krodhīśvarī, together with the blessings and powers of wisdom, and enjoys them intimately himself. Glorious Padmanarteśvara is the Lord of the World, and he summons his supreme consort, glorious Pāṇḍaravāsinī, and the consort of the Buddha Vairocana, the sublime bliss, Buddhalocanā.

Again it is said:

"The three realms without exception,
all the great kingdoms of the buddhas,
boldly, by the strength of his prowess,
having seized them, he enjoys them intimately."

Having seized the great qualities possessed in the continuum of glorious Vajrasūrya, the kingdom of the three realms, and the consort Māmakī, he enjoys them intimately.

"The great accomplished lady of all the buddhas,
the sublime supreme sovereign lady:
if, having seized them entirely, one enjoys bliss,
what need is there to speak of other enjoyments?"

He summons the great qualities and the wisdom of glorious Paramāśva, and the consorts, the Great Accomplished Lady of All the Buddhas and the Supreme Sovereign Lady, and enjoys them. These are enjoyments that transcend the world, for his own sake; they should be understood in accordance with the meaning of mantra, form and Dharma.

Now, in order to work for the sake of others by way of enjoying the worldly ones:

"From the Mighty One, the Auspicious Steadfast One;
from the Stealer, the Accomplished One; from Smara, Rati:
having seized them by the force of boldness,
he gives them to the faithful."

By the power of bringing them under control through the path, he first gathers the wives of the great worldly gods, enjoys them himself, and gives them to other beings. These are the great gods who possess the power to subdue all the hosts of fierce gods and fierce goddesses of the three worlds. Their master is Viṣṇu, the Mighty One, and his wife, the sovereign lady Lakṣmī, the Auspicious Steadfast One, is summoned by the power of mantra, mudrā and meditative absorption. Maheśvara, Mahādeva, the Great God (Śiva), steals the radiance of all beings, and his wife, Umā the Accomplished, is summoned. And the wife of the god of desire, Smara, the lord Kāmadeva, the glorious Garlanded One, Rati, is summoned.

The sequence of seizing them by the force of boldness is as follows. One who, by the power of great compassion, has generated the mind toward supreme awakening, should, by way of the vast conduct of the perfections, keep his own commitments and also undertake this for the many aims of benefiting others. In a place where there are signs of the great god, endowed with the four excellences, having blessed the three doors, he meditates on himself as the deity of the family. He purifies and makes clear himself and beings, contemplates the sense faculties, pleases the noble ones with the ten branches, enters the immeasurables, and sets up the protection wheel. Focusing on the absorption of the ground, the absorption of the support, the absorption of union, the wisdom maṇḍala, the maṇḍala in front, and the nondual maṇḍala, he brings forth clearly the signs of recitation and so forth.

He draws the maṇḍala of Viṣṇu: on a blue maṇḍala marked with eight petals and smeared with the five products of the cow well mixed, he scatters blue flowers. He should prepare substances known as the seals of all offerings and substances known as the torma seals of all offerings, and in particular a white incense compounded of seven ingredients, or the pills of Avalokiteśvara, grape wine mixed with bezoar and the three sweet substances in a vessel of turtle shell, and a torma compounded of nine kinds of food.

There he surrounds the support wheel with the mantra of Viṣṇu, places it in an ivory stūpa, and sets it inside a jeweled vessel or a bowl filled with blue flowers, incense and the like. He pleases the maṇḍalas generated from his own seed syllable and mantra by summoning, absorbing, making offerings and so forth, and when he makes them stable with his own mantra, signs will be seen. Then the god, in a terrifying form, offers flowers and remains there. Showing the seal of commitment and making offerings, he overpowers him with mantra and mudrā and places him inside the stūpa; he should join the vessels mouth to mouth and wrap them in blue cloth.

Again, for the goddesses, the second: he smears with saffron the support wheel of Lakṣmī, adorned with mantra, fills a jeweled vessel with white flowers, and should summon her with her own mantra and mudrā. With a hundred thousand recitations of the mantra, a rain of flowers falls and the sound "sang sang" resounds; with a hundred thousand recitations of the secret mantra she shows her form. When he overpowers her, shows the seal of commitment, empowers and blesses her, she will show a joyful face. Then the goddess says "alala" to the practitioner, gives him jewels, and remains before him.

When commands are given to her, she comes under one's control and accomplishes the activities one desires. Through this she enters in the manner of mantra and also petitions her own great god, and they, together with their retinues, become like servants. In the same way one brings under control the other wives of the great gods and so forth. In extensive form, the root tantra says:

"That very yoga is the Blessed One.
Through the union of Vajrasattva
one joins fully with the family maṇḍala,
and through the clear rites of meditation and recitation
one seizes the sovereign of accomplishments, supreme bliss,
and gives it to others.
Through the application of the force of boldness
one bewilders the Mighty One and the rest,
and the hosts of enjoyments and the wives
one enjoys oneself and gives to others.
The supreme place of practice is delightful,
a place that bears the signs of a great god,
a grove of trees with fruit and flowers;
one should practice on the bank of a river.
Endowed with the four excellences,
one undertakes the rite of the substances and the ground.
On a blue, square maṇḍala,
with four gates, garlands and the elements,
in the center an eight-petaled lotus
is beautifully adorned with blue flowers.
Arranging various offerings and tormas,
one should set out special offerings.
A wheel well drawn on cloth,
with eight spokes and a beautiful double rim,
one smears with four substances,
bezoar, the juice of jasmine, indigo and ren de,
at the center, the tips and the rim;
it should be made a cubit in size.
In the center, haṃ maṃ he raṃ be kṣa;
in the east, ma no ri ba suṃ;
in the south, bruṃ kṣa kruṃ tat ṭa;
in the west, suṃ bag ṣan ca ra;
in the north, ni raṃ dhaṃ me ha;
in the intermediate directions, kruṃ dhaṃ ru baṃ si;
uṃ thā śu ṭa khaṃ bha be taṭ;
na mo sarba su ga ta kṣa.
One draws the signs, five at each edge,
and the vowels and consonants on the rim,
with what is made from the continuum of the elements.
Endowed with five kinds of Śaṅkara flowers,
the ivory emblem together with the wheel
is placed well, and incense and scents are heaped up.
Through the union of Vajrasattva and the others
one should summon the Mighty One with his retinue;
pleased by the substances of commitment,
they should be made stable with mantra and mudrā.
To the secret mantras of the nine groups
one joins oṃ and svāhā at the beginning and the end,
and through the recitation that makes the single root clear
one applies them in sequence to summoning and the other activities.
Through that, the sign appears in a terrifying manner:
black, greatly fierce, in a wrathful manner,
with wheel, trident, arrow and bow,
roaring and moving through the sky.
Then one binds him with the mudrā of vajra utterance and suppression,
and he offers flowers before one.
One shows the overpowering seal of commitment,
makes offerings, and should proclaim the commitments.
When he has gone to his own abode,
one suppresses him again and seals him with the mudrā;
separating him from his domain, in a jeweled casket
one covers the stūpa with blue cloth.
oṃ aṃ bhu ba ga ta na ra ya ni ja jā
Having urged her with the commitment mantra,
one should draw the signs of the wheel.
Undertaking it as before,
one should smear it with fine saffron.
In the center, de bi siddhi jaḥ;
on the directions, the intermediate directions and the rim one draws the corresponding mantras.
One fills it with white Śaṅkara flowers,
and with her own mantra, beautifully adorned with oṃ and svāhā,
and with her mudrā, one summons her.
When the count of a hundred thousand secret mantras is complete,
a rain of flowers falls from the sky,
and various sounds and voices resound.
With two hundred thousand, an unpleasant form appears;
with three, a lovely form is shown:
taking form, Lakṣmī, the color of a lotus,
bright red, holding a trident and a lotus.
One shows the overpowering seal of commitment,
