and should empower and bless her.
With the radiance of her joyful face expanding,
she speaks in return to the practitioner:
'Great wonder, alala!'
and gives the jewel in her hand.
Commanded before one and empowered,
she accomplishes the activities one desires.
By the very power of the application of mantra,
she enters the maṇḍala of secret mantra.
Through the rite of releasing the mudrā,
she petitions the god, the Mighty One,
and again, through the assembly with its retinue,
she accomplishes every desire as soon as it is recalled.
oṃ haṃ cakra na ya ra ni bisnu kamba ladha ha rū pa hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
oṃ na mo bi mu sa ma de bi kaṃ pa la ra da nan hūṃ smṛ ta ya phaṭ svāhā
The great seal of the wheel and trident
comes to be applied to the activity of accomplishment.
The yogin of Vajrasattva,
who makes the bliss of the Lord abide,
on a dark blue triangular maṇḍala
adorned in the center with the sign of a trident,
with a square, gates and corners,
encircled by lotuses, fire and light, and garlands,
beautifies it in various ways
with all the ornaments of offering,
and sets out the substances of sun, pearl and lamp,
placing them in a turtle-shell vessel.
Incense of five kinds of scent,
pills of seven kinds of scent,
nectar, bezoar and white butter,
together with a vessel of mother-of-pearl, should be placed there.
The wheel drawn on birch bark or the like,
bearing the sign of a trident,
with a hub, eight spokes and two rims,
should be made twelve finger-widths in size.
In the center, kaṃ śī śva ra ya;
in the east, ma hā de ba ho;
in the west, ba ri su tha gha;
in the south, parṇa śa ba ri;
in the north, hūṃ phaṭ taṭ thu muṃ;
in the intermediate directions, lam ba ghu ghu hūṃ ja;
pra mo sa ra bhinda ya;
ni nur ratta bri ha ra;
with svāhā at the end after hūṃ, and oṃ at the beginning,
the rim bearing fifty letters.
In a beautiful jeweled vessel containing honey
it should be placed with flowers and scents.
With mudrā and mantra one brings down the great god,
summons and absorbs him, and settles him in the wheel.
Maheśvara in wrathful form,
with jeweled earrings of sun and moon and garlands,
pale blue, utterly terrifying, holding a skull cup,
three-eyed, bearing the mark of matted locks,
holding a trident, an axe and a sword,
and a skull cup filled with flesh and blood,
together with his retinue, in various forms and with various sounds,
shows his form through three hundred thousand mantras.
With the mudrās and mantras of commitment and overpowering
one accomplishes him in accordance with the activity.
Having made him swoon with the mantra of fearsome splendor,
he is bewildered by the hosts of mudrās.
The goddess, pale green, holding a skull cup,
with ornaments and garlands, bearing human emblems,
empowered, blessed and delighted,
abides in the manner of the mudrā of union.
The goddess's mind is stirred by passion,
and she petitions the practitioner as a god:
'Whatever accomplishment you desire,
I shall swiftly fulfill, and I shall be your servant.
In this sublime secret maṇḍala,
please show the wisdom of bliss.'
Through the activities of releasing the mudrā,
Maheśvara is roused,
and by the power of empowerment and the full application of commitment,
he generates the wisdom of bliss:
'Lord, great bliss, alala!
Supreme of the three vajras, alala!
You produce wondrous bliss for us;
we shall accomplish whatever you wish and need.'
oṃ na mo bi ca ra ṇi ma hā de ba

ma hā ru kaṃ ba la hi ha ha ha ha hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
With the trident mudrā together with its mantra
one applies it to all activities.

In a delightful place adorned with various sites,
conjuring hosts of various goddesses,
beautified with red flowers,
one should draw a red maṇḍala.
In the center, beautiful with a garland marked with arrows,
one draws it with its courtyard and gates.
Inside a full vase, complete with its set of ornaments
and containing the essences of jewels,
one should place the mantra wheel.
Marked with eight-pointed arrows,
with a hub, a rim and a garland,
it is drawn on cloth sixteen finger-widths across.
In the center, dhaṃ haṃ hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ;
in the east, phreṃ phaṭ phaṭ tad tvāṃ;
in the south, siddha gre ta su;
in the west, raṃ ta ma hri hi;
in the north, gha gha sa ra druṃ;
in the intermediate directions, hu lu hu lu he;
nā ga te śca ra ru ka;
saṃ bho ga ta tra ta tar;
haḥ; groups of five each in the directions and intermediate directions,
and on the rim the hosts of vowels and consonants.
Through applying the mantra jaḥ hūṃ baṃ hoḥ,
one clearly impresses the seal of the great god:
pale yellow, beautiful with jeweled ornaments and garlands,
holding shining arrows, with an army of intoxication,
the fierce goddess, victorious in her place, born of jewels,
conjuring enjoyments in the realm of desire.
When urged by the king of secret mantras,
he shows his form after a count of three hundred thousand.
oṃ dhaṃ kā ma de ba ru lu bhyo na mo bhyo ha ri sañca ra ghaṃ saṃ bhyo bhyo bhyo hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
By the power of the arrow mudrā together with its mantra
he is urged to his own activity.

Having thoroughly applied oneself to yoga,
the desired activities are set in their place.
In a place adorned with every offering,
there one should begin the maṇḍala:
square, in the shape of Mahendra's maṇḍala,
marked with vajras, golden in color,
in the center marked with a lotus, with a rim,
drawn with its courtyard and gates.
On an eight-pointed wheel
with two garlands at the rim:
in the center, oṃ i iṃ sarba;
in the east, pra ma bri śaṃ ru;
in the south, śāntiṃ nāgaṃ hūṃ;
in the west, hūṃ phaṭ bri gu ti;
in the north, tri ga mu raṃ su;
in the intermediate directions, brahmin, ku ma ru;
ga tra ru paṃ laṃ ba saṃ;
ba ra ba ra saṃ ba ra;
ending with phaṭ, these are the nine groups.
Five should be drawn in each place;
the rim is marked with five letters,
and one draws the adornments at the tips of the wheel.
In a vessel anointed with saffron and fine scents,
decorated with jeweled ornaments,
one places the wheel with white rice and the mantra.
With his own mantra and mudrā one summons and absorbs him,
shows the overpowering seal of commitment,
makes offerings, and should urge him to the activity.
Peaceful, golden in color, four-faced,
with beautiful ornaments, holding a garland of wheels:
through a count of three hundred thousand secret mantras
he shows signs of three kinds.
Having bewildered him and made him swoon,
one should bring the goddess under control:
Brahmāṇī, holding a jeweled garland,
beautiful, all-formed, golden in color.
Empowered and blessed, she is made to sport;
her body is stirred, and the wisdom of joy and bliss expands.
oṃ pra ma ni gha ta pa ra hri na dha ya oṃ āḥ hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā
By the power of the wheel mudrā together with its mantra
one applies it to all activities.

The yogin of all-good conduct,
in order to gather the abode of the king above the earth,
on a white maṇḍala endowed with ornaments
should scatter white flowers.
In the center, a lotus and a three-pointed vajra;
one draws it with rim, courtyard and gates.
On the eight tips of the wheel,
marked with three-pointed vajras,
on cloth fourteen finger-widths across,
one draws with indigo mixed with lhang tsher.
In the center, a a bi ma le;
in the east, indra bhi ha ha;
in the south, bhrūṃ su a a aṃ;
in the west, caṇḍi śva ri tvaṃ;
in the north, he he rutta ne;
in the intermediate directions, na mo śa ke lu;
he he mu ni taṃ ṭra ṭra;
ba ra sa mo ba ra hūṃ;
arranged in the nine places with the power of hūṃ,
and on the two rims the consonants and vowels.
With grapes and other flowers
one makes offerings at the three times;
having inserted it with the king of secret mantras,
one should make it stable with mantra and mudrā.
Indra, like a snowy mountain, thousand-eyed,
marked with a three-pointed vajra, ornaments and garlands:
applying him to the activity through three hundred thousand mantras,
he sports with all the goddesses.
oṃ indra ye saṅgha ma nu ra ga kṣa indra bhrūṃ ghe tatta ghe taṭū ha ma ha no phaṭ svāhā
By the power of the mantra of the three-pointed vajra
he grants whatever accomplishment one desires.
Even for one who has not recited, practiced,
meditated on or retained it,
the extent of the qualities
of bearing the mudrā cannot be measured.
For activities of protection, increase and so forth,
the deities of the center and periphery and the protection mantras,
interspersed with the hundred syllables and the name mantras:
if one worships them well in one's home,
one who wants a son will find a son,
one who wants prosperity will gain prosperity,
life and treasury will increase,
and one will always be free of illness and happy."

So it is said. Thus, having brought the hosts of great gods under control, they become like servants and fulfill all one's desires, and the yogin purified through that very practice also gives to others.

Again, in order to enjoy the kingdoms of the three worlds without exception:

"The kingdoms of the three worlds without exception,
together with all the lords of the world:
having seized them by the force of boldness,
he gives them to the faithful."

The kingdoms that exist in all three worlds without exception, the magical displays of the qualities of the kings of the gods, the kings of the nāgas, the universal monarchs among humans, the kings of the yakṣas and so forth, and even the substances accomplished from their own karma, he gathers in; what need is there to mention anything else? Thus the root tantra says:

"That very yoga is the Blessed One.
Through the union of Vajrasattva,
having made the family maṇḍala clear,
and having completed the rites of recitation and meditation,
in a delightful arrangement of the world
the needs of perfection are accomplished.
A fine and beautiful jeweled vase,
endowed with twenty-seven kinds of ingredients,
with a maṇḍala of nine squares,
is set out in the nine places with the ornaments of offering.
By the stages of the four of approach and accomplishment,
if one recites the secret mantra of the deity of one's own awareness,
interspersing it with the application,
it will be accomplished with three hundred thousand recitations.
na maḥ artha siddhi gha ha na lo ca na ma ma siddha ku ru ku ru hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ phaṭ svāhā
Through twenty-nine weeks
one applies it to the activities and takes the accomplishment.
All activities, such as that of the excellent vase,
are accomplished by this, without doubt."

Thus, if even great activities are accomplished, what need is there to mention others?

If Brahmā, Viṣṇu and Mahendra,
the supreme lord and the excellent lord,
turn toward one from every side and swiftly bestow,
what need is there to speak of other enjoyments?

Mahābrahmā is the lord of all the three realms; Viṣṇu is the teacher of the gods; Maheśvara is the lord of the desire realm; Indra is the lord above the earth, supreme among patrons; and the excellent Kāmadeva is the lord of the Paranirmitavaśavartin heaven. The result to be desired is engagement in the supreme activity:

There is nothing at all it does not give.
Of all that is to be done for the sake of buddhahood
there is nothing whatever it does not do;
it is both the giving and the doing.

This conduct, which abides in the meaning of suchness and is endowed with the branches of approach and accomplishment, produces the fruit of liberation and accomplishes everything. The meaning is that, for the sake of the supreme accomplishment, one should enjoy everything and give it to others as well.

If one asks how these are accomplished:

If even the buddhas and bodhisattvas
turn toward one and bestow,
what need is there to speak of the supreme activities, boundless,
and of other accomplishments?

The things that arise from the great qualities of the Buddha, which even the mighty worldly gods and nāgas, and the transcendent noble ones, the śrāvakas, pratyekabuddhas and bodhisattvas, are unable to accomplish, the yogin accomplishes. Why? Because they arise from knowing the intrinsic nature of suchness. As the root tantra says:

Union with the state of great bliss
that arises from undefiled wisdom:
there is nothing it does not accomplish.
The awakening of a buddha is not difficult,
for by joining oneself with the awakening mind
one's own intrinsic nature is accomplished.

And also:

By the worldly and the transcendent,
with their lesser views, it is not accomplished in their separate streams.

As for the benefit of practicing these, for one's own sake:

The supreme accomplishment, without doubt,
utterly crushing all the hosts.

That which has become supreme among the results one desires to achieve: these are what arise from the body of great bliss. How so? It is the culmination of all paths and results, because, as its own great qualities appear in undefiled wisdom, it tames the hostile Māras together with their hosts.

Then, for the sake of others:

The great accomplishment of all the buddhas,
the universal monarch (cakravartin), is fully accomplished.

That very point is stated in the root tantra:

The glorious victor, the culmination of accomplishment,
the vast assembly of the buddhas' emanations,
turns the wheel of Dharma
and accomplishes every deed without exception.

The manner of this is:

Through Union with All Buddhas,
the Ḍākinī Māyā, Supreme Bliss,
the great miraculous power of all buddhas
fully enters into emanation.

Having known and realized the meaning of the view as in the second chapter, one enters into confidence by way of the fifth, and it becomes clear; thereby one accomplishes the path of the Great Seal (Mahāmudrā) by the third, and if one practices in accordance with the meaning by the fourth, then the great miraculous power of all buddhas, which arises from the spontaneously present appearance of the six bodies, emanates as the six great family maṇḍalas and so forth, and through countless appearances that bring relief to beings, it accomplishes the benefit of sentient beings.

Further, concerning working for the benefit of sentient beings through various deeds: for the sake of those on the path who are to be tamed by training in the Characteristics Vehicle, the three verses running from "Sometimes, through the great awakening mind," to "sometimes proceeding in accord with conduct," were spoken. That is to say: at some times, taking great compassion as the basis, he generated and trained the two kinds of precious mind directed toward supreme awakening. At some times, taking many accumulations of merit as the basis, in order to train in the five paths he practiced the collection of perfections and the thirty-seven factors of awakening. At some times, in the abode of Tuṣita, having become the holy bodhisattva Śvetaketu, he dwelt in a place exalted above the gods, radiated rays of light, and displayed inconceivable appearances of meditative absorption and so forth. At some times, through the combined power of his former aspirations and his present great compassion, hearing the sound of exhortation from the ten directions, he entered in the form of an elephant the womb of Queen Mahāmāyā, and there taught the Dharma to the retinue. At some times, in the noble land, in the Lumbinī grove, he was born by opening the door of his mother's right side, like lightning flashing out of darkness, and took seven steps toward each of the four directions; and through youthful play and the like he brought worldly affairs to completion. At some times he went forth from home into homelessness and practiced great austerities. At some times, abiding in one-pointed meditative absorption at the foot of the tree of awakening, all the stains of obscuration were purified and the accumulations were completed. At some times he conquered the hosts of the four māras and attained complete awakening on the vajra seat. At some times he turned the wheel of Dharma in stages, in accord with the mental streams of those to be tamed. At some times he subdued the six non-Buddhist (tīrthika) teachers and others through deeds of skillful means. And at some times, through the emanation body's display of passing into nirvāṇa, he took hold of lazy disciples and showed all phenomena to be conditioned.

Moreover, with regard to emanation, he simultaneously works for the benefit of the worlds of the great trichiliocosm through deeds of great compassion: in some lands beginning with establishing all sentient beings in the awakening mind, and in some lands acting for their benefit by displaying the passing into nirvāṇa. The meaning of these things is taught at length in the extensive scriptural collections. For individuals of special faculties, he works their benefit through the Secret Mantra Vehicle.

For those to be tamed by the body:

Sometimes the victor over the three worlds,
the very lord of all accomplishments.

At some times and in some places, for the sake of sentient beings, he shows a body in whatever way suits them. As the root tantra says:

Sometimes peaceful, in the form of Brahmā;
sometimes wrathful, in the manner of Yama;
sometimes he takes on the form of a learned one;
sometimes he blazes like the fire at the end of time;
sometimes he acts as the protector of beings,
and sometimes he destroys beings;
sometimes he is an assembly of countless maṇḍalas,
sometimes he appears as a solitary hero;
sometimes he arranges the world and its inhabitants,
and sometimes he empties the world and its inhabitants;
sometimes he sends forth peaceful and wrathful forms,
and sometimes he gathers the maṇḍala back in.

Thus, by means of various maṇḍalas, he brings relief to the multitudes of those to be tamed, in accordance with their aims.

For those to be tamed by the vehicle of speech:

Sometimes supreme in all the ritual systems,
the unsurpassed lord of accomplishment.

In accordance with the particular distinctions of the aggregates, elements and sense sources, and on the basis of the two, the provisional and the definitive meaning, he elaborated and taught many graded vehicles. As the root tantra says:

To lead childish beings of lesser view,
he taught the path of lesser thought.
By the force of feeble austerities in lowly states,
the bad destinies of saṃsāra are not seen;
yet, while the mass of conceptual obscurations persists,
the wisdom of liberation is not seen.
A partial liberation is grasped.
There is devotion to abiding in wisdom and compassion:
the liberating vehicle, a door of endless ways,
fully joins them to the grounds of liberation.
For those devoted to Kriyā and Caryā,
the Secret Mantra, practice of the meaning, the supreme Great Vehicle:
entering the abode of one's own wisdom,
and joining the vast conduct of mantra to the deity,
worldly activities are fully accomplished,
and one reaches the culmination with its designated fruit.
To those of supreme intellect, with thoroughly trained wisdom,
the profound secret way is taught:
the supreme lord of one's own great bliss,
the manifold array, appears as liberation.

Again, by way of vast deeds:

These and other endless supreme deeds:
Supreme Bliss, lord of all buddhas,
in the dance that is sure as a means of taming,
delights and plays through emanation.

Through endless deeds of qualities accomplished by direct experience, he acts for others. In that very body of great bliss, the lord of all buddhas, the various means of taming by body, speech, mind, qualities and enlightened activity are liberated, and thereby he teaches and practices the definitive meaning. As the root tantra says:

By body, speech, mind and qualities,
and by enlightened activity arising from the manifold,
the dance that is sure as a means of taming
is wholly liberated in the meaning.

The manner in which, by way of practice, one's own aim is spontaneously accomplished in the character of the result:

The great accomplishment, supreme of all,
the steadfast one, lord of great sovereignty,
in form, life, complexion and strength
like glorious Vajrasattva.

By the power of fully practicing the path,
the supreme great accomplishment is achieved,

and the eight sovereignties that always abide in one's own stream, and the fourfold glory, are accomplished.

Its further qualities:

Attended upon by beings,
worshiped by all the tathāgatas,
glorious and supreme among all goddesses,
Vajrasattva is fully accomplished.

One who has attained the accomplishment of glorious Union with All Buddhas is joyfully attended and honored by all the beings of the three realms, is worshiped by all the tathāgatas, and becomes the chief of all goddesses, just like Vajrasattva.

Further, the manner in which the benefit of others is brought to culmination by way of the result together with its qualities:

The many realms of sentient beings,
without exception, he stamps with the seal;
the deity and awakening, so hard to gain,
he steadfastly bestows on sentient beings.

Those possessing the collection of aggregates endowed with consciousness, without an agent, phenomena that bear their own characteristics, are the realm of sentient beings. He stamps all of them without exception with the seal of body, speech and mind, and steadfastly bestows on sentient beings the accomplishment of the deity and unsurpassed awakening.

If one asks whether this is only from time to time:

Supreme Bliss, lord of all buddhas,
acts for the benefit of all sentient beings,
and remains in existence through all eons,
entirely, with none left over.

Through that very emanation of the six bodies he works for the benefit of all sentient beings, and even for the sake of benefiting a single one he remains for an eon.

These verses teach the stages of working for the benefit of sentient beings through the yogin's conduct by way of the path, and through the deeds of the culminating result. From here on, in particular, the meanings of the sixth and tenth chapters are also connected.

Summarizing these, the chapter is characterized as "The Supreme Accomplishment, the Excellent Fortune of All Diversity": it is the Glorious Chapter on Commitment. Because the yogin of Vajrasattva applies himself to and enjoys the various activities of conduct and accomplishment, it is "excellent fortune." Because that very one enjoys the supreme result, it is "supreme accomplishment": it is the enjoyment of the commitment of being inseparable from the maṇḍala of goddesses.

This concludes the explanation of the meaning of the fourth chapter.

Now the account of for whose sake, by whom and how it arose, and what is taught. In order to inspire confidence through the Teacher's intention concerning what happened in the past and through a special narrative setting, and in order that the meaning be put into practice through the activity of following, the stages of meditative absorption of the fifth chapter were taught. As the Seven Chapters says:

Through the setting of the miraculous array
he gives those who enter confidence.
The Dharma to be practiced in accord with it
joins them to the state of meditative absorption and wisdom,
in sequence, with signs and without signs;
in particular, the direct body of the three secrets.

The setting of the miraculous array is this. In the time of the eon called Excellent Ornament, under the teaching of the Buddha Glorious Excellent Host, a monk named Banner of Liberation, through practicing the scriptures of the Tathāgata wrongly, came forth as an arrogant one with a host, together with twenty consorts. In order to tame them and others like them, those on the path of the common afflictions, the great Vajradhara himself, the appearance of the wisdom of great emptiness, the sixth, the embodiment of all the families, emanated and appeared in the guise of Vajrasattva together with an assembly of twenty goddesses. The full setting of these events is taught at length in the root tantra.

Here I shall explain the meaning of just a little of what has been taught. First, the characteristics of body and wisdom:

The yoga of this is the Blessed One,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
Union with All Buddhas,
the Ḍākinī Māyā, Supreme Bliss.

These objects of the six faculties, appearing as immediately present, are the appearance of the great self-arisen wisdom. When one joins that very thing with one's own great wisdom, realization becomes manifest; one gains mastery over all the great qualities, appears in the body of the Blessed One Vajrasattva, and is the very nature of all the tathāgatas. If one asks what its manner is: it unites everything by way of the aspect of the two buddhas, and it has the character of the Ḍākinī Māyā, Supreme Bliss. The word samayoga has a root that signifies everything, like the word sendha. It enters into five states of the aspect of the ḍākinī, the sky-goer: going in the sky by the force of wisdom, going in the sky by the power of miraculous ability, going in the sky through accomplishment, going in the sky by nature, and going in the sky through karma. All of these are the nature of Supreme Bliss. From that, it appears in the manner of six bodies:

The perfectly accomplished one alone
is applied in whatever way one wishes.

The very mode in which all phenomena abide as the unborn expanse, the genuine way of their own characteristic, is to be known as the essence of suchness as it is. Realized and accomplished by way of the three kinds of wisdom, it abides as the single one, in the sense of nonduality. From that very thing, as the inexhaustible appearance of one's own great qualities, and in the manner of accomplishing the benefit that appears in the streams of others, it is arranged in whatever way one wishes, in the various forms of the appearance of the two kinds of body. Then the characteristic of the spontaneously present six bodies is, in summary, also arranged as three: the nondual body, the emanation body and the body of great bliss. These are also said to teach the general characteristics of the three bodies. As the root tantra says:

The great yoga that unites the state of body and wisdom,
the Blessed One,
is Vajrasattva himself,
the embodiment of all buddhas.

And the Seven Chapters also says:

The perfectly accomplished one alone,
the great bliss in which all buddhas are gathered,
the profound yoga, complete in the three bodies,
the Ḍākinī Māyā, Supreme Bliss, bliss.

Now, in order to show the intended meaning from the deeds of the Teacher:

For all the rites of the ritual systems,
the holy accomplishment, supreme of the supreme.

The signs of the conceptual views of beings deluded about the ground, and of the tīrthikas and others deluded about the path, the five mountains, the six mountains, the twenty mountains, the sixty-two mountains, the twelve mountains, the four mountains, the sixteen mountains and the two mountains, are destroyed by the antidotal concept. Relying on that, in order to join beings to the path of supreme accomplishment by way of worldly accomplishment and common accomplishment, he taught the holy supreme accomplishment, the supreme of the supreme. As the root tantra says:

In order to destroy that very conception,
he taught the deities of various maṇḍalas
and the meaning of various tantras,
and joins beings to the supreme of supreme accomplishments.

The manner in which he emanates in accordance with the objects toward which compassion arises:

By applications of various activities,
by the various rites that are wished for,
in order to tame sentient beings
and to accomplish every happiness.

The conceptual thoughts
of those sentient beings to be tamed are endless:

they range from one, two, three, four, five and six up to countless. In order to tame them and establish them in bliss, he taught the applications of activity separately by way of various forms of body, speech, mind, qualities and enlightened activity, and he tamed those who abide on the path of the common afflictions, such as the monk Banner of Liberation. As the root tantra says:

The hosts of afflicted conceptual thought,
from one up to countless:
to tame those on the common path,
the lord of the family shows various forms in his body;
by the distinctions of various rites,
the state of great bliss, of Vajradhara.

Well then, if one asks what the first characteristic of great compassion is like:

That very yoga of the Blessed One:
if, through the emanation of all buddhas,
one emanates for the benefit of all sentient beings,
it will be accomplished very swiftly.

That is, emanation by way of body, emanation by way of wisdom, emanation by way of family, emanation by way of those to be tamed, emanation by way of all of these in harmony, and emanation by way of what is out of harmony. As the root tantra says:

From that very yoga of the Blessed One
all the buddhas send forth emanations:
body, wisdom and families,
and hosts of activities, harmonious and inharmonious.
Even within a single maṇḍala, as various bodies,
through emanation's own activity,
they emanate for the benefit of all sentient beings:
undefiled wisdom, suchness.

These teach the characteristic of emanating the maṇḍala and acting for the benefit of beings in the time of the original events. Now, with regard to those who follow, in order that, knowing it as set forth from the second chapter onward, they may enter into and accomplish its meaning, one should practice the maṇḍala of the gathering through the stages of meditating on meditative absorption. First, at the best and middling times:

Each day, or else each month,
or likewise each year,
having duly blessed it,
one should perform the dance of the Buddha Supreme Bliss.

First, as taught in the Arrangement of the Secret Meaning of Vajrasattva, one focuses on all beings with a mind of great compassion, vows to liberate oneself and others, joins body, speech and mind, and generates capacity. Having blessed and clarified the three doors, through gathering the accumulations toward the noble ones, gathering the accumulations toward sentient beings, the protection wheel, the meditative absorption of the ground, the meditative absorption of the support, the meditative absorption of union, the yoga that follows the meditative absorption of the three objects, and so on, one should bless the mudrā consort by way of the three times, best, middling and least. Worshiping the noble ones with nondual bliss, and having worked for the benefit of sentient beings, one should take hold of the wisdom of bliss.

As the Seven Chapters says:

From the place on the left side, in the proper way,
unite in whatever way one wishes.
Like a dancer is the seal of illusion,
and it is clear, like a rainbow.
In order to make the wisdom of passion clear,
the light of various blazes massed together
dissolves, and she is blessed as queen.
Through the great commitment of wisdom,
signified by the sign of Supreme Bliss and of illusion,
having joined the lotus and the jewel,
when the great bliss of the expanse arises,
self-aware wisdom is light within it.
Just as, clear in the sky,
the stainless sun shines with its light,
that awakening mind is utterly pure.
Having wholly passed beyond the three realms,
and having pervaded all without remainder,
what is desired is thoroughly pleased.
Grasp the gathered light in the primordial form.
Through that great accomplishment,
make diligent effort in whatever way one wishes.

The support for gathering the accumulations:

All that has the nature of the elements,
and likewise what arises from the life-force and the channels:
in the guise of one's own deity
fully make the mudrā of practice.

First, specifically from the vast point of view: in order that those who enter the maṇḍala of meditative absorption may make the object of their focus clear and accomplished, one should fully make the mudrā of practice, that is, a body matching the guise of one's own deity, with its hand emblems, crown ornaments and so forth, made from precious metals such as gold, silver, copper and iron, or made from materials that come from living creatures, such as horn, ivory and bone, or arising from the materials of plants and trees.

Again, from the profound point of view: by way of the three kinds of nonduality of method and wisdom, the appearances of the three winds, the four cakras and the six faculties are the essence of the elements; and the emanation and gathering of the three kinds of letters, the emanation and gathering of the four joys, and the emanation and gathering of faculties and objects, are what arise from the life-force channel. Thus one should establish the mudrā of practice in the guise of one's own deity, in which method and wisdom are nondual.

As for the manner of entering into that very thing:

Whatever maṇḍala one relies upon,
with the offerings of various mudrās,
the whole assembly of disciples, in each one,
having made offerings, accomplishes it.

In whichever maṇḍala of the six families it may be, the very deity whose empowerment and commitments one has received is one's special deity. One should abide clearly in its maṇḍala through meditative absorption, worship the maṇḍala with the hosts of offerings emanated from various mudrās, and accomplish it.

These verses teach that one should gather the accumulations and accomplish the practice by way of the clarity of the maṇḍala of meditative absorption, and thereby the meaning of the sixth and third chapters is also connected. As the Seven Chapters says:

Through the clarity of one's own meditative absorption,
gathering the accumulations of merit and wisdom,
and through the activity of accomplishing that,
the signless body of great bliss.

This teaches the manner of emanating vastly from that very maṇḍala of glorious Vajrasattva in accordance with one's own maṇḍala:

Beyond that, through all the ritual systems,
the emanations of all the buddhas,
emanated as is fitting:
those accounts I shall fully explain.

All the circles of maṇḍalas and all the yoginī tantras unfold from this one. Its abiding as the general embodiment of them all; and more particularly its abiding as the embodiment of the six great family maṇḍalas; and still more particularly the origin of the one maṇḍala that gathers one's own family, the four root maṇḍalas, the ten maṇḍalas of transformation, the twenty maṇḍalas taught in sequence and the thirty-six maṇḍalas of extensive emanation, together with the stages of their practice, are taught at length in the root tantra. Here I shall tell their history only in brief.

Thus the appearance as various maṇḍalas is the emanation of the six buddhas, and that very thing is the appearance of Vajrasattva. That itself is the characteristic of the relief brought by the six buddhas. Therefore:

The hero, the reality of all buddhas,
Vajrasattva, Supreme Bliss,
in the delight of the supreme secret
ever abides as the self of all.

The characteristic of the great Vajradhara himself taming, by way of great passion, those who abide on the path of the common afflictions: he is called Vajrasattva, and he has the aspect of the six buddhas. Their nature:

This yoga is the Blessed One,
eternal, holy and steadfast;
always, as though arising now,
its essential nature is hard to pass beyond.

This very buddha of the six spontaneously present bodies has the characteristic of the great yoga: it is the Blessed One. He always abides in his own nature and remains for the sake of beings. The body of great bliss, by nature unceasing, abides in the expanse of great bliss, the great permanence. The nondual body permanently abides in the aspect of nondual equality. The essential body permanently abides in the manner of pervading all. The wisdom body permanently abides with one-pointed clarity. The enjoyment body permanently abides as the support adorned with all qualities. The emanation body permanently abides by arising without interruption. Therefore it is "eternal, holy and steadfast." Because it abides thus in the manner of the aspects of the six bodies, it is "holy and steadfast." Because that very one rests in the nirvāṇa that does not abide in any extreme, it always abides, as though arising now, as an unchanging essential nature, and so it is "hard to pass beyond." As the root tantra says:

The great yoga, the Blessed One,
through the permanence of the six spontaneously present bodies,
abides in the essence of its own nature
and remains unchanging through the three times.

The manner in which he acts for the benefit of those to be tamed through various means:

For those who wish for various rites,
through various applications of activity,
the Buddha Vajradhara and the rest
were made in order to tame them.

In order to benefit persons who delight in the concise and those who delight in the very extensive, he taught, by way of various rites, various applications of activity: different stages of meditating on the deity, different mantras and mudrās, stages of practice, and different stages of enlightened activity. These were made by the bodies of the Buddha Vajradhara and the others in order to tame beings on the conventional level.

As the Seven Chapters says:

In whatever ways beings are to be tamed,
in just those ways, for beings,
through various means of taming,
he fully applies himself to the benefit of sentient beings.

Now, to teach the benefits, it says:

All that is stable and moving, buddhas and the rest:
this becomes the reality of them all,

and so on. These outer and inner things, the world and its inhabitants, are of the nature of the maṇḍala of support and supported; they are the nature of the great Vajradhara. The rest I have not explained, since it is easy to understand.

This concludes the explanation of the relief brought by glorious Vajrasattva.

Now, freedom from the extreme of attachment. It concerns those who had entered the path of the nirvāṇa of one-sided peace, and all the beings of small mind and weak wisdom. In the time of the eon called Hard to Grasp, under the teaching of the Buddha Lion of Supreme Strength, there was a monk called Patient One. Through the power of practicing the true meaning wrongly, he came forth as one called Fierce Mass of Darkness, together with an assembly of twenty consorts. In order to tame them, from glorious Vajrasattva himself the wheel of the maṇḍala of the Buddha Vairocana appeared and brought them relief. The time when this took place:

In time past, it happened long ago,
by the great self, the permanent,

which is said for the sake of both those to be tamed and the tamer. "In time past" means that in the time of the eon Hard to Grasp, under the teaching of the Buddha Lion of Supreme Strength, the maṇḍala of the Buddha Vairocana arose earlier as the antidote to the one with the Fierce Mass of Darkness who had appeared. If one asks from what cause it arose: because they held the view of a great self as permanent, he acted for those in saṃsāra by way of the permanence of body, speech and mind.

From that, in order to teach the general characteristic:

In the scriptures of the Tathāgata,
it happened long ago in the manner of beings.

If one asks through what condition it arose: by way of two things, practicing the meaning wrongly and understanding it wrongly. The manner is the manner of beings who abide on the path of the general and the particular afflictions, and the manner of the antidote that destroys them.

Again, if one asks what the object is toward which compassion was generated:

At that time all sentient beings
strove at utter emptiness;
by applying themselves to the reality of cessation,
they ceased and passed completely into nirvāṇa.

In the time of the eon Hard to Grasp, sentient beings of little courage, weak wisdom and inferior faculties, ripened one-sidedly by the power of karma, sought to accomplish their own aim through the nirvāṇa of one-sided peace. Therefore, by way of the cause, they strove at the view of utter emptiness; by way of the path, they applied themselves to the reality of cessation; and so, by way of the result, they ceased and passed completely into nirvāṇa. That is, in the manner of nirvāṇa with remainder of the aggregates and without remainder, they abandoned all the things of saṃsāra and wished to attain the fruit of an arhat.

The manner in which great compassion arose toward them:

That very yoga, the Blessed One,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
in order to purify all beings,
brought forth emanations of buddhas everywhere.

In order to purify those of lesser view who wish to be liberated from saṃsāra, from the emanation of the body of glorious Vajrasattva himself there arose the maṇḍalas of the Blessed One, glorious Vairocana, the nature of the wisdom of the expanse of reality: one maṇḍala that gathers the family, three root maṇḍalas, ten maṇḍalas of transformation, twenty maṇḍalas taught in sequence, thirty-six maṇḍalas of extensive emanation, and fifty-eight maṇḍalas of arrayed ornament. Within the general count of seven hundred and thirteen maṇḍalas, Vajrasattva has seventy-one maṇḍalas; Vairocana, Heruka, Padmanarteśvara and Vajrasūrya should each be known to have equally one hundred and twenty-eight maṇḍalas; and the Play of Paramāśva should be known to have one hundred and thirty maṇḍalas. Their numbers and divisions are taught at length in the seventh, eighth, ninth, tenth, eleventh, twelfth and sixth chapters of the root tantra. Since this is a somewhat condensed tantra, they are not elaborated here. Therefore it is said, "brought forth emanations of buddhas everywhere."

Of those maṇḍalas taught at length, if one asks as the antidote to what the maṇḍala of the Buddha Vairocana arose: as the antidote to that very cessation. If one asks in what manner: as the antidote to accomplishing the nirvāṇa of one-sided peace. In order to purify that, the meaning of suchness is taught:

Not empty, and not non-empty,
nor is it observed even as a middle.

The emptiness established by the partial views and the like imagined by the tīrthikas; the abiding in the extreme of one-sided peace, empty of a person, as imagined by the śrāvakas; the annihilationist view, isolated by its own view, established by cutting off the extreme of cause and effect, as imagined by the Sautrāntikas; and the view established by the pratyekabuddhas, who, having stopped ignorance, hold one portion of the element of phenomena, the essence of selflessness, to be emptiness: these do not abide in accordance with the meaning, because they contradict the common direct perception, which appears as the objects of the six faculties, and the special direct perception, which is clearly experienced.

Well then, if one asks whether, by refuting views such as the annihilationist view of emptiness, it is something that abides permanently and eternally: that is the conceptual view of ordinary beings, which engages in what is imagined as two through the aspects of apprehended and apprehender. Therefore, because that essence of the six spontaneously present bodies truly exists, it is not empty; and because it is free from the extremes of conceptuality, it is not non-empty either.

Well then, if one asks whether, accomplishing the meaning of the Middle Way through a view free from both extremes, one practices the bodhisattva path together with its result: that too, for the sake of the task that arises from the accumulation of undefiled merit, all make their own reality clear. Those branches of gathering the accumulation of merit, which arise from fully practicing physical and verbal conduct, become aids produced by others.

If one asks whether that accumulation of wisdom is seen by way of clearly revealing its own intrinsic nature: here there is no means of seeing it in that way. For the reasonings such as the refutation of arising in terms of the four alternatives, and neither one nor many, measure it by way of inference, and that is merely a presumptive, conjectural cognition. Why is that? Because direct perception here relies on the eight similes of illusion, and by that much alone, without the meaning of experience, one cannot indicate the body of great bliss. That body is pointed out by the instructions of the holy guru, and its own characteristic is clearly experienced and trusted by way of relying on method, relying on wisdom, the Great Seal (Mahāmudrā), and so on.

Moreover, a center is established in dependence on extremes; since the extremes are not established, the center too is not established. Because everything abides pervaded by the nondual bliss of the appearance of the great self-arisen wisdom, it is not apprehended even as a middle. To show this very point clearly:

Joining with the perfection of wisdom,
Through method they are the very nature of compassion;
Therefore that very reality, the method of compassion,
Shines clear as the perfection of wisdom.

In the meaning of how all phenomena are awakened, one joins with the perfection by way of the three seals; and from nonduality with the body of great bliss, one displays the emanation body, the maṇḍala of method, whose very nature is great compassion. Therefore that very reality has the meaning of the nonduality of the perfection of wisdom together with the maṇḍala of the method of great compassion; it shines clear in the manner of the nonduality of the three bodies. As the root tantra says:

To overthrow the emptiness of those of lesser view
And the mountain of conceptual examination,
By way of the mere Middle Way of all phenomena
He taught the perfection of meaning for entering it.
By practicing on the provisional path
One will not find the definitive result.
Through the power of joining with the three seals,
Spontaneously present, clear appearance without intrinsic nature;
From the meaning of the perfection of wisdom
The maṇḍala of compassionate method shines clear.

Well then, if one asks with what intention, rather than teaching that very meaning, the stages of meditating on the maṇḍala were taught, along with the various ritual sections that distinguish the manifold explanations: it was in order to turn persons who enter, with their various differences of faculty and disposition, away from ordinary conceptuality, and to teach the meaning of nonconceptuality by way of conception.

For phenomena that are free of concepts,
For the sake of beings, things are fully conceived;
Through devotion to nonconceptuality,
All should be examined by conception.

Here the first line teaches the intrinsic nature. The second line teaches the characteristic of purpose. The third teaches setting one on the path. The fourth teaches the etymology of the antidote. If one asks why these were taught: they are to be connected with the etymology of the word "kalpa," conception. Its meaning was taught in the Seven Chapters and has already been explained above.

Now, among the songs of praise spoken in order to please the deities in all the maṇḍalas, first comes the expression of their qualities:

Like the sound of an echo,
It turns into words of Dharma;
From that comes a song of the Great Vehicle
That fully expands the true qualities,
To the deities of the maṇḍala.

Verses about their own qualities, like an echo, are sounds that are ultimately empty, without grasped object or grasping subject; therefore they turn into words that teach the suchness of the Dharma, the three samayas. From that very fact, the song of the Great Vehicle that indicates suchness and expands all the true qualities expresses the meaning that is distinguished from others by its great focus, great view, great wisdom, great practice, great activity, and great result. This teaches the characteristic of sound and the characteristic of meaning. If one asks whom it praises:

The three samayas of the three times,
It praises all the Tathāgatas.

Samaya has the sense of that which is not transgressed. It praises that which has become the very nature of all the buddhas, the Tathāgatas, of the nondual three bodies, unchanging throughout the three times and abiding permanently.

Among these, first, to show that the Tathāgata family, arising from the wisdom of the dharmadhātu, by way of the body maṇḍala, though it does not stir from the meaning of sameness, shows unceasing compassion:

Unequaled, unmoving, possessing the nature of supreme sameness,
Embodiment of compassion, you dispel the suffering of beings;
Bestower of all accomplishments of boundless qualities,
Stainless, unmoving, unequaled, possessing the sublime supreme Dharma.

All phenomena that appear thus are, in their meaning, the body of the sameness of the nature of reality, which has become the support from which all qualities arise. In terms of its intrinsic nature it is called the essence body; in terms of its clarity it is called the wisdom body; and in terms of the nonduality of these two, it is the Dharma body. Since no conceptuality of signs whatsoever can move it, it is called "unequaled, unmoving." The enjoyment body that appears thus, adorned with the major and minor marks, the powers and the fearlessnesses, is called "possessing the nature of supreme sameness." The emanation body, in forms endowed with the major and minor marks and in forms not endowed with the marks, as the embodiment of great compassion, dispels all the sufferings of beings by various means, and bestows on beings all the accomplishments of boundless qualities. Thus these six bodies are stainless, unmoving, unequaled, possessing the sublime supreme Dharma.

Now, to show that the aspect of the maṇḍala of the vajra family, whose nature is mirror-like wisdom, is, by way of the mind of all the Tathāgatas, beyond any example by which to honor it:

Even the subtlest fraction of your qualities is immeasurable;
No example can match them, not even space;
Boundless in accomplishments, unequaled, beyond example,
You surely bestow the supreme accomplishments on the realm of beings.

The aspects of the Sugata's qualities are the very nature of the six kinds of wisdom. Because they are extremely subtle and hard to realize, they cannot be measured; and since no example can match them, not even the mere pervasion of the element of space, they have become the source from which boundless accomplishments arise. From that which is beyond example, to which no phenomenon whatsoever is similar or equal, the supreme accomplishment, the body and wisdom of the Sugata, is bestowed unfailingly for the sake of sentient beings.

The jewel family, whose nature is the wisdom of sameness, appearing as the maṇḍala of qualities by way of aspiration and great compassion:

Arisen from the force of ever-stainless compassion,
Possessing the nature of fulfilled aspirations that never cease,
Boundless in fervent striving to accomplish the aims of beings,
The very nature of great compassion, you ever appear.

Having undertaken austerities for many eons, one has cleared away the whole mass of obscurations; through the power of gathering the two accumulations one has come to possess great compassion; and through the power of the vast aspirations made at the time of training on the path, focused on the realm of sentient beings, deeds in keeping with those aspirations appear in the mindstreams of those to be trained. Through boundless activity one strives to accomplish the aims of beings, and because this is joined with the very essence of the qualities, the very nature of great compassion ever appears.

Again, to indicate the supreme ultimate accomplishment through the speech maṇḍala of the lotus family, whose nature is discriminating wisdom:

Though you have attained the sublime state of a Sugata,
Immeasurable, beyond all measure, utterly complete,
Your compassionate conduct, granting the supreme accomplishment to the three realms,
Is unmoving and never comes to cessation.

Through the power of the capacity gained by entering the path that is supreme, one attains the result that is supreme. Though one abides having brought one's own work to completion, for the sake of beings one beholds them with stainless great compassion and acts for their benefit.

Again, to show the swift granting of accomplishments and so on through the activity maṇḍala of the karma family, whose nature is all-accomplishing wisdom:

How wonderful! Endowed with the supreme and most excellent nature of reality,
Having attained the supreme state among all who grant the supreme,
You ever bestow the supreme accomplishment upon the three realms.
Protector who fully knows the three times without obscuration,
Grant me the sublime accomplishment of the three samaya meanings.

This is because, among the paths to supreme liberation, the one that is supreme is this very path: by way of it, one thoroughly joins with and attains the supreme result in a single lifetime. Therefore it is marvelous; that is the meaning of "endowed with the supreme and most excellent nature of reality." It is shown that the deeds of the result, together with their qualities, appear from that very path. By bestowing the supreme accomplishment on all the beings of the three realms, one sets them on the path of great awakening; and with wisdom unobscured throughout the three times, one knows every aspect of things as they are and in all their extent. Because one abides as the very nature of great compassion, it says, "Grant me the accomplishment of the three samayas."

Thus, to show the qualities of this praise, two verses are spoken, beginning:

This is the vast true quality
Of all the buddhas.

This king of praises expresses the vast qualities, such as the body and wisdom families, of the great Vajradhara, the very nature of all the buddhas of the three times. If even reciting it a single time brings about the accomplishments of all the buddhas, what need is there to mention reciting it by way of the three samayas, having meditated on oneself as the maṇḍala of the family and being endowed with reflection in accordance with its meaning? The meaning of the words is easy to understand, so it is not explained. These verses set forth the Teacher's intention together with a praise of his qualities.

Now, with regard to followers, the manner of the accomplishments that arise from meditation and practice is shown:

The body that one never tires of beholding,
Having revealed itself as the nature of all,
Saying "Excellent!",
Grants all the supreme accomplishments.

Revealing the mass of qualities of one's own maṇḍala, adorned with the major and minor marks, which one never tires of beholding, he proclaims words such as "Excellent!", gives prophecies, and bestows the supreme accomplishment. If one asks how this happens:

The joy of accomplishing the nature of all,
All intentions completely fulfilled,
All the supreme attainments of buddhahood,
And the sublime yoga too he bestows.

The nature of all the common worldly accomplishments arises just as one delights to accomplish it, all intended actions are completely fulfilled, and he bestows as well the supreme accomplishment, the body of a buddha, and the sublime yoga, the wisdom of great bliss of the enlightened mind. In a manner that awakens zest for the result, these verses show the qualities of attaining the accomplishments that a follower achieves by practicing in accordance with the text.

Now the method of practice is shown:

Then, its practice
And the utterly permanent accomplishments:
That by which, in this very existence,
Utterly permanent accomplishment is attained, is explained.

"Then": with the short sound, the word means "immediately"; with the long sound, it becomes a word of auspiciousness; so it means the auspiciousness of immediate practice. The accomplishments are the result arising from the two kinds of accomplishment. The method of achieving them is the sequence of rites such as the four branches of approach and accomplishment. By this, in this very existence, one enjoys the accomplishment of the Great Seal (Mahāmudrā). The rite that follows approach and accomplishment is this:

By snapping the vajra fingers,
One should gather all the buddhas;
With palms well joined,
One claps the palms three times.

Endowed with the application, and preceded by such steps as blessing the tongue and hands, one snaps the vajra fingers three times and recites the mantra vajra samājaḥ; thereby the wisdom maṇḍalas are gathered and arrive. Then, when requesting them to depart, one claps the palms of delight three times and says vajra tuṣya ho, thereby requesting the deities of the maṇḍala to depart. After the wisdom deities have thus departed, the samaya maṇḍalas too are gathered into oneself in sequence, and that too, in the manner of the nāda, like the smoke of incense fading away, should be settled in nonconceptual suchness. From that state, one again meditates on oneself as the deity of the family, is empowered and blessed, is protected with armor and its fastenings, and should rise at the end of the session. The conduct for this is:

Whatever faculties they arise from,
Make them into its very essence.

The yogin himself should not lapse from samādhi in any of the four modes of conduct.

With a mind in meditative equipoise, by way of the practices with signs and without signs, one should join the appearances of the six faculties in union and abide in them, in the manner of the bodies of method and wisdom, father and mother, and of the union of expanse and wisdom. As the root tantra says, "In the eye, the buddha Vairocana," and so on. And again, from the Seven Chapters:

The faculties appearing as objects
Enjoy the wisdom of bliss.
By way of the two stages,
With signs and without signs, one enters;
Joining with the abode of one's own wisdom,
One is freed from all conceptions of signs.

Now, to teach the rite of meditation and recitation:

Through the application of utter equipoise,
Joining in union with all the buddhas,
With passion according to one's inclination,
And with the śatapa and the rest,
So that one never grows weary,
One should always recite beyond measure.

One rests in equipoise by way of the two stages with signs and without signs, and, in the manner of joining with that very state, one joins in union by way of the two buddhas, the actual and the realized. By way of any one of the family maṇḍalas, one engages, in the manner of passion, with songs, music and the mudrās of dance according to one's inclination, and one takes up the songs of śaṭapa, śatajakoha, cośapañcama, niridantaka, ritasita and surṇapipari. In the intervals between sessions, one performs any of the forms, whether the two recitations, the two drops, the two subtle ones, or the two purities, for as long as one does not grow weary, and one should remain constantly in this until the five kinds of signs become clear. As the root tantra says:

Until the signs of the rite
Of meditation, recitation and so forth have become clear,
Abide in the application of utter equipoise
And make the family maṇḍala clear.

The detailed stages of practicing this are to be learned from the Arrangement of the Secret Meaning of Glorious Vairocana. To keep it from ever lapsing:

Having summoned them everywhere, in all things,
By meditating on the abode of all the buddhas,
Oneself, always and in every aspect,
Accomplished as all the buddhas,
One clearly sees, and attains accomplishment.

At all times and in every aspect, having meditated on oneself always in the form of all the buddhas, one will achieve all the accomplishments, highest and middling. As the root tantra says:

Everywhere, from every side, in all things,
By meditating on the abode of all the buddhas,
The very nature of all the buddhas,
One will meet with all the buddhas.

As for the benefit of practicing these, one verse was spoken because those who practice according to the text of the definitive vehicle will in this very life achieve the definitive buddha, the assemblies of heroes and ḍākinīs, and the states of the vidyādharas of the family of Vajradhara.

Now, to show the benefits of the concluding praise, three verses are spoken, beginning:

Vast praise of the qualities of all the buddhas,
By singing this supreme king of praises once...

By singing even once this song that vastly shows the qualities of all the buddhas by way of the body of the Sugata, the wisdom family and the signs, one attains the bliss of inexhaustible accomplishment, gains mastery over all phenomena and accomplishes all the Tathāgatas, and through the maṇḍalas of body, speech and mind and so on, the mindstreams of sentient beings are liberated. This concludes the explanation of the meaning of the chapter of Glorious Vairocana.

Now, in order to discipline, in both common and special ways, those trainees who engage in aversion, whether those on the causal path, those on the path of conditions, or those characterized by the result, the maṇḍala of Glorious Heruka was taught by way of the maṇḍala of the vajra family, which appears in the nonconceptual mirror-like wisdom of the enlightened mind. For this chapter, the passage beginning,

In the past time, there arose formerly
The great self, permanent...

was spoken. Here, first, the time is shown. In the past time, during the eon called Holder of Six, a buddha called Moon of Heroes appeared in the world. In his teaching there was a master, Wheel of Dharma, who had a disciple called the Black Renunciant. Because he practiced the true meaning perversely, he was born in the three lower realms; and when he was freed from there, he became the māra called Matraṅ Rudra, who destroyed the three worlds and destroyed the gods and others, and the teachings as well. As the antidote to tame him, the maṇḍala of Glorious Heruka arose formerly; that is the meaning.

If one asks from what cause: because he practiced in error by way of the eternalism of permanence, the black Rudra arose; and from the essence of the permanence of the three bodies, the essence of Glorious Heruka arose. Its general characteristic is stated thus:

From the scriptures of the Tathāgata,
The ways of beings that arose before.

This should be applied to both the one to be tamed and the one who tames. Again, the characteristic of Rudra, the object toward whom compassion arises:

At that time all sentient beings,
Through utter wrath, ferocity and malice,
Fell into the Howling Hell.

Abiding on the causal path, by the power of practicing wrongly by way of the affliction of aversion, those on the common path and those on the special path, through extremely wrathful body, extremely fierce speech and extremely malicious mind, fell into the Howling Hell. When he was freed from there, the earlier causes and conditions and the aspects of experiencing their results were exhausted, and through the power of the ripening of the result concordant with its cause, he became the māra Matraṅ Rudra. To show this very point:

Then, having passed from there, that being,
Taking rebirth through his habitual tendencies,
Became an exceedingly great malevolent one,
His mind blessed by Māra.

By the power of the ripening of fierce karma, he arose in a fierce form; and further, intoxicated by fierce deeds, he harmed sentient beings through his greatly malevolent practices.

If one asks through what kind of meditation:

Fever spirits, poison and jewel poison,
Sorceresses, yakṣas and grahas,
Māras and vināyakas as well,
Become exceedingly great malevolent ones.

Through practices arising from foul breath, he makes fevers appear that stupefy the life of beings, beginning with the one-day fever and the two-day fever and extending to the various kinds. Through practices of poison arising from foul odors, he steals the lives of others by sight and by touch, and he deceives others in the guise of a jewel. Destructive and sorcerous mantras of application, emanations of his body radiating in various forms, and moreover the hosts of great malevolent ones, such as male and female yakṣas, māras and vināyakas, he swiftly sends forth and makes them pervade every place. His deeds are these:

He destroys the three abodes,
Devours even the practitioners,
Makes the gods and others fall,
And destroys the teachings as well.

The way in which, because of the deeds he carried out thus, the worldly gods went for refuge one after another is shown in two verses, beginning:

Then the gods together with Indra
Went before Brahmā.

Śakra, lord of the gods, who dwells on the summit of Mount Meru, together with the thirty-two minor lords, went to Brahmā for refuge. Brahmā himself could not give them refuge, so the gods together with Indra went before the great Viṣṇu. He too could not give them refuge, so they went for refuge to the Great God (Śiva), the three-eyed one. He too could not give them refuge, so they requested refuge from the great Vajradhara. As the root tantra says: "Then that Rudra gathered the great hosts of ḍākinīs and sorceresses and dispatched curses through various practices. With various weapons accomplished through his former deeds and with fierce secret mantras, he vanquished the gods of the Four Great Kings and went to the upper level above the earth. Dwelling in the Pāruṣyaka grove, he began through various practices to attack even the Vaijayanta palace." Words such as these are spoken at length.

Again, the gathering of all the buddhas through the application of Vajrasattva is taught in two verses, beginning:

Then the Blessed One, the Vajra Bearer,
Through the application of Vajrasattva...

Endowed with the application of the body, speech and mind of the great Vajradhara, he gathered all the buddhas with the sound of the vajra finger snap: crossing his two arms in the manner of the vajra binding, he made them all hear the sound of the vajra finger snap. If one asks whether the sound of a single finger snap can gather all the buddhas: since it arises from the application of body, speech and mind, it has a special power. Thus it says:

If, alarmed by his mere command,
All the buddhas came,
Together with their retinues and the hosts of their circles,
What need is there to mention others?

Then, to show that the maṇḍalas invited in this way abide filling the world systems completely, like a sesame pod packed with sesame seeds, it says:

Through the snapping of the vajra fingers,
The world systems without exception
Were filled by all the buddhas, together with the Vajra Holders,
Like sesame seeds.

Then, having held the council of a great assembly, they resolved to display the deed of great compassion:

For one exceedingly malevolent and fierce,
Peace will be of no benefit;
As the very essence of wisdom and method,
All the Tathāgatas became wrathful ones.

Those of wrathful form, such as the black Rudra, will not be tamed by peaceful bodies. From the cause, which is wisdom, the characteristic of the expanse, and method, great compassion, and by the condition of all the Tathāgatas gathering into one, they appeared as the resultant wrathful maṇḍala. If one asks whether a wrathful body can tame one whom a peaceful body does not tame:

If even those of wrathful character, when enraged,
Burn up the three worlds,
What need is there to mention the three realms without exception
Burned by the wrath of all the buddhas?

If even independent wrathful ones, who hold the afflictions as their own and act on them, can burn the three worlds beneath the earth, upon the earth and above the earth, what need is there to mention that the body of the great Vajradhara, the nature of all the buddhas of the three times gathered into one, transforming into wrathful guise in a twofold manner, burns all three realms without exception in a single instant? These verses teach the manner of the unsurpassed secret intention of the Teacher in the past, and the yogin who follows should train in the same way at such stages as the boundary maṇḍala.

Now, of the teaching that sets out together the characteristics of the ancient event and the things the following yogin should do, first comes the generation of the deity:

Then, on the tip of the nose,
Hold the mind truly firm;
From the long, warm breath of the nose
Arose the application of the Victor's mudrā.

According to the distinctions of the three phases, one truly holds the mind firm and one-pointed on the tip of the nose, outer, inner and secret. From the long syllable hūṃ with the visarga, together with the three kinds of warmth, one should bring forth, through the mudrā of the union of method and wisdom of the great Vajradhara, victorious over the four māras, the maṇḍala that is the magical display of the union of body, speech and mind of Glorious Heruka.

Now, among the characteristics of the deeds of the result, first, to show the guise of the principal deity:

That very Blessed One, the yogin,
Transformed into a vajra wrathful one and came forth;
Glorious Vajraheruka
Utterly destroyed them to ash with his blaze.

Through the application of glorious Vajrasattva, and from the nonduality of method and wisdom, he transformed into the body of the great vajra wrathful one and came forth. If one asks how: the great Vajradhara, endowed with the glory of every mass of qualities, with body, speech and mind inseparable, vanquished all adverse forces. Having become, through the characteristic of the three doors of liberation, the very nature of delight, passion and activity, from the marks of the five wisdoms in their true meaning, he burned and razed everything with a maṇḍala of greatly blazing fire. Further, by way of body color, light rays and so on:

In the blazing state of the blue being,
A red maṇḍala of light blazes;
Fierce as the time of the great destruction,
He is like the fire of a charnel ground.

Since his mind is endowed with the five kinds of wisdom, a red maṇḍala of light blazes upon the color of precious blue beryl, signifying that he is not separate from the two passions. Just as, at the time of the destruction of the container world, fire and the other elements destroy all things, he abides in a fierce body; he is like the fire of a charnel ground. Further, the majesty of his wrathful guise and his ornaments:

Greatly blazing, his hair ablaze,
Wearing a head ornament of many skulls,
Fierce as the time of the great destruction,
Utterly terrifying like a charnel ground;
Possessing such modes as wrath,
His faces change in various ways,
His brows transformed with wrathful frowns,
He dances with a blazing gaze.

The body of the great glorious one, accomplished from the appearance of his own qualities, is adorned with the thirty-two majesties that arose for the sake of taming others, the sixty-one wrathful signs, and the marks of the nine dances. His blazing vajra hair stands straight up. His eyes blaze like the maṇḍalas of the sun and moon, radiating light. He is marked with various head ornaments and ornaments such as a garland of skulls, and adorned with the eight great charnel ground attires, so that he is fierce as the time of the great destruction. His four faces, utterly terrifying like a charnel ground, change in various ways through such modes as wrath, abiding fully in the modes of fierceness, delusion, passion and peace. The tips of his brows are raised upward, yet he abides with the nature of joy; by way of the ten powers he has the majesty of ten wrathful frowns; and he moves and abides with four kinds of blazing dance gestures. As the root tantra says:

The Blood Drinker (Heruka), dark blue, with terrifying faces,
Possessing light rays of great blaze;
The right face, peaceful in nature, is white;
The central one, dark blue, is the fierce face;
The left, the deluding one, is yellow;
The rear, the enamoring one, is red.
Between the brows, wrathful frowns flicker like lightning;
Red eyes, bared fangs, terrifying faces;
Hair and beard beautiful, yellow-black;
His bodily hairs curl upward like vajras.
Erotic, heroic, terrifying,
Laughing, scolding and frightening,
Compassionate, fierce and peaceful:
He has the dance gestures of body, speech and mind.

The deeds of the principal deity's activity:

Together with Paśupati, Mahendra and Upendra,
Along with the Sun, the Moon, Yama and Brahmā,
Having set the three abodes ablaze,
He utterly demolished them like ash.

Maheśvara in the form of Paśupati, who abides on the path of desire; Mahendra, lord of the gods and lord upon the earth, who abides on the path of pride; Kāmadeva, known as Sun and Moon because he illuminates all, who abides in jealousy; Viṣṇu, who in wrath is like Yama, abiding in aversion; and Brahmā, of the path of delusion: these he vanquished, burned the hosts of the three abodes, and demolished them like ash. If one asks why, when elsewhere these are taught as tamed one at a time, here he tames them all: it is because the teaching is intended in terms of what predominates, and because each arises as a portion of the one.

Again, by way of the hand emblems:

With vajra and vajra khaṭvāṅga,
And by ringing the sound of the bell,
Having struck them all down, he then
Revives even those turned to ash.

Here the emblems of the first two hands are mentioned; the rest are shown in the context of the ninth chapter. They are clearly taught in the root tantra:

The first right and left hold the vajra
And the vajra khaṭvāṅga together with the bell;
The two below them spread the skin
Of Bhairava in the manner of the wind;
The third pair hold arrow and bow;
The fourth right holds a conch filled with blood;
The fourth left holds a skull cup,
Filled with great compassion.

Again, in some contexts he appears in such forms as one face and four arms. As the root tantra says:

In some, with one face and four arms:
The two right hold vajra and conch of blood,
The two left a skull cup together with a noose,
The vajra khaṭvāṅga in the crook of the arm.
In some, the vajra and a red skull bowl,
With the khaṭvāṅga as a support at the back.

Thus, after clearly showing the signs of that Heruka who arose from the great Vajradhara, such as his body color and faces, his activity, and the forms of his hand emblems, the characteristics of his guise and ornaments follow. The meaning of "Utterly terrifying like a charnel ground" is given in the root tantra:

With the skins of elephant and tiger
He wears the garments of his upper and lower body;
With nāga kings and garlands of skulls
He adorns the eight places of his body;
The flayed hides of great gods and heroes
He wears as a great shawl;
His body is smeared with ash
And marked with streaks of blood and fat;
Marked with the five mudrās,
Above his ears, sun, moon and jewel garlands.

Thus, in the manner of transforming into the terrifying guise of the body of great blaze, he brought under his power all the hosts of māras, of the arrogant ones and of the great gods, tamed the hosts of the fierce, consumed all their flesh, blood and bones, and caused even those dwelling in saṃsāra to faint. Yet those who had thus been turned to ash he did not abandon, out of great compassion, and he made them take up consciousness once again.

...and he accomplishes all manner of activities as he pleases. If one objects: "Above, he was displayed by means of the great gods; then again he shows another form unlike that; and again he acts as their refuge and binds them," there is no contradiction. He abides as their very nature; he emanates in accordance with the way they are to be tamed; he acts as the refuge that, in a single instant, sets in bliss those oppressed by the suffering of such affliction; and further, he liberates both the one and the other and places them in the state of great awakening. What fault or flaw is there in that? These are signs that appear from the special qualities of greatness.

Moreover, since method without wisdom is impossible, just as a shadow arises from a sprout lit by the radiance of light, he generates the Mother and sets her to her activity:

From the blazing sun of him
Arose a terrible shadow;
He generated her, and she is able to drink the blood
Of all the vicious ones, Māra and the rest.

And the meaning of this is given in the root tantra:

Arising from his own essence,
Krodhīśvarī, in a charming and graceful pose,
Her body pale blue in color, a body of sky,
In the manner called seralkha,
With the khaṭvāṅga resting against her back,
Her left hand holding a skull cup,
Her right hand bearing the triple banner,
Adorned with ornaments of blazing jewels:
He drew her forth and blessed her,
And she became endowed with method and wisdom.
All the bhūtas, Māra and the other arrogant ones,
She bewildered,
And the flesh and blood of the vicious hosts,
All of it, she consumed in a single moment.

These things appear exactly so with regard to the Teacher's intention; with regard to the followers, they should be meditated upon extensively by means of the limb of union and the limb of subsequent union.

Now, in order to generate the retinue by the stage of supreme union:

That king, together with them,
Sports in the charnel grounds,
All united equally in the vajra,
With the blazing maṇḍala of ḍākinīs.

Glorious Heruka, being endowed with the nature of the seven qualities, is like a great king. The goddesses of the retinue arise from the radiance of his body, wisdom and qualities; therefore they dwell together with him and sport in the eight charnel grounds. Who are they? The eight great mātṛkās, Gaurī and the rest; the four goddesses of activity, the Arrow-Holder and the rest; the four offering goddesses, Puṣpā and the rest; and the four gatekeepers, Hayagrīvā the Lofty and the rest. All twenty goddesses are united equally in the essence of vajra body, speech and mind, together with the hosts of the blazing maṇḍala of ḍākinīs.

What activity do they perform?

Having purified the realm of beings
Who are extremely vicious and fierce,
In pure buddha fields
They placed them in the awakening of the buddhas.

They purify the realm of beings who abide on the general and particular paths of the afflictions, such as the māra Matram Rudra, and place them in the awakening of the buddhas. How is this done? By joining them to pure buddha fields. Glorious Heruka tames those on the general path of the afflictions born of aversion, while the goddess Gaurī, by way of pacification and through the activity of mind, purifies the beings who dwell in the realm of aversion and firmly establishes them in the awakening of the buddhas, whose nature is mirror-like wisdom, in the pure buddha field of the vajra family. The other goddesses are to be understood in the same way: Caurī purifies those who dwell on the path of pride; Pramohā purifies those who dwell on the path of desire; Vetālī purifies those who dwell on the path of envy; Pukkasī purifies those who dwell on the path of delusion; Caṇḍālī purifies those who dwell on the general path of the afflictions; Ghasmarī purifies those who dwell in wrong views; and Śmaśānī purifies the conceptual thoughts of doubt and indecision.

Further, by way of vast activity, in order to show the maṇḍala of the wrathful ones:

By Vajra Desire-Wrath and the rest,
By all the unsurpassed ritual systems,
The realm of beings, boundless as the limits of space,
Was purified.

Glorious Heruka sent forth countless maṇḍalas: the maṇḍala in which he is surrounded by the ten great wrathful ones, and the maṇḍalas in which each wrathful one forms a maṇḍala of his own family; and thus he purifies the mindstreams of beings. The stages of subsequent practice should be learned from the Arrangement of the Secret Meaning of Glorious Heruka.

This concludes the explanation of the meaning of the chapter on Glorious Heruka.

Now, in order to tame those on the path of the cause, condition and result of delusion, who bear the general and particular characteristics of the afflictions, hold to the extremes of eternalism and nihilism, and dwell in wrong views, the wisdom of equality appears; and in order to show how the maṇḍala of glorious Padmanarteśvara, of the lotus family, the speech of all the tathāgatas, works for their benefit, first the time of the intention is shown:

In the past time, it arose long ago,
The great being, the eternal...

In the past, in the time of the eon endowed with essence, the Buddha Padmaśrī appeared in the world. Within his teaching, a monk called Supreme Victor practiced the meaning of the Word perversely.
