He held to the extremes of eternalism and nihilism, and being possessed of wrong views, he wandered in the three lower realms and experienced suffering. Released from there, he became one called Arrogant One Dwelling at the Extreme, attached to the path of the afflictions and clinging to his own views. As the antidote to tame him, there emerged from the body of the great Vajradhara himself the great maṇḍala of glorious Padmanarteśvara, together with the dance and methods of magical emanation. From what did it arise? Through the causes and conditions of his clinging to his own view as permanent, the assembly of the common and particular forms of those to be tamed appeared, and the Sugata who tames by way of the permanence of the three bodies arose. Therefore, the general characteristic of those to be tamed and the one who tames is stated in the scripture of the Tathāgata:

The benefit of beings arose long ago.

That is, at the time when the meaning of the Word was practiced perversely, the object for which great compassion arises came about long ago in the form of beings; and, through the power of practicing the scripture of the Tathāgata just as it is, there is an emanation in accordance with that characteristic of great compassion.

Here, the characteristic of the object for which compassion arises is this:

At that time all sentient beings
Came to dwell in wrong views;
Through their exceedingly evil views
They fell into the great Howling Hell.

At that very time, in the eon endowed with essence, through the power of karma all beings without exception dwelt on the path of the affliction of delusion, were utterly bewildered by wrong views, and dwelt in the extremes of eternalism and nihilism. Among them, moreover, he saw the monk Supreme Victor and his followers, and the forms of their causes, conditions and results.

How the characteristic of great compassion arose as the antidote to them:

That very yoga, the Blessed One,
In order to purify the awakening mind,
Through union in lotus passion,
Came to abide as Vajradharma.

The magical display of speech by the great Vajradhara himself is the lotus family: from the essence of the naturally pure awakening mind, as a means of taming others, he appears as Vajradharma. How so? The root tantra says:

His body is white in color, tinged with red,
Beautified by many faces and fifty hands.
His right hands are marked with lotus and vajra,
Wheel, jewel, staff and the rest,
And his left hands
Hold the stems of single lotuses.
Beautified by the major and the excellent minor marks,
He radiates twenty-five rays of light.
Hosts of garlands of ornaments, jewels and the rest,
Silken garments and ornaments, and a crown hanging down;
Seated well in the auspicious cross-legged posture,
He gazes with pride, in the manner of passion.

Further, with regard to the followers, also in the manner of union in lotus passion, the Seven Chapters says:

With the head ornament of the family of blessing,
The lord of the cause, Vajradhara,
Glorious Padmanarteśvara,
His attire, signs and ornaments became clear.

If one asks why, on this occasion, differing names such as Vajradharma are given to the Buddha Amitābha: in terms of the distinction of his nature he is called Dharma; by way of his activity he is called Avalokiteśvara; and by way of the result of his various activities he is called Padmanarteśvara.

The characteristic of his nature is this:

Just as this red lotus
Is not stained by the faults of attachment,
So it is purified of the various faults
Of the habitual tendencies of the three realms.
Take, for example, the maṇḍala of a lotus.

Just as the lotus maṇḍala is born from mud and yet is not stained by its filth, so too this body, the magical display of the lotus family of speech, which arises from the pure essence of the awakening mind, is liberated from the various faults of the three realms. Therefore, because he acts and abides in a manner not separated from the two kinds of passion, he is called Vajradharma. This is also stated in the root tantra, the Lasso of Method:

A lotus parted from the mud dries, hardens and rots;
Just so, abandoning saṃsāra is not the conduct.
Freed from both extremes, he acts,
The body of boundless light that illumines all.
The secret, unsurpassed scripture of the Great Vehicle
Clearly reveals that very maṇḍala.

The characteristic of his activity is this:

Because he sees everywhere,
The Victor too becomes the Lord:
The glorious lord Avalokita,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata.

Having seen extensively the distinctions of faculties and sense sources, in accordance with the karma of those to be tamed, and intending to work for the benefit of others through the corresponding activities of body, speech and mind, he vanquishes the māras by arraying his own qualities; and being endowed with the eight kinds of lordship, he possesses one-pointed great compassion. He who sees everything, at all times and in every way, is Vajrasattva, the very nature of all the tathāgatas.

His manner is this:

Because all desire is pure,
The Blessed One grants the supreme and the holy.

As for the afflictions such as desire, which arise from erroneous conceptions: some hold that one is liberated by indulging in them, some hold that one is liberated by abandoning them, and some hold that one is liberated by purifying them. None of these is complete purity. To know them, without abandoning them, as being of pure nature is the meaning of the unsurpassed secret. Because, by engaging in that very nature, he grants the supreme accomplishment and brings about the holy, he is the Blessed One.

Further, by way of the result of his various activities:

Through various methods of taming
He delights in taming the various.
Avalokita, the lord and victor everywhere,
Became Padmanarteśvara.

In order to tame beings in their own manner, he displays many faces and hands bearing forms of various attire, and many reflections appearing from his body. The ninth chapter says:

Through various kinds of dance and method,
Transforming fully and dancing,
He made the supreme lotus sign into a lotus,
And his eight hands turned the wheel.

And it also says, "He holds various supreme forms, those of all the tathāgatas and others," and so on. Therefore, whoever has the disposition of seeing everywhere is called by that name.

If one asks from what that essence of various activities arises, and what it is like:

The Lotus Māyā King, in equal union,
That Ḍākinī Māyā, Supreme Bliss.

That which arises from method and wisdom, the essence of the nonduality of expanse and wisdom, has the characteristic of the nonduality of the three bodies. Its manifestations, the maṇḍalas of the deities of the lotus family, are the supreme Ḍākinī Māyā.

Further, in order to show his vast deeds by way of various activities:

By glorious Padmanarteśvara and the rest,
By all the unsurpassed ritual systems,
The realm of beings, boundless as the limits of space,
Was purified.

Beings who engage in the conceptions of their own various views are countless. For their sake he showed and taught the maṇḍalas that arose in the past and many stages of practice for the followers. The many maṇḍalas that appear, the root maṇḍala and the rest, are likewise manifestations of glorious Padmanarteśvara, father and mother, and he in turn arises from the blessing of the glorious great Vajradhara. With such a body he purifies beings, however many there may be, and together with magical displays through other doors, he purifies the realm of sentient beings.

The stages in which yogins are to put these into practice should be learned from the Arrangement of the Secret Meaning of Glorious Padmanarteśvara.

This concludes the explanation of the meaning of the chapter on glorious Padmanarteśvara.

Now, in order to take care of beings of general and particular afflictions, who have become exceedingly miserly through craving and clinging and through viciousness and ferocity, there is shown the way in which glorious Vajrasūrya, of the jewel family, the qualities of all the tathāgatas, appearing from discriminating wisdom, brings them relief. Of this, first, the time of the intention:

In the past time, it arose long ago,
The great being, the eternal...

In the past, in the time of the unstable eon, a buddha called Sūryaśrī appeared in the world. Within his teaching, a monk called the Wealthy One practiced the meaning of the Word wrongly. Swept away by the river of the afflictions, he wandered in the three lower realms, and when he was released from there he was born as one called the Arrogant Miser. As the antidote to tame him, from the body of the great Vajradhara himself, the various magical displays of the maṇḍala of glorious Vajrasūrya were proclaimed; so it is said. From what cause? From the great being, the eternal one.

Further, the general characteristic:

From the scripture of the Tathāgata:
The way of beings arose long ago.

This is the general characteristic of those to be tamed and the one who tames: through the power of practicing the meaning of the teaching wrongly and of practicing it as it is, karma and compassion came to be linked.

If one asks what the characteristic of the object for which compassion arises is like, two verses are spoken, beginning:

At that time all sentient beings
Became exceedingly vicious and harsh.

At that very time of the unstable eon, beings in general and particular groups, their minds corrupted by the māras and the gods of the māra class, became extremely vicious in body, extremely harsh in speech and extremely miserly in mind. Therefore they fell into the realms of the hungry ghosts and the hells of Yama, and remained there for a long time. Released from there when that karma was exhausted, in the human realm they were extremely poor and destitute, tormented by the sufferings of hunger and thirst, and they killed one another. When that karma was exhausted, one became a preta rākṣasa called the Arrogant Miser, who robbed all beings of their lives and laid waste the world systems.

In order to show the characteristic of great compassion as the antidote to tame him:

In order to purify the exceedingly vicious,
That very yoga, the Blessed One,
The great wealth of the ultimate meaning,
Came forth as Vajrasūrya.

In order to purify, through every door, those arrogant ones together with their hosts and factions, who cannot be tamed by anyone else, glorious Vajrasattva came forth as glorious Vajrasūrya, a body endowed with all the hosts of the jewels of undefiled qualities. If one asks what he is like, the root tantra says:

Like a golden mountain at sunrise,
Beautiful with four faces and four hands,
In his right hands he holds an arrow and a sword;
In his left, a bow and a sun disk marked with a vajra.
Encircled by a maṇḍala of five-colored light,
Smiling, his left eye raised upward.

And further, for those who are not tamed by peaceful means, he also appears in a wrathful body:

In some places, from being slightly wrathful,
His body has the luster of red coral;
His two feet are seated well in the cross-legged posture,
And he is beautified by the ornaments described before.

Such a body, together with his consort and the maṇḍala of his retinue, endowed with the luster of the sun's disk, he sent forth for the benefit of beings.

Now, to show the deeds of his nature:

Ākāśagarbha in all directions
Fulfills completely the hopes of all;
The supreme jewels, the queen and the rest,
All the jewels, he rains down from the sky.

To the one who possesses the aspects of nature, qualities and activity, names in keeping with these are given: the Blessed One Ratnasambhava himself is known as Ākāśagarbha, Vajraratna, Vajrasūrya and Trailokyavijaya. Through the magical display of goddesses, in such forms as the very essence of empowerment, the one who fulfills hopes with treasures, the one with the form of a jewel banner, and the magical display of offerings, he pervades all places like the essence of space, and fulfills completely the hopes of all directions, just as they are wished. In what form? He rains down from the sky, like rain, such things as the precious queen, the precious minister, the precious general, the precious horse, the precious elephant, the precious wheel and the precious jewel.

Then, for those who are not tamed by such peaceful means, in order to transform into a wrathful one and act for their benefit by way of superior activity:

For those who are exceedingly vicious and wrathful,
Peaceful means bring no benefit;
So through the sun of wisdom and method,
By fierce burning he accomplishes all.

Those misers, the preta rākṣasas together with the hosts of Yama, cannot be tamed by other means. Showing a wrathful body as the fitting antidote, endowed with method, wisdom and great compassion together with the splendor of a hundred thousand suns, he cuts off and dries up all the torments of beings. How does he do this?

Through action, through repelling,
And especially through seizing,
Having made them swoon, of the three abodes
He gathers all the hosts of wealth.

Shooting some weapons, such as arrows, by means of mechanical devices and the like, he drives beings into virtuous action; shooting weapons such as the wheel in secret, he turns them back from sinful action; drawing them back again with the lasso and the like, he seizes all their power and miraculous abilities; and through his special activity he vanquishes all hostile adversaries, makes himself victorious over the three realms, and gathers the hosts of wealth. This is also stated in the root tantra:

Vajrasūrya, the great Victor,
With the mass of the radiance of his own light
Illuminates all the directions
And dispels intense torment.
He drives beings into virtuous deeds,
Turns them back from the path of nonvirtue,
And seizes all their qualities,
Making himself victorious over all the three realms.
Then again, by the power of his great qualities,
He bestows on all whatever they desire.

Now the characteristic of vast activity is shown:

By Trailokyavijaya and the rest,
By all the unsurpassed ritual systems,
The realm of beings, boundless as the limits of space,
Was utterly set free.

The one who possesses the power to subdue the three worlds is called by that name, and he is Vajrasūrya himself. Through the various different maṇḍalas that arise from him he works for the benefit of beings: this is the characteristic of completion in the form of his particular activities just as they are. These show the intention that arose in the past, and in accordance with it they also appear in the form of the practices of the followers; this is taught in common. The stages of their practice should be learned from the Arrangement of the Secret Meaning of Glorious Vajrasūrya.

This concludes the explanation of the meaning of the chapter on glorious Vajrasūrya.

Next, in order to tame those on the general path, such as the hosts of the great arrogant gods, who dwell on the path of envy, swollen with pride in their own views, lazy in their conduct and disrespectful toward the true path, and in particular the Arrogant One of Various Magic together with his faction, there is shown how the maṇḍala of the Blessed One, the glorious Play of Paramāśva, the embodiment of the vast activity of all the tathāgatas, whose appearance is of the nature of all-accomplishing wisdom, is magically displayed. First, the time of the intention:

In the past time, it arose long ago,
The great being, the eternal...

In the time of the eon called Great Splendid Light, a buddha called Dundubhisvara appeared in the world. Within his teaching, a layman called Candra practiced the meaning of the Word wrongly and wandered for a long time in the three lower realms. Released from there, he arose as one called the Arrogant One of Various Magic, together with a host of wives, and he stirred up the afflictions of the great gods. As the antidote to tame him, from the body of the great Vajradhara himself the maṇḍala of the Play of Paramāśva arose long ago; so it is said. From what cause? From the two kinds of permanence, by way of the adverse factor and the antidote. Their general manner is this:

In the scripture of the Tathāgata,
The way of beings arose long ago.

That is, through the condition of practicing the meaning of the Word perversely, and through the power of understanding it as it is, the characteristics of both those to be tamed and the one who tames arose in the manner of beings.

Here, the characteristic of the object for which compassion arises is this:

At that time all sentient beings
Came to have exceedingly little diligence;
Through their exceedingly little diligence
They fell into the hell of Avīci.

At that very time, in the eon of Great Splendid Light, all sentient beings, under the power of laziness and indolence, were themselves without diligence, and they also reviled others who did possess diligence. Because of this they experienced the sufferings of the lower realms and the rest, and when released from there they dwelt among the hosts of the Arrogant One of Various Magic and the like. The characteristic of great compassion as the antidote to tame them is this:

That very yoga, the Blessed One,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
In the manner of fierce union,
Came forth as glorious Paramāśva.

This shows the arising of the maṇḍala of glorious Paramāśva, endowed with the might and prowess to overpower adversaries. In what manner? The root tantra says:

He has four faces and eight hands,
And likewise four legs.
His faces are red and black
And white, and he shows a green horse head.
The color of his body is green,
His hands are red, his legs are black.
His right hands raise the triple banner,
The crossed vajra, the sword and the arrow;
His left hands show a fist with various implements,
And hold a spear, a staff and a bow.
With his right foot he presses the Triangle Goddess and Śrī,
With the second, the Liberating Goddess and Ratī;
With his left, the Three-Eyed One and Viṣṇu,
With the second he crushes Brahmā and Kāmadeva.
He is beautified by the sixty-one marks of wrath
And by the ornaments of great blazing.

Now, in order to act brazenly by way of various deeds:

Through the conviction that all is pure,
Brazenly, through the power of the doer of evil,
With the means of overpowering adversaries,
He is exceedingly vicious, wrathful and fierce.
The realms of beings of many kinds,
All the worlds, he utterly destroys.

He is moistened by the water of love and compassion and regards all beings as an only child. Because all is completely pure, he is free of killing out of aversion and of the inferior conceptions of delusion. By the strength of sinless power and miraculous abilities he acts brazenly. With the might and skill to overpower adversaries such as the Arrogant One of Various Magic, he liberates them and partakes of all their flesh and blood; and pacifying the respective pride of the great gods, he made swoon Brahmā, the foremost of the deluded; Maheśvara, the foremost of the passionate; Viṣṇu, the foremost of the hateful; Śakra, the foremost of the proud; and Kāmadeva, the foremost of the envious, together with their retinues, servants and classes.

Further, in order to tame all by means of corresponding conduct:

As seducer, thief and robber
He purifies everything;
Through deceit and through illusion,
And likewise through brazen force,
Having prevailed by the five arts of combat,
He was then victorious over the three worlds.

On some occasions, by the means of seduction, he enjoys the wives of others and becomes victorious over the three worlds. On other occasions, in the manner of a thief, he gathers kinds of wealth into the vessel of qualities and the like; in the manner of a robber, he seizes openly; in the manner of a swindler, he deceives; and in the manner of a conjurer, he employs illusion. Thus he purifies all beings, and having prevailed by the five arts of combat, he becomes victorious over the three worlds.

The five arts of combat are explained in the twentieth mudrā of the ninth chapter:

Thrown by a device and not thrown,
Likewise thrown by hand,
Thrown and then held back,
And wielded by strength: these are the five.
The examination of the combat arts of the self-arisen ones
Rests in the five mudrās.

If one asks whether such deeds are partial:

The abodes of beings and the migrations,
Having been tamed entirely, are accomplished.

What appear in the manner of the four modes of birth are the abodes of beings.

The beings who arise from them are the sentient beings of the six classes. Gathering all their extremes without remainder, he tamed all their conceptions and brought each to accomplish its own result. His manner is this:

Through various methods of taming
He ever guards his own kingdom.
To the lustful he becomes lustful;
To the wrathful, the supreme of the wrathful;
To the peaceful, the supreme of the peaceful;
The fierce he subdues with ferocity.

To tame those attached to the path of desire, his central face is red; to tame the wrathful, who abide in the continuum of aversion, his left face is shown black; to tame the peaceful, who abide on the path of delusion, his right face is shown white; and to tame the fierce, who abide on the paths of pride and envy, he is shown with a green horse head, wrinkled in wrath.

Now, to show how he pressed the great gods beneath his feet and gathered their wives to himself, verses are spoken. Pressing beneath his feet the supreme lord Mahādeva (Śiva), who holds sway over all the enjoyments of desire, he summons and enjoys the goddess She Who Reverses Austerities, emanated from the eye of the Great God. Pressing down Viṣṇu, the Dark One of the discus, who possesses supreme power and miraculous ability, he summons and enjoys the goddess Rukmiṇī, emanated from his heart. Pressing down Brahmā, the lord of creatures, he summons and enjoys the goddess Tārā, the Peaceful One, who arose from union. Pressing down the god of desire, Kāmadeva, he summons and enjoys the goddess Ratī, the Mistress of Delight, emanated from his radiance. This is also stated in the Ninth Chapter, in the passage beginning:

By the power that overpowers with supreme prowess, brazenly he subdues the Lord;
The great union, hu lu hu lu, draws forth the three and the six from the Three-Eyed One.

Now, to show that he is the embodiment of all activities, six lines are spoken. It is through the maṇḍala of Vajra Paramāśva that all activities are brought to completion, and he is the very nature of Vajrasattva; so it is said. The method of performing their activities is this:

Accomplishing by method and by prowess,
Having accomplished through fierce union,
The entire kingdom of the three realms
And having attained that Vajra Holder,
The boundless supreme awakening mind
He accomplishes through fierce union.

Paramāśva is the maṇḍala of particular activities. At the time of pacifying, he abides as the principal of the maṇḍala of the Buddha Vairocana; at the time of magnetizing, as the principal of the maṇḍala of Padmanarteśvara; at the time of enriching, as the principal of the maṇḍala of Vajrasūrya; at the time of wrathful action, as the principal of the maṇḍala of Glorious Heruka; and at the time of the embodiment of all the families, as the principal of the maṇḍala of glorious Vajrasattva. Therefore he is the embodiment of all activities: he brings about the attainment of the entire kingdom of the three realms and of the state of Vajradhara, and he accomplishes the boundless awakening mind through fierce union. This is also stated in the Seven Chapters:

Paramāśva, generated from haṃ,
Sports in what is common to all.

And from the Condensed Ritual as well:

In the center of whatever maṇḍala
This Paramāśva is placed,
That very maṇḍala becomes his,
And he tames every land.

Further, he acts by way of complete purity. Because he has in view the state of the final result, it is said:

Through the conviction that all is pure,
Brazenly, through sinless power,
Overpowering adversaries with prowess,
He fully unfolds the awakening of the buddhas.

The benefits of their deeds are these:

The difficult accomplishments of the deity,
The awakening of the buddhas, great bliss,
The supreme state, permanent:
These are attained through one's own yoga.

First, the way accomplishment is attained for one's own sake: the deity's accomplishments, so hard to achieve, such as the eight great common accomplishments, and the supreme accomplishment, the body of great bliss, the awakening of the buddhas, which is the supreme undefiled state and abides permanently, are attained through one's own yoga.

Then, for the sake of others:

The realms of beings without exception
He seals without exception,
And the difficult, deathless awakening,
Stable, he bestows on sentient beings.

He seals them with the four mudrās, the magical display of the activities of the six families, and joins them fully to the awakening of their respective families and reveals it to them.

The characteristic of such deeds is this:

Through the magical display of Paramāśva,
Entirely, with none left out,
He remains in existence through all eons
And works for the benefit of all beings.

This shows how, taming beings in accordance with the activities of the families, he abides forever for the benefit of sentient beings.

Now, by way of vast deeds:

Thus by glorious Paramāśva and the rest,
By all the unsurpassed ritual systems,
The realms of beings, boundless as the limits of space,
Were fully accomplished.

By way of various dissimilar wheels magically displayed from a single family, such as the root maṇḍala, in the manner of the unsurpassed ritual systems of body, speech and mind, he spreads forth to the boundless limits of space and works for the benefit of sentient beings. The stages by which yogins put this into practice and accomplish it should be learned from the Arrangement of the Secret Meaning of Glorious Paramāśva.

This concludes the explanation of the meaning of the chapter on glorious Paramāśva.

Furthermore, the way he works for the benefit of all beings on a vast scale by way of various chapters is taught extensively in the great root tantra. In order to summarize what is taught there, the common maṇḍala in which the families are gathered, the various individual maṇḍalas, and the many maṇḍalas that arise from them, two verses are spoken. Of these, the first gives the accounts of how the various maṇḍalas arose, the places into which they spread forth, and the trainees and those who tame them:

These and others, infinite and supreme,
Equal and unequal with the dharmadhātu,
Pervading to the limits of the expanse of space,
Are the signs of the accounts of the self-arisen ones.

The source of all qualities, the very embodiment of all the bodies and wisdoms, is the great Vajradhara himself. It is the wisdom of that great being who has neither beginning nor end, and the six buddhas, the protectors who appear through the distinctions of family. Although each of them arises singly, they appear from family and wisdom, and infinite wheels of maṇḍalas arise, the magical display of their qualities and activities. In the abodes of buddhas and of sentient beings they spread forth into the equal and the unequal, pervaded throughout by phenomena and their true nature, and they pervade and expand to the limits of the expanse of space.

Moreover, how self-arisen emanations appear as remedies that purify the karmic obscurations of each of the various classes of trainees is exemplified by various accounts and stories. These very accounts, as taught in the root tantra, are explained here in summary.

First, how the maṇḍala of glorious Vajrasattva appears for the sake of trainees such as the categories of the arrogant ones. The buddha in whom the three bodies are nondual, the glorious great Vajradhara himself, abiding in the meditative absorption of various magical displays and so forth, sent forth rays of light that thoroughly overpower in a haughty manner, and by the power of the king of mantras he brought forth glorious Vajrasattva, of the same appearance as himself, in two forms, the bodies of method and wisdom: oṃ mahā sukha vajra satva aḥ jaḥ hūṃ vaṃ hoḥ su ra ta stvaṃ. Enjoying the taste of great bliss in his own body, by rays of light of delight he arranged the seven branches of the miracle of wisdom, thoroughly terrifying all the hosts of the arrogant ones; and crushing the pride of their great chief, he abides as the remedy. Again, he displayed the seven branches of the arrangement for vanquishing the other arrogant ones of the retinue, and from his play, nondual with the consort Samantabhadrī, the feminine light of wisdom, he emanated the twenty goddesses of the retinue, who thoroughly subdued the trainees corresponding to them.

Moreover, he brought forth the body of the goddess glorious Supreme Bliss, who appears from mirror-like wisdom and in whom the whole family of compassion is gathered into one, and he spread forth her secret mantra: jaḥ vajra dṛṣṭi sa ma ya ma ṭa. She purifies the state of the Dark Lady of the Charnel Ground and abides in the direction of the natural maṇḍala; by peaceful activity she trains the others too in vast activity, and makes them nondual with the maṇḍala of her own family. In this same manner, for the other goddesses as well, the characteristics of family, wisdom, the activity performed, trainee and tamer should be applied at length.

Thus, the goddess who appears from the jewel family of qualities, whose nature is discriminating wisdom, how wonderful, Blissful Lady, was brought forth, and by hūṃ vajra kī li kī li hūṃ she tamed Jewel Glory-Stealer completely. The goddess Illuminatrix, who appears from the wisdom of equality and in whom the whole lotus family of speech is gathered into one, was brought forth in bodily form, and by vaṃ vajri ṇi sma ra raṭ she tamed the Lady Whose Body Is Wholly Wrapped in Lotuses. The goddess Disciple Lady, whose nature is all-accomplishing wisdom and in whom the whole family of activity is gathered into one, was brought forth, and by hoḥ vajra kā me śva ri stvaṃ she tamed the Fierce Destroyer of Places. These are the appearances of the four wisdoms, the sugatas of the four families.

Again, other goddesses emanated from the four great mothers of the families were brought forth. By oṃ aṃ ma dhu vajri ghuṃ ghuṃ the goddess who takes the form of buddhas and bodhisattvas was tamed. By oṃ vajra me ghe gu lu gu lu bhūṃ the Lady Wheel of Dharma tamed the Fierce Troop-Holder Lady. By oṃ ma he śva ra ca vajri oṃ oṃ Trailokyavijayā, Victor over the Three Realms, tames the host of evil-doing women who accomplish the three. By oṃ khaṃ vajra śe śi ri hūṃ hūṃ the goddess Desire Creeper Lady tamed Swift-Minded Embodied Lady. These are the occasion on which the hosts of the root arrogant ones were tamed by the root maṇḍala.

Again, the ḍākinīs of the outer maṇḍala are tamed by the ḍākinīs of the outer maṇḍala. Vajra Flute Lady arose, and by oṃ he ra ti vajra vi lā si ni traṭ she tamed the Lady Who Loudly Sends Forth Thunder. Vajra Vīṇā Lady was brought forth, and by he re ti vajra gī te te he, with three strings, she tamed the Fierce Lady. Vajra Great Drum Lady was brought forth, and by he re ti vajra tra se ha ha vaṃ she tamed the Lady Endowed with All Names and Words. Vajra Clay Drum Lady was brought forth, and by he ra ti vajra nṛ tye ve pa ve pa jaṃ she tamed the Fierce Lady of Sound and Voice. Vajra Puṣpā was brought forth, and by oṃ vajra puṣpe she tamed the desire goddess Form-Holder. Vajra Incense Lady was brought forth, and by oṃ vajra dhuppe she tamed the Three-Pointed Fragrant Lady. Vajra Lamp Lady was brought forth, and by oṃ vajra ā lo ke she tamed the Lady Garland of Lightning. Vajra Scented Water Lady was brought forth, and by oṃ vajra ghandhe she tamed the Fierce Moving Lady. Hayagrīvā the Lofty was brought forth, and by oṃ vajra aṃ ku śi jaḥ she tamed the Fierce Summoning Lady. Sow-Faced Noose Lady was brought forth, and by oṃ vajra pā śe hūṃ she tamed the Fierce Binding Lady. Vajra Illumination Lady was brought forth, and by oṃ vajra śaṃ kha le vaṃ she tamed the Lady of Subject and Object Hard to Turn Back. The Ash-Destroying Vetālī was brought forth, and by oṃ vajra ghaṇ ḍe hoḥ she tamed the Wrathful Lady Who Reduces to Ash.

In this way the families of goddesses emanated from the maṇḍala of glorious Vajrasattva purify the chief of the arrogant ones. And further, in order to tame the inconceivable troops and classes that dwell in her family and retinue, each goddess in turn also works for their benefit by way of an expanded maṇḍala, and so seventy-one different maṇḍalas arose within the family of Vajrasattva. In the same way the other family maṇḍalas are also distinguished, and then, as explained above, through the divisions of the maṇḍalas they become countless. Teaching them only by way of their chief figures is the condensed method of the chapters; the extensive elaboration should be understood in the manner of the expanded version.

Furthermore, to teach it by way of the Blessed One, glorious Vairocana: for the sake of trainees such as the Fierce One with the Host of Darkness, from the glorious great Vajradhara he brought forth the body of the buddha Vairocana in two forms, father and mother, and by uttering oṃ bhrūṃ khaṃ he tamed the chief of the arrogant ones himself. Again, in order to tame the hosts of the retinue, he brought forth Buddha-Awakening Lady, Heroine, Samantabhadrī, Holder of the Undefiled Vessel, Lady Possessing All Buddhas, Beautiful Lady, Great Cloud Lady and Eye of Space Lady, and by uttering their individual mantras they tamed She Who Roars Loudly, Hero's Weapon Lady, She with the Garland of Beauty, Queen of the Supreme Mind, All-Formed Lady, Great Beauty, Fierce Lady of the Great Sound and She with Changing Eyes.

Again, he brought forth twelve goddesses, beginning with Wheel Vīṇā Lady, and by uttering each her own secret mantra they tamed Holder of the Three Flowers, Ravisher of the Minds of Beings, Holder of the Three Banners, Utterly Wrathful Lady, Holder of the Flower Arrow, Black Lady Who Moves in Water, She with the Garland of Red Women, Profound Lady of Various Supreme Scents, She Who Leads by a Fierce Form, Utterly Wrathful Mind Lady, Skeleton Binding Lady and Great Lady Who Stupefies.

Furthermore, the way he works for beings through the maṇḍala of Glorious Heruka: the trainees are those of the general and the particular paths, such as Rudra. As the remedy for them, from the body of the great Vajradhara there arose Glorious Heruka in nondual union with the great consort, and by uttering his own mantra he tamed their chief himself. Furthermore, he brought forth the goddesses Gaurī, Caurī, Pramohā, Vetālī, Pukkasī, Caṇḍālī, Ghasmarī and Śmaśānī, and by uttering each her own mantra they tamed Fierce Lady of the Three Words, She with the Troop of Fangs, She of Ugly Form, Place-Destroying Weapon Lady, All-Roaming Lady, Stealer of Life, Worker of Terror and Messenger Who Accomplishes Aims. Again, he brought forth the goddesses Arrow-Holder, Khaṭvāṅga-Holder, Wheel-Holder and Holder of Various Banners, and by uttering their individual mantras they tamed the rākṣasī Scatterer of Human Blood, Fierce Trident Lady, Swift-Minded Cutter and Holder of Various Arrows. Again, he brought forth the eight wrathful ladies beginning with Flower Lady, and by uttering their individual mantras they tamed Skeleton Scatterer, She Who Has the Scent of Incense, Indra's Garland Lady, the rākṣasī Blood Scatterer, Holder of the Charnel Ground Garland, Swift Binding Lady, Great Restraining Lady and Destroyer of the Three Lineages.

Furthermore, by way of glorious Padmanarteśvara: for the sake of various trainees such as the Arrogant One Dwelling at the Extreme, from the glorious great Vajradhara himself he brought forth Padmanarteśvara, father and mother, a body in which method and wisdom are nondual, and by uttering his own secret mantra he tamed the king of the māras. In order to tame his retinue, he brought forth the goddesses Beholder Lady, Tārā, Earth-Holder, Wrathful Frowning Lady, Lady Lord of the Fierce Lotus, Various Lotus Lady, Lotus-Robed Lady and Lady Lord of the Vajra Lotus, and by uttering their individual mantras they tamed She Who Sees All Directions, Great Lady Hard to Turn Back, Lady of the Place, She with the Terrifying Face, She Who Emits a Terrifying Sound, She with Wings of Flame, She Clad in Human Skin and She Who Agitates All. He brought forth twelve, beginning with Lotus Flute Lady, and by uttering their individual mantras they tamed Singer, Guardian of Wealth, Destroyer, All-Doer, Holder of the Flower Parasol, Armor of the Wheel, All-Roaming Lady, Destroyer of Stations on the Path, She Who Shatters and Disturbs Diligence, Restrainer of All Places, Doer of Terrifying Deeds and Fierce Terrifier.

Furthermore, for the sake of trainees such as the Arrogant Miser, the way the maṇḍala of glorious Vajrasūrya appears: from the glorious great Vajradhara he made the body of Vajrasūrya, father and mother, appear, and by uttering his own mantra he tames the chief of the arrogant ones himself. In order to tame his retinue, he brought forth the goddesses Empowering Lady, Treasure-Possessing Lady, Lady with the Supreme Banner, Great Offering Lady, Arrow-Holding Lady, Khaṭvāṅga-Holding Lady, Sun Lady and Vajra Peak Lady, and by uttering their individual mantras they tamed the Adorned Mātṛkā, Invincible Heroine, All-Enjoying Lady, All-Devouring Lady, Image Lady, Victorious in Battle, She with the Eyes of the Sun and Burner of the World. He brought forth twelve outer goddesses, beginning with Jewel Flute Lady, and by uttering their individual mantras they tamed the Great-Bellied Preta Lady, Enemy-Seizer, She with the Force of the Wind, the Fanged Yakṣiṇī, She Who Seizes All and Binds by Oath, Transformer of the Discordant, Self-Luminous Lady, Scatterer of Foul Smells, Summoning Lady, Binding Lady, Fierce Lady of Disease and Black Club Lady.

Furthermore, by way of the Play of Paramāśva: for the sake of various trainees such as the Arrogant One of Various Magic, from the glorious great Vajradhara himself he brought forth Paramāśva, father and mother, a body in which method and wisdom are nondual, and by uttering his own mantra he tamed the king of the māras himself and the Great God and the hosts of the arrogant ones.

All of their retinues he tamed by way of two special goddesses and various common goddesses. Various Lotus, Various Vajra Lady, Queen of Great Passion in the manner of the maṇḍala of the four activities, and All-Enjoying Lady of Activity vanquished the troops of the same class as them. Thus, since he magically displays vast maṇḍalas, and through the maṇḍalas that appear in each of them he also tames countless hosts of trainees, this is called the Various Chapters.

As for the nature from which they all arise: in the expanse of space glorious Vajrasattva is fully accomplished. Just as, for example, sesame oil is pressed from sesame seeds and pervades them all, so all things of the container world and its contents are pervaded without interval by the maṇḍala of deities, and these arise from the nature of glorious Vajrasattva.

Furthermore, by way of the gathering of deeds together with their result:

By the lord of accomplishment, perfectly accomplished,
All are made to enter into their own nature.

By carrying out the rites of the actions to be performed by way of their respective activities, all the appearances of qualities become clear and are joined to the state of the intrinsic nature. "The seal of the account" means the explanation of the vast characteristics of what happened before and what followed after.

This concludes the explanation of the meaning of the fifth chapter.

What serves as the path for accomplishing all aims of oneself and others is the work of the accumulations of merit and wisdom. At the time when the two paths, the point that distinguishes this from other vehicles, are practiced as one, it joins one to the state of all qualities; and by bringing all the activities of the three doors to completion it gives rise to the whole path together with its result, and accomplishes all of one's own excellent qualities. As the root tantra says:

By the power of thoroughly refining
The great accumulations of merit and wisdom,
All results are perfected,
And one is joined to the state of one's own wisdom.

Here, first, the promise to explain the feast maṇḍala:

Then furthermore, in every respect,
Supreme in its various forms,
The Union with All Buddhas,
Ḍākinī Illusion Supreme Bliss, is explained.

In terms of what is to be shown, it follows the perfected branches with the line "and beginning the stages of the feast"; in terms of the wording, it comes immediately after the fifth chapter, and so the two are connected.

Moreover, so that yogins may accomplish the path through the particulars of their conduct, they should engage in the various rites of the feast according to their own inclination and means. Having understood the particulars of place, time and so forth, they should take up and carry out the various stages of the feast gathering, the feast accomplishment, the feast offering, the festival of Heruka and the banquet of the heroes' time, just as they appear in the rites of each teaching. By the power of practicing them, and by the power of the supreme perfection of causes and conditions, the path is refined and one is joined to the supreme result. By this one accomplishes the unsurpassed bliss, whose essential nature is mental joy and which becomes the ground and support of excellent qualities. Therefore the Seventh Ritual Section says:

Whatever the yogin's work and practice,
That is accomplished by the feast.
By joining the accumulations of merit and wisdom,
One becomes supreme in various forms.
The means of that joining are fivefold:
The stable place, the three times, the preliminaries,
And the rite of the five accomplishments;
By practicing these, all actions are brought to completion.
Therefore the supreme limb of the path
Is the great accumulation of merit and wisdom.
Whoever, without first joining the path of accumulation,
Undertakes the stages of the actions to be accomplished,
Like a seed without its support,
Will not ripen well nor come to accomplishment.

The meaning of this is as follows. In order to explain the point that the higher and middling actions of the feast maṇḍala are carried out in the manner of Union with All Buddhas, in the manner of Ḍākinī Illusion Supreme Bliss, it is taught in the sixth chapter; the stages of accomplishment should be learned from the individual rites. As the root tantra says:

Union with All Buddhas,
Through the enjoyment of the joy and bliss of the feast,
Accomplishes the bliss of Ḍākinī Illusion.
Supreme emanation for the sake of all,
Through the two, the accomplishment of the feast gathering
And the banquet of the host of offerings,
It accomplishes excellent aims.
The body of the secret, the great secret itself,
Abides in a vajra-like state.
The supreme king of feast maṇḍalas,
The mode of the two accomplishing limbs,
The wheel of the means of dance, I shall fully explain.

In order actually to accomplish the maṇḍala in accordance with the meaning thus explained, first the characteristics of the deity are shown:

The glorious one, the reality of all buddhas,
Arisen from the bliss of Vajrasattva,
Delighting in the supreme secret,
Ever abides as the self of all.

He who is surrounded inseparably by the host of goddesses is "the glorious one." He abides as the body of Vajrasattva, the embodiment of all buddhas, in the manner of method and wisdom united in embrace. Thus he enjoys the bliss in which what is indicated and what indicates are linked; having displayed the magical array of the maṇḍalas of the six families, he enjoys the realm of trainees; possessing the intention of the five secrets, he delights in the six supreme actions; and through the delight of the four enjoyments he ever abides in the activity of radiating and gathering as the embodiment of all the wheels. As the Seventh Ritual Section says:

The body of glorious Vajrasattva,
Where method and wisdom are nondual,
Abides in the secret place of great bliss,
Possessed of the five secret intentions.
Delighting through the six supreme actions,
He enjoys the four delights.
Supreme embodiment of all the wheels,
Performing actions, radiating and gathering, he ever abides.
Through the body of the maṇḍala of the six families,
He acts as chief of the hosts of matching families.

Thus, for those who accomplish the actions of the feast maṇḍala, a preliminary rite is needed first. In this, the adornment of the maṇḍala is:

The glorious pledge of all buddhas,
The bliss of Vajrasattva's appearance,
The supreme divine substances of the tathāgatas,
Adorned with precious ornaments and the like:
When the sacred canopy is hung,
Furnished with bells and supreme banners,

Garlands made from materials such as the gems beryl (vaiḍūrya), kartika, aśmarāga, sapphire (indranīla), topaz (puṣparāga), spug, diamond, emerald, andarnyil, niman, crystal, zi stone and pearl, whether actually obtained, emanated by the mind, or blessed by mantra, mudrā and meditative absorption; tassels, adornments of lattices; parasols of cotton, silk and so forth; victory banners, pennants, canopies, curtains, fringed hangings and the like: these arise from the appearance of Vajrasattva, and that bliss itself is the pledge of all buddhas, in which the attachment of qualities and the appearance of activities abide inseparably. Furthermore, the great mansion, adorned with the divine substances of the tathāgatas such as the precious porticoes, through the sound of the ringing bells and so forth, proclaims the profound and the vast, and so makes manifest and clear the suchness of each.

Furthermore, in order to set out all the vast hosts of offerings:

Vajra words and offerings and the rest,
Songs and music emanated,
Preparations of flowers and incense,
Endowed with lamps, scents and the like,

One should set out the hosts of offerings together with the offering goddesses: as particulars of form, goddesses fashioned as images of the seven precious things, the eight offering substances, the eight auspicious substances, the excellent vase, the wish-fulfilling tree, the wish-granting cow, the wish-fulfilling jewel and the like. As particulars of sound, one should set out flutes, vīṇās, clay drums, round drums, conches, copper horns, pearl rattles, nine-stringed zithers, cymbals, clappers and the like, both those that actually make sound and those fashioned as musical instruments and images. As the host of scents, one should set out special substances made from materials such as aloeswood (aguru), turuṣka and incense, together with gośīrṣa sandalwood, serpent-essence sandalwood, saffron, camphor and the like. Those produced from compounded and from natural ingredients should also be set out in their respective places. As the host of tastes, one should set out the six tastes, sweet, sour and so forth, and the various kinds of banquet dishes made of compounded substances. As particulars of the tangible, one should hang up upper garments, lower garments, diadems, earrings, armlets, necklaces, long necklaces, garlands and other ornaments.

Furthermore, all the substances of protection, arrows, ritual daggers, swords, white mustard seed, guggulu, ash, the respective hand emblems, sand and urine, should be arranged in their respective places. The substances of offering and torma should be prepared and set out as explained in the Knowledge of the Mudrās of All Offerings and the Knowledge of the Torma Mudrās of All Offerings. The limbs of the general offering, flowers, incense, lamps, scented water and food, should be arranged in the eight places. The rites of blessing them and so forth should be learned from the sādhana.

If one asks what action is accomplished by practicing in this way, then, to explain the meaning of the accomplishment:

Union with All Buddhas
Accomplishes Ḍākinī Illusion Supreme Bliss.

One should begin by way of the maṇḍala in which the families are gathered, and one should begin by way of the maṇḍalas of the families in sequence. First one accomplishes by way of the wheel of merit, and when that has been completed, one should accomplish by way of the feast wheel of wisdom. As the root tantra says:

The vast maṇḍala of the gathered families,
The accomplishment of the maṇḍalas in sequence,
And any maṇḍala at all
Of a single family or a single hero:
One fully applies the mudrā of accomplishment.
This teaches entry into the stages
Of the great accumulations of merit and wisdom.

To teach that these aims to be accomplished should be accomplished, by a person who is a fit support endowed with excellent qualities, in a place that he desires:

Or else one accomplishes in pleasant places,
Or in parks and the like.

The meaning is that those who do not have the excellent place of their earlier undertaking should accomplish it in a place of their own choosing, such as a pleasant spot or a park.

As for the actions to be accomplished there: first, for the accumulation of merit, one who has completed beforehand the rite of the ground and the rite of the preparatory stay should lay out the lines of the maṇḍala and draw it. Then, in order to set out only the seats in their respective places without disturbing the colored powders:

There first one arranges the seats,
Covered with various lotus cloths,
Soft and pleasant to the touch.
These are the seats that are wholly pure.

Whatever the maṇḍala, as many yogins as there are, according to the number of deities, should each sit on their own seat. The cushion itself should actually be covered with a lotus cloth, or the cloth should be marked with a lotus. If the number of yogins is greater than the number of deities in the maṇḍala, they should be placed two, three or four at a time in the positions of the offering deities and the activity deities; and if the number is smaller, crafted images should be set in their places. As the root tantra says:

As in whatever maṇḍala it may be,
One should take on the guise of the mudrā.
Those who are more than their own positions
Should be placed and set in position
As human deities of offering and activity.
Where they are incomplete, fill the gaps with images.
Through their respective yogas,
They take on the signs while seated upon lotuses.

Thus one who is endowed with these preliminary limbs should enter the stages of the feast gathering. First, in order to gather the accumulation of merit, the magical display of the maṇḍala through the stages of meditation is:

Union with All Buddhas:
Through the magical display of the lord of union,
Glorious deity Vajrasattva
Then performs the magical display.

In terms of the maṇḍala of the gathered families, there are two hundred and sixty deities; in terms of the sequence of the maṇḍalas of the individual families, one hundred and twenty-eight deities; by way of the individual family maṇḍalas taken separately, twenty-one deities, with the two goddesses of Paramāśva in addition; and by way of the maṇḍala of the hero, father and mother, thirty deities: these are magically displayed.

If one asks by way of what, it arises from the nondual essence that is the meaning of union with all buddhas. If one asks in what manner, it is by way of the magical display of the lord of union. If one asks through what condition, it arises from the glorious deity Vajrasattva. If one asks through what result it engages in activity, the higher maṇḍalas bring to accomplishment the respective tasks of those above.

In this way the vast maṇḍalas too are gathered into the six kinds of family maṇḍala. Of these, the manner in which the maṇḍala of Vajrasattva is magically displayed is as follows. The body of Vajrasattva appears from the very nature of the glorious great Vajradhara, beautiful with a face like a conch, a jasmine flower or the moon. From the radiance of his wisdom stream rays of light of the five precious substances, and he is beautified by the bodies of the buddhas of the six families that arise from them. With a playful, delighted, smiling gaze he sits in the manner of passion. He is marked by four faces, white, blue, yellow and red, and wears a jeweled crown and diadem. With the first right and left hands he holds a five-pointed vajra proudly at his heart and rings a khaṭvāṅga with a bell. The lower two hold arrow and bow. He is adorned with the thirty-two excellent major marks and the eighty excellent minor marks, and beautified by the five mudrās. On his upper body he wears an upper garment of white silk with golden stripes, and on his lower body a garment of fine cloth, with a girdle of jewel garlands. He is marked by a throat ornament, a necklace, armlets, bracelets and anklets, and a long necklace. He sits in union with Samantabhadrī, who resembles himself, or is like the goddess Supreme Bliss.

In the four directions around him sit the four secret goddesses, each embracing one of four men of light who resemble him. In the east is the goddess Supreme Bliss, white in color, beautiful with garments and ornaments of many kinds, gracefully drawing bow and arrow in her hands; she sits upon a lotus maṇḍala. In the south is Blissful Lady, golden in color, holding in her hands the signs of bhaga and liṅga, and adorned with vajras and jewels. In the west is the goddess Illuminatrix, of red hue; in her right hand she holds a crossed vajra, and embracing the body of the lord, with her left she holds a skull vessel filled with nectar, with a khaṭvāṅga set in the crook of her arm. In the north is Disciple Lady, the color of sapphire (indranīla); with her right she embraces the lord, and in her left she holds a vase of nectar marked with the body of a buddha.

Again, in the eastern outer square is Buddha-Awakening Lady, of white and red hue, shooting at once four arrows marked with the four heads of Brahmā. In the south is the Lady Wheel of Dharma, of white and yellow hue; on the middle finger of her right hand she spins a white eight-spoked wheel and a blue eight-spoked wheel stacked one upon the other, raising them as if to throw; her left hand rests gracefully as a fist upon her hip. In the west is Trailokyavijayā, of pale red color; in her right she holds a three-pointed trident (triśūla) raised aloft, and with her left she holds a five-pointed vajra at her heart. In the north is the Lady of Desire, green in color; in her two hands she holds a wish-fulfilling tree with its tendrils swaying in the wind, and she dances. Thus all these goddesses of the root maṇḍala are beautiful with jeweled ornaments and attire, and they sit in a graceful posture, united in embrace with consorts of their own light.

In the southeastern square is Vajra Flute Lady, the color of beryl (vaiḍūrya), sounding a flute marked with a vajra. In the square of Nairṛti, the southwest, is Vajra Vīṇā Lady, golden in color, sounding on a vīṇā marked with a vajra the notes of the six melodies. In the northwestern corner is Vajra Great Drum Lady, the color of aśmarāga, beating a round drum marked with a vajra. In the corner of Īśāna, the northeast, is Vajra Clay Drum Lady, the color of sapphire, beating a clay drum marked with a vajra.

Again, in the outer southeast is Vajra Puṣpā, red, holding a basin of flowers marked with a vajra. In the southwest is Vajra Incense Lady, white and blue, holding burning incense marked with a vajra. In the northwest is Vajra Lamp Lady, red, raising a lamp marked with a vajra. In the northeast is Vajra Scented Water Lady, green, sprinkling scented water marked with a vajra.

Again, at the eastern gate is Vajra Hayagrīvā the Lofty, the color of water crystal, with a hook in her right hand and in her left a skull of nectar together with a horse's head. At the southern gate is Sow-Faced Lady, the color of beryl, holding a tusk in her right hand and a noose in her left. At the western gate is Illumination Lady, of clear red hue, holding in her two hands a chain marked with sun and moon. At the northern gate is Ash-Maker Lady, green, ringing a bell in her right hand, and with her left drawing forth the body of the buddha Amitābha from within a skull bowl of nectar. These twenty goddesses too are beautified by ornaments, attire and maṇḍalas of light. In the condensed form, all the goddesses are of the same color, as taught in accordance with the meaning stated in the ninth chapter.

Again, for the emanation of the maṇḍala of Vairocana: the buddha who appears from the great Vajradhara himself is golden in color, endowed with the light of a hundred thousand suns, and sits like a massive snow mountain. He has four faces, colored gold, kārtika, sapphire (indranīla) and aśmarāga. In his four hands he holds a Dharma robe, a begging bowl, a ringing staff and a volume of the sublime Dharma. He wears the attire of a wheel-turning king, is beautified by the seven precious things and by every enjoyment of the senses, and sits in union, inseparable from his consort of his own light, the mother Buddhalocanā.

To his east is All-Buddhas-Proclaiming Lady, the color of crystal, beautified by every jeweled ornament, holding in her hand a lotus marked with tongues of flame. To the south is the heroine Supremely Victorious Lady, golden, holding cymbals in her two hands. To the west is Samantabhadrī, the color of ruby, holding in her two hands a five-pronged vajra and a jewel casket. To the north is Irresistible Jewel-Vessel-Holding Lady, multicolored, holding a vessel filled with jewels and a Dharma conch.

Again, in the outer eastern court is All-Buddhas-Holding Lady, saffron-colored, holding at her heart the body of the buddha Akṣobhya. To the south is the goddess Fair-Faced Lady, the color of a mirror, holding in her two hands a mirror and a yak-tail whisk. To the west is the goddess Great Cloud Lady, the color of beryl, who with her two hands brings down hail from a maṇḍala of vajra clouds. To the north is Sky-Eye Lady, pale yellow, holding in her two hands a lotus maṇḍala marked with an eye. The outer goddesses, the four beginning with Flute Lady, the four beginning with Flower Lady, and the four beginning with Hayagrīva Tall Lady, are arranged as taught for the maṇḍala of Vajrasattva, with this slight difference: they wear peaceful attire, and their ornaments and hand emblems are marked with the shape of a wheel.

Again, for the emanation of the maṇḍala of glorious Heruka: the resultant Heruka who appears from the great Vajradhara himself has a body the color of beryl, with rays of great blazing light. His right face is white, of peaceful nature; the central face is dark blue, of wrathful aspect; the rear face is red, the maker of passion; and the left face is yellow, the maker of delusion. His brows are knit in ten wrathful furrows, and from his red eyes fly terrifying sparks of fire. His hair and beard are tawny black, coiled and standing upright, and his body hairs curl to the right in the manner of vajras. His mouth is open, his fangs are bared, his tongue is curled, and his palate is struck. He abides with the radiance of a smile, and with the radiance of the erotic, the heroic, the hideous, the comic, the furious, the terrifying, the compassionate, the marvelous and the peaceful.

His first right and left hands hold a vajra and a vajra khaṭvāṅga marked with a bell. The two below them hold the hide of a great Bhairava, spread out like the wind. The third pair hold an arrow and a bow. The fourth right hand holds a great conch filled with blood, in the posture of drinking; the fourth left holds a great conch filled with hearts.

Again, in some sources he has one face and four arms: the two right hands hold a vajra and a conch filled with blood, the two left are marked with a skull cup and a noose, and he carries a vajra khaṭvāṅga in the crook of his arm. Again, in some sources he has one face and two arms, holding a vajra and a great conch filled with blood, and leaning back against a khaṭvāṅga.

The bodies of all of these are marked with a fresh elephant hide, clusters of snakes, a tiger skin, the flayed hide of the Great God (Śiva), a garland of skulls, and streaks of blood, grease and ash. They are adorned with the five mudrās, have earrings of sun, moon and jeweled garlands, and sit in union with the consort Krodheśvarī.

In front of him, in the east, is Gaurī, whitish yellow, with a peaceful radiance. She has four faces, and in her eight hands she draws a bow and arrow, with which she has purified the four heads of Brahmā; she stands in a dancing posture with the left leg extended. In the south is Caurī, reddish yellow, with a fierce radiance. In her left hand she holds a hook, and with the middle finger of her right she raises and holds an eight-spoked wheel; leaning on a khaṭvāṅga, she stands in the posture of a raised right foot. In the west is Pramohā, reddish black, with two faces, the upper one the face of a sow, and four arms. The first two hold a vajra and a bowl filled with liquor, and with the lower two, clenched into fists, she lifts a wheel. She stands in a posture with the right leg extended and has a deluding radiance. In the north is Vetālī, white, in the manner of raising the dead, standing in a dancing posture of joy. In her right hand is a skull cup with the proper marks, filled with nectar, and her left makes the three-banner mudrā.

In the direction of fire is Pukkasī, fierce and multicolored. In her right hand she brandishes a five-pronged vajra, and in her left she holds a wish-fulfilling tree with its shoots; her face changes with the glances of the dance. In the corner of Nairṛti is Caṇḍālī, dark blue, in the manner of exhaling. In her right hand is a bellows filled with wind, marked with a vajra, which she scatters, and in her left she holds a trident, dancing. In the corner of the wind is Ghasmarī, yellowish black, in the manner of licking a corpse. She holds a sword in her right hand and a hearth in her left, and stands in a dancing posture with the left leg extended. In the corner of Īśāna is Śmaśānī, dark blue, like the four-armed Heruka: her two right hands hold a vajra and a conch of blood, her two left hold a skull cup and a noose, and she carries a khaṭvāṅga in the crook of her arm. All these goddesses are adorned with garlands of skulls and knotted snakes, wear fresh human hides and silk garments, and are adorned with jeweled garlands.

Again, in the outer southeast corner is Arrow-Holding Lady, the color of crystal, holding an arrow and a bow in her hands. In the southwest is Khaṭvāṅga-Holding Lady, yellow, holding in her hand a khaṭvāṅga with a bell and a banner. In the northwest corner is Wheel-Holding Lady, reddish yellow, holding in her hand an eight-spoked wheel. In the northeast is Variegated-Seat-Holding Lady, green with the luster of crystal, holding in her hand a banner together with a bell.

Further, the four outer goddesses beginning with Flower Lady and the four beginning with Hayagrīva Tall Lady are like those of the maṇḍala of glorious Vajrasattva, with this slight difference: they abide in wrathful attire and are distinctly marked with a blue wrathful vajra.

Again, for the emanation of the maṇḍala of glorious Padmanarteśvara: the emanated body that comes from the great Vajradhara himself, glorious Padmanarteśvara, is clear white in color like a mountain of crystal, with fifteen faces full of radiant luster. Beautiful with a hundred hands and twenty-four feet, he sits on a lotus maṇḍala. All his hands hold lotuses; those on the right are adorned with a vajra, a wheel and so forth, and the lotuses on the left also have the luster of their various colors. From his body radiates light of twenty-five colors; he is decked with ornaments and garments of various jewels and endowed with the major and minor marks. In some sources he is made with four faces and eight arms, or has the form of one face and four arms, and has the radiance of the sign of standing like a trunk of coral. With his own radiance, the consort Pāṇḍaravāsinī sits embracing him.

In the east is Beholding Lady, with the luster of ruby. With her left hand she holds a red lotus, closed, and with her right she opens it. In the south is Tārā, green. With her left hand she holds a green lotus by its stalk, resting it at her waist, and with her right she draws it forth. In the west is Earth-Holding Lady, blue, holding a white lotus in her left hand and an eight-spoked wheel in her right. In the north is Bhṛkuṭī, green, holding the stalk of a blue lotus in her left hand and raising the mudrā of a trident with her right.

Again, in the outer east is Fierce Lotus Mistress, dark blue, holding a red lotus in her left hand and raising a vajra upward with her right. In the south is Various Lotus, blue, holding a various lotus in her left hand and raising an eight-spoked wheel upward with her right. In the west is Lotus-Garbed Lady, yellow, holding a lotus in her left hand and a makara banner in her right. In the north is Padmeśvarī, red, holding a red lotus in her left hand and brandishing a five-pronged vajra at her heart with her right. These goddesses too are clothed in jeweled ornaments and silk garments. Further, the four goddesses beginning with Flute Lady, the four goddesses beginning with Flower Lady, and the four gatekeepers beginning with Hayagrīva Tall Lady are arranged as explained for the maṇḍala of glorious Vajrasattva, with this slight difference: they have faces bright with passion and are draped with garlands of lotuses.

Again, for the emanation of the maṇḍala of Vajrasūrya: from the great Vajradhara he is brought forth in the body of Vajrasūrya. His radiance is clear, like the light of a sun disk rising upon a golden mountain. He has four faces, and his eyes gaze upward to the left. His first right and left hands hold an arrow and a bow; the lower right holds a sword, and the lower left a lotus beautified by a sun disk and marked with a five-pronged vajra. He is adorned with the seven precious things of great qualities and so forth, sits on a lotus-maṇḍala seat, and is embraced, inseparable, by the consort Māmakī. On some occasions, in order to tame hosts of fierce beings, his body is red in color, with a glow of light, he has a slightly wrathful face, and he is draped with garlands of various jewels.

In the east is the goddess Empowering Lady, with a clear red luster, holding in her hand a jeweled crown marked with a five-pronged vajra. In the south is the goddess Treasure-Possessing Lady, yellow, holding in her hand a vase filled with jewels. In the west is Lady with the Supreme Banner, with a whitish red luster, holding in her hand a jeweled victory banner with its finial. In the north is Stūpa Lady, bluish green, holding a jeweled vessel filled with precious substances.

Again, in the outer east is Bow-and-Arrow Lady, reddish yellow, holding in her hand the emblem that matches her name. In the south is Vajra Khaṭvāṅga Lady, blue, holding in her hands a sword and a khaṭvāṅga. In the west is Vajra Sun Lady, reddish yellow, whose hand is marked with that very thing, the sun. In the north is Vajra Fire Lady, reddish green, holding in her hand a hearth marked with a vajra. From all these goddesses too arises light of five colors, and they are adorned with ornaments of various jewels. The twelve outer goddesses beginning with Flute Lady are also like those of the maṇḍala of Vajrasattva, with this slight difference: through the flourishing of qualities they radiate the allure of jewels and are full of luster, and their bodies and hand emblems are distinctly marked with precious gems.

Again, for the emanation of the maṇḍala of the Play of Paramāśva: the resultant buddha who appears from the great Vajradhara himself has a green body and three faces, green, blue and red. Above his head a green horse's head stretches up its neck and neighs. His body is green. His four right hands hold the three-banner mudrā, a crossed vajra, a sword and an arrow. His four left hands hold a various lotus, a single-pointed spear (śakti), a club and a bow. With the first hoof of his right feet he tramples the three siddhis and Lakṣmī, and with the second he tramples Śāntī and Rati. With the first of the left he tramples Rudra and Viṣṇu, and with the second he has trampled Kāmadeva and Brahmā. He has the radiance of gazing in the modes of wrath, grace, ferocity and peace, and is decked with ornaments of various jewels and with special emblems.

On the right is Various Lotus, light radiating from her greenish yellow body, holding in her hand a various lotus. On the left is Various Vajra Lady, light radiating from her bluish green body, holding in her hand a crossed vajra. In front, the radiance of his own light, the consort Samayatārā, sits embracing him in union. The other goddesses of the retinue accord, according to the specifics of their activities, with the goddesses of the other family maṇḍalas, with this slight difference: their bodies and hand emblems are marked with crossed vajras, and their heads are adorned with the body of Amoghasiddhi.

The various wheels in which other goddesses act as the principal of the maṇḍala, and the various worldly gods of the maṇḍala of the condensed families, arise in accordance with the inclinations of those to be tamed. They are set out by way of hospitality and are images of enlightened activity, so although they are counted in the maṇḍala, they are not explained here, since they are not emanated deities. When followers who practice come to accomplish them, they should know them as taught at length in the sādhana of the feast gathering.

Now, to show the manner in which these deities are seated: their seats are to be laid out for all of them just as they are arranged. The deities of the maṇḍala of glorious Vajrasattva, joining right and left feet, dance in the manner of bliss and sit in the cross-legged posture of a sattva. The deities of the family of the buddha Vairocana sit in the poised posture of a sattva, the right limb pressing down and the left limb extended beyond all the rest. The deities of Vajra Heruka, joining right and left, dance the dance of bliss, in the half-cross-legged manner, pressing a foot upon their own thigh. The deities of Padmanarteśvara, joining right and left in the half-cross-legged manner, abide in the royal posture. The deities of Vajrasūrya sit in the vajra cross-legged posture, with both knees aligned. The deities of Paramāśva, joining right and left in the manner of stretching and bending, are locked in the wrathful cross-legged posture. All of them abide on lotus-maṇḍala seats, each with its own attire and characteristics.

What, then, is the celestial palace of the gods that is the dwelling place of these maṇḍalas of supported deities? It is said:

It is square, with four doors,
beautified by four porticoes;
it should be furnished with gatekeepers,
vajra, jewel, lotus and the rest.

Its outer perimeter is adorned with three successive walls and the eight charnel grounds. Within it is the celestial palace, square, with four doors, enlarged by all the great qualities of the buddhas. Through training in the four immeasurables, one enters by the four doors of liberation. It has ornaments such as porticoes, signs that symbolize the thirty-seven factors of awakening and the rest, and it is furnished with the gatekeepers of its own family, who by the emblems of the family display the signs of their own wisdom together with the host of its qualities. This is also said in the root tantra:

In the center of a golden wheel and a crossed vajra,
square, its door frames marked with four porticoes,
its walls on each side made of the five precious substances,
with the light of the twenty-five self-arisen colors,
adorned with eight supreme pillars marked with vajras,
beautiful with garlands of jewels, pearls and flowers,
thronged with goddesses of form, sound, smell, taste and touch,
with parasols, victory banners, canopies and yak-tail whisks,
with half-moons, vajras, jewels and pennants,
and bells sounding sweet tones, all of it resounding.

By this, the inner palace should be understood according to the distinctions of the maṇḍala of the condensed families and the maṇḍalas of the individual families.

Then, in order to set out there the maṇḍala that supports the deities, it is said:

Women who resemble one's own deity,
well accomplished,
fortunate, marked with one's own mudrā:
with them the gathering maṇḍala should be examined.

In the manner of the maṇḍalas of the individual families, the goddesses are adorned with their own emblems, colors and signs. Abiding in their own samādhi, having perfectly accomplished the body of that deity, they should take their places in their respective directions in the manner of mudrās arisen from the pledge of good fortune. In this way, by means of the four mudrās, through the stages of emanating and gathering back, in order to perfect in an instant all the appearances of the qualities, the gathering maṇḍala should be examined. The word gana bindhura is explained as the gathering of every excellence of good fortune and wealth. The word ghana vihara means that by engaging in it, such signs become clear, and by examining it for a long time, the result that accords with it is accomplished.

Now, in order for the yogin to accomplish the five nectars in the midst of the gathering, six verses are spoken, beginning:

The grove of fire, when accomplished,
is explained to be called the great red.

For one who would make his own samādhi clear, and in order to accomplish the aims he desires by relying on special substances of accomplishment, these teach the method of accomplishing the five nectars, together with their companions, through the branches of approach and accomplishment. Among them, the first verse makes clear that the great vermilion drawn forth by mantra, being the support of the two lamps, possesses its own sound, and that relying on it the accomplishments of sound, which are established by essential power and by nature, become manifest. The second verse teaches that vajra water, vajra excrement and great vermilion are combined and prepared and placed in a lotus vessel, and that one then observes the signs of the five kinds of wisdom of the mind. The third verse explains the qualities that come when the signs of that are clearly accomplished, and abiding without departing from them. The fourth verse teaches its defining characteristics together with the explanation of its name. The fifth verse teaches the method of accomplishing the bell of accomplishment that is applied to the five nectars, and its benefits. The sixth verse gives the rite of putting these accomplishments into practice, together with its result: at the time when brahmins perform the great sacrifices of the sun and the moon, just as the symbolic arrangement of the royal bhṛṅgāra ewer is set out, one drinks the prepared dadura dish as if tasting it; the principal and all the retinue take it three times with the mudrā of the ring finger; and the permanent accomplishment of body, speech and mind arises.

The benefits of accomplishing these are spoken in three verses, beginning:

By this blissful rasāyana,
the very self of all the buddhas,

By the power of accomplishing the nectar as rasāyana, all the fruits of good qualities in this world, and also the accomplishments of the world beyond, are attained: one gains the fourfold glory of Vajrasattva and becomes the very self of all the buddhas.

The rite for protecting that nectar is this:

When protection is made with this, one becomes
glorious like Vajrasattva;
through the yoga of Ḍākinī Saṃvara
one accomplishes the very self of all the buddhas.

With a drop of bodhicitta marked with the five syllables, one places a drop on the five sense faculties. Once protection has been made, the bodies of the five families become clear and are accomplished right there. And by also drinking it inwardly and anointing the three places with it, one accomplishes the body of glorious Vajrasattva, free of the three stains. This is also said in the root tantra:

When all the places
are adorned with drops of bodhicitta,
then, through the maṇḍala of the families,
one is protected and becomes supreme in accomplishment.

These teach the stages of accomplishing the nectar. As for the means of putting it into practice in its full sequence, it should be understood through the five rites; since there would be much to say, it is not explained here.

Having thus gathered the accumulation of merit, in order to accomplish the result by way of the path that accords with its cause, the adornment of the person is taught for the purpose of performing the feast gathering of wisdom:

Then, having bathed, the body fragrant,
with clothes and ornaments as one can afford,
having put on various garments,
wearing garlands of flowers, the breath fragrant.

Having duly performed the three baths, outer, inner and secret, one anoints the body with fragrant substances, freshens the breath with mouth perfume and the like, puts on whatever various garments and ornaments one can afford, hangs on garlands of flowers, and abides in one's own attire. As for the perfume that makes the breath fragrant, the ninth chapter says:

Kakkola, small cardamom,
cyperus, camphor and musk:
these, finely ground,
are mixed with khadira and molasses.
Cinnamon, patra and lodhra,
black pepper and camphor are added;
kneaded with hasakari and sesame oil,
this is the mouth perfume.

Further, to teach the varieties of the methods of union:

By the fortunate one of all the various kinds,
arousing slowly, slowly,
through the practice of covering with a garment,
I shall stir what arises.

By way of the six families, sixty-four varieties of the methods of union are taught. Finding the mudrās of one's own family and taking delight in them, one sings songs of arousal; one moves in dance steps and sways, gazes, smiles, embraces, unites, drives in the tamāla peg, places nail marks at the nine places, and moving without violence in the discipline of play, one should bring down the bodhicitta. What goes before these is arousal by signs, the practice of supplication, becoming intimate through conversation, joining hearts, and telling tales of passion. Through the great bliss that arises from this, the continua of method and wisdom are transformed in stages, and then come making the maṇḍala clear, taking the accomplishments, offering to the noble ones, conferring empowerment, and acting for the benefit of beings.

Again, the activity of bodhicitta:

By the rising and the seeing
of the perfect buddhas, sun and moon,
one is freed from all evil
and all merit is accomplished.
Endowed with all the certain paths,
one is adorned with transcendence.

One's own suchness, the bodhicitta of great bliss that is seen through the purity of the accumulations of merit and wisdom and abides with the characteristic of passion, appears like the sun and the moon. The signs that illustrate it, the two kinds of bodhicitta, and the nondual bliss as well, are its very nature. To experience it clearly and have it dawn directly is to see the buddha in whom the three bodies are nondual: it is seeing one's own face oneself. By its power one is freed completely from all evil and accomplishes all merit, and one becomes adorned with every array of great qualities endowed with all the good paths.

The purpose of arousing with the goddesses' songs at the time of play is this:

Making stable and accomplished, arousing:
the bliss of complete arousal.
