By singing songs that arouse, for the sake of oneself and others, all the accomplishments and the hosts of qualities of the three doors to become stable, the two, joy and bliss, are aroused; and in terms of the result, one urges the deity to act for the benefit of beings from the nondual body. Their song is this:

Emptiness, supreme yoga, by the power of being and nonbeing,
the pledge abides close by in space itself.
Sugata, O Sugata, please arise!
Vajrasattva, fortunate and glorious, you are the lord.

First, the body of nondual great bliss is the nature of the wisdom of great emptiness. Having the character of the two kinds of yoga, it transcends the extremes of being and nonbeing and abides as the essence of both. Free of both the aspect of permanence, eternal and unchanging, and the aspect of emptiness free of extremes, it abides, from the aspect of the nonduality of appearance and emptiness, as the nature of great bliss. Its example is that it abides like space, and the pledge that never departs from its own character abides close by. Although such a Sugata neither rises nor sits, from the side of the minds of beings, "rise in a form body with its emanations of qualities, and attend to me" is what is meant. In what form? "Vajrasattva, together with the fortunate assembly of twenty goddesses," it says. The benefit of that song is this:

When these are sung,
even one who has utterly passed into nirvāṇa,
even the dead, is raised again;
what need is there to speak of renewed existence?

When the melting has become, through the two, a drop of light, from it arise the four goddesses, the result that accords with its cause, namely the earlier training in the path and gathering of the accumulations. They sing songs of arousal from the four directions, and the principal rises from the samādhi of great cessation. If one asks whether this is accomplished even by arousing with that song: if even śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas who have utterly passed into nirvāṇa are roused from their one-sided view of peace, what need is there to speak of other beings in renewed existence?

If one asks how it is to be sung:

Equally endowed with the four paths,
beautified by transcendence.

From the four directions the four goddesses sing immeasurable songs in measures of half a mātrā, two, four and eight in order, by way of the four branches, applying them to all the great qualities of the buddhas; thus one is aroused by songs that beautify one's own appearance with transcendence.

This is also said in the root tantra:

The song of half a mātrā
dispels torment,
and like beads on a string of pearls,
the mātrā makes the activities clear.
The flowing song of two mātrās,
like the roar of a lion stretching,
cleanses the torment of attachment
and gives birth to nondual wisdom.
The flowing melody of four mātrās
releases the bonds of the extremes;
a clear flow with the humming of bees
makes clear the meaning shown by four words.
The song and melody of eight mātrās
moves the Protector from his abode;
with the sound of the precious gau lute
it applies the various activities.
Equally endowed with the four paths,
it becomes the supreme arousal.

Further, in order to accomplish the accomplishments of the six maṇḍalas in the gathering maṇḍala, with their purpose and characteristics:

The all-following is fully accomplished,
which fully accomplishes the three worlds.

Since it is supreme among all the accomplishments to be accomplished, and joins one to all qualities, it is "the all-following." When it is fully accomplished, it overpowers every abode of the three worlds and displays all the appearances of qualities in the stream of speech. The way in which, through this, the accomplishments of all the family maṇḍalas are brought to completion is this:

Bowed to by the three worlds,
teacher of the three worlds,
giver of happiness to the three worlds:
the three worlds are accomplished for me.

By gaining mastery of all the qualities of the buddha Vairocana, he emanates the array of his body throughout the three worlds and becomes the object of everyone's homage. Gaining mastery of the fruits of the qualities of Heruka, he displays a body of great ferocity and destroys the hosts of Rudra and the others who make the three worlds arrogant. Gaining mastery of the fruits of the qualities of Vajrasūrya, he brings forth and ripens for the three worlds the enjoyments of those to be tamed, such as the seven precious things. Holding mastery over all the fruits of the qualities of Padmanarteśvara, he accomplishes the three worlds in the essence of pure, sublime accomplishment.

Further, it is accomplished:

Joined with the glory of Vajrasattva,
through keeping company with all the goddesses,
the fortunate one of all the various kinds
accomplishes all aralli.

Gaining mastery of Vajrasattva, the self of all the families, and, for all activities, of the hosts of great qualities that are the fruit of Paramāśva, one fully accomplishes what the three worlds desire in the essence of aralli.

Again, the way in which the wisdom free of outflows, the ultimate aim, is accomplished:

With all the goddesses of the boundless expanse of space,
delighting in bliss,
Vajrasattva is perfectly complete;
the sun and moon rise clearly.
Vajrasattva perfectly united,
the Saṃvara of the Ḍākinī's Illusion arises.

For one's own sake, by relying on the form of a woman and by way of relying on the ultimate Dharma, all things that appear and that are included in existence, as the family maṇḍala, in the manner of method and wisdom, enjoy nondual bliss. The nature of Vajrasattva appears clearly, like the rising of the disks of the sun and moon: this is perfect union. Therefore, by the power of clearly experiencing one's own characteristic, one awakens to buddhahood as the six bodies of wisdom free of outflows, spontaneously present, and so the Saṃvara of the Ḍākinī's Illusion arises.

That was arousal by song for the purpose of emanating for the benefit of others. Among these, first, the intention together with its characteristics: two and a half verses are spoken, beginning:

Even by visualizing just once,
all the accomplishments of bliss arise.

By singing just once this song that visualizes and applies the aspects through the qualities of the hosts of deities of the maṇḍala, the accomplishments of the two blisses arise, body, speech and mind become stable, and accomplishment is aroused. Therefore, endowed with various modes, one should sing variously, by way of thick and thin melodies, distinct long and short turns, and high and low pitch; and so that each may arouse the other in the maṇḍala of Vajrasattva, one should sing with the nature of passion. Its verse is this:

Having clearly worshipped with the offering of the pure lotus of joy, of passion in its many forms,
please accomplish bliss with the bliss of the supreme, permanent yoga.

First the four goddesses of the families spoke by way of family and wisdom, which illustrate their own qualities. Possessing the two kinds of passion and enjoying the bliss of union, they accomplish many aspects of qualities: this is by way of the bow and arrow of Saṃvarā. The signs of bhaga and liṅga, which illustrate great bliss in the form of two embracing two, are the lotus of joy; "how wonderful!" because the blissful lady is free of all the stains of the afflictions. The pure offering is Illuminatrix, who conquers the three realms and is moistened with the essence of nectar. Since she makes clear the body of the buddha, which satisfies others with the deathless nectar that confers all the qualities of Maheśvara, "having clearly worshipped" is said. If one asks what the essence of these lady teachers is: "Please accomplish the bliss of the yogin, supreme, permanent and unbroken," it says.

Again, by way of activity, in order to arouse through their own actions:

Oh! Very essence of the pledge of supreme bliss,
supreme bliss, utter yoga, supremely fortunate one,
delight, your own pledge, flawless: play forever, play, ho!
How wonderful! Since the lord of the supreme secret is your own pledge,
the supreme goddesses of the utter secret.

Endowed with various wondrous miracles, she dispels the delusion of others; and by the power of engaging in the supreme activity she abides always in the essential nature of her own pledge, the great undefiled bliss. Thus, through that very activity of the family of the goddess Supreme Bliss, she liberates the disciples who are to be trained; and with the sign that marks the continuity of undefiled bliss arising without interruption, she enjoys the very bliss of method and wisdom, and so is freed from the extreme of that very thing. This is "How wonderful, blissful lady!": she abides delighting in the supremely fortunate. By the pledge of body, speech and mind she cares for others, and, having conquered the three realms, she constantly enjoys the streams of nectar-water that are free from the flaw of stain; thus, through the activity of the goddess Illuminatrix, she plays at all times, and "play!" is said for all occasions. Applying activity in various ways, the defining mark of her deeds, she has gained mastery over the secret meaning of various actions and so has become lord. Showing a guise that bears the bodies of all the buddhas, she is the Disciple Lady, because she abides in her own pledge. Therefore, by way of their deeds, delighting in the assembly that arises from qualities and endowed with the meaning of the utter secret, they are "the supreme goddesses."

Now, in order to urge by way of excellence:

You clearly know the various ways of taming the mindstreams of beings;
all pledges have become the essence of all things, stable and moving.
Nature of all victors, bliss of yoga, Ḍākinī Māyā, ho!
Oh, sublime one, fully accomplish and reveal the great permanent siddhi!

Having seen all the mindstreams of the various disciples, produced by their individual karma, she clearly knows the various methods of taming that accord with them; and through her all-encompassing conduct that works for their benefit, she turns them back from the paths of their individual views and various conceptions and joins them to the nature of their own suchness, the great pledge, so that they become the essential nature of all phenomena. That which shows the special method of the activity of offering performed by them arises from the very nature of the great Vajradhara: from the expanse of the bliss of yoga, the nature of all victors, he displays the maṇḍala of the bliss of the ḍākinīs. "In that very manner, accomplish in the mindstream of the yogin the siddhi of the six spontaneously present bodies": that is the meaning.

Again, in order to urge by way of particular activities, such as the mantras of the four goddesses, jaḥ hūṃ baṃ hoḥ and so forth are taught. These should be applied in order, each to its own activity.

The benefit of singing the songs in this way is taught in two verses, beginning:

These kings of song
abide in the glorious Vajrasattva.

Since they are easy to understand, I have not explained them.

For persons who delight in the condensed, four kinds of song are taught in order to accomplish all siddhis by way of the four activities. Of these, the first urges, for the sake of taming sentient beings, the activity of magnetizing to work for their benefit, from the maṇḍala of Vajrasattva, through the maṇḍala of the glorious Padmanarteśvara. The second urges, from Vajrasattva, by way of the maṇḍala of Vajrasūrya, to work through the activity of enriching for the benefit of sentient beings who delight in desirable things. The third urges, from Vajrasattva, by way of the maṇḍalas of the glorious Heruka and of Paramāśva, to work through wrathful activity for the benefit of vicious sentient beings. The fourth urges, from Vajrasattva, by way of the maṇḍala of Vairocana, to work through pacifying activity for the benefit of sentient beings who are deprived of life and who abide in the one-sided extreme of peace. Moreover, by way of each individual retinue of the families as well, they are urged to work for beings in the manner of the four activities; this should be applied according to the occasion.

Next, the taming of sentient beings whose mindstreams are marked by many kinds of desire: red light radiates from one's own body, and that very family maṇḍala emanates the maṇḍala of Ḍākinī Māyā and applies it to activity in five ways. The pledge that urges it in order to bring this to completion is te na te te hūṃ. In order to tame sentient beings through the eight kinds of dance-method that delight in the assembly of increased qualities, and to work for their benefit by emanating maṇḍalas that accord with this, three hūṃ syllables are uttered. In order to tame those whose own nature takes a wrathful guise, subjugation by the mudrā of the nine moods is urged by the pledge mantra rulu rulu hūṃ bhyo hūṃ. In order to bring happiness to the many sentient beings whose life and vitality have ebbed away and so forth, and to emanate pacifying activity by way of the eleven limbs of method, there is hūṃ śi śa bi śa hūṃ.

The condensed meaning is as follows. Whatever yogin, focusing on the aims of self and others, enters in the manner of the maṇḍala of Ḍākinī Māyā, and, in order to enter upon the stages of activity through the texts of the individual family maṇḍalas or through just one of them, should practice and engage by way of the distinction between two siddhis: the siddhi that relies on meditative concentration and the siddhi that relies on recitation.

Furthermore, first, transforming the mudrās and the particular views and so forth of others in the maṇḍalas of the retinue, such as that of the goddess Supreme Bliss, and remaining one-pointed in the activity of magnetizing, one should recite the mantra. oṃ ba bhu bi bhi ta ra ga santa ti e śeṣ ni śeṣa sarba bi na ya sarba tathāgata jvale prajvale kurba bi kurba sarba śambare te na te te hūṃ: this is the continual approach, three hundred thousand in number. Having examined with pa śaṃ ku ru hūṃ, when the signs have become clear, by applying it to activity kings are brought under control, and ministers, women, and all domains such as kingdoms come under one's power. By reciting it constantly, all the qualities of the sugatas are joined to one's mindstream and accomplished; and even in this present life, whatever beings see one will give rise to a mind of attachment. Such qualities are also taught extensively elsewhere.

From that, those who wish for enriching use oṃ ba bhu bi bhi ta ra ti santa ti e śe ṣa ni śe ṣa sa tra bi na ya sarba tathāgata jvale prajvale kurba bi kurba sarba śambare hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ: this is the mantra of the continual approach. puṣṭiṃ ku ru svāhā is the mantra for applying it to the activity.

A person who wishes for the wrathful, by way of his own guise and the signs, uses oṃ ba bhu bi ta rau tra santra ti e śe ṣa ni śe ṣa satva bi na ya sarba tathāgata jvale prajvale kurba bi kurba sarba śambare rulu rulu hūṃ bhyo hūṃ: this is the continual mantra. With māraya phaṭ it is applied to the activity.

Whoever wishes for pacifying should engage by way of that same method: oṃ ba bhu bi bhi ta ji ba santa ti e śe ṣa ni śe ṣa satva bi na ya sarba tathāgata jvale prajvale kurba bi kurba sarba śambare hūṃ śi śa bi śa hūṃ is the continual approach. With śāntiṃ ku ru one applies it to the activity.

The qualities of these are inconceivable. If even a single recitation accomplishes their respective activities, what need is there to mention approaching them according to the prescribed number and reciting them continually? The activities are accomplished. To show their qualities, three verses are taught, beginning:

That and the like, endless and supreme,
equal and unequal to the dharmadhātu.

Since they are easy to understand, I have not explained them.

Now, to show the offering of the awakening mind:

Arising from all the senses,
arrays of great clouds of offerings,
with all the infinite hosts,
make offering to the nature of all.

By the light rays of the awakening mind, the vast offerings of the twelve goddesses are emanated, delighting all the buddhas of the directions and times; beings are purified; the yoga becomes clear; and again, having made oneself clear as father and mother, one delights them with the offering of joy and bliss; and the maṇḍala of the support and the supported becomes clear. As the root tantra says:

From the light rays of great bliss,
twelve goddesses alike in manner
delight with great joy
the buddhas of the directions and times, fathers and mothers.
Becoming atoms in the shape of letters,
they enter one's own mouth
and gather in the place of great bliss.
From the four cakras they emanate in order;
the abode of Bratakara arises,
and great bliss mingles with passion, without duality.
By burning the mindstreams of the beings of the three realms,
the maṇḍalas of each of the six classes are accomplished.
Urging the mindstream of pure yoga,
the threefold maṇḍala appears clearly.
In the place of regathering, the celestial palace,
with seats and ornaments, arises as before.
Having marked the place with the bindu,
by emanation the maṇḍala of letters becomes clear.

From that the maṇḍala of the deities is clearly generated. If one asks, "Why, in a context that mentions nothing but offering, does it show all the activities?": because all of these too delight the noble ones, they are a rite of offering. As the Seventh Chapter says:

Clouds of awakening mind, hosts of offerings,
are emanated everywhere without remainder;
offering, and fulfilling the aims of self and others,
the great rite of delighting is emanated.

Its benefit is:

Through the Union with All Buddhas,
Ḍākinī Māyā Saṃvara,
the body that is the nature of all things
is accomplished entirely, with nothing left out.

By the power of practicing the aspects of this very meaning, the things of all phenomena, purified, are joined to the nature of great bliss; the various emanated bodies that appear are clearly displayed; and all the categories of qualities are clearly perfected.

Now, in order to clearly show and bless the maṇḍala of the twenty goddesses:

Through casting the mudrā of the Teacher,
if women too are accomplished,
they hold the sign together with the vajra
and fully become yoginīs.
Having blessed and accomplished these
to be like their own deities,
whatever is taught is like one's own deity,
well accomplished by women.

By this, the rite of blessing is to be performed by the deities arrayed in the maṇḍala; and by the mudrās of the body, speech and mind of the Teacher, the eight inner goddesses of the maṇḍala of each family accomplish their individual guises, and by the power of being adorned with the insignia of the signs, they are accomplished through blessing as like that very deity of the family. This shows the eight goddesses emanated from the four families and the four consorts.

Again, the offering goddesses such as the Flute Lady:

Unprecedented music, cymbals and dance
will be accomplished by emanation.
Through devotion to the bliss of divine form, sound, scent,
taste and touch,
one sees oneself as one's own deity
and will also make offerings.

The goddesses emanated from one's own qualities, in an arrangement never seen before, emanate in dense clouds throughout the ten directions and gather back, with divine music, cymbals, dance, and songs of shifting melody. Transforming the various divine forms, various sounds, various scents, various tastes, and the host of tangibles, the bliss of the various offerings, according to inclination, they strive in the rite of delighting the lords of their respective families.

Again, the offering of the four goddesses such as the Flower Lady:

Union of the offerings of the deities,
passionate one of supreme good fortune,
in every way, for eons,
truly accomplishes bliss.

As above, they emanate various offerings of the five sense pleasures and, by way of the purified undefiled aggregates, enjoy all phenomena equally; and with the enjoyment of the fortunate Vajrasattva, the nature of the fourfold awakening mind, they delight their own lord. The deities of activity are emanations that arise incidentally, and so should be understood by implication.

To show the greatness of such blessing:

Because the buddhas are blessed as illusion,
it is union in Vajrasattva.

The blisses of all the siddhis,
and the joy of the supreme maṇḍala, one will experience.

By the power of being blessed into the body of Ḍākinī Māyā, in whatever individual guise, by way of the arrangement of the maṇḍala, the emanated deities, and the maṇḍala of the six buddhas, one is united with the two guises of Vajrasattva, and one will see the blisses of all the siddhis and see the two kinds of maṇḍala.

If one asks what the essential nature of such a maṇḍala of the assembly is:

By the very power
of union in Vajrasattva,
everywhere the good fortune of all that is various
is accomplished by one's own bliss itself.

Its meaning is given in the root tantra:

The great accumulation of merit and wisdom,
in the shape of the wheel of the bliss of liberation,
joining the good fortune of all that is various,
accomplishes great bliss, the essence of the assembly.

Further, the defining mark of the vast offering is taught in three verses beginning:

The great forms of all the buddhas,
and with the bliss of melody, scent and taste.

All the things gathered in appearance and existence are emanated from the radiance of the great self-arisen wisdom, and in their own form they make offering to themselves. Therefore the great siddhi that abides in the place of all the enjoyments of desirable qualities of greatness becomes clear. As the root tantra says:

By emanating one's own enjoyments,
one should make offering to one's own maṇḍala.
The enjoyments of all that is desired are perfected;
with hosts of precious atoms,
having delighted the nondual maṇḍala,
one plays in the abode of one's own wisdom.
All that is to be realized, everything,
is perfected as the enjoyment of Samantabhadra.

The method of their offering:

Then, through the true and utter secret,
with the four women
and the offering of the mudrā of dance,
all the offerings should be presented.

By way of the four families, the four wisdoms are made luminous. By the four goddesses who signify the secret mudrās through the suchness arising from oneself, the immeasurables, and, by way of activity, through the dance mudrās of mantra and samādhi, all the offerings such as flowers and incense should be presented: so it says.

If one asks in what manner:

Homage to the self of supreme joy!
Accomplish the sublime state of the tathāgatas!
oṃ hūṃ Please accomplish the sublime pledge!
Purify all the great offerings!
oṃ hūṃ Accomplish all the great offerings!
Please play in the abode of great equality!

The six lines and the intervening mantras should be properly combined: the mantra is placed at the beginning of the lines and of every offering, and the last line and the first are joined to the end of each mantra. It then becomes like this: oṃ āḥ hūṃ "Homage to the self of supreme joy! Accomplish the sublime state of the tathāgatas! Please play in the abode of great equality!" Saying he ra te bajra bi la si na traṭ, one should do it like a flute garland. Again, after the verse, saying oṃ bajra puṣpe, one should do it in the manner of scattering flowers. In the same way it is to be applied at length to the others as well. Their meaning should be understood in terms of the families, the wisdoms, the immeasurables, and urging toward the work of activity. Thus, with this mudrā of offering, one should do it in the manner of scattering flowers.

In order to apply it to all the works of offering:

This mudrā of song
abides in the glorious Vajrasattva,
and can be applied to the infinite works
of offering without exception.

Transforming them by their individual mantras, mudrās and samādhis, one should apply this verse to all the works of offering. Elsewhere too, all these songs taught above can be applied to all the works of invitation, presenting offerings, praising and urging, at the times of approach, accomplishment, the feast gathering, and the activities. As the root tantra says:

With the various urgings of song
one should apply them to infinite works;
by whatever approach, accomplishment and so forth,
all the limbs of the rite are accomplished.

The benefit of offering in this way:

For those who have not become accomplished,
and who lack the means for offerings,
if they offer with this mudrā,
all offerings are swiftly accomplished.

Those for whom the paths of long investigation have not become clear, and those who have not gathered substances actually at hand, emanate a vast assembly through the mudrā offerings above; and by the power of emanating the maṇḍala well, everything will be accomplished.

Now, from the stages of the work to be done for the disciple, the method of entering the maṇḍala is taught:

With these applications of offering,
and by the four goddesses,
having made offerings of flowers and the like,
one should take on all the disciples.

In the assembly maṇḍala of the vajra master, by means of the mantras, mudrās and samādhis of all the offerings, these are applied to all the substances at hand and blessed. Then, in the manner of the four goddesses, garlands of flowers and so on, pellets of incense and so on, lamp stands, garlands and so on, and perfumed compounds, arising from both natural and prepared substances, are offered with oneself leading the way; after that the others also offer them, and the master should bring them in.

Then, in order to make supplication on behalf of the disciple and bring him in, one should enter upon the sequence of the rite to be performed between sessions, the rite of bringing him inside the curtain, and the subsequent works. Their rite is as follows. First, the vajra master should do just as he himself entered in the preceding maṇḍala; meditate on the purity of the maṇḍala; make supplication with the verses of the awakening mind; perform the declaration of truth; generate the four empowerments in his mindstream; and perform the rites of pleasing the deities and the guru. After that, the disciple is placed on a platform, and there follow the rites of purifying him with water from the vase, presenting offerings, covering his face, holding a garland and generating the mind, questions and answers, going for refuge, supplication, and bringing him in again.

Therefore:

Those who desire siddhis, according to what they desire,
having fully performed the dance of joined palms,
should have their faces covered
with a beautiful cloth and be taken in.

So that they may obtain, just as they wish, the various siddhis, both the lesser siddhis and those that become supreme, their faces are covered with cloths of six colors that accord with the families. They make supplication for the aims they wish for in their respective maṇḍalas, and, having circled three times with the dance-method of joined palms, they bind the mudrā of the vajra holder: the palm of the right hand is placed face up upon the palm of the left hand turned face down, and the little fingers and thumbs are joined crosswise. Holding a garland of jewels, they should be brought inside the curtain.

This includes the method of bringing them in; gathering the accumulations; enjoining secrecy; proclaiming the pledges; giving the oath-water to drink; and, by the four rites that add the pledge of body, making them possessors of the vajra of body, speech and mind; and making supplication, the method of the descent, the signs of the descent, the granting of bliss, and, with the five great qualities, causing the wisdom to descend.

Therefore the rite of entry is:

According to how they wish to obtain siddhi,
the disciples are made to scatter flowers;
whichever goddess the flower falls upon,
that deity will be accomplished for them.

In order to examine the signs of their own siddhi, they scatter a flower onto the maṇḍala, and by that very means the omens are examined. This includes bringing down the siddhi through the declaration of truth, scattering the flower, examining the siddhi, and adorning them with a garland.

What is to be done after this:

Then, having untied the face covering,
one should present the supreme fee.

The eyes are opened with a golden spoon and the face covering removed; having looked at the maṇḍala, they present the offering fee, and instructions should be given.

After that, in order to confer empowerment:

Through the empowerment of the precious buddhas,
one empowers just as they were empowered.

This includes making supplication, setting up the platform, placing the disciple on a seat and adorning him, purifying the disciple, and meditating on him as the deity of the family and making offerings: after these four preliminary limbs, the awareness empowerments should be conferred. Just as the former victors were empowered by the precious buddhas with the crown and the rest, so one confers the empowerments that signify the five wisdoms of the five families and whose nature is to accomplish the aim: the water empowerment, the crown empowerment, the vajra empowerment, the bell empowerment, and the name empowerment. One teaches their benefits and, following the sequence of proclaiming the pledges, adds them on. As for the vows, one should give them, along with the faults of breaking the vows, the accomplishment of purifying existence, what is to be abandoned as incompatible, and the instruction on enjoying the five sense pleasures in a manner endowed with view and conduct.

Then, having performed in order the rites of the vajra master empowerment, in order to confer the secret empowerment, the text teaches:

The great red together with camphor,
mixed with red sandalwood,
placed in the center of the assembly;
the left, holding the sign, with the vajra.

Of the two, empowerment by way of the assembly maṇḍala and of accomplishment, and empowerment relying only on a maṇḍala of images, the first is meant. The two maṇḍalas of sun and moon, mingled into one, which arise from the play of the father and mother, the nonduality of method and wisdom, are mixed with red sandalwood, poured into a skull cup endowed with the marks, and placed in the center of the assembly maṇḍala. Endowed with the yoga of the deity, one blesses it; and by tasting it like a drink of peace with the mudrā of the ring finger and thumb of the left hand, the sign-holding vajra, one will obtain the supreme siddhi.

Moreover, at a time when empowerment alone is sought, it is taken from the secret place with the speaking mudrā and placed in the place of enjoyment; but since here it is taught for the purpose of gathering the accumulations and accomplishment, what is to be done is taught differently.

The condensed meaning is as follows. In a secluded place adorned with ornaments, one makes the request to the vajra master and invites him to be seated; having thoroughly examined a wisdom consort endowed with qualities, one presents her, empowered and blessed, to the master and pleases him. Then again the disciple makes supplication; the master speaks the reply granting it; for the sake of the empowerment, the goddess is purified with bathing and so forth and generated as the deity; she is empowered and blessed and the play is performed; the awakening mind is accomplished in the center of the maṇḍala, or else it is let fall into the disciple's mouth, and the reply granting it is spoken; and the disciple places it in the place of enjoyment and meditates according to the meaning.

After that, in order to confer the great secret empowerment, one phrase is drawn in two senses:

Endowed with the yoga of one's own deity,
with the tips of the ring finger and the thumb,
if one tastes it like a drink of peace,
one will obtain the permanent siddhi.

And also by the meaning of the passage that occurred above, beginning:

Then, bathed, the body fragrant,
with clothes and ornaments as available,

one should urge and make supplication to the master. Then follow placing the goddess in his hand and the reply; the goddess urging her companion; the companion speaking the reply; speaking her qualities; for the sake of the empowerment, the meditation of method and wisdom in a secluded place; examining the signs by way of various methods of union; taking the awakening mind together with what it signifies and its meaning, and pleasing the master; and the rite of conferring the empowerment of great bliss.

Their benefit:

Even by the hosts of ḍākinīs
of the Union with All Buddhas
one is not bound and not deluded,
and they will not harm him.

Yogins who practice by way of the awareness empowerment alone and engage in the proper manner are not bound by the hosts of fierce gods and goddesses such as the worldly ḍākinīs, and even sudden afflictions such as fevers cannot harm them.

The sequence of their pledges and vows:

Then, having proclaimed the oath,
in accord with all the buddhas,
the supreme and permanent decree,
the pledges and vows should be given.

The sequence of proclaiming the pledges after first conferring the disciple's empowerment is for the purpose of proclaiming the oath and giving the vows.

By the light rays from the heart that make clear the application of the family maṇḍalas, all the family maṇḍalas, in the manner of the empowerment, proclaim it with speech in harmonious melody.

The fault of breaking the pledges is taught in two verses beginning:

If you break the vows
of the Union with All Buddhas...

If one acts against the meaning of what one has vowed by way of this very maṇḍala, one is punished by the buddhas and bodhisattvas of the common vehicle, by the vidyādharas among the yogins, and others; and in particular the maṇḍalas tear out one's heart, drink one's blood, and depart.

The purpose of the pledge of accomplishing the purification of existence is to be grasped:

For the sake of the whole realm of sentient beings,
in order to purify existence completely,
through union with the unceasing nature of reality
one should accomplish permanence itself.

In order to purify all the signs of grasping conceptions of oneself and of all beings without exception, one joins the whole of the three realms of existence to the defining mark of the complete purity of cause, condition and result; and by the power of having made clear the unceasing nature of reality, the nonabiding nirvāṇa, one will accomplish the permanent essential nature, the meaning of the six spontaneously present bodies. As the root tantra says:

In order to accomplish the purification of evil,
the reality that joins cause, condition and result,
the pure expanse like the sky,
the cause of nonabiding nirvāṇa,
is accomplished as unceasing permanence itself.

And again:

The great pledge that abides in its intrinsic nature,
making clear body, speech and mind,
by the power of purifying into the nondual dharma,
the six bodies are to be accomplished.

Now, in order to abandon what is incompatible with the world:

Having entirely abandoned your own nature,
do not torment yourself with austerities;
practice blissfully, as bliss allows:
here, in time to come, you are a buddha.

Whoever, not knowing his own defining mark, wishes to obtain the excellent fruits of qualities and enters the austerities of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) and the various austerities of the śrāvakas, tormenting his own body and speech with pain, engages even in this world in the cause of suffering, and elsewhere too will not meet the wisdom of bliss. Therefore the five kinds of sense pleasures are appearances arising from one's own wisdom; since the power of qualities is unobstructed, by the power of practicing them according to inclination and by way of bliss, all activities are accomplished through the qualities of vidyādharas and the like, and by that one will attain the wisdom of a buddha.

The condensed meaning of these is that first, in order to accomplish all that is to be done, the defining mark of the support is taught: one should know the qualities of the master, the qualities of the disciple, what is to be abandoned as incompatible, and so forth.

Further, in order to enjoy the five desirables while endowed with view and conduct:

In all the enjoyments of yoga,
be joyful, with no fear whatever;
there is no evil, so do not fear;
the pledges are hard to transgress.

For the special purpose, one enjoys the five poisons, the afflictions of everything comprised by outer objects and inner elements, together with every object of the enjoyment of desires without exception, along with the family maṇḍalas and the hosts of goddesses, and brings the aims of self and others to completion. Through the profound empowerment, one knows and realizes all the unobstructed hosts of the emanations of movement, abodes and senses as the enjoyment of yoga, and enjoys them; and one should abide at ease.

Further, the stages of practice:

Accomplished: practicing alone, practicing with one,
practicing together, and the lovely subsequent practice:
this is unsurpassed buddha awakening;
always rely on it rightly.

The practitioner who engages in the work of self and others by way of the four persons, having completed the preliminary limbs such as full awakening, meditates on a single deity, a solitary hero, or on just one father and mother, whichever; meditates on that deity's consort and the persons of light in the manner of union; meditates on the circle of the maṇḍala in the form of practicing together with the host of the retinue; and meditates on the form in which, from the deities empowered and blessed by it, the light rays radiate and gather and beautify self and others. Since all are thus set in the unsurpassed essence of the awakening of the buddhas, one should rightly and always approach it.

If one asks whether this is certain:

Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
declared thus; the glorious
perfectly complete buddhas
and the vajra holders declared it.

It always arises by the power of meditating on the inexhaustible qualities of greatness. These are clear as the primordially abiding essence, and the arrayed miracles of ornament appear clearly through the power of pure yoga. This very thing was spoken by Vajradhara, lord of all families, and by all the maṇḍalas arising from him.

Now, in order to teach the rite of causing the wisdom to descend at the beginning of the empowerment above:

Endowed with yoga and endowed with the secret,
having accomplished my own wisdom seal,
by dancing in the proper manner
one grants bliss to the disciples.

With the four kinds of yoga, together with the secret mantra and the mudrā, one meditates on the four kinds of maṇḍala, together with the signs of the letters, which arise from the method of causing the wisdom of the secret seal to descend. From that, with the mudrās of body, speech and mind, one causes one's own signs to descend; having accomplished the seal of one's own wisdom and abiding one-pointedly, one grants the bliss of yoga; and, moving in the dance-method in whatever manner, one grants the wisdom of bliss to the disciple.

What is to be done after that:

From then on the excellent disciple,
by following the wisdom of the seal,
like the supreme Vairocana,
accomplishes the nature of all buddhas.

Abiding one-pointedly on the signs of his own experience, the body trembles and shakes, and he is fully adorned with the clear forms of the major and minor marks; like the buddha Vairocana he accomplishes the supreme body and becomes the nature of all buddhas.

Having thus gathered the accumulations, performed the accomplishment and conferred the empowerment, the method of worshipping the deities and the master afterward is this:

Then flavors, food and dwelling,
song, cymbals and the like:
these are offered to the Protectors.
The great vajra master,
oneself and others too, are worshipped with every desirable thing;
the perfect fruit of the desired aim is accomplished.

Having accomplished the collection of desirable enjoyments, and further, actually arranging all the offering substances without exception and also by way of mental emanation, one first presents offerings to the hosts of deities of the maṇḍala. After that, having worshipped the second buddha, the master, in the same way, oneself and the other yogins who have been meditated as the maṇḍala of deities should also be worshipped with the nine kinds of desirable enjoyments.

Now, in order to teach the branches of engaging in the maṇḍala:

Then, having fully accomplished oneself,
firmly established as master of all,
by engaging in the proper manner,
one should proclaim the true Dharma.

The vajra master should first, through the stages of approach, make his own mind-stream fit for the work. Having completed the meditation according to the rite taught in the arrangements of the secret meaning, and the activity of recitation, then, in order to apply himself to the purposes of gathering the accumulations, accomplishment, empowerment, the rite of consecration and so on, he completes all the activities such as the ten principles. Possessing an intelligence skilled in constructing the maṇḍalas of the individual families, and being firmly established as master of all, he should engage in the proper manner, through the rite of the ground, the rite of the preparation, and so forth.

The sequence is as follows. For gathering the accumulations and for accomplishment, one begins from the very outset with the rite of the ground and engages in it. For the stages of empowering the disciple, one begins once any of the five kinds of approach has been completed. At the start of the rite of the maṇḍala's time, knowing the purpose of the maṇḍala, one should perform in full: examining the ground, requesting the ground, blessing the site, commanding the lord of the ground, supplicating for the protection of the ground, and the rite of protection. Hence it says, "by engaging in the proper manner."

Further, for the preparation of the deities, one smears the maṇḍala and strews it with various flowers, sets out the offerings, meditates on the protection wheel, makes supplication in the stages of meditation, and pleases them. Hence it says, "by engaging in the proper manner."

Again, for the preparation of the disciple, there are: giving the commands; arousing enthusiasm; giving the subsequent instructions; gathering the accumulations in the presence of the lord, generating the mind of awakening and taking up the vows; purifying the three places so as to make things auspicious; the sequence of giving the tooth-stick; removing faults and casting it down; examining the omens and signs in it; cleansing with scented water; giving the kuśa grass; tying the protection cord; arousing enthusiasm; offering torma at the place of preparation and having the disciple lie down on a mat of kuśa grass; the master inwardly pleasing the maṇḍala; teaching what is to be done about good and bad signs; and the blessing.

Again, one should engage in the proper manner for the preparation of the vases: teaching their material, shape and color; adorning them with the emblems of the family and blessing them; the suchness of purity; dissolving the maṇḍala of the family into them and, through complete recitation, their becoming mantra water; the rite of making that auspicious; and, after the accomplishment of the image maṇḍala and so on, arranging them in their respective places.

Again, "engaging in the proper manner" means that one should engage in the works of constructing the image maṇḍala, accomplishing the maṇḍala, the torma, the fire offering and the consecration. The extensive rites for these are taught in the seventh chapter, so they are explained clearly there. The sequence for performing them as general activities is taught in the maṇḍala rite, and it is not elaborated here.

Therefore this verse should be understood as the general meaning of all the branches of the rite. The first line teaches the branch of prior engagement. The second teaches the defining characteristics of the support. The third teaches, in summary, the ten branches of the rite of the maṇḍala's time. The fourth teaches the instruction that does not conflict with these. As the root tantra says:

Then, having fully accomplished oneself,
through the power of familiarity with clear yoga,
by whichever branches of approach,
one first undertakes the preliminaries.
The stages of the ten principles and so on,
abiding as master of all,
the intelligent great hero
fully applies to the aims of self and others.
The collection of rites of the maṇḍala's time,
by engaging in the proper manner,
from the ground up to the remaining rites,
carrying out the aims of the activities unmixed,
in keeping with the instructions that accord with the meaning,
in order to take in the beings who enter there,
one should proclaim the true Dharma
and bring the meaning of the teaching to fulfillment.

Now is taught the manner in which, as the characteristic of enlightened activity, beings in general are tamed through the collected families:

The glorious mode of Vajrasattva
fully accomplishes all ritual procedures,
and, as that which fully applies all ritual procedures,
by its very nature it is the supreme of the great.

That very great Vajradhara, nondual with the three bodies, delights in gathering into one the vast assemblies through the various particulars of the faculties and sense sources of those to be tamed, and, for the sake of those who wish to enter its door, taught the maṇḍala of the collected families. The radiance of twofold glory from the Blessed One's own body fully accomplishes all the ritual procedures of the six families, and through all the rites of the ritual procedures applies itself to the vast welfare of others. Because it reveals the mode in which ultimate suchness and conventional magical display are nondual, the rite of the mudrā is clearly accomplished just as its own essential nature is.

The condensed meaning is this: by way of the great maṇḍala of the collected families one constructs the great maṇḍala of the assembly, and, relying on that, in order to accomplish the maṇḍala of the assembly, the intelligent yogin should be endowed with the defining characteristics of the support.

As the Seventh Chapter says:

Having opened the mind through wisdom,
on a site that has been thoroughly examined,
the master taught this definitive scripture:
the lord of accomplishment, perfect in every way,

and so on; and as the root tantra says:

Fair of face, peaceful in nature and character,
delighting all people, with a mind like the sky,

and so on. Having come to know, through the meaning of these passages, the distinctions between qualities and faults, one who is endowed with the four perfections should proceed as follows:

The substances and the site that have been examined,
think of them as the syllable that transforms into anything;
having radiated light rays in every direction
and pervaded everything without exception,
contemplate them in the manner of the hoped-for and the sublime.

Through the meaning of this passage and the like, one gathers the perfections, and then performs, in their proper order, the rite of the ground, the rite of the preparation, the construction of the image maṇḍala, and the accomplishment of the maṇḍala.

As for the particulars of the maṇḍala: on the outside is a square, encircled by tiers such as the vajra garland, and including the eight sites of the great charnel grounds. Within that is a square with four doors and their tiers, and within that are the outer and inner courtyards together with the garland of palaces. Inside these, one should lay out the sites of the nine great maṇḍalas: the areas of the directions and intermediate directions, divided by the beams of eight pillars, together with the center.

In the center is the maṇḍala of Vajrasattva, a peaceful five-pronged vajra, white and red, with light rays. In the east is the wheel palace of Vairocana, beautified by a garland of wheels. In the south is the maṇḍala of Heruka, a crescent moon turned inward and divided into nine squares, encircled by blue wrathful vajras with splayed prongs. In the west is the palace of Padmanarteśvara, a lotus maṇḍala encircled by a garland of red lotuses. In the north is the maṇḍala of Vajrasūrya, encircled by a yellow garland of jewels. In the four squares of the intermediate directions are the maṇḍalas of the four activities of Paramāśva, adorned with the colors and emblems of the four kinds of enlightened activity.

Thus the thirteen inner deities are set in their respective places in those nine central palaces. Outside them, in the inner courtyard, the four offering goddesses, differing in color and ornaments and with matching hand emblems, should be placed, nine in each corner. At the inner doors, four groups of nine wisdom goddesses are set out, and at the outer doors, four groups of nine worldly goddesses of the same appearance should be placed.

In the outer courtyard, the maṇḍalas of the emanations that conform to worldly beings and those of the beings who exist in their own right are brought together into one. In the east are the great gods and the guardians of the directions. In the south are the hosts of the eight great ḍākinīs. In the west are the eight great nāgas, each with its own retinue. In the north are the chiefs of the six classes of beings with their retinues. The inner doors are marked with ornaments such as archways, together with an arrangement of the great qualities of the buddhas. At the outer doors, the round worldly courtyard is adorned with worldly ornaments.

Then, in order to fully accomplish all ritual procedures, one gathers the accumulation of merit, removes the obstructing spirits who cause hindrance by means of an extensive rite of offering torma, and makes the sequence of boundaries firm by means of the protection wheel and so forth. Then one should accomplish the practice through the feast gathering of the accumulation of merit and the feast gathering of the accumulation of wisdom, endowed with the branches of approach and accomplishment.

Then, in order to apply it fully through all ritual procedures, one brings the aims of oneself and others to completion through the rites of the gathering and vast enlightened activities, and should please the deities and present to them the fire offering and the remainder torma. Through the remaining rites, which are the concluding branches, one brings the accomplishment of the maṇḍala to completion: one circumambulates the outer edge of the maṇḍala and makes the aspiration never to be separated; one makes up for whatever was excessive or deficient; one recites verses of auspiciousness; one takes the remaining accomplishments; one makes offerings and praises to the deities; one withdraws the three kinds of maṇḍala in their proper order; one releases the boundary maṇḍala and gives torma to the protectors and the hosts of guests; one pleases the vajra master and the others with an offering fee; one makes aspirations for the sake of beings and proclaims the gift of the Dharma; and one remains without falling away from the yoga of conduct. In this way one should apply it fully.

By this, in order that the essential nature of the great maṇḍala of the collected families be clearly accomplished, one applies oneself fully to the mudrā and abides in it by way of the great maṇḍala and the great mode of accomplishment, and so the supreme attainment in keeping with it will be accomplished. Its mode is also explained in the section of the Ninth Chapter, and it should be understood in accordance with the meaning taught extensively in the root tantra as well.

Now, for the sake of those who delight in the maṇḍalas of the individual families, in order to accomplish the work of the maṇḍala through their respective enlightened activities, it is taught in six modes. First, by way of Vajrasattva:

Through the application of various activities
and through various means of taming,
in order to tame sentient beings,
it is taught in modes other than that.

Here the various enlightened activities performed by the means that is great compassion are displayed. Because it possesses special means for setting all those it tames on such a path, in order to set them there by that very means, there is practice through the assembly arising from the form of Vajrasattva and through accomplishment by means of the assembly.

Here, too, the condensed meaning is the same as before. A person who is the support, possessing special defining characteristics, in his own place as described in the seventh chapter, and endowed with the four perfections, namely a consort, a retinue, implements, ornaments and the like, should accomplish the practice by way of his own maṇḍala. This is practiced in the same way as the activities of the collected families: drawing the image maṇḍala; accomplishing the maṇḍala; practicing through the four branches of approach and accomplishment, relying on the activities of the accumulations of merit and wisdom; pleasing each deity individually through song and answering song, sign and answering sign, mudrā and answering mudrā, and the various mudrās of dance; enjoying the feast of food and drink; abiding in the place and in the mudrā of the sacred commitment; and sporting through each one's own activities.

For the sake of the person who follows, the maṇḍala of Vairocana should be accomplished in the same way as above. Likewise, in order to teach the means of applying oneself to the enlightened activities of accomplishing Vajra Heruka, Padmanarteśvara, Vajrasūrya and the Play of Paramāśva, six verses were spoken, beginning:

That very yoga is the Blessed One,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata.

The sequence of accomplishment is taught extensively in the rite of the feast gathering, so it will not be elaborated here. Further:

In order to accomplish by way of the support,
resembling one's own deity,
in order to accomplish one's own blessing,
by applying it as the very nature of the image,
one should dance with the application of mudrā.

In order for ordinary beings to join their body, speech and mind to the body, speech and mind of the Sugata, they display and apply to themselves the image of the body, the sign of speech and the mudrā of mind: these are what they dance. The condensed meaning is this: in the assembly maṇḍala, with any one of the mudrās of body, speech or mind, through the four branches of approach and accomplishment, when the five kinds of signs have become clear, one should take the accomplishment of the mudrā.

Now, in teaching the results of the foregoing, first comes the general characteristic. Two and a half verses were spoken, beginning:

Through the various kinds of sacred commitment,
if one investigates in this way.

In order that all the activities arising from accomplishment may be experienced in the mind-stream from moment to moment, one should apply them to the two kinds of path, the means of practice. Focusing on the clear activities of the basis, the support and so on, and having carried out the stages of conduct that accord with that very mode, then, through the power of teaching in accordance with the meaning, the accomplishment of Ḍākinī Māyā, the supreme bliss that is equal union with all buddhas, will be accomplished.

Specifically, in order to teach the qualities and results proper to each of the family maṇḍalas, such as the result of accomplishing the maṇḍala of Vajrasattva, six verses were spoken, beginning:

Everywhere, entirely, all,
through all the miraculous powers of the buddhas.

By the signs of the meaning of the five families and the five wisdoms, it applies itself to all phenomena; and having liberated its own disciples through the deeds of the five special enlightened activities, it brings them to the result of accomplishment, which has become the supreme quality. Their results are not explained, since they are easy to understand.

Again, to conclude the result, one and a half verses were spoken, beginning:

These and others, infinite and supreme,
the dharmadhātu, equal and unequaled.

The result arising from accomplishments gathered vastly like clouds, the immeasurable and infinite supreme essence, gathered by the equality and inequality of the dharmadhātu, unfolds without any final limit, and it is accomplished as glorious Vajrasattva himself, adorned with the wisdom of great bliss.

Now, to conclude the enlightened activity, one and a half verses were spoken, beginning:

Glorious, delighting in play, magically manifesting,
it sports through variety.

From the body of glorious great bliss, through the manner of the deeds of the wheels of the maṇḍala, the special enlightened activities, fourfold, eightfold, sixteenfold, twentyfold and up to three hundred and sixty fold, sport through various actions for the sake of liberation; and through the supreme bliss of every good fortune it fully accomplishes the realms of sentient beings by way of the various enlightened activities toward those to be tamed.

Further, to conclude the assembly maṇḍala, one and a half verses were spoken, beginning:

These and others, infinite and supreme,
the dharmadhātu, equal and unequaled.

The yogins who have entered the door of the assembly maṇḍala should first know the sequence of accomplishment, and by practicing in accordance with its meaning, the assembly that accords with that very result of qualities is made manifest.

"The ritual procedure of the maṇḍala of the ḍākinīs, equal union with all buddhas": here a general term is applied to a specific particular. Like a word that suits whatever occasion the officiant meets, it determines the procedure of that very mode, and so that same term for the occasion applies also to the deities of the assembly maṇḍala. Therefore it is the knowing of the ritual procedure of the ḍākinī maṇḍala.

This concludes the explanation of the meaning of the Sixth Chapter.

Those who do not know the meaning of how the characteristics of all phenomena abide, and who have not understood the benefits that come from accomplishing activities, are deluded about all the doors of action. They are like one who carries out on the sick the secret command of the king of the host of Renbu, or like a crowd of blind people engaging with all the treatises they have examined. Those who are unable to accomplish these will not be helped by relying merely on the story of what they know: it is like the jewel ornament on the crown of the nāga Takṣa, or like nectar that devours poison at the time one's karma has reached its end. Those who are unable to put these into practice will not be helped even by accomplishing and accumulating: they are like the brahmin woman Excellent Beauty, or like the queen Precious Enjoyment.

Those too, through the ancient stories and the like, did not accomplish it. One who lacks the branches of practicing the means of accomplishment will not engage in it: lacking confidence, he will not generate in his mind-stream the experience of the clear meaning. This is to set out on a path for which there is no account in the treatises at all, and to undertake an action for which the causes and conditions have not come together. It is like the householder Discernment entering the carpenter's house, or like accomplishing the wheel taught for the activity of King Rāhu.

All of these, too, have not applied themselves to activities by way of the perfections and have not trained their own mind-stream, so they cannot increase their strength and engage. They are like the enjoyments of Ajita, or like the wheel of the householder Having a Friend.

Their basis and support arises from what has the characteristics of the first path. Those who wish to undertake some other activity in which that path is to be relied upon become like the fourfold army of King Golden Protector; therefore a self-willed mind should be refuted.

Their characteristics are these: the support has degenerated, they lack a proper place, they have not entered the maṇḍala, and they have neither seen nor obtained the aims that arise from it. They are like the parrot Lord of Speech, the brahmin's daughter Blue One, the son of King Protector of the Land, the son of Garlanded Threefold Beauty, and King Refuge Lion.

Therefore the topics that come from the seventh chapter come first of all. As the root tantra says:

Relying on whatever practices of activity,
without the perfections assembled,
whoever practices in that way
will accomplish nothing whatsoever.

Their general characteristic is given in the Seventh Chapter:

Endowed with a perfect support and place,
having completed the preliminary approach,
the ground and the preparation,
constructing and accomplishing the image maṇḍala,
torma, fire offering and auspicious activities,
entry, empowerment and the rest:
in order to ripen and liberate oneself and others,
one should accomplish them in accordance with the sublime scripture.

Of these, first, in order to teach the image maṇḍala in brief:

Then, what is further to be explained:
Vajrasattva, the glorious deity,
supreme among all in its various forms,
the magical display of all the wheels,
in the delight of the supreme secret,
one should accomplish all as oneself.

Since the explanation of the image maṇḍala has now come up: for the purposes of empowerment, gathering the accumulations, protection, the rites of consecration and so on, through stages of action other than the many activities described above, one who is endowed with the application of knowing the mudrās of all the maṇḍalas should apply them to the ritual procedures of all the maṇḍalas by way of the various particular postures.

In ancient times, the ways in which maṇḍalas were magically displayed and explained on the summit of Mount Meru appeared on a vast scale. From the maṇḍala of glorious Vajrasattva, he taught in the manner of the magical display of all the wheels, by way of the hero endowed with seven branches. And he taught the special means of accomplishing that very maṇḍala, beginning from the person who is the support, in order to display magically all the wheels that are explained as the branches of accomplishment by the following person who has completed the branches of the rite such as empowerment. The last two lines mean that one should accomplish it in a place secluded in three respects, outer, inner and secret, endowed with every perfection. As the root tantra says:

He emanated the vast arrayed maṇḍala
on the summit of Mount Meru,
and, according to the perceptions of those to be tamed,
the mode of the magical display of the maṇḍala;
in order to take in the beings who follow,
he explained that very maṇḍala,
and in places outer, inner and secret
he taught the accomplishment endowed with perfection.

Then, in order to teach the place in detail, six and a half verses were spoken, beginning:

In secluded places,
or in one's own pleasure grove and the like.

First, the half verse teaches the place of Vajrasattva. Then one verse teaches that one who is endowed with a perfect support should engage, with perfection, in the branches of accomplishment and in the preliminaries of the maṇḍala and so on. After that, five verses teach, in order, the places for accomplishing the maṇḍalas of glorious Vairocana, Heruka, Padmanarteśvara, Vajrasūrya and Paramāśva.

Having thus understood the general characteristic, the specific meaning is this. In a place secluded from everything unfavorable to accomplishing the maṇḍala, one should make a building with three tiers. In the outer courtyard one should place the outer guardians and the articles for dance, music and the like. In the middle courtyard one should station the outer yogins, such as the action vajra, and the hosts of disciples. In the inner chamber one draws the image maṇḍala and beautifies it with ornaments, implements and so on, and the principal yogins should stay there. If such places are not available, one constructs the maṇḍala and undertakes the sequence of accomplishment in one's own pleasure grove, on the bank of a river, on the shore of the ocean, in a meadow adorned with flowers, or the like.

If one asks in what form of support the accomplishment should be done: abiding oneself as the great mudrā of glorious Vajrasattva, together with the host of goddesses of every kind of good fortune, one constructs one's own maṇḍala, accomplishes the maṇḍala through such forms as meditating on it, and, once it has become clear, one should undertake the various enlightened activities.

When the activity is partial and the accomplishment is partial, success comes through the power of the coming together of causes, conditions and dependent connections; when one practices approach and accomplishment at one's ease, one may undertake them in whatever place is comfortable.

In the time of pacifying, in order to construct the maṇḍala of the buddha Vairocana, one should accomplish it in a place that has become a support for any of the Three Jewels, especially a place where a sacred support has been erected, a temple or the like in a hermitage five hundred yojanas from a village, or on mountain summits endowed with the eight qualities.

In the time of wrathful activity, the place for accomplishing the maṇḍala of glorious Heruka is to be a charnel ground endowed with nine qualities, a place where the great worldly gods are worshipped with offerings, the seven places of the mātṛkās, or the like.

In the time of magnetizing, the accomplishment of Padmanarteśvara should be undertaken in a lotus garden, a garden of red flowers, a place where a stream swirls to the right, the confluence of rivers, a place by the ocean, or the like.

In the time of enriching, in order to accomplish Vajrasūrya, one should do it in the palace of a great king, a place where many great festivities are held, the house of a wealthy householder, a lotus garden, or the like.

In order to accomplish the various activities, the places of Paramāśva should be: a dwelling of the Saṅgha suited to each of the various activities, nearby places, the residence of a king, the quarters of prostitutes and butchers, the bank of a river, or dwellings superior to others.

Now, in order to gather ornaments and implements in that very place one has chosen:

With various kinds of attire
and various kinds of accomplishment,
for the purpose of Vajrasattva,
fully accomplish the maṇḍala and the rest.

For the sake of accomplishing the maṇḍala, one should prepare and arrange various things such as ornaments, attire and offering implements; this has already been explained in detail above. Its meaning should be applied in particular to the maṇḍala of Vajrasattva.

In order to teach, along with the particular ornaments and implements of the other family maṇḍalas, the arrangement of ornaments and seats:

For the buddhas, the red of the lotus;
for Vajrapāṇi, variegated;
the color of Lokeśvara is white;
that of glorious Vajrasūrya, yellow;
all colors, and whatever is at hand,
canopies, victory banners and ornaments,
the glory of fully accomplishing variety,
belong to Paramāśva, the vajra-holder.

The ornaments of the maṇḍala of the buddha Vairocana, the ornaments of the yogin, the offering substances and so on should be made red. This is because they are not separated from the two kinds of passion, and because they overpower the one-sidedly peaceful nirvāṇa. Likewise, Heruka's are variegated, since he liberates the hosts of arrogant spirits in various ways by accomplishing various fierce rites. Padmanarteśvara's are white, because he purifies the mind-stream of delusion, pacifies all the extremes of superimposition and denial, and purifies the signs of permanence and annihilation. Vajrasūrya's are yellow, because he liberates the hosts of the greedy and the miserly by means of various hosts of precious qualities. Paramāśva's are not fixed to one color: the ornaments of the yogin, the canopy ornaments of the maṇḍala and the offering ornaments are to be described in various colors, just as one wishes.

Now, in order to draw the image maṇḍalas in conformity with the form of the natural maṇḍala:

Just as the place and the retinues were,
just as the arrangement and the beings were,
just as the arrangement of the retinues was,
so the supreme wheel brought it about.

Just as in ancient times, on the summit of Mount Meru, the Sugata himself blessed and protected the place, so too the followers should purify whatever place of accomplishment they use through the rite of the ground and so on. Its sequence is as follows.

One makes the four kinds of examination: examining the ground by way of its ornaments, by way of its shape, by way of its action and function, and by way of consciousness, wisdom and mudrā. Then one requests the ground from its visible owners or buys it for a price, and in order to request it from the invisible ones one abides in yoga. Having pleased them with torma, in order to cleanse the ground one purifies it with substances, samādhi and mantra; one examines the mahoraga; and from removing the faults and building the platform up to inserting the sacred essence, one should smear it with the mantra of the pure nature of reality and with scents.

Then, in order to bless the ground, one sets out offerings and torma and meditates on oneself as the deities of the family of the principal and retinue; one blesses the stacked elements, the vajra fence and so on; one makes it firm with the mantra and mudrā of the crossed vajra; and in order to command the lord of the ground, one strikes the earth with the palm and calls forth the goddess, presents offerings and torma to her, supplicates her, and the goddess grants permission. For the protection of the ground there are: the activities of the principal and retinue yogins; urging by the goddesses of the four directions by way of the four immeasurables and so on; supplicating by way of the common and the general characteristics; the attire of the body; gazing with the eyes; the emanation and absorption of the four syllables; brandishing weapons and reciting mantras, sending forth the hosts of wrathful ones, and issuing commands; circling the maṇḍala and striking with the kīla; and the particular postures. With these, the rite of the ground is summarized.

As for "just as the retinues were": in ancient times the supporting place, the celestial palace, was beautified with ornaments, with its particular multi-storied buildings, and there was the manner of the hosts that support the deity maṇḍala, and the persons entering there abided just as the retinues did. And by the word "arrangement" is meant that, just as the maṇḍala ornaments such as the vases and the offering implements were assembled, so the followers too should set these up in the preparation.

For the preparation of the deities, one smears the maṇḍala and strews flowers with mantra and mudrā, sets out the offerings, meditates on the protection wheel, performs the activity of the two clear focuses in the stages of meditation, and supplicates and pleases them.

For the disciple, there are: giving commands; arousing enthusiasm; giving the subsequent instructions; for the gathering of the accumulations, making a pledge in the presence of the lord; purifying with fourteen branches in the extensive form and with seven branches in the condensed form; the stages of generating the mind of awakening and of the vow; purifying the three places for the sake of auspiciousness; the sequence of giving the tooth-stick; removing faults and casting it down; examining the omens and signs in it; cleansing with scented water; giving the kuśa grass; tying the protection cord; arousing enthusiasm; offering torma at the place of preparation and having the disciple lie on a mat of kuśa grass; the master inwardly pleasing the maṇḍala; teaching what is to be done about good and bad signs; and the blessing.

For the vases, there are: teaching their material, shape and color; adorning them with the emblems of the family and blessing them; dissolving the maṇḍala of one's own family into that very purity and, through complete recitation, their becoming mantra water; the rite of making that auspicious; and, after the accomplishment of the maṇḍala, arranging them in their respective places. With these, the rite of preparation is summarized.

Therefore the meaning of "just as the beings were" refers to the various conceptions of those to be tamed. In the way that seven hundred and thirteen emanated maṇḍalas arose, so the image maṇḍalas too are taught as such a collection of arrangements of maṇḍala wheels, in keeping with that number.

If one asks, "Is it accomplished by practicing with a fabricated maṇḍala, without having seen the sequence of the maṇḍala arise directly?" then, in order to teach that the three kinds of maṇḍala are alike in merit, it says:

"Just as the constant merit
from directly seeing the wheel
of the deities of glorious Vajrasattva,
so too is that of what is made later."

The emanated maṇḍala, in which the particular appearances of the six kinds of family wheels arising from the emanation of the great Vajradhara himself were first emanated, the maṇḍala taught and emanated on the summit of Mount Meru; the clear meditation in meditative absorption in accordance with its meaning; and the later representation by means of images for those who did not see these: all of these become alike and equal in merit.

Thus, after the teaching of the rite of the ground, the rite of abiding in preparation and the benefits of the maṇḍala, one should begin the activities such as constructing the image maṇḍala. First, for the activity of the lines and for dividing the portions, it says:

"The one with wisdom who lays the maṇḍala's lines,
establishing the sameness of all directions,
in the directions east, west and north,
by laying four lines, makes the maṇḍala eightfold."

The master, endowed with the ten kinds of reality and knowing all the particulars of outer, inner and secret activities, begins the maṇḍala rite with the activity of laying the lines first. A line assistant together with an action consort (karmamudrā), or else four supreme companions in practice who have entered the meditative absorption of the four goddesses, make supplication from the four directions, each adorned with their own mudrā. With a line cord made of five colors, its thickness a twentieth part of the door and its length reaching twice the maṇḍala, one focuses on all directions as sameness. Having removed all conceptions of auspicious and inauspicious, with a mind of a single nature one erects in the sky a multi-storied mansion as the dwelling of the deities, and there invites the deities of the preparation to be seated in the manner of lords. Then, circling with the dance steps of the mudrā, and in accordance with that, one should lay the lines on the ground, without mistaking east, west, north and south.

Therefore, from the Seventh Chapter:

"The master endowed with all the characteristics,
knowing true wisdom, reality itself,
endowed with the conduct of his own deity,
the knowing one, adorned with the twelve signs."

This teaches the characteristics of the master. Again, from the root tantra:

"A new line cord of proper measure,
spun by goddesses with mantra,
its length twice the maṇḍala,
its thickness a twentieth part of the door,
is placed in a vessel, a precious skull cup,
and generated with the family wisdom mantra."

And from the Compendium of Reality Tantra (Tattvasaṃgraha) as well:

"The buddha families, summoned
by the gaze of the eye, should be made to enter."

These teach the characteristics of the line cord and its generation as the family deities. Again, from the Condensed Rite:

"With the hook blazing with twenty,
those abiding as one without duality
are pleased with offerings, gifts and praise,
and with the principal's mantra one twists the cord."

By this, the wisdom maṇḍala is summoned and absorbed nondually, worshipped, and the cord twisted. Again, from the Compendium of Reality Tantra: oṃ dipta dṛṣṭi aṃkuśi ja oṃ vajra samaya sūtra mātikrama hūṃ oṃ āḥ hūṃ. By this it is made stable. And from the Amoghapāśa Tantra as well:

"The supreme companion in practice, standing in the west,
at the secret mantra of the three realities,
jjaḥ, from the heart of oneself as the principal,
draws it straight to one's own brow."

By this the line is stretched. From the Condensed Rite:

"Supreme in strength, O hero, please attend!
With the sound of the vajra line,
having well accomplished the maṇḍala of space,
you urge it on to the welfare of beings."

By this, one urges with the sound of the line; and, from the Purification of the Lower Realms, oṃ anyonyānugata sarva dharmā spharaspharānugata sarva dharma atyantānupraviṣṭa sarva dharmā hūṃ. By this one urges toward passionate union. From the Condensed Rite:

"The supreme companion, with the vajra mudrā,
draws jaḥ three times to the place of the navel;
placing the vajra fist on the ground,
one should lay the first line cord."

And again, from the root tantra as well:

"After the four lines of east, west, north and south,
one lays lines on the sides and the corners.
Of the first sixteen major portions,
excluding the strip of the courtyard,
divide each into four minor portions.
The outer and inner sixteen, the portals and doors,
the walls with their ornaments, the place of the sense pleasures,
the enclosure and the charnel grounds, assess as fitting.
The center is the wheel of one's own family;
divide each maṇḍala into four parts."

By this one lays the major portions of the maṇḍala and divides them in detail. And again:

"Having completed the rite of laying the lines,
one worships and praises the wisdom cord,
and with mudrā, secret mantra and meditative absorption
requests it to depart to its own abode."

By this the wisdom cord is pleased and requested to depart. Then, for dividing the portions, from the Vajraśekhara:

"Endowed with union with the pride of the family,
with the two eyes whose centers are sun and moon,
casting their lashes toward the ten directions,
with hi divide the doors and the portions.
oṃ vajra vegākrama hūṃ"

By this the doors and portions are divided with the vajra gaze. And again, from the root tantra:

"oṃ Supreme nature of all emanation,
without entity, clear, endowed with peaceful light,
vajra holder, bringing forth the great treasure,
bless the maṇḍala as pure!
For those whose minds are clear in the mantra of sameness,
the thorns of faults become purified."

By this one makes supplication and purifies the thorns of faults. After that, by this very verse one views all directions as sameness. Since the activity of the maṇḍala rite is taught through the instruction of the holy lama, the limbs of the rite have been arranged on that basis. The four lines are the two Brahmā lines and the two diagonal lines, making four. By laying them, the maṇḍala comes to have eight small pointed sections; the purpose of this is that its measurements will not shift.

Again, after that, in order to examine and accomplish the maṇḍala, it says:

"Who is able fully to examine
the maṇḍala just as it truly is?
Through the nature of one's own deity
the examination of the maṇḍala is accomplished."

The ultimate maṇḍala and the wisdom deities emanated from it cannot be seen by all who are to be tamed. If one asks, "Then how should one view the maṇḍala?" it is by meditating nondually with the wisdom maṇḍala of one's own nature. Even those who are unable to practice in that way will attain accomplishment through the power of relying on and entering the image maṇḍala.

These, moreover, are to be distinguished in terms of three kinds of person.
