The first should apply it to all the qualities of the Sugata by way of the practice with signs and the practice without signs. The second should abide by way of the nonduality of the maṇḍala of intrinsic nature and the maṇḍala of meditative absorption. The last attains accomplishment by focusing on and examining the image maṇḍala through the application of a support.

Furthermore, so that one endowed with yoga may make up for what is excessive or deficient and accomplish the rite, it says:

"Through yoga of oneself as one's own deity,
one should accomplish all the accomplishments.
If the rite has too much or is incomplete,
there is no fault: it will be accomplished."

If one who is endowed with the two kinds of meditative absorption, nondual with oneself, examines just that, one will not be obscured by the loss of even subtle limbs of the rite; it is accomplished as a limb of accomplishment. Thus, from the Secret Union Tantra:

"Through practice with signs and without signs,
the very embodiment of the two mudrās,
abiding in meditative absorption and wisdom,
one will accomplish all the accomplishments.
The subtle limbs of the rite,
excessive or deficient, will not become faults.
Therefore, in the maṇḍala and other rites,
one accomplishes through yoga of oneself as one's own deity.
Whoever is weak in yoga
will not obtain the accomplishments.
Therefore, with every diligence,
one should undertake the aim of the great yoga."

Now, to teach the particulars of the maṇḍala's shape and so forth, it says:

"Universally various, auspicious,
having a universally various wheel,
for the sake of universally various accomplishments,
the maṇḍala is universally various."

The inner sign of the maṇḍala is as follows. It has eight compartments, divided by four vajra lines; in the outer ring around it are eight round compartments arranged in the manner of a wheel; with the rim and the dividing lines, together with the vajra fence, it is made beautiful, auspicious, by the light of the qualities of Vajrasattva, and is marked by the form of a universally various wheel. This teaches the special abode of Vajrasattva.

Then, with the half verse, the outer signs of all six maṇḍalas, from the outer courtyard up to the outer vajra fence, are taught: a square with four doors, the ornaments of the portals, beautifully crowded with various enjoyments of the sense pleasures, encircled by three garlands of lotus, vajra fence, and vajra light. Outside of that are the eight great charnel grounds, abiding in a terrifying manner, and one should draw the eight qualities of the origin, and the outer iron mountain wall with its vajra garland and fence. Therefore it is called "the universally various maṇḍala." Thus, from the root tantra:

"On an even maṇḍala of proper measure,
well divided with the vajra lines,
a square palace with doors,
beautifully adorned with four portals,
with walls of the five precious substances
running around the courtyard and the door frames,
with nets and half nets,
precious bricks and flowers arrayed,
crowded with goddesses of the six sense pleasures,
beautiful with half moons and vajra emblems,
parasols, victory banners, yak-tail fans, and garlands,
pennants, bells, and a host of sweet sounds."

Or else, to teach its own shape, ornaments, signs and so forth, and the outer charnel grounds and so on, it is also said:

"Eight compartments divided by four lines;
a rim with various lotus emblems and vajras;
in the corner compartments, four full vases,
rich with the essence of nectar, beautiful with ornaments and signs.
The outer compartments all in the manner of a wheel,
with the dividing lines of vajra garlands.
Beyond where the three outer fences stand,
within the outer iron wall, the vajra fence,
in the directions and corners, the eight great charnel grounds.
From the faces of the eight trees the field protectors emerge with half their bodies,
their faces in accord with the seats of the eight guardians of the directions,
holding a curved knife in the right and a skull cup in the left.
From within eight lakes eight nāgas emerge with half their bodies,
palms joined, reverent to the Blessed One.
At the foot of the eight trees dwell yogins with their retinues;
clouds and stūpas, self-arisen hosts of ḍākinīs;
many vetālas, piśācas, and bhūtas dwell there,
mingled with corpses, birds, dogs, jackals, and wolves.
At that time, to the host of fierce ḍākinī deities,
the charnel grounds appear like the continent of Jambudvīpa.
Outside, wish-fulfilling trees, vases, pennants, and pools
make it beautiful with the enjoyments of desire."

And again, from the root tantra:

"A square with four doors,
evenly divided with proper measure,
beautiful with a fence of yogic garlands,
adorned with eight portals.
The courtyard is the measure of one major portion.
The beams on the eight supreme pillars
are adorned with garlands of their respective emblems,
and jewels, vajras and so forth blaze as necklaces and earrings."

Now, to teach the particulars of the inner shapes and so forth of the other family wheels, it says:

"The buddha maṇḍala is round,
the vajra holder's like a crescent moon,
the lotus lord's in the shape of a lotus,
the supreme jewel's in the shape of a jewel."

The inner pattern of the maṇḍala of the buddha Vairocana is an eight-spoked wheel, with an outer rim of a garland of wheels, encircled by eight compartments in the manner of a wheel. The shape of the maṇḍala of Vajra Heruka is like the shape of a wind maṇḍala, a half moon, with the red side shown toward the east; in the center it is divided into nine compartments, and the inner palace with its doors is encircled by a garland of blue vajras. That of Padmanarteśvara is a lotus maṇḍala with a garland of lotuses, encircled by a garland of eight compartments in the manner of a wheel. That of Vajrasūrya is an eight-cornered jewel, its center divided into nine compartments, encircled by a garland of yellow jewels. The particulars of their signs are clearly taught in the sādhanas that set out the meaning explained in the root tantra, and are taught in the Condensed Rite, so there is no doubt about them.

Again, to teach the maṇḍala of Paramāśva, it says:

"However one wishes, with whatever is at hand,
at whatever time, and however is comfortable,
the glory illuminating all maṇḍalas,
Paramāśva is the vajra mudrā."

Paramāśva is the one who holds sway over the enlightened activities of all the families: sporting in all the abodes of the maṇḍala and with all the goddesses, he should be accomplished in accordance with what those to be tamed through the four activities, pacifying, enriching, magnetizing and wrathful, wish for, and by way of whatever resources are at hand.

Moreover, having understood the times and the actions for performing the respective enlightened activities, one should draw the maṇḍala of Paramāśva however is comfortable: on any wheel at all, one should encircle his own various emblems with vajras.

Having thus divided the portions of the maṇḍala, one should perform the activity of the colors by the rite common to all the families: gathering the materials for the colors; generating them as deities and pleasing the nondual ones; drawing the pañcarāga in the order of the colors; worshipping it and laying it down; making the colors auspicious; and examining the omens of the colors as good or bad. Then one should set out the emblems of the respective families; this will be explained below.

Then, to teach what the practitioner must abandon as unfavorable, it says:

"Never having entered a maṇḍala,
having abandoned the pledges,
and not truly knowing the secret,
one accomplishes nothing at all in practice."

Those who, first, are not endowed with the characteristics of a fit support; who have not thereby pleased the holy lama; who have not entered the image maṇḍala and have not properly received empowerment; who do not abide by the pledges; and who do not truly know the meaning of the secret, will only weary themselves in all their practice, and the fruit will not be accomplished.

Furthermore, the person who is the support should abide by the common vows:

"One should not kill living beings,
nor abandon the Three Jewels,
nor give up the awakening mind,
nor give up the fortunate master."

These are precisely the vows to be kept according to the scriptures of all the vehicles. Beyond that, according to the scriptures of the unsurpassed secret Great Vehicle, they are to be kept in a special way. All the sentient beings dwelling on the paths of the six destinies in the ten directions abide as the very essence of the maṇḍala of gods and goddesses; and since even a single one of them is adorned with countless deity maṇḍalas of the aggregates, elements and sense sources, killing even a single living being destroys a great many assemblies of maṇḍalas. Likewise, to abandon the Three Jewels of the common and special vehicles is to have no place of reliance, so that nothing one does will be accomplished. To abandon and damage the ultimate and conventional awakening mind cuts the root of the family, and the fruits of awakening wither. By abandoning the fortunate master, one is parted from the eye doctor who guides, and parted from the intelligence of wisdom, and the sprouts of good qualities will not grow. Therefore one must not transgress these vows.

Again, to teach the distinction between fortunate and unfortunate persons who are the support, the seven verses beginning:

"Universally various, fortunate one,
by accomplishing oneself, fortunate,"

are spoken. First, glorious Vajrasattva applies himself to all phenomena and accomplishes them as his own nature; since he abides in essence as the awakening mind, whose nature is great bliss, he is fortunate. Those to be tamed who are on the side opposed to him are those who abide in the vehicle of the bodhisattvas and progressively train in the stages of the grounds and paths: since they abandon the awakening mind and abide in the doctrine of selflessness, they are parted from the nature of great bliss, and so they are unfortunate.

The buddha Vairocana, not being parted from the two kinds of attachment, acts to bring all the beings of the three realms of existence onto the path of unsurpassed awakening, and so he is fortunate. Those to be tamed who are on the side opposed to him accomplish the path of a one-sided, merely peaceful nirvāṇa and abandon everything belonging to saṃsāra, and so they are unfortunate.

Glorious Heruka, by way of all conceptions, tames all the fierce ones on the path of aversion, and by various skillful means he places beings on the path, and so he is fortunate. Those to be tamed who are opposed to him are the pratyekabuddhas, who apply themselves to a one-sided emptiness and are without concern for the welfare of those to be tamed, and so they are unfortunate.

Padmanarteśvara sees all phenomena as having the essence of attachment purified, and so purifies all beings; since he acts for an aim in harmony with himself, he is fortunate. Those to be tamed who are opposed to him are the classes of non-Buddhists (tīrthikas), who all abide in the two views of eternalism and nihilism and hold wrong views, and so they are unfortunate.

Vajrasūrya gathers all of the holy Dharma, thoroughly purifies it into a buddha field, and establishes the kingdom of the holy Dharma, and so he is fortunate. Those to be tamed who are opposed to him are those who abide in worldly austerities: having no affection even for their own children and lacking any stream of compassion, they take no other beings as their concern, and so they are unfortunate.

Paramāśva, as the lord of wrathful application, gathers all worldly and transcendent kingdoms and leads all beings onto the path of liberation, and so he is fortunate. The wondrous sages of the Great God (Śiva), having abandoned all the enjoyments of desire and not knowing the suchness of selflessness, pursue the extreme of permanence, and so they are unfortunate.

The vajra masters, who practice by way of the cause, bliss, accomplish the path of bliss and make clear the purity of the fruit, bliss, and so they are fortunate. Those to be tamed who are opposed to them are the disciples who have bad views and who hate the pledges.

Now, to teach the benefit of knowing the meaning of the secret, it says:

"The meaning of the secret is the very truth.
By clearly knowing this,
in accordance with the word of all the buddhas,
one will be accomplished as all the buddhas."

By clearly knowing the meaning of the empowerment received in reliance on the image maṇḍala, the meaning of the conferral of the pledges, and the meaning of the practice, if one practices accordingly, the ultimate fruit will be accomplished. Therefore the first two lines teach the ground, the third teaches the path, and the fourth teaches the fruit; this should also be understood as a general characteristic.

Now, to teach the manner of entering, the two verses beginning

"When, abiding wholly in the master,
one enters into the wheel,"

are spoken. It is a vajra master endowed with perfect qualities who must undertake the actions and tasks concerning disciples and so forth; others may not.

The qualities of such a master are given in the root tantra:

"Endowed with a mind intent on the welfare of others,
having the common and special kinds, cultivated by nature, taken up and attained,
skilled in qualities, whichever they may be,
skilled in the ten principles and other activities:
maṇḍala, mudrā, meditative absorption, mantra,
summoning, worship, the applications of the rites, the great protection,
the concluding dissolution and the request to depart;
especially through the stages of the activities,
illuminating, vast, keeping beings in view,
he abides like the ocean, Mount Meru, a jewel, a pool,
the sky, the earth, and gold."

One endowed with such qualities, having entered the great maṇḍala of secret mantra, should introduce the qualified disciples who wish to follow after him. Thus, from the Seventh Chapter:

"Gentle, mild, and pleasant in speech,
endowed with faith, diligence, wisdom and intelligence,
diligent in the work of study, reflection and meditation,
with a mind of love for all sentient beings,
abiding in compassion, joy and equanimity,
single-pointedly delighting in the maṇḍala, the tantra, the lama,
and the profound Dharma,
fearless before all the treatises,
steadfast, striving for the aim of awakening."

Such disciples should be accepted. As explained in the maṇḍala rite, one should cleanse them with the water of the vase, make offerings, cover their faces, have them hold a garland, generate the mind of awakening, perform the questions and answers, the going for refuge, the supplication, and the introduction once more. The questions and answers for introduction are these: stating the characteristics that come from the scripture of the vajra master's secret maṇḍala, the master asks, "What do you delight in?" and the disciple answers, "In order to accomplish those excellent fortunes, I wish to enter the maṇḍala and receive empowerment."

The means of introduction is this:

"If one knows this true secret,
one is introduced as one wishes;
if one does not know the true secret,
one should be introduced by the right."

Those who have previously entered the place of the maṇḍala and know the meaning of the signs should, in the manner of the earlier procedures, introduce themselves into the maṇḍala as they wish. If they are without the signs, they should be introduced by way of the right, the skillful means.

If one asks how the subsequent procedure goes:

"Having thus entered the maṇḍala,
one likewise offers the fee;
knowing the secret rite itself,
one accomplishes the supreme unchanging."

Having led the disciple inside the curtain and completed the five kinds of rite, one then bestows the vajras of body, speech and mind, completes the five kinds of rite of the descent of wisdom and the five rites of entering, and then performs the subsequent actions: opening the eyes, removing the face covering and viewing the maṇḍala, offering the fee, and giving the commands. Through this, knowing the secret rite itself and entering and practicing in accordance with its meaning, one accomplishes the supreme unchanging.

As for the order of the fee, the nine verses beginning

"To the universally various lama
one must surely offer the supreme fee,"

are spoken. First, one should please glorious Vajrasattva and the vajra master, who abide in the manner of nonduality. One should please them with things one holds dear and finds very hard to give, with things dear to one's heart, and with special kinds of substances. If one asks why, it is because one wishes for the inexhaustible aim of that very one. If one asks whether he is able to accomplish anything, he is nondual with the universally various Vajradhara: being alike in the special qualities of body, speech and mind, he is even greater by way of the benefit he brings. Therefore one must surely offer the supreme fee. Or else, by accomplishing whatever actions truly delight his mind, as he wishes, or please his heart, even small things become great, and this becomes the supreme fee.

If that disciple, though he has various substances that are supreme requisites, offers to the vajra master those that are poor and inferior and that he himself does not need, he will fall through that act of contempt, will always circle on the path of suffering, and will experience unwanted fruits. Therefore the qualities of the vajra master and the fruits of serving and honoring him cannot be measured, and likewise the bad karma accumulated through disrespect toward him cannot be measured either. Therefore, from the Vairocana Net of Illusion (Māyājāla):

"First of all, accomplishment
comes from following the master;
if there is no master,
accomplishment will not be attained.
Therefore the characteristics of the master are explained:
steady, disciplined, endowed with intelligence,
patient, honest, without guile or deceit,
knowing the application of the secret mantra tantras,
diligent in the work of the maṇḍala,
giving fearlessness to all sentient beings,
delighting in the Great Vehicle,
fully knowing the ten principles:
such a one is declared to be a master.
By worshipping such ones well,
buddhahood and awakening will be accomplished;
whoever reviles them
will experience suffering."

And again, in the glorious Guhyasamāja too, the master who has obtained empowerment in this tantra is taught to be the awakening mind. The awakening mind is the source of all buddhas without exception. Therefore it says that all the buddhas and bodhisattvas of the ten directions, on meeting that master, prostrate and make offerings. Furthermore, in tantras such as the glorious Candraguhyatilaka as well, many benefits of worshipping the vajra master and faults of showing him contempt are explained; since they are very elaborate, I leave them aside.

Furthermore, to the maṇḍala of the buddha Vairocana and to its master one offers a well-adorned daughter and a beloved wife, and, at a time when the vajra master has no need of them, one ransoms them back for a price. To the maṇḍala of glorious Heruka and its vajra master one offers a man with the auspicious marks, oneself or anyone else, and ransoms him back. Again, to the maṇḍala of Padmanarteśvara and its masters one offers fine pearls, jewels, a pair of white cloths joined together, and white horses, and ransoms them back. To the maṇḍala of Vajrasūrya and its masters one should offer the jewels of royal wealth, clothing and ornaments, and ransom them back.

To the maṇḍala of Paramāśva and its masters one offers horses decked with ornaments, elephants, chariots and so forth, and ransoms them back.

Then, in order to empower the disciple who has thus entered the maṇḍala, the eight verses beginning

"All the natures of the elements,
and likewise life and roots,"

are spoken. First, those who meditate on the deity of the buddha Vajrasattva, and the disciples empowered in the maṇḍala, should be marked with a jeweled crown. Its materials are those that come from all the natures of the elements, and those that come from living creatures and from roots. From these one makes a crown of five points, with jeweled shrines having doors and decked with ornaments, and in those places the five kinds of buddhas are seated, adorned with their respective attire and the signs of their mudrās; with mantra, mudrā and meditative absorption one should set them in the five places. To bind that jeweled crown there is a silk diadem with white and red streamers. Further, as a sign that the sugatas of the five families are adorned with the light of the six wisdoms, the six perfections and the six kinds of enlightened activity they perform, it is marked with six skulls on a jeweled cord, tied at the ends in the manner of a headband; and in a form similar to oneself, the lord of the family abides on the central point of the crown, like a crest jewel on the crown of the head.

Again, the crown of Vairocana is of wondrous jewels, made of a single material and a single color, with a single point; within a shrine with a door it is marked with the body of the one buddha of the family and with a single skull, adorned with various jewels, and adorned with a garland of wheels and hanging silk streamers.

If one asks, "Is it not pointless for the buddha's body to be marked with the lord of the family, that is, with himself?" there is no contradiction. Because he abides in the manner of the cause, through the blessing of the family of all the sugatas, he appears as two by virtue of the distinction of those who hold the characteristics of the fruit, emanated from the great Vajradhara for a special purpose.

That of Heruka is a jeweled crown marked with a vajra; within a shrine with doors his own body is seated, and it is marked with a garland of vajras. That of Padmanarteśvara is a lotus jewel crown marked with his own body and encircled by a garland of lotuses. For Vajrasūrya, it is an eight-cornered jeweled crown marked at its point with a blazing precious jewel; it is adorned with his own body and encircled by a garland of jewels. Since Paramāśva acts for others by way of the four families, his crown is marked with any one of these: the emblems of the five buddhas of the crown of glorious Vajrasattva, the jewel of the crown of Vairocana, the lotus of the crown of Narteśvara, the vajra of the crown of Heruka, or the jewel of the crown of Sūrya; and it is adorned with a garland of his own crossed vajras of various kinds.

Now, of the general teaching on setting out the emblems in the image maṇḍala and on the emblems of empowerment, first, by way of the maṇḍala of Vajrasattva, the passage beginning

"Universally various, fortunate one,
the seal of the emblems of the self-arisen ones,"

is spoken. In the hub at the center of the maṇḍala is the emblem of Vajrasattva: the sign that symbolizes the meaning of the expanse and wisdom, which illuminates the self-arisen wisdom of the universally various, fortunate one. It is the vajra emblem that is called by that name, because it is the symbol of him together with the four goddesses of the maṇḍala. Thus, from the Seventh Chapter:

"The great reality, the five secrets,
are held to be the five-pointed vajra."

And again:

"Through union with all the goddesses,
universally various, fortunate."

Then, since other mudrā emblems arise, the plural "ones" is used. Therefore, by the plural word one should understand the emblems of Vajrasattva: the vajra, the vajra khaṭvāṅga together with the bell, and the arrow and bow as well.

Again, those of the goddesses: on the eastern segment, the arrow and bow of Saṃvarā, marked with a five-pointed vajra. On the southern segment one should set out the emblems of the wondrous supreme bliss: the bhaga and the fortunate liṅga. On the western segment, the skull cup, khaṭvāṅga and crossed vajra of Illuminatrix. On the northern segment, within the skull cup of Disciple Lady, a crossed vajra marked at its center with the body of the buddha Amitāyus.

Again, on the outer eastern segment, the four heads of Brahmā pierced by the four arrows of Buddha Awakening Lady, which purify them. On the southern segment, the white and blue eight-spoked wheels of Dharma Wheel Lady. On the western segment, the five-pointed vajra and the triśūla of Trailokyavijayā. On the northern segment, the wish-fulfilling tree of the Mistress of Desire, adorned with flowers and shoots.

At the eastern door, the green horse head of Hayagrīva Tall Lady, emerging from the nectar within a skull cup and neighing, and a hook marked with a vajra. At the southern door, the fangs and noose of Vajramukhī, marked with a vajra. At the western door, the iron shackle of Vajralocanā marked with a vajra, and the sun and moon. At the northern door, the body of the buddha Amitābha emerging from within a skull cup filled with nectar, and a bell.

In the corners of the outer compartments are a flute, a lute, a great drum and a clay drum, each marked with a vajra. In the four corners of the inner compartments and in the outer corner sections are vases of nectar. In the four corners of the courtyard are a flower, incense, a lamp and perfumed water, each marked with a vajra: these are the mudrās of the respective goddesses.

In the maṇḍala of glorious Vairocana, at the center are a wheel, a begging bowl, the Dharma robes, a ringing staff, and a volume of the holy Dharma. On the eastern spoke of the wheel is a lotus with its stem, marked with the tongue of Buddha All-Enjoying Lady. On the southern spoke are the cymbals and drum of the Heroine. On the western spoke are the five-pointed vajra and blazing jewel of Samantabhadrī.

On the northern spoke are the jewel vessel of the Lady Holding the Inexhaustible Vessel, filled with all its own ornaments, and the conch of the Dharma. Then, in the outer eastern section is the form of the Buddha Akṣobhya, belonging to the Lady Endowed with the Buddha Body. In the southern section are the mirror and the fly-whisk of the Lady with the Beautiful Face. In the western section is the Great Cloud Lady: from a cloud disk marked with a vajra, a hail of vajras falls. In the northern section is the Lady with the Eye of Space: a lotus disk marked with an eye. The signs of the remaining twelve outer goddesses accord with the maṇḍala of Vajrasattva; the distinguishing difference is that they are marked with a wheel.

At the center of the maṇḍala of glorious Vajra Heruka are a black five-pointed vajra, a khaṭvāṅga adorned with a bell, a skull cup, and two bowls filled with the great red and the great heart. In the eastern section are Gaurī's sharp arrow marked with a vajra, and a bow. In the southern section are the hook with which Caurī draws in, a wheel, and a khaṭvāṅga. In the western section is the skull cup of the Subtle Lady, filled with liquor; and in the northern section of the wheel are Vetālī's skull cup filled with the water of nectar, and a vajra banner. In the southeastern section are Pukkasī's branch of the wish-fulfilling tree, stirred by the wind, and a five-pointed vajra. In the southwestern section are Caṇḍālī's sword marked with a vajra, and a trident (triśūla). In the northwestern section are Ghasmarī's hearth marked with a blazing vajra, and a sword. In the northeastern quarter, although the emblems of Śmaśānī are not stated explicitly, they are like the emblems of the glorious four-armed Blood Drinker: a blazing vajra, a khaṭvāṅga, a noose and a skull cup.

The emblems of the other goddesses of the retinue are also to be set out just as they appear in the root tantra: in the four corners, emblems matching the names of the goddesses Arrow Holder, Khaṭvāṅga Holder, Wheel Holder and Holder of Various Banners are to be set out. In the four corners of the courtyard, the four goddesses beginning with the Flower Lady, and at the four doors, the door goddesses, accord with the maṇḍala of Vajrasattva, except that they are adorned with wrathful vajras.

In the maṇḍala of Padmanarteśvara, at the center, seven lotus emblems should be drawn: a lotus with petals of various colors, and likewise red, green, white, blue, yellow and pink ones. On the eastern petal is the lotus of the Lady of Clear Vision, red at the heart and round in shape. On the southern petal are Tārā's mudrā of the woman's fist and a green lotus. On the western petal are the eight-pointed disk of the Earth-Holding Lady and a red lotus. On the northern petal are Bhṛkuṭī's three-pronged staff and a black lotus. Further, the words "another group of lotus signs" indicate the emblems of the outer goddesses. On the outer eastern petal are the red lotus and trident of Padmacaṇḍeśvarī. In the south are the lotus of many colors and the eight-spoked wheel of the Lady of Various Lotuses. In the west are the yellow lotus and the makara banner of the Lady with the Lotus Garment. In the north are the pink lotus and five-pointed vajra of Padmeśvarī. The twelve outer goddesses are like the earlier ones, except that they are marked with lotuses.

In the maṇḍala of Vajrasūrya, at the center are a bow, an arrow, a sword, and a sun disk with blazing firelight. In the eastern section is the Empowering Lady's five-pointed diadem marked with a wish-fulfilling jewel. In the southern section is a treasure vase. In the western section is the banner of the Lady with the Supreme Banner, marked with a precious jewel. In the northern section is the sign of offering of the Lady of Great Offering: a jewel basin filled with various jewel ornaments. The words "the compilers" indicate the signs of the four outer goddesses: signs matching the names of each of them, the Bow-and-Arrow Lady, the Sword Lady, the Sun Lady and the Fire Lady. The outer goddesses beginning with the Flute Lady are like the earlier ones, distinguished by being marked with jewels.

In the maṇḍala of Paramāśva, at the center one should draw his own eight signs and the signs of the two goddesses. The others should be differentiated according to the occasion of each activity. Those of the emblems explained above that are not clear at this point have thus been explained by supplementing them from other sources.

In order to conclude the setting out and accomplishment of these mudrā signs, the benefit of empowerment by means of them, and the mudrā of Paramāśva, it is said in common:

All the buddhas and the rest,
all things, stable and moving, without exception,
the seal of the signs of the self-arisen ones,
extend to the limits of the expanse of space.

Because this is true and undeceiving:

Thus spoke the Blessed One, the Tathāgata,
glorious Vajrasattva.

This is stated because it came forth as a condensation of these things from the great root tantra, and it is also set down because the compiler compiled the meaning just as the Teacher explained it, free from the extremes of superimposition and denial. "The mudrā of all the maṇḍalas" is so called because it seals and makes clear by means of the image maṇḍala and the signs set out in it.

This concludes the explanation of the meaning of the Seventh Chapter.

Those who know the basis to be accomplished and are directed toward the result should be equipped with the branches of the rite, in order to apply themselves to the two kinds of activity of accomplishment. Therefore this clearly teaches the mudrā of homage and the mudrā of offering.

If one asks why, when the activities such as invitation, and the various mantras and mudrās common to them, have already been taught, this in particular is set out in one place: the rite common to both paths is the act of pleasing, and that depends on homage and offering. Therefore, although they have already been explained incidentally in each place in the great root tantra, here, that is, at the time of gathering them into one place, it is fitting to set them out separately. As the root tantra says:

Of all the branches of the rite,
the activities of homage and offering
enter into every occasion;
the diligent, by engaging in them, apply them everywhere.
Through the power of pleasing the deity and the guru,
one comes to be joined with the sense pleasures and the activities.

For this reason this eighth chapter is taught in common, in the manner of a branch of all of them, and it is not assigned to any one part alone. Of this, first, in order to teach it in brief:

Then, explaining further:
of the deity, glorious Vajrasattva,
in every aspect, various and supreme,
all the offerings are emanated.

In order to please the wheel of the maṇḍala and the deities, both common and particular, such as one's own guru, from the emanation of glorious Vajrasattva all offerings of every kind, supreme among all, are emanated: the offering of homage, arising in the forms of body, speech and mind, and the mudrās of offering, by way of outer, inner and secret, are emanated by way of song, dance and the like, and so the maṇḍala is pleased. As the root tantra says:

From the emanation of Vajrasattva
many hosts of offerings are sent forth;
with mudrās of every kind
all the maṇḍalas are to be pleased.

Now, among the offerings made by way of the mudrās of homage, first, that of the maṇḍala of Vajrasattva:

The two hands, right and left,
turn the fingers and the little fingers gracefully;
the glorious left, in the manner of haughty grace;
the right should make the three-banner gesture.

So that the binding of the mudrās may be potent and powerful, after first blessing the hands, the mudrā arising from the vajra fist is turned three times in the lotus circling, in three directions, left, right and front. Then the left hand, clenched in a fist in the manner of holding a bell, is placed at the hip, and one assumes a posture of haughty grace. With the ring finger and thumb of the right hand joined, holding the vajra in the manner of the three-banner gesture, one tilts it back and places it at the heart. If one asks in what manner:

With reverence, and raised up, supreme,
oneself as glorious Vajrasattva
gazes with smiling eyes:
this is the mudrā of the stirred intention.

In the manner that arises from the nonduality of oneself and Vajrasattva, the smile born of all-pervading passion is "with reverence," and abiding there in union with the consort of one's own light is "raised up." Preceded by such a mudrā, one gazes with smiling eyes, and with stirred intention one should pay homage to the hosts of deities of the maṇḍala of glorious Vajrasattva. As the Seventh Chapter says, "The host of vajra music of illusory joy, the maṇḍala appearing to others, beautifully adorned," and so on: with the verses of homage by way of the five, body, speech, mind, qualities and activity, one should pay homage while reciting the mantra oṃ āḥ hūṃ ati pu ho bandhanaṃ karomi garupati alalaho oṃ ati hūṃ. These should also be applied extensively on the occasions of the other family maṇḍalas.

As for the benefit of paying homage in this way, two verses are spoken, beginning:

The Buddha, the Vajra, the Lotus Holder,
Vajrasūrya, by the three realms . . .

That is, the maṇḍalas of the six supramundane families, the buddhas and bodhisattvas common to the family maṇḍalas, and all the hosts of the great worldly gods, the arrogant ones, pay homage and come to perform all activities.

Further, in order to teach the mudrās of homage of the other maṇḍalas, such as that of the Buddha Vairocana, five verses are spoken, beginning:

Turning the palms of the glorious vajra,
having bowed with stirred intention . . .

First, for the maṇḍala of Vairocana, the manner is like the one above, except that the joined palms of the wheel mudrā are touched to the forehead; by reciting the words oṃ ati pu oṃ, everyone is pleased by the vast offering of the Buddha, and everything is accomplished. For Heruka, one raises the left eyebrow and, in a dance posture, makes the little fingers like fangs and places them at the mouth; by reciting oṃ ati pu hi hi ru lu ru lu hūṃ, all the great arrogant gods of the world bow to one's work like servants. For Padmanarteśvara, the palms are joined in the lotus like a bud, and the four, the thumbs and little fingers, are opened like the petals of a lotus and held together at the heart; with oṃ ati pu hri one is joined to the state in which all phenomena are pure, and one flourishes. For Vajrasūrya, preceded by a whirling dance, one brings all the limbs of the body down to the ground and joins the palms at the three places; by paying homage with oṃ ati pu traṃ, one is enriched with all the families of precious qualities. For Paramāśva, preceded by a whirling dance, one binds the mudrā of the crossed vajra in the manner of vajra haughty grace and, with smiling eyes, recites oṃ ati pu haṃ; thereby all the three realms are overpowered. This has explained the meaning of knowing the mudrās of homage.

Now, of pleasing the maṇḍala by way of the mudrās of offering, first, in order to teach the mudrā of the principal deity of the maṇḍala of Vajrasattva:

However many tantras of dance there are,
and likewise all that teach the ways of action,
those are the seal of Vajrasattva,
accomplishing one's own aims.

By way of all the movements explained in the treatises on dance, such as those called the eight soarings, the six changes, the limbed, the great word, the strength of the bee, the necklace, the lion's stretch and the play of the seven positions, together with the flute rhythms that accord with them and the rhythms of the vīṇā, the muṣṭihara and so forth, one changes through dances and various mudrās, together with the ways of action, and so one should accomplish one's own aims by way of the mudrā of Vajrasattva.

In order to teach still other ways of doing these, two verses are spoken, beginning:

Having shown the manner with haughty grace,
dancing in the way that accords with how things are . . .

In accord with the particular activity of each, such as the gathering maṇḍala, and in their specific ways, one combines the mantras, offerings, mudrās and samādhi of all the outer, inner and secret offerings, and by means of the dance technique of circling from the left side one should carry out the actions to be done. That is, one binds the mudrā of glorious Vajrasattva and, as illustrated by the mudrās that appear below, the mudrā of gazing, the mudrā of song and the rest, one should please the lord of the family.

The condensed meaning is this. Whoever has completed the stages of meditation should, on occasions such as offering to the maṇḍalas, by way of the practice, please them by: urging the maṇḍala, inviting, requesting them to be seated, paying homage, sending forth offerings, the mantras of presenting them, melodies, mudrās, summoning and absorbing the deities, making the great mudrā clear and pleased, urging the accomplishments, stabilizing with the samaya mudrā, supplicating by way of the four offerings, supplicating by way of vajra song, supplicating by way of various activities, the mudrā of the face, the mudrā of the smile, the offering of nectar and the secret offering.

As for their benefit, one verse is spoken, beginning:

If one seals with this mudrā,
one keeps company with all the buddhas . . .

Here, "keeping company with all the buddhas" means that by the power of this mudrā one is joined to the very nature of all the body, speech, mind, qualities and activity of the Sugata. This is the explanation of the mudrā called Glorious Good Fortune.

Now, in order to teach the offerings of the goddess Saṃvarā and the others, two verses are spoken, beginning:

By the union together with great pride,
raising it up . . .

With the haughty grace proper to each of the twenty goddesses and the rest, one turns through the dance technique three times, raises the hands toward the cheeks and turns them again, joins the palms and places them at the crown of the head; then, releasing them, after binding the respective mudrās, one should sing the song of hūṃ and present all the offerings. This is the explanation of the mudrās of the activities of Glorious Good Fortune, great bliss.

Now, in order to teach the offering mudrās of the other family maṇḍalas, verses are spoken, beginning:

Of the two hands, right and left,
the fingers and little fingers turned gracefully . . .

Of these, first, for Vairocana, one joins the palms in the wheel that arises from turning in the dance, turns them to the left, raises them and places them at the crown of the head, and so applies oneself to the activity with the song of oṃ. For Heruka, from turning in the dance one releases the palms upward and forms them into the mouth of Vajrayakṣa or into a skull cup; in the manner of filling it with offerings and torma, one is equipped with the attire of the charnel ground, and applies oneself to the activity by way of the fierce vajra laughter. For Padmanarteśvara, one claps three times the vajra-jewel palms that arise from circling in the dance, sings the song of hri, and applies oneself to all activities. For Sūrya, one joins the vajra-jewel palms that arise from circling in the dance, sings the song of traṃ, and applies oneself to all activities. For Paramāśva, after circling in the dance, according to the task of each activity, one binds and releases the palms of the crossed vajra, and sings the song of the mudrā and mantra of Vairocana adorned with his own heart syllable haṃ.

Now, in order to begin by way of the four offering goddesses, and to present these offerings and the rest after binding the mudrās above:

Scattering upward and pouring downward,
circling, and likewise anointing:
the offerings of flowers, incense, lamps
and perfume arise.

With the mantras and mudrās of the goddesses, one should apply oneself to these activities in such ways as these: tossing flowers upward, pouring incense downward, circling lamps toward the ten directions, and anointing the hearts of oneself and the deities with perfume. Moreover, this last verse also serves to indicate the offering goddesses.

If one asks how great the measure of these offerings should be, in number and so on:

A single one, or even a thousand,
or else with whatever one has:
by accomplishing them in harmony with the samaya,
all offerings are accomplished.

From a single vessel up to a thousand, they may be filled with jewels and hosts of offerings, or else arranged according to whatever one has. Those filled with what one actually possesses, and also the empty ones for what one does not possess, should be offered after emanating them vastly with the mind. That is to say, the practitioner who is endowed in harmony with the samaya should accomplish and present the hosts of offerings in the form of the combination of mantra, mudrā and samādhi.

As for their benefit, four verses are spoken, beginning:

If these mudrās are bound,
all the buddhas are worshipped . . .

Since they are easy to understand, they are not explained.

"The excellent union equal with all offerings, the samaya conception": since it arises from the emanation of one's own great qualities, and one offers to oneself, it is union in equality; and since one enjoys them without separation, it is called the samaya of Glorious Good Fortune.

This concludes the explanation of the meaning of the Eighth Chapter.

Even those who practice the Dharma of the path will not succeed if they are not equipped with the perfect branches of the rite; it is like the arrangement of King Horse Ear. Likewise, secret mantra whose branches are incomplete, and actions in which the interdependent factors have not come together, are like the arrangement of the brahmin woman Excellent One. Therefore the results to be accomplished depend on a perfect cause and perfect conditions. Even well-built chariots cannot reach their destination without relying on horses; and by the power of truth even deceptive acts are accomplished, like the feast of the magician Eater. For this reason, one engages in all activities by being equipped with the twenty-seven mudrās, and they become aids through which all their activities come about. As the root tantra says:

Among the actions to be accomplished,
even what is to be undertaken by a practice one is familiar with,
if causes, conditions and interdependence have not come together,
and it is done by adverse means,
like the peacock and the śirīṣa,
though practiced for an eon, it will not succeed.
The means for ripening and liberating,
the conduct of the branches of the means,
and the power of causes, conditions and the rest coming together:
by these the desired result is accomplished in the application of the act.
In whatever maṇḍalas of mudrā,
if one has not accomplished them through the host of mudrās,
the fruit of good conduct will not arise;
therefore one engages with them complete.

Therefore, to accomplish whatever path is to be accomplished, one must act with the twenty-seven mudrās. As the Seventh Chapter says:

The clear stages of mudrā,
filled with actions such as approach and accomplishment,
are the path to be accomplished;
when that is clear, one applies oneself to the action of the result.
The twenty-seven assembled branches
are widely known as the actions of mudrā;
in particular, they are explained
as the branches and aids for engaging in each specific action.

The occasions on which these come in have already been explained above. In summary, they should be understood as four mudrās. The first, sixth, seventh and eighth are the great mudrā. The second, third, fourth, sixteenth and nineteenth are the samaya mudrā. The ninth, tenth, eleventh, thirteenth and eighteenth are the Dharma mudrā. The fifth, twelfth, fourteenth, fifteenth and seventeenth, and from the twentieth up to the last, are the action mudrā.

As for the way their words are connected: first, since one must rely on the body of the great mudrā, the mudrā from which everything arises is taught. Having sealed with that, one must next rely on the samaya mudrā, so the second is taught. Since that in turn arises in dependence on the mudrā of the great samaya, the third is taught. The basis of these is the mudrā of union, and since one must be endowed with it, the fourth is taught. In order to carry out actions through it by way of various unions, the fifth is taught. In order to know how to accomplish actions through that, one must apply it with the distinctions of the attire of others, the stages of smiling and the particular gazes, so the sixth, seventh and eighth are taught. In order to purify speech through that and to do recitation and the like, and offering, praise and the like, the ninth, tenth and eleventh are spoken. In order to please the deities through that, one must undertake the dance mudrā, vajra song, offering and torma, so the twelfth, thirteenth, fourteenth and fifteenth are taught. Since, after pleasing them with offerings in this way, one must empower the deities and the disciples, the sixteenth is taught. Since through that one must apply oneself to the activities of accomplishment and to activities such as protection, the seventeenth and eighteenth are taught. In order to engage in action by abiding in the signs of the symbols, the nineteenth is taught. In order to apply it also to other kinds of accomplishment and action, the twentieth up to the twenty-seventh are taught. As the root tantra says:

All the various mudrās without exception
are elaborations of the four mudrās;
their twenty-seven branches
teach the rites of action.
The accomplishing of all the siddhis
and the vast rites of activity
are made clear and accomplished
by the twenty-seven mudrās.
The various nondual maṇḍalas are clear;
devoted to the various samayas,
by reciting the mantras of the various dharmas,
one practices the various activities everywhere.
Through union equal with all the buddhas,
the supreme samaya of all the buddhas,
the abode of all the conduct of Ḍākinī Māyā,
the rites of action are clearly accomplished.
Through the power of applying oneself to one's own action,
they are accomplished by the clarity of the wisdom of mudrā.

Therefore all the elaborations of mudrā are included in the twenty-seven mudrās, and these are included in the four kinds of mudrā. Hence the Seventh Chapter also says:

All the elaborations of mudrā and the rest without exception
are taught to be included in the four mudrās.

Of these, first, in order to teach briefly the characteristic of the great mudrā:

Then, explaining further:
the deity, glorious Vajrasattva,
emanates all the mudrās
of the supreme ones, various in every way.

There is a further distinct characteristic, different from the earlier branches of accomplishing the maṇḍala of mudrā. First, the various mudrās from which arise all the particulars of each deity maṇḍala, such as the colors of the bodies, the attire, the ornaments, the postures and the hand emblems, are the appearance of glorious Vajrasattva. If one asks in what way: he emanates all the mudrās, endowed with attire various in every way and supreme.

Now, in particular, among the characteristics of the mudrā from which all of the six maṇḍalas arise, in order to teach the characteristic of the principal deity of the maṇḍala of Vajrasattva:

The sattva, the very reality of all buddhas,
Vajrasattva, supreme in bliss,
in the delight of the supreme secret,
the self of all, abides forever.

Glorious Vajrasattva arises from the appearance of the Buddha, the great Vajradhara himself, in whom the three bodies are nondual, and he abides as the very self of the six families.

His characteristics, such as the color of his body, are these:

Because he delights in every bliss
and because all siddhis are pure,
Vajrasattva, supreme in bliss,
has a body like the full moon, tinged with red.

Because he enjoys the unsurpassed Dharma of bliss and mental ease, he has a red radiance in the guise of great passion; and because he enjoys the two kinds of bodhicitta, in which all siddhis are pure, his accumulations are perfected, so that he has a face white and full like the moon of the sixteenth day. As the Seventh Chapter says:

Because the sacred syllable is accomplished,
because all dharmas are completely pure,
and because that very secret is pure,
Vajradhara is the color of the moon.
Why is the Blessed One of great passion?
Because he is devoted to every passion,
that same Vajradhara
has a blazing color of various reds.

In order to rule out other appearances of his light:

Because he is passionate toward every passion,
the Blessed One of great passion,
this mudrā of Vajrasattva,
has light blazing in various reds.

Because with great compassion he regards all the beings of the trichiliocosm without partiality and acts for their benefit, glorious Vajrasattva is beautiful in the mudrā with four faces and four arms; and from the light rays of various colors that radiate from his body, through the maṇḍalas of the six families, he displays bodies with ornaments and attire that set others to their activities. As the root tantra says:

With the light of the sun rising on a dustless snow mountain,
beautiful with garlands of rays of the five jewels,
a graceful, delighted, passionate face with smiling eyes,
with a radiance white, blue, golden and red;
many faces and four arms, full of grace and laughter.
In the right hand a blazing five-pointed vajra,
brandished with pride and held at the heart;
in the left a khaṭvāṅga with a crossed vajra
and a bell with a banner, ringing;
the two lower hands hold arrow and bow.
He is beautiful with the five mudrās, the marks and the signs,
an upper garment of white silk with golden patterns,
a lower garment of tsegu cloth,
a belt of jeweled garlands,
choker, necklace and armlets,
anklets, bracelets and a pearl necklace.

Now, in order to mark that Buddha with the crown of the five families, the lord of the family and the garland of skulls, three verses are spoken, beginning:

In the three realms there is no other jewel
like the jewel of the Buddha . . .

In the worlds of the trichiliocosm, the supreme of all riches is the precious jewel, because it is rare, a source of every good quality, and precious. Although such jewels can produce defiled substances, they are not of the nature of undefiled wisdom, and so they are conditioned substances. Because it is distinguished from them, it is the precious and supreme Jewel. Moreover, the buddhas as conceived in the common vehicle have not entered the maṇḍala of secret mantra and are not endowed with the samaya; therefore they have no crown ornament, for they have not experienced the wisdom of great bliss. Here, as the result of the empowerment by the goddess in the maṇḍala of secret mantra, the head is adorned with the lord of the family. As the Seventh Chapter says:

When one has been invited into the maṇḍala, bathed by the goddess,
and the three stains are purified,
one should contemplate the result of the empowerment:
the crest jewel of the Buddha.

And this is not like the jewels of others; it arises from the appearance of one's own undefiled wisdom.

Then the stream of water from the goddess's vase of nectar arises from the light of the nondual maṇḍala of deities. Its essence is the wisdom of emptiness, and because it perfectly accomplishes all the hosts of signs, it is like a stream of water. Through the power of the accumulations of merit and wisdom, and through the very essence of the aspiration of a pure superior intention, its spontaneously accomplished nature makes the result clear. Marked with the signs of the six wisdoms and the six families, it fills all the worlds of the trichiliocosm with the maṇḍalas of the buddhas.

As for their benefit, three verses are spoken, beginning:

Glorious Vajrasattva in union,
joined with all the goddesses . . .

Since they are easy to understand, they are not explained. "Thoroughly joined in supreme joy" means that he is characterized by abiding in the guise of the great mudrā by way of union and the rest. This explains, from the mudrā called "Glorious Good Fortune, Vajrasattva's play of supreme bliss," the nature of the principal deity and his characteristics, such as the color of his body.

Now, in order to explain the cross-legged posture of the nondual play of father and mother:

By the union of right and left,
dancing in the guise of bliss,
when seated in the cross-legged posture of the sattva,
he utterly vanquishes all the hosts.

Glorious Vajrasattva, father and mother in union, with right and left legs crossed, by the union of absorption in nondual play, dances in the guise of bliss and with the hosts of deeds of the two activities, the radiating and gathering of light rays; and abiding in the cross-legged posture of the sattva, he vanquishes all the hosts of Māra. If one asks how: the principal deity turns the soles of his right and left feet up onto his thighs and places the mother on his lap, and with her legs she embraces the method. This is the explanation of the cross-legged posture of glorious Vajrasattva.

Now, among the characteristics of the great mudrā of the goddesses of the maṇḍala, in order to teach the distinctions of color they share, four verses are spoken, beginning:

A moon-colored, smiling face;
because all siddhis are pure
and because the passion of the lotus is completely pure,
the middle of the thighs is white and red.

In this tantra, which gathers all the goddesses in harmony, their colors are taught as alike. For the sake of the full development of the two accumulations of merit and wisdom and the purity of all siddhis, the disk of the face resembles the full moon and has the radiance of a smiling countenance. The inside of the two thighs is white tinged with red, as a sign of possessing the two kinds of bodhicitta. The hands act without wavering from any of the activities, and as a sign of having mastered the enjoyment of that greatness, they shift like rainbow light, like the leaves of the bramata twisted together. Likewise the two calves of the legs are blue.

Because, as the lord of bliss, he is joined with the entire assembly of qualities, and in the guise of great bliss displays all the desires of himself and others, the backs of the thighs and the four sides are white with a yellowish radiance. From his body radiate light rays of various colors, and from their tips the maṇḍalas of the six buddhas fill all the world systems of the trichiliocosm; in this way he displays hosts of divine substances having the colors of jewels. These too abide on lotus-disk seats in the samādhi in which all phenomena are completely pure. Their ornaments, on the head and elsewhere, are adorned with the signs of the five buddhas, and they are adorned with various jewel ornaments and the like. With the light of their bodies they make all the worlds of the trichiliocosm flourish, just as, for example, the Blessed One (the Buddha), entering the state of the array of light rays, filled these Sahā worlds with various buddha maṇḍalas and in an instant tamed the eight classes of gods and spirits who were to be trained.

Now, among the separate teachings on the characteristics of the particular goddesses, first, in order to teach the goddess Saṃvarā:

Performing, averting,
and seizing too she does supremely.
Through the mudrā of embracing the self,
the seizing of all the limbs is accomplished.

By drawing the bow and arrow of calm abiding and insight joined as a pair, she rouses the heart of the Lord, and in the manner of passion she embraces the consort of her own light. As for their benefits, a single verse is spoken, beginning:

If one forms this mudrā,
one soars, going and coming elsewhere at will,

and it is easy to understand. The name "Glorious Vajra Arrow, Play of Supreme Bliss" is given by way of her hand emblem.

Again, in order to teach the other goddesses, it says, beginning:

Passionate in the pledge in various ways,
by performing the dance in which bliss descends,

How wonderful! The Bliss Lady, indicated by the sign of the pledge of the Passionate Lady, holds the marks of the bhaga and the liṅga and embraces the consort of her own light. Vairocanī leans her back against a khaṭvāṅga as against a support, embraces the consort of her own light with her right hand, and holds a crossed vajra with her left. Disciple Lady embraces the consort of her own light with her right hand and with her left holds a skull cup containing the body of a buddha. Regarding these goddesses, the words "the great supreme deity of the center" mean that they are entwined with the male consort of their own light, who is like Vajrasattva, and are included within the inner vajra palace. For that reason they are also called the root assembly of mudrās. The benefits of binding the mudrās, taught as common and as special, are easy to understand.

Again, in order to teach the other, outer goddesses, it says, beginning:

In accordance with the scriptures, just as they are,
through glorious, perfect and vast dances,

These are the mudrās of the four goddesses beginning with Buddha Awakening Lady, the four beginning with Hayagrīva Tall Lady, the four beginning with Flute Lady, and the four beginning with Flower Lady. The way they are bound has already been clearly taught above, and their pure suchness is clearly taught in the fifth rite, so it will not be elaborated here. In order to teach their benefits all together, it says, beginning:

These and others, infinite and supreme,
equal to the dharmadhātu and unequaled,

Again, in order to teach the songs of exhortation, the two verses beginning

Of the Union with All Buddhas,
of Ḍākinī Māyā and Saṃvara,

teach the purpose and the characteristics of the songs; and then, in order to exhort with verses, it says, beginning:

Vajrasattva, glorious and fortunate one, you are my very self: accomplish us and bring us to realization, I pray!
O you who are the nature of all tathāgatas, bliss, Ḍākinī Māyā, supreme bliss of every kind!

This means: "Glorious Vajrasattva, who by the method of the dance, together with the play of the mudrā of bliss, enjoys the fortunate goddess, abides inseparable from my own nature; therefore, I pray, make clear in my continuum those great qualities of that very state which are not yet clear." And again: "You are the nature of all the goddesses of the retinue, the bliss of all tathāgatas, the varied bliss of Ḍākinī Māyā, and the accomplishment of the supreme in taming in various ways." By way of the dance of various unions, suchness is taught; the four kinds of mantra indicate speech, the four pledges of enlightened activity indicate mind, and its qualities are taught together with their results. This explains the meaning of the words "the mudrās from which all of the maṇḍala of glorious Vajrasattva arises."

Now, in order to teach the mudrās from which all the deities of the maṇḍala of glorious Vairocana arise, the passage beginning

That very yoga, the Blessed One,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,

teaches the general characteristics of the maṇḍala's host of deities, their body colors, their nature, their hand emblems, their ornaments and attire, their activities, their cross-legged postures, the characteristics of their retinues, and the different kinds of songs. Among these, the characteristics of the central lord are:

In order to purify all liberations,
he abides as the glory of the buddha mudrā.

In order to purify the śrāvakas and others who abide in the aim of renunciation from existence by the mundane and supramundane paths, glorious Vairocana, who displays the mudrā of great bliss, emanates in the guise of a universal monarch (cakravartin) or in the guise of the Blessed One Śākyamuni. His body color is described thus:

Because all aims are pure,
like the color of refined gold;
because of passionlessness and the rest, and great passion,
white, with a clear radiance of many colors.

Since he has realized the wisdom of the dharmadhātu and joins all phenomena of saṃsāra and nirvāṇa into the spontaneously present essence of the six bodies, he is not changed by other conceptual thoughts of signs; therefore the main body and the central face are yellow. Again, in accordance with the inclinations of those to be trained, the disk of the body is also taught as white, with the right face yellow. Because he has abandoned all afflicted conceptions, such as that of a self, he is without attachment, hence the white aspect; because he possesses various activities, hence the green aspect; and because he has mastery over all phenomena of great passion, he abides in clear red; thus he has four faces.

By his four kinds of mudrā he holds the mudrās of his own emblems and is emanated, beautified with ornaments and attire. As the root tantra says:

Four faces, yellow, white, green and red in color,
a body colored like a snow mountain at sunrise;
in his hands he holds the Dharma robes, the alms bowl,
the ringing staff, and a volume of the holy Dharma.
In the guise of a universal monarch,
beautified by a wealth of enjoyments,
beyond the common ornaments of Brahmā,
he is adorned with distinctive signs.

Again, by way of the jewel ornaments:

For fully establishing the kingdom of Dharma,
there is no jewel greater than the Dharma.
Therefore the bodhicitta of the Great Vehicle:
the Victor possesses the vajra jewel.

That is, he overwhelms all the enjoyments of worldly kingship with the kingdom of Dharma, and overwhelms all hosts of jewels with the bodhicitta of the unsurpassed Dharma of the Great Vehicle; as a sign of possessing it, he is adorned with a jewel crown and jewel ornaments.

His karma mudrā is:

The thumb and middle finger of the right hand
touching all the fingers
of the left hand clenched in a fist:
this is known as the root mudrā.
This is the clear perfection of wisdom
of all the buddhas.

He binds the mudrā in the manner of turning the wheel of Dharma, and so, by making clear the entire meaning of the Perfection of Wisdom, of phenomena and of the nature of reality, he fully establishes the kingdom of Dharma.

As for the activities they perform, two and a half verses are spoken, beginning:

Giving birth to awakening in beings,
and turning the wheel of Dharma,

By way of making the mudrā clear, he generates vast bodhicitta and with the wheel of Dharma cuts off all adverse factors; then he joins beings to the vast way of the Perfection of Wisdom and makes clear the suchness of the result.

In order to teach their benefits, one and a half verses are spoken, beginning:

Whoever sees this one, so endowed,
and worships in every way,

If, by making the maṇḍala of samādhi clear, one meditates one-pointedly and worships, it is like seeing and worshiping all the wisdom maṇḍalas. As soon as one has generated the mind of awakening and entered the path, the result of buddhahood is accomplished, and one turns the wheel of Dharma.

The mudrā called Glorious Pledge: because glorious Vairocana does not abandon all the phenomena of saṃsāra but enjoys them, and so destroys the one-sided nirvāṇa of peace, he abides in the manner of the great mudrā that indicates his own suchness.

In order to teach the cross-legged posture of the father and mother:

The right limb pressing down
is victorious over every left limb.
When one assumes the sattva cross-legged posture,
all the māras are utterly destroyed.

The right thighs of the father and mother each press down on their own left thighs. Having assumed the cross-legged posture with the feet crossed, they enter the nondual union, and so tame those who abide in the extremes of existence and of peace. The name "glorious Vajrasattva posture" is given because it resembles the cross-legged posture of Vajrasattva. This completes the explanation of the characteristics of the lord.

Now, concerning the characteristics of the goddesses of the retinue, the qualities of their mudrās are taught, and below, the names of the goddesses are to be applied and understood. Of these, the passage beginning

Having clenched the vajra fist,
and covering it with the left hand,

teaches the binding of the mudrās of the eight special goddesses. Preceded by a turning dance, with their right hands they form their own fists as taught, and they cover them with the palm of the left hand.

Among these, the vajra fist belongs to the goddess Lady Who Proclaims All Buddhas. From a clenched fist, the tip of the thumb is inserted between the ring finger and the little finger, and the index finger is extended and bent slightly. As it says:

At the middle joint of ring finger and little finger,
the thumb should be placed well;
then, having clenched the fist,
the index finger should be bent well.

The goddess Glorious Lady has the Heruka fist:

From the clenched vajra fist,
the two little fingers are fully extended,

so that they are made like fangs. Samantabhadrī has the fist of the lotus woman:

From the clenched striking fist,
the middle fingers are placed on the thumbs.

That is, from a clenched fist the tips of the thumb and the middle finger are joined and made like the petals of a lotus.

She who holds the unbearable vessel
has the jewel-holding fist.

From the previous one, the middle fingers are joined and the joints are made angular. As it says:

The jewel-holding fist
is the fist of the supreme vajra jewel.

The Buddha Lady of Seven Limbs has the clenched buddha fist:

Having clenched the vajra fist well,
insert your own thumb inside.

The Fair-Faced Form Lady has the woman mudrā: the fist is raised at the heart, made in the manner of holding a mirror. As it says:

The fist bound at the heart
is there called the woman mudrā.

Great Cloud Lady has the fist of Paramāśva:

From the clenched vajra fist,
all the fingers are fully gripped;
all heroic deeds
are accomplished entirely, without exception.

Lady with the Eye of Space has a fist like the fist of the Glorious Accomplished Eye:

With the index finger pressing the thumb,
press the index finger with your own thumb:
this is the mudrā of the Glorious Accomplished Eye,
with the index finger as the pupil of the eye.

Within the clenched fist of each of these goddesses her own hand emblem is contained. The fists of the six families are explained in the section called the mudrās of all actions; the woman mudrā is explained among the mudrās of the Lord of Siddhis; and the mudrā of the Glorious Accomplished One is taught among the pledge mudrās. At the time of binding them, by reciting each one's mantra and vividly meditating on each one's body, one seals them with the great mudrā. The goddesses Flute Lady and the rest bind their mudrās as explained for the maṇḍala of Vajrasattva, and their respective mantras are recited together with the word "cakra." The method should be understood in the same way for the subsequent maṇḍalas as well.

Now, among the songs of exhortation, first the general characteristic. One and a half verses are spoken, beginning:

Of these who know the mudrās,
of the supreme song of the buddhas' emanation,

As for the mudrās given above, from knowing the meaning of those that come below, one sings the song of the buddhas' emanation, and by the power of the exhortation everything is accomplished; from the cessation of everything, one is joined to everything. By this, even those attached to the path of nirvāṇa are roused, and since it accomplishes the display of the mudrās of body, speech and mind, one should sing thick songs, thin ones and the rest, melodies with trills and turns that combine the four kinds well, and tones like the voice of Brahmā.

The actual songs of exhortation: one verse is spoken, beginning:

In many world systems, countless as atoms, elaborated in particular,
the aims of the Victors, as numerous as the subtlest atoms,

This means: "In all the vast arrays of world systems, set crosswise, upside down, above and below, facing one another, and so on, you act for the benefit of all sentient beings through various deeds suited to them," and "Join me to those modes of activity in the form of a single instant, and bring them to accomplishment!" Their benefit is spoken thus:

By singing these songs,
the union with all of space,
Ḍākinī Māyā, supreme bliss,
will be accomplished, without doubt.

Thus, in order to make offerings after the exhortation, the passage beginning

Then, with the four women
and by one's own true secret,

means that the four offering goddesses, and the outer, inner and secret offerings emanated by samādhi, are spread out vastly and offered. This explains the meaning of knowing the mudrās of the Tathāgata.

Now, from the great mudrā of the maṇḍala of glorious Heruka, first the general characteristic:

That very yoga, the Blessed One,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
in order to purify those hostile to the pledge,
appears clearly in the mudrā of the charnel-ground dance.

Out of the great qualities of glorious Vajrasattva, in order to destroy Rudra and the others who are hostile to the pledge, glorious Heruka should be made to possess the attire of the eight great charnel-ground ornaments and the like.

Now, among the specific characteristics: for what purpose is the body of the lord taught in this way? For the sake of those toward whom compassion is generated:

For the extremely vicious and fierce,
peaceful means will bring no benefit.

As the antidote to that, the characteristic of great compassion is:

Of the yoga of wisdom and method,
the yoga becomes holy supreme bliss (Saṃvara).

The signs of his attire are given in the passage beginning:

The wrathful one, arisen from the Buddha's wrath,
is supreme in destroying all to ash,

Of his eight arms, the two below the first pair hold the skin of Maheśvara (Śiva) stretched out; the third pair hold arrow and bow; the fourth pair hold skull cups filled with great flesh and, under the term "honey," with great blood; and the first two hold the vajra and the khaṭvāṅga that are mentioned below. The meaning of the rest is easy to understand and so is not explained. His activity is given in the passage beginning:

By emanating the various mudrās
together with glorious Heruka,

He is called Padmanarteśvara because glorious Heruka masters all phenomena through various methods of dance.

In order to teach the characteristics of the father and mother, the passage beginning

In the manner of resting against a support,
leaning back against a khaṭvāṅga,

also makes known the consort's body color, her ornaments and attire, and her hand emblems. The qualities of his mudrā are spoken in the passage beginning:

Women and other supreme jewels,
all things he draws forth from the sky,

and this is easy to understand. The characteristics of the cross-legged posture of father and mother are:

Through the union of right and left,
by performing the dance in which bliss descends,
in the half cross-legged posture,
pressing the foot with one's own thigh,

That is, rising up with a stately bearing, the father embraces the mother's thigh with his right foot and extends his left, placing it on the ground; and the mother likewise abides embracing him with her foot in the half cross-legged posture. Their activities, together with their qualities, are given in the passage beginning:

Rudra, Indra, and Upendra,
the sun and moon, Yama, and Brahmā,

The words "united in blissful touch, the fortunate, glorious Vajra Fierce One abides" mean that he is united with emanations from the light rays of the nondual bodhicitta, and by that very activity destroys the hosts of the fierce ones.

Now, among the goddesses of the retinue, first the general characteristic:

Then, further, it is fully explained:
in the maṇḍala of the Buddha Ḍākinī,
together with glorious Heruka,
the various mudrās abide.

The characteristics of the goddesses, apart from the attire of the lord, abide in the manner of various mudrās. First the attire of their bodies is taught, and they abide in variety by way of distinctions of gaze, distinctions of hand emblems, distinctions of dance methods, and distinctions of names. Of these, first the distinction of body:

Peaceful, fierce, supreme in bewildering,
dancing in joy, together with transforming,
through the practice of raising the dead,
they abide fully in the mudrā of bliss.

Here the word "abide" teaches the distinctions of their faces and their manner of sitting. Gaurī is the Sealing Lady; she has a peaceful face and sits in that same manner. In the same way, apply this to both face and manner of sitting. Caurī is called the Heart-Drawing Lady, in the fierce manner. Pramohā is the Bewildering Lady, in that same manner. Pukkasī is called the Dancing Lady, having the manner of joy. Ghasmarī is the Striking Lady, in that same manner. Vetālī is the Slaying Lady and sits in the manner of transformation. Śmaśānī is the Slaying Lady and sits in the manner of rousing the dead. Caṇḍālī is the Profound One and sits in the manner of the dead rising. Thus all the goddesses, too, are inseparable from their respective male consorts and enjoy bliss; therefore they are the mudrās of bliss.

Again, in order to teach the distinctions of the goddesses' gazes:

Peaceful, fierce, and bewildering,
upward, breathing out, dancing, the body:
the gaze mudrās, supreme of accomplishment,
by that supreme one of secret union.

Of these, the peaceful gaze has the eyes half closed, like an elephant in the state of mating. The fierce gaze is in the manner of angry eyes. The bewildered gaze looks about unclearly, like one drunk on liquor. Raising the dead is looking slightly upward. The manner of breathing out is looking toward the ten directions. Dancing is looking alternately to one's right and left. Distraction is looking as though planting kīlas in the ten directions. Eating corpses is looking forward again and again. "Supreme of accomplishment" means applying those gazes to their activities and abiding in the supreme bliss of secret union.

The distinction of hand emblems: five verses are spoken, beginning:

Supreme, joined with flowers and incense,
liquor, water, and branches,

The first teaches the eight signs of offering and seals what is to be accomplished. The second seals the activities. The third indicates the sequence of the attire. The fourth and fifth teach the hand emblems of activity.

The distinction of names: two verses are spoken, beginning:

The first is the seal of the Sealing Lady;
the second is that of the Heart-Drawing Lady,

Again, the mudrās together with their responses are given in the passage beginning:

Then, by all these mudrās,
the responses to the mudrās are unsurpassed,

That is, so as not to be overcome by the practices of others and the like, and in order to accomplish all of one's own siddhis, one should bind the mudrā of glorious Heruka and the mudrās of the goddesses of the retinue in their proper order, and apply them to all activities.

In order to please the deities of the maṇḍala and to apply them to their own activities, among the exhortations by song, first the general characteristic:

Then this knowing of the mudrās,
the song of the union of the glorious vajra,
which fully accomplishes all activities,
I shall explain in particular.

This is the singing of a song that clearly shows one's own suchness, in the manner of joining nondually with the mudrā of glorious Heruka and accomplishing all activities. Of these, first, in order to exhort by way of the four faces of the lord:

Having gained the seal that transforms into gazes of every kind,
you bewilder by kissing and embracing of every kind;
please partake of all the feasts of offering and bliss of every kind,
and please show the wonders of supreme bliss of every kind.

The first face, by way of fierceness, seals the transformation of various gazes; the second face, by way of bewilderment, kisses and embraces; the third face, by way of devouring, partakes of all offerings and praises; and the fourth face, by way of charm, shows wonders.

Now the songs of exhortation by the eight goddesses of the retinue are spoken:

Seal, bewilder, and please partake!
Supreme bliss, wondrous, abide in bliss!

This is that of the four inner goddesses.

hūṃ a la la ho, make it manifest!
The yoga of supreme bliss, emanated by illusion!

This is the activity of the four inner goddesses.

Please enter the pledge!
Be victorious over all afflictions!
Please partake of all without remainder!
Clear away all afflictions!

This is that of the four outer goddesses. The benefits are easy to understand. This explains the meaning of the words "the vajra mudrā of glorious Heruka."

Now, from the great mudrā of the maṇḍala of glorious Padmanarteśvara, first the general characteristic:

That very yoga, the Blessed One,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
in order to purify all desire,
abides as the glory of the lotus mudrā.

That great mudrā possessing the characteristics of the lotus, which serves to purify those who are driven by the view that grasps at a self and who cling to the two extremes of permanence and annihilation, is what is so called. Of these, in order to teach the purity of the lord's body color:

Because of unchanging, holy purity,
pure like a sphere of crystal;
because all desire is purified,
a light of manifold blazes massed together.

He is not changed by other discordant signs or by the habitual tendencies of conceptual thought; because he has mastered the complete purity of all phenomena, from form up to omniscience, he has the radiance of a crystal jewel. Because by the great compassion of non-abiding nirvāṇa he knows the methods for training many disciples and displays deeds suited to them, a radiance of twenty-five colors shines forth.

Now, in order to teach the ornaments and signs, it says, beginning:

Adorned with the jewel of the Buddha of Life,
ornamented with garments and various ornaments,
