So that the sign of the family does not change, and in order to seal the result, he is adorned with the Buddha Amitāyus, a deed in keeping with the family. For the sake of his conduct, he is adorned with various garments and ornaments, and with sugatas arising from the light rays of his body he fills the trichiliocosm. Further, he is marked with mudrās from twenty-five faces, or with four faces marked by fifty hands, or marked by eight and four; the lotuses in the right hands are marked with a vajra, a wheel, a jewel, a sword, a trident, a staff, a pestle, the eight auspicious signs and so on, while the left hands hold plain lotuses. Through these he emanates the maṇḍala of the family, and through the lotus ḍākinīs he displays various methods of taming by illusion and establishes beings in the awakening of the buddhas.

The characteristics of the cross-legged posture in which father and mother sit with faces joined:

Through the union of right and left,
abiding in the half cross-legged posture,
having assumed the royal posture,
he dispels all suffering.

On a lotus-flower seat, the right leg is extended, resting on the ground, and the left is drawn in, in the cross-legged manner; thus he abides with his face joined to the consort's. This is the explanation of the mudrā called "Glorious Lotus King Abiding."

Further, the characteristic of the great mudrā of the goddesses of the retinue is spoken in the passage beginning:

With the proud left hand,
a red lotus is held at ease,

These eight goddesses hold in their left hands lotuses matching their respective colors, abiding with a stately bearing, and their right hands bear the attire of the dance of their respective mudrās.

Of these, first, the goddess All-Seeing Lady holds a red lotus at ease with her left hand, and with her right, in the tongs mudrā with the tips of thumb and index finger joined, she draws it to her heart and opens it. Thus she is "the pure glory of the All-Seeing," enjoying the pure Dharma. Tārā holds a blue lotus and draws it to her heart with the woman's fist. Thus she has the mudrā of enjoying what has become supreme awakening. The Adorned Lady holds a white lotus in her left hand and turns the wheel of Dharma with her right. Thus with the wheel of Dharma she cuts the wheel of saṃsāra. Bhṛkuṭī holds a dark-blue lotus in her left hand and dances with her right in the three-banner mudrā. Because by this very means she turns back the mudrās of others, it is called "the response to mudrās." Padmacaṇḍeśvarī holds a lotus in her left hand, resting it proudly against her body, and holds a jewel in her right. Because by this she fulfills all the desires of others, it says, "Padmacaṇḍeśvarī, supreme jewel." The Lady of Varied Lotuses raises a green lotus in her left hand and holds a trident in her right; because she conquers others with the three activities, it says, "Varied Lotus, victorious by the trident." The Lady of the Great Glorious Lotus holds a yellow lotus in her left hand and a makara banner in her right; therefore it says, "By this she arouses the one who dwells in the lotus." The Mistress of Desire holds a madder-red lotus in her left hand, takes up a three-pronged vajra in her right, and dances, bringing the beings of the three realms under her power.

Now, among the songs spoken to please them, first, the general characteristic is easy to understand. Further, in order to exhort the lord, it says, beginning:

Holding various supreme forms, of all the tathāgatas and the rest,
supreme in taming beings of every kind, possessing the supreme siddhi,

"Having the supreme forms of all and the rest" means that he displays the various bodies of buddhas, bodhisattvas, and the great haughty gods of the world, and works magic for the sake of beings. Therefore it says:

I am the Buddha Vajradhara;
I am Brahmā, Viṣṇu, and Īśvara;
I am the sun and the moon.

And in the yoga tantra Vajraśekhara too it is taught that, through the emanations of this very family, various things of saṃsāra and nirvāṇa appear in order to tame beings: "Taming beings, having every form."

Again, the song of the goddesses of the retinue:

Glorious lotus illusion,
dancing in the pure manner that tames in every way,
Lotus, Lord of the Pledge, please perform the dances of the buddhas!

By this and the rest it is taught together with its qualities. This explains the meaning of the words "the mudrā of glorious Padmanarteśvara."

Further, from the great mudrā of the maṇḍala of Vajrasūrya, first, in order to teach the general characteristic:

That very yoga, the Blessed One,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
in order to purify supreme wealth,
abides in the mudrā of the glorious sun.

In order to set those who abide on the path of avarice and are stained by corrupt moral discipline in the four kinds of giving that are completely pure in the three spheres, the one who possesses the form and the splendor of the sun is so called.

Now, among the characteristics of the lord, first the manner of abiding in a peaceful guise:

Ākāśagarbha, toward all directions,
completely fulfilling every hope,
women and the other supreme jewels,
all jewels, he rains down from the sky.

He who, in a body of space, abides like a mountain of gold, and from every direction rains down like rain the seven, the precious queen and the rest, which fulfill the hopes of beings, is Ākāśagarbha himself. Beyond that, being shown in a wrathful guise in particular in order to tame the hosts of the fierce is spoken in the passage beginning:

For the extremely vicious and fierce,
peaceful means will bring no benefit,

Possessing the light rays of a hundred thousand suns, he dispels the torment of others; possessing various fierce attire, his body abides red and yellow like a blazing sun disk, and through various fierce methods he tames them.

In order to teach the distinctions of their hand emblems and the rest, the passage beginning

The vajra bow and the supreme arrow,
held with a most elegant bearing,

teaches that the four hands hold a bow, an arrow, a sun and a sword; with a smiling face he lifts his left eye upward and gazes at the rain of the seven kinds of jewels falling from the sky. His activity is easy to understand.

In order to teach the distinction of his cross-legged posture, the passage beginning

Binding the two vajra knees,
having assumed the vajra cross-legged posture,

means that he binds his two knees in the shape of a precious jewel, sits with his face joined to the consort's, and destroys all obstructors.

Further, in order to teach the attire of the great mudrā by way of the characteristics of the goddesses of the retinue:

Empowering, and the Treasures,
the Supreme Banner, Great Offering:

these are the four inner goddesses.

Showing the ways just as they are,
and fully accomplishing their own aims:

these are the outer goddesses and their respective actions.

In order to please those deities of the maṇḍala and engage them in activity, among the songs of exhortation that are spoken:

Then this knowing of the mudrās,
the song of the supreme king of illusion,
which fully accomplishes all activities,
I shall explain in particular.

Having bound the various dances and the individual mudrās, one focuses on the maṇḍala of the body that has become clear, and, possessing mantra and mudrā, one should engage them in activity. Further, in order to take up the activities of the four goddesses by way of the four kinds of action, in the aspect of the four faces:

Bestowing supreme awakening on the clouds of beings, raining down every kind,
accomplishing all my hopes, show it swiftly! a la la ho

By way of the first face and the goddess Empowering Lady, the hosts of beings, like clouds, are empowered with bodhicitta. By way of the second face and the goddess Treasure Lady, various supreme jewels are rained down and given. By way of the third face and the goddess Lady with the Supreme Banner, all phenomena are overpowered, and the method that accomplishes all my hopes is swiftly shown. By way of the fourth face and the goddess Lady of Great Offering, the offerings of the four joys are emanated.

Furthermore, so that the goddesses may urge him on to the special activities, the songs and their benefits were spoken, beginning:

"Please bestow on me the special empowerment of the inexhaustible treasure.
All these special things are for the sake of the precious women."

This is the explanation of the great mudrā of glorious Vajrasūrya.

Furthermore, concerning the great mudrā of the maṇḍala of glorious Paramāśva at play:

First, the general characteristic:
that very yoga, the Blessed One,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
glorious, engaged in fierce union,
abides as the king of horses.

This is the manner in which he emanates from the glorious great Vajradhara into the form of Paramāśva.

Now, among the attire of the great mudrā, the aspect of his intention is taught by the passage beginning:

Through conviction that all is pure,
by the power of sinlessness, with head held high,

This shows that, in order to crush the arrogance of the four great gods, he possesses four kinds of enlightened activity by way of his four faces.

Furthermore, the attire of the mudrā:

Four-faced and four-armed,
four-footed with four hooves,

and so on. His four upper arms branch into eight forearms. In the left hands, clenched in fists, he holds a variegated lotus, a spear, a staff, and a bow. In the right hands he holds a triple banner raised aloft, a crossed vajra (viśvavajra), a sword, and an arrow.

The benefit of binding these mudrās is taught by the passage beginning:

If one seals with this mudrā,
even one who has utterly fallen,

and so on; it is easy to understand.

The vajra rousing: with the mudrā of the lotus and the threatening forefinger, he makes the hosts of the arrogant faint, and then revive again.

His stance is taught by the passage beginning:

Through the arrangement of right and left,
in the manner of extended and bent,

and so on. With the first of his extended right legs he presses down the goddess Accomplished by Three and the Glorious Lady. With the second he presses down Tārā and Rati. With the first of his bent left legs he presses down the Three-Eyed One (Śiva) and Viṣṇu. With the second he presses down Brahmā and the divine son Kāmadeva.

The wrathful abiding: he presses them down with the extended and bent stance in the attire of the great wrathful one.

The song urging him is the passage beginning:

Then this, called the mudrā,
the song of glorious Paramāśva's abiding,

and so on. In order to tame those who dwell in passion with the goddesses of desire, more drunk with desire than a thousand mad elephants, and to establish them in bliss, the first face is shown as amorous. In order to tame Viṣṇu, who kindles the fire of anger and whose character is more terrifying than a thousand Yamas, the second face is shown as fierce. In order to tame Śakra (Indra), whose peaceful form bears a countenance surpassing a thousand Brahmās, the third face is shown in a peaceful manner. In order to tame by the power of the boldness that comes from conviction that all is pure, the fourth face, the horse's head with its wrathful frown, sends forth a delighted laugh, sounding aloud with the power that overwhelms opponents.

Furthermore, by way of his four feet he presses down the four great gods and enjoys their wives.

Now, the wife of Maheśvara was produced from an eye. When Śakra, lord of the gods, was holding a great sacrifice, the other gods gathered first without being summoned, and only after they had assembled was Maheśvara invited. "What fault of mine is it, that I am called last of all?" he said, speaking harsh and violent words, and in his anger he made an extra eye appear on his forehead. By its mere glance it sent forth into the world many things: diseases of every kind, famine, rains of venomous weapons, strife, and the like, and he harmed all the beings in the world.

To tame him, the Buddha Amitābha, from the immeasurable light of the rays of his body, made appear an emanation of the wrathful protector Acala. This stupefied the mind of the Great God (Śiva) and subdued his three doors. Disheartened, he shed tears, and through the power of his own aspiration they became the goddess Accomplished by Three. While he was enjoying her, she was carried off by the might of Paramāśva.

Furthermore, Viṣṇu emanated the goddess of his own aspiration. Brahmā emanated a goddess through the power of yoga. And Kāmadeva gathered the luster of flowers and the luster of others, and as passion stirred in his mind he increased his own luster; from this the goddess was produced. In this way Paramāśva enjoys the Glorious Lady, Tārā, and Rati. Their stories are told in the Tantra of the Array of Speech.

Someone may say: "Does it not seem contradictory that, according to those to be tamed, these goddesses are sometimes protected, sometimes made his retinue, sometimes made his consort, and sometimes pressed down beneath his feet?" There is no fault, because in the great miracle of wisdom they are displayed through various other methods according to how beings are to be tamed, and in order that this very point be grasped.

Again, a verse was spoken in order to urge him on by way of two goddesses. In it: just as for anyone the intent to befriend a kinsman and the intent to attack an enemy are accomplished through one-pointedness, and are not accomplished in that way when accompanied by doubt and wavering, so too a person entering the door of this Great Vehicle of secret mantra should abandon the thought of wavering. Therefore the root tantra says:

Doubt brings delusion;
actions will not be accomplished,
one will not know the occasion for engaging,
and even if one practices, there will be no fruit.

By the second it is taught: as for befriending and attacking, it would be well if such thoughts in terms of worldly affairs did not arise at all. Attachment to kin and aversion to enemies are momentary in aspect, unstable in nature, contrived, and illusory; so who could rely on them? One should not dwell on them. Whoever befriends the deity and the higher deity in his mind and practices without separation from them, and attacks the enemies of conceptual thought, the hosts that oppose the path, engages in the activity that makes the Mother's dominion firm, and joins himself to the fruit of all good qualities.

Again, by the third it is taught: in this way, as for gods, humans, and the rest, it would be well if the thought of helping or harming anyone did not arise in the first place. But if, once one has made a commitment to them, one cannot accomplish their aim, that is not acceptable even as a mere illusion, because such a one only appears to be a friend, is the reflection of a friend, an illusory friend. It is not right to leave this undesignated; who would speak of it so? Likewise, since the deities of one's commitment have no mind of attachment, aversion, clinging, or conceptualization, how could they have any thought of granting or not granting accomplishments? If they did have such a thought, who would call them deities? It would not be fitting.

By these, the characteristic of the great mudrā is taught by way of the six families; that is, the manner in which the great karmamudrā is accomplished by way of the six kinds of equal union is taught. Glorious Vajrasattva, being the nature of all the families, is called Equal Union with All Buddhas; he engages through activities of various kinds. Glorious Vairocana is called Equal Union with All Space, because he joins all phenomena to the nature of the Perfection of Wisdom and pervades them with the wisdom of the dharmadhātu. Glorious Heruka is called Equal Union with All Vajras, because with vajra-like body, speech, and mind he destroys the hosts of Rudra, and is the radiance of mirror-like wisdom. Glorious Padmanarteśvara is called Equal Union with Various Lotuses: he knows the various natures of those to be tamed, and by joining all phenomena in the completely pure expanse he possesses the mode of the wisdom of equality. Vajrasūrya is called Equal Union with All Kingship, because through the hosts of flourishing qualities he liberates the mindstreams of those who engage, and possesses the mode of discriminating wisdom. Paramāśva at play is called Equal Union with All Actions, because he pacifies the conceptions of the great gods, and, abiding as the embodiment of the four kinds of enlightened activity, joins all things through the mode of all-accomplishing wisdom.

So the root tantra says:

Through the deities of Vajrasattva,
equal union with all buddhas,
in the manner of every kind of rite,
various beings are liberated.
Through the maṇḍala of Vairocana,
equal union with all space,
in the manner of the perfection of wisdom,
the views of the lesser are set free.
Through the maṇḍala of Heruka,
equal union with all vajras,
the host of vajra body, speech, and mind
destroys the adverse forces of Rudra.
Through the maṇḍala of Padmanarteśvara,
equal union with various lotuses,
the various pure dharmas are accomplished
and conceptions of superimposition and denial are pacified.
Through the maṇḍala of Vajrasūrya,
equal union with all kingship,
the qualities of the array are increased
and the tormented are freed from the bounds of karma.
Through the maṇḍala of all actions,
equal union in various purity,
the mindstream of the Great God Rudra is tamed,
and by the power of enlightened activity all are overcome.

The ritual limbs for this are as follows. Having prostrated to the maṇḍala in front and pleased it with offerings, one performs the summoning and absorption with the four mantras, makes the body of the great mudrā clear and pleased, and after that one should sing the song that urges the accomplishments. This is the explanation of the meaning of the first mudrā, from which the body colors and so on of the entire maṇḍala of deities arise.

After urging thus with song, since the second, the samaya mudrā, must be taught, the mudrās of the maṇḍalas are taught. First, in order to teach the maṇḍala of Vajrasattva, the passage was spoken that begins:

Pressing the thumb with the index finger,
press the index finger with your own thumb,

First, turn the hands three times in a dancing gesture, interlace the fingers, and join the palms in the vajra palm. From that, dissolve the separate maṇḍalas gathered from the radiance of the syllables, make into one the vajra binding that arises from the vajra of glorious great joy, and press the root of the thumb with the index finger, making it like the eyeball. Again, from that, separate the fists, turn them three times in a dancing gesture, join the remaining fingers with the palms hollow, and display it at the place of each deity; then, reciting each one's mantra as before, body, speech, and mind are made firm. The benefit of binding the mudrā in this way is not explained, because it is easy to understand.

Furthermore, in order to teach in sequence the samaya mudrās of the five maṇḍalas of Vairocana and the rest, together with their benefits, the passage was spoken that begins:

Joining the palms evenly in the vajra palm,
bend the tips of the two index fingers,

For Vairocana's: interlace the fingers as in the vajra palm, bend the tips of the two index fingers, insert them between the two thumbs, and make it like an uṣṇīṣa; by joining the palms hollow and placing them at the forehead, one is protected. For Heruka's: open the vajra palm upward and bind it like the mouth of Vajrayakṣa gaping toward the sky; by this, actions such as devouring the hosts of the malevolent are accomplished. For Padmanarteśvara's: make it like the petals of a lotus opening; it is applied to the action of purifying wrongdoing. For Vajrasūrya's: make it like the form of a jewel; it is applied to the action of guarding one's own kingdom. For Paramāśva's: it is his own mudrā, applied to the tantra of all actions.

The ritual sequence for these is as follows. After urging the accomplishments, one makes them firm with the samaya mudrās together with their mantras; immediately after that, one should supplicate by way of the four offerings, and supplicate by way of the vajra song. Thereby the sugatas will never at any time transgress their pledge, and will grant accomplishments to oneself. This is the explanation of the meaning of the words "samaya mudrā."

Third, the great samaya mudrā is auspicious, because one is joined with one's own deity at all times and makes the special blessings clear.

This is to be understood by way of three aspects, body, speech, and mind. Of these, first, the manner of union:

Through vajra body, speech, and mind,
having become endowed with the mudrā of signs,
by the mudrā from which all things arise,
make yourself into your own deity.

In order to accomplish the ordinary body, speech, and mind of the yogin and others as the body, speech, and mind of the sugatas, at the three places there are three syllables resting on a moon, a lotus, and a sun, each with rays of its own color. With these, the entire wisdom maṇḍala is gathered into the three places, and it is accomplished as the three signs of body, speech, and mind; then, by reciting the mantra, it is accomplished as nondual with oneself. As for their benefit, the passage was spoken that begins:

By this, the glory of all buddhas,
and likewise all the heroes,

The ritual limbs are these. First, imagining oneself as any one of the family deities, in order to generate potency, one performs the blessing of the limbs, the blessing of the three doors, the purification of oneself, and the purification of beings; this also applies at the stage of making the deity clear. After setting in motion the three subsequent unions, one performs in common the blessing of body, speech, and mind, and this should also be applied to the other ritual limbs, such as the empowerment of disciples. So the root tantra says:

At the beginning and at the end of all actions,
and on other such occasions,
with the mudrā of body, speech, and mind
one should bless everything.

Thus, being endowed with the samaya of body, speech, and mind, since it is the support of all accomplishments, it becomes a great mudrā; hence it is called the great samaya mudrā. This is the explanation of the meaning of the words "great samaya mudrā."

Furthermore, fourth: if one does not abide in the mudrā of union, one's own reality will not become clear; therefore, at every stage of view and meditation, that very thing should be known. Hence the distinctions of characteristics, the objects of focus, the methods of union, and so on are taught together in one place. The root tantra too says:

By the characteristic of the four unions,
through the mudrā one enters the ground of all actions;
as the action that goes before,
it gives rise to the wisdom of liberation.

From that, the two verses beginning:

First, that of Vajrasattva:
glorious Vajrasattva in union,
endowed with form and shape just as they are,

teach his reality together with its qualities. He engages by way of two modes, the signless and that with signs; joining the nature of the complete purity of all phenomena in the manner of the two kinds of maṇḍala, and abiding through the mudrā of Vajrasattva, he causes all the appearances of the six faculties to abide as the nature of the uncontaminated wisdom of great bliss. And in a manner in accord with that, the distinctions of the four unions and so on appear clearly.

Again, the passage beginning:

As for the goddesses in particular,
the characteristic of the mudrā of union
is neither attachment nor non-attachment,
and not apprehended even in the middle,

teaches that, by way of the nonduality of what is indicated and what indicates, they are joined to their respective characteristics and actions, together with the benefits of this.

Again, by way of the Buddha Vairocana and the others, the meaning of the passage beginning:

Neither existent nor nonexistent,
and not apprehended even in the middle,

was spoken. In it, first the mode of the complete purity of all phenomena is taught, and its nature is taught; from that the method of union is taught; and this is to be understood by way of its qualities and enlightened activities. The meaning of the verses has already been explained above, and their application to the family maṇḍalas is not explained, since it is clearly evident. This is the explanation of the meaning of the words "mudrā of union."

Now, fifth, in explaining the meaning of the words "mudrā of all unions," it is first taught by way of Vajrasattva:

All the unions there are,
the various kinds of action made clear:
the seal of glorious Vajrasattva
perfectly accomplishes all accomplishments.

First, just as it is set out in the treatises on dance and song, in order to apply all the mudrās of union to the actions of the feast gathering, of enlightened activity, and of conduct, one begins with the mudrā of glorious Vajrasattva, and should apply it to accomplishing the actions of all accomplishments. Their benefits are easy to understand.

Again, by way of the goddesses of the retinue, four verses were spoken, beginning:

Doing and averting,
and especially seizing,

First, by way of the goddess Saṃvarā: from her hand she shoots flower arrows, and by the action of all unions she causes the bodhicitta to descend, averts, and increases it with great qualities; and she applies them to other actions as well, engaging each in its proper task, averting actions that should not be done, and seizing the collections of others' qualities. So the root tantra says:

Having shot forth the four arrows,
she urges the passionate heart of the lord,
causes the clouds of bodhicitta to descend,
and through the actions of averting and the rest
she performs various enlightened activities,
and becomes victorious over the four māras.

The second, by way of Wondrous Bliss: holding bhaga and liṅga, by the mudrā of expanse and wisdom, in the manner of kissing, embracing, and so on, she seals the beings of the three realms and applies them to the three special activities. So it says:

By the sign of bhaga and liṅga
she seals with the dance of bliss,
and the worlds of the three realms
she enjoys through the three activities.

The third, by way of the goddess Vairocanī: she blesses the entire world, the vessel and its contents, as nectar and enjoyments together with palaces, and partakes of them, and with the weapon victorious over the three realms she applies them to the four kinds of enlightened activity. So it says:

The great trichiliocosm, an excellent vessel all around,
is filled with the nectar of the enjoyments of union;
with the trident weapon, having made it
victorious over the three realms, she enjoys all.

The fourth, by way of the goddess Disciple Lady: she holds vessels of nectar adorned with the bodies of buddhas, and in the manner of various songs and dances she enjoys all unions; in the manner of one victorious over the three realms she engages in the five activities. So it says:

With the seal of the bliss of all unions,
she holds the sign adorned with buddha bodies;
with various dances and songs
she engages in her own five kinds of activity.

Thus, from the maṇḍala of Vajrasattva, the characteristic of all the unions of one's own principal deity and the four family goddesses is taught, and by that the other goddesses of the retinue and the other family maṇḍalas should also be understood. As for the ritual sequence, by way of various unions, just as it is set out in the respective treatises, one should apply them on the occasion of every action. This is the explanation of the meaning of the words "mudrā of all unions."

Furthermore, sixth, from the words "mudrā of the countenance," first the passage was spoken that begins:

The characteristic of Vajrasattva:
supreme bliss of all the goddesses,
the beautiful face transformed,

In their respective maṇḍalas, as a limb for engaging in every action, one joins by way of the countenance: until the mudrā of body, speech, and mind becomes clear, one should remain in the guise of a transformed countenance.

Furthermore, the other mudrā of the goddesses of the retinue is the passage beginning:

The skilled woman, great in illusion,
transforms her beautiful countenance,

This is applied to the arts of the sixty-one feminine wiles. Transforming her beautiful countenance, she abides in the manner of engaging in her own activity: the radiance of her face bears painted markings, she glances with sidelong eyes, and she carries herself with graceful pride and deference. She has become supreme among all the guises of women.

Furthermore, in order to teach those of Vairocana and the others as well, the passage beginning:

In equal union with the awakening of the buddhas,
gazing and smiling with utmost joy,

teaches them together with their respective qualities. First, that of the Buddha Vairocana: he joins in equal union in a guise in accord with the goddesses of his retinue, such as the Lady Who Proclaims All Buddhas, and in a peaceful manner, with a joyful countenance, he abides in the secret gaze. Glorious Heruka together with the goddesses of his retinue is not taught here, because one should refer to what was explained in the section on the great mudrā. That of Padmanarteśvara: endowed with the four modes beginning with charm, his beautiful countenance is transformed, and his face bears painted markings. Vajrasūrya, slightly baring his fangs, with the angry smile of a king's countenance, should be applied to the actions of victory. That of Paramāśva: by way of his four faces, the guises of his countenance abide separately. The central face, intoxicated with passion, is as if flirting; the right is fierce, baring its fangs, and remains terrifying as if devouring the malevolent; the left has a joyful, smiling guise; and on the crown is a green-yellow horse head in wrathful attire.

As for the ritual sequence: in the actions of offering, praise, and so on, having supplicated, one should make the nectar offering and the secret offering with the mudrā of the countenance and with the mudrā of the smile, which is explained below. This is the explanation of the meaning of the words "mudrā of the countenance."

Now, seventh, from the words "mudrā of the smile," first:

The lord Vajrasattva,
supreme bliss of all the goddesses:
this supreme mudrā of the smile,
pressing the beautiful lower lip with the teeth,
delights all the buddhas.

Among the mudrās of the deities' faces, this is taught in particular by way of the eyes and the lips; since the gaze is treated separately, here it should be applied only to the particular feature of the lips. Being endowed with the guise of Vajrasattva, he presses his lower lip with his teeth and, with the creases of a smile, is delighted.

Again, in order to explain the characteristics of the goddesses, four verses were spoken, beginning:

Flirting with the bliss of passion,
pressing the raised lower lip with the teeth,

The first is that of the goddess Saṃvarā; the second is that of Wondrous Bliss; the third is that of Vairocanī; and the fourth is that of Disciple Lady. In this very manner they perform their respective activities. By this rite one should apply it to the other family maṇḍalas as well. This is the explanation of the meaning of the words "supreme mudrā of the smile."

Now, eighth, from the words "mudrā of the gaze," first by way of Vajrasattva:

Fully joined in the experience of bliss,
through the transformation of the half-closed gaze,
by sealing, stupefying, and devouring,
he sustains even the three realms.

Beyond the mudrā of the countenance, the distinctive manner of gazing is taught here: the lord abides with body and face in union, and, in the manner of experiencing bliss, gazes with half-closed eyes, and thereby applies it to the four activities.

Again, that of the goddesses Saṃvarā and the rest is the passage beginning:

The skilled woman, supreme in illusion,
performing the dance of the beautiful gaze,

The goddesses apply themselves to their respective actions through all the manners of gazing proper to their own activities. For the other family maṇḍalas, of glorious Vairocana and the others, four verses were spoken, beginning:

In equal union with the secret of the buddhas,
the gaze utterly peaceful, abiding in bliss,

These are Vairocana's peaceful gaze, Heruka's wrathful gaze, Vajrasūrya's gaze of angry smiling, and Padmanarteśvara's gaze that brings all passion under control. Paramāśva is not mentioned here because he belongs to all actions: on any occasion, he has the gaze and the manner of that very occasion. This is the explanation of the meaning of the words "mudrā of the gaze."

Now, ninth, from the words "mudrā of the Dharma," first, for Vajrasattva, namo stu sarba buddhe bhyaḥ and so on was spoken. Abiding in the union of the body mudrā and so on, one imagines on the tongue an eight-petaled lotus, with light rays radiating from its filaments and anthers. In its center one meditates on a white and red five-pronged vajra arisen from hūṃ, its central bulb marked with the syllable hūṃ. The light rays arising from it pervade the ten directions and gather into one all the secret mantras of the speech of the sugatas; one should then imagine that they abide in the form of letters circling clockwise in sequence, in the manner of saying, "I shall praise and pay homage to all the buddhas." By the power of meditating and reciting in this way, one accomplishes one's own vast qualities; this is easy to understand.

Furthermore, for the Buddha Vairocana and the others there is oṃ ru ru sphu ru tiṣṭha siddha lo ca ne sarbārtha sā dha ni svā hā and so on. For Vairocana, the wheel resting on a lotus is encircled by that very mantra.

For glorious Heruka, the wrathful vajra resting on a lotus is encircled by the mantra beginning su bha ga sta ma hā de bi. For Padmanarteśvara, the lotus resting on a lotus is encircled by the mantra beginning oṃ tā re. For Vajrasūrya, the jewel resting on a lotus is encircled by the mantra beginning trāṃ. For Paramāśva, the crossed vajra resting on a lotus is encircled by the mantra beginning śrī bajra. As for the ritual limbs, this should be applied in order to purify the three doors, to seal with the Dharma mudrā, and to accomplish such actions as recitation and praise. This is the explanation of the meaning of the words "mudrā of the Dharma."

Again, tenth, in explaining the meaning of the words "mudrā of song," first one and a half verses were spoken, beginning:

The general characteristic:
the secret of the voice, high and low,
modulating, refining, and reciting,

Before taking up any song whatever, one first makes one's own family maṇḍala clear. Then, by way of the respective mantras, syllables, and verses, with melodic phrases high and low, with modulations and with cadences, led by hūṃ, which signifies the deities who are utterly peaceful in the three times, one utters the melodies of praise and so on.

For their specific meaning, the family maṇḍalas are taught in sequence, beginning:

The glorious mudrā of the song of hūṃ
fully accomplishes Vajrasattva.

For glorious Vajrasattva, one sings the song of hūṃ freely, and the goddesses utter melodies such as the fifth and the sixth notes. Vairocana, like one chanting the hymns of the Vedas, utters oṃ two and a half times with the voice of Brahmā. Glorious Heruka, by way of six notes, utters the sound of the eight syllables ru lu and makes clear the radiating and gathering of light rays. For Padmanarteśvara, one sings songs of passion in that way. For Vajrasūrya, by way of hi, the song is deep, with even cadences, and drawn out. By way of Paramāśva, for the four actions, on their respective occasions, one should sing songs that are deep and slow, thin and quavering, sweet in tone, and fierce. As for the ritual sequence, this should be applied as a limb of the offering in the feast gathering, of praise, and of other rites. This is the explanation of the meaning of the words "mudrā of song."

Now, eleventh, in order to teach the general characteristic of the families by way of the words "mudrā of drums and cymbals," three verses were spoken, beginning:

The flute and the one-stringed lute,
likewise the vīṇā and the dense-stringed lute,

One purifies and blesses the various kinds of musical instruments, and they are held by the respective hosts of goddesses. If they are played with dance and melody, fourfold glory is accomplished; and even by merely setting them out without playing them, minor actions are accomplished. Here the heart mantras, mudrās, secret mantras, and vidyā-mantras have the character of the four activities, the four families, the four mudrās, the four goddesses, and so on.

For their specific meaning, the family maṇḍalas are taught in sequence. First, by way of the goddesses of Vajrasattva: in jaḥ hūṃ baṃ hoḥ, the inner goddesses urge him to his own action with the seeds of the Dharma. ru ta ma ta is that of Buddha Awakening Lady. sma ra dha is that of the Lady of the Wheel of Dharma. hu lu hu lu is that of the Lady Victorious over the Three Worlds. pra si tya is that of the Lady Vine of Desire. ṭakki hūṃ jaḥlha are words of play from the goddesses. ki is that of Flute Lady. du is that of the Lady of the One-Stringed Lute. hūṃ is that of the Lady of the Vīṇā. dhuṃ is that of the Lady of the Dense-Stringed Lute. ṭakki is that of Horse-Faced Lady. hūṃ jaḥ is that of Sow-Faced Lady. hu lu is that of Sun and Moon Lady. And again hu lu is that of the Lady Who Reduces to Ash. a la la la la ho is that of the four beginning with Flower Lady. With śuddhya siddhyā darśa ya sarban, one urges in various ways. The passage beginning ta ghendhe is the sound of Vajrasattva clapping his palms. The passage beginning hen don is the sound of the mātṛkās clapping their palms. The passage beginning ta ghe ghe is the continuous drumming of Saṃvarā and the others. The passage beginning aṅ aṅ is the drumming of the three samayas of the maṇḍalas of Vairocana. de koṅ koṅ koṅ koṅ is that of Gaurī. dhe bajra bajrā ṇya ra ṇya ra is that of Caurī. Again, koṅ koṅ koṅ koṅ is that of Pramohā. hūṃ bhyo four times is that of Vetālī. ru lu ru lu hūṃ bhyo is the mantra shared by the Great Glorious One and the goddess Śmaśānī. dheṅ koṅ koṅ is that of Pukkasī. Again, koṅ koṅ four times is that of Caṇḍālī. dhe koṅ koṅ is that of Ghasmarī. The passage beginning padma urges from the body, speech, and mind of glorious Padmanarteśvara. The passage beginning trāṃ trāṃ trāṃ urges the maṇḍala of Vajrasūrya by way of names. The passage beginning ṭakki ni hūṃ jjaḥ urges by way of the four actions of Paramāśva. As for the ritual limbs, this is applied to the actions of offering and praise, for the sake of pleasing the deities. This is the explanation of the meaning of the words "mudrā of drums and cymbals."

Now, twelfth, the words "mudrā of dance": the union that gathers all is the passage beginning:

Vajrasattva, by union with himself,
with the two hands bound in the vajra,

Endowed with the union of each family, with finger snaps arising from the vajra fist and with the sound of the palms, one invites all the buddhas and applies them fully to their actions. Here the right hand, being the nature of skillful means, is applied to the action of clapping, while the left, being the nature of wisdom, is spoken of as the sound that joins in that very action.

Now, in order to teach the maṇḍalas of the condensed families by way of the characteristics of the nine dances, the text says, beginning:

Grace and heroism and compassion,
laughter and wrath and terror,

These maṇḍalas of the condensed families arise from the ninefold dance of body, speech and mind of the great Vajradhara himself. Thus, to tame the non-Buddhist (tīrthika) Mahādeva (Śiva), he appears as the maṇḍala of glorious Vajrasattva with the twenty goddesses, beautiful in ornaments and attire. To tame the mighty god of heroism, he appears as the Buddha Vairocana together with a host of goddesses. To tame the compassionate lord of the gods, Śakra (Indra), he appears in the body of Vajradhara Heruka. For the sake of taming Brahmā, who roars with laughter, he appears in the body of Padmanarteśvara. For the sake of taming the wrathful Vināyaka, he appears as Vajrasūrya. For the sake of taming Viṣṇu, whose form is terrifying, he appears in the body of glorious Paramāśva. For the sake of taming the crooked-nosed vināyaka, whose form is repulsive, he appears in the body of Śākyamuni. For the sake of taming the marvelous Rāhula, he appears in the body of a ḍākinī. For the sake of taming the peaceful nāga king Śeṣa, he appears in the body of the Buddha Vairocana. And so he acts for the benefit of all beings without exception.

Further, in order to teach the manner of accomplishment, two verses are spoken, beginning:

A form one never tires of beholding,
he displays as the self of all.

At the time of approach and accomplishment, through the power of the mind's thorough familiarization, the inner signs become clear; and the blessing of the Sugata that accords with them grants every desired action, just as things appear from a wish-fulfilling jewel. Therefore the supporting maṇḍala is shown adorned with the major and minor marks and in manifold forms: a body one never tires of beholding, uttering words such as "Excellent!", bestowing the supreme accomplishments, fulfilling the hopes of all beings, and granting bliss free of defilement.

Again, for the sake of offering through the application of mudrās, the passage beginning:

With these applications of mudrā,
one fully applies one's own offering,

means this: in texts such as those on the feast gathering, the mudrās that are applied to the branches of approach and accomplishment are these. Through the mudrās and the answering mudrās of the host of goddesses emanated from oneself, one applies vast offerings, and one should abide in their application.

Again, in order to apply the mudrās of challenge and response to various actions, the passage begins:

Everywhere diverse, the fortunate ones;
everywhere the master himself dances.

Concerning this, the vajra master and the assembly of disciples carry out the preliminaries to the great maṇḍala of the gathering, and in order to undertake, with everything complete, the actions to be done, they seal the outer and inner boundaries. From the karma vajras who stand as the wrathful door guardians, those entering from outside display mudrās, and from inside the answers to them are then to be shown. Indicating these with various signs arising from body and speech, they should enter in due order.

After that, the vajra master should engage the assembly of disciples in signs and answering signs, and by way of the maṇḍalas of the six families they should be applied to their respective characteristics and enlightened activities.

After that, two yogins, with dance postures, first perform the dance of the great mudrā of glorious Vajrasattva, together with the mantras and activities of each of the goddesses. After that, the challenger binds, in due order, the mudrās of the maṇḍalas of Vairocana together with their mantras; and the respondent in turn binds the mudrā of Heruka. In the same way, for Padmanarteśvara, Vajrasūrya, and the play of Paramāśva, and again up to the maṇḍala of Vajrasattva, the challenger and the respondent perform the mudrās in due order. When these are complete, with songs of exhortation they stir each other's mindstreams, and they should delight one another with the offerings of the gathering.

Here, first, the general characteristic of exhortation is the passage beginning:

Then this, which is called mudrā,
fully reveals all the vows,

which teaches abiding in the sacred commitments by way of signs and answers. As for the particular songs: in some places, with the passage beginning "The stainless moon disc," one exhorts by way of the maṇḍala of Vajrasattva; in some places, with the passage beginning "Utterly terrifying," one exhorts by way of Heruka; in some places, with "Beautiful in radiance, of great splendor," one exhorts the maṇḍala of Vajrasūrya; with "Endowed with bliss and peace, it appears in the form of the Victor," one exhorts the maṇḍala of Vairocana; in some places, with the passage beginning "All beings," one exhorts the maṇḍala of Padmanarteśvara; with the passage beginning "Vajrasattva," one exhorts the maṇḍala of Paramāśva; and with the passage beginning "In many forms," one exhorts the hosts of goddesses with special songs. These, and the passages teaching their benefits, I have not explained, since the meaning of the words is easy to understand.

After exhorting with songs in this way, the patron of the gathering, the newly entered, and those who were defeated, having visualized the others as the maṇḍala of the families, should honor the others with a variety of feasts and present gifts, in order to request empowerment and sacred commitment. Thus it says:

Having danced thoroughly in this way,
whoever among them has been defeated
shall offer to the gathering the food of the gathering,
together with gifts.

That is, those who were defeated at the time when signs were shown with the mudrās of gesture and dance should give a feast of the feast gathering to those assembled.

Their benefits are taught in the passage beginning:

Through these rites of offering,
one will be freed from all wrongdoing,

and this is easy to understand. As for the branches of the rite, they should be applied to the rite of bringing in the yogins at the time of accomplishment and of the feast gathering.

This is the explanation of the meaning of the words "mudrā of dance."

Now, thirteenth, from the words "mudrā by the offering of vajra song": first, by way of the maṇḍala of Vajrasattva together with its goddesses, the text says, beginning, "Supremely joyful, passionately attached, sealing with the most subtle bow and arrow of joy." The meaning of these verses is: first, praise by way of the qualities of the deities; second, exhortation to act for beings; and third, exhortation for the sake of accomplishment.

Thus glorious Vajrasattva, in the manner of the two, joy and bliss, is passionately devoted to the two kinds of action. Saṃvarā, with the bow and arrow of joy, pierces the subtle meaning of the nature of reality and seals all beings. Wondrous Bliss accomplishes bliss for me in the nonduality of the expanse and wisdom. Vairocanī raises the banner of bliss and enjoys it. And the Disciple Lady abides together with the body of Amitāyus, the lord of joy. Thus, delighted by the qualities of the goddesses, he plays in the works of enlightened activity just as Paramāśva acts.

Again, by way of the various activities of the goddesses: Saṃvarā plays in supreme bliss; Wondrous Bliss rouses with bliss and plays; Vairocanī illuminates with one-pointed compassion and plays in the activity of the four joys; and the Disciple Lady performs the actions in the manner of the Ḍākinī Māyā of all the tathāgatas.

Again, the four goddesses bestow such supreme accomplishment on me: the accomplishment of supreme bliss, the bestowing of bliss through bliss, the entering of the sacred and becoming blissful, and the turning of the wheel of bliss. Thereby the exemplary bliss is stirred, and the undefiled path that relies on it is released; the awakening mind increases and expands, and because it becomes stable it is victorious over all phenomena; and the wheel of the dance of bliss arises constantly from the mindstream. Their benefits are easy to understand.

Again, among the mudrās of offering by vajra song of the other family maṇḍalas, first, by way of Vairocana:

Vajrasattva gathers,
the vajra jewel is unsurpassed,
the vajra Dharma is spoken,
and vajra action is performed.

The four of the wheel, the Flute Lady and the others, engage in their own activities. The Flute Lady gathers all phenomena through the awakening mind; the Vīṇā Lady spreads the unsurpassed host of precious qualities; the goddess Clay Drum Lady proclaims the speech of the complete purity of all phenomena; and the Great Drum Lady applies herself to the host of completely pure activities. Therefore, in these songs of exhortation by the four goddesses, one should first utter oṃ and at the end, endowed with the respective mantras and mudrās, perform the acts of offering; and this should also be applied in the contexts of the other family maṇḍalas.

If one asks why this common offering is spoken in the maṇḍala of Vairocana: it is because through this maṇḍala the acts of offering and praise are performed in a special way, since it rouses those whose karma is the Lesser Vehicle and the one-sided peace of nirvāṇa, and, in the manner of a wheel-turning king, engages in all the phenomena of saṃsāra. As the root tantra says:

The protector, the great Vairocana, in his body,
in order to accomplish the great path of passion,
rouses those on the paths of cessation
and makes manifold offerings and praises.

Therefore the offerings explained in the context of Vairocana should be understood as common to all.

If one then says that Vajrasūrya also abides in a king's attire and engages in the offering of precious qualities: it is because of the disciples to be tamed, and because he performs other, special activities. Therefore six special activities are taught for the six family maṇḍalas.

Again, by way of Heruka:

hūṃ Homage to the vajra bow and arrow of action!
Homage to the glorious vajra sacred commitment of passion!
Homage to the utterly terrifying vajra khaṭvāṅga!
Homage to the glorious vajra bell ringing loudly!

These are: the bow and arrow of calm abiding and insight joined in union; the hide of the Great God (Śiva), as a sign of never being parted from the sacred commitment of the two passions; the five-pronged vajra that represents the five kinds of wisdom; the three-pointed khaṭvāṅga, as a sign of victory over the three realms; the skull cup filled with flesh and blood, as a sign of having purified the three poisons and consuming the world and its inhabitants all at once; and, on the crossbar of the khaṭvāṅga, a banner with bells, ringing. When these are joined into the single maṇḍala of Vajrasattva, the preceding words show the taming of the four kinds of awakening mind. Again, this is shown by way of Vajrasūrya, and this very passage represents the four secret goddesses.

Further, by way of Padmanarteśvara, the passage beginning, "In some places, extremely fierce, toward fierce people he appears fully in the form of a fierce woman," teaches, by way of the four faces, the activity of taming by way of the four hosts of arrogant spirits.

Again, by way of Vajrasūrya, with the passage beginning, "Empowering all, supreme lord, inexhaustible supreme treasure, please bring it to accomplishment for me!", one exhorts to action by way of the four faces of Vajrasūrya and the four goddesses. For Paramāśva, with the passage beginning, "In some places he also takes the form of a sage," one exhorts the four activities by way of the four faces.

The condensed meaning is this: in the maṇḍala of Vajrasattva, one gathers into oneself and exhorts the accomplishments of the four family goddesses; in the maṇḍala of Vairocana, one asks that the offerings of the four kinds of awakening mind be granted to oneself; in the maṇḍala of Heruka, that one be endowed with all the activities of the deeds of the principal deity; in the maṇḍala of Padmanarteśvara, that one be endowed with the power to crush those to be tamed; in the maṇḍala of Vajrasūrya, that one gather all the hosts of qualities; and in the maṇḍala of Paramāśva: "Bring me to accomplishment, just as you tame through the aspects of the respective activities."

As for the branch of the rite: after the outer and inner offerings have been presented with the respective mantras and mudrās, the supplication is made.

This is the explanation of the meaning of the words "mudrā of the offering of vajra song."

Now, fourteenth, from the words "mudrā of all offerings": first, in order to teach in detail the sequence of offering to Vajrasattva, the text says, beginning:

Flowers white and red,
blue and yellow, all gathered together,

One should make offering with any of these: special flowers possessed of natural fragrance and color; those having the light of the five kinds of precious substances; those touched up with dyes such as lac and indigo; those arising from moist and from dry ground; those arising from the bodies of living creatures; those arising from the magical displays of the gods; or those arising from one's own samādhi. Further, the kinds of incense, the kinds of lamps, and the substances of scented ointment are likewise those that appear in their natural form and those arising from the power of compounding them.

Now, in order to teach in brief the offerings of the other maṇḍalas, the text says, beginning:

Red flowers to the Buddha,
to the Vajra Holder, varied ones,

This teaches the manner of offering to each respective maṇḍala with various special flowers, incense, lamps and scents. This is the explanation of the meaning of the words "mudrā of all offerings."

Further, fifteenth, from the words "mudrā of the torma of all offerings": the common torma, made from the outer elements, is taught in the passage beginning:

The Graceful Lady and the Young Girl,
the Intoxicating, and likewise nectar,

The Graceful Lady is grain beer. The Young Girl is mead. The Intoxicating is molasses beer. The Lone Hero is grape wine and honey mixed together. Passion is salt; that same salt, when it has aged for a long time, is hard passion. The Crooked One is sour juice. The Maidservant is the vegetables one likes. Salt is the various kinds of salt. "And so forth" means broth, meat, beer, roasted flour, and various foods and drinks. "Khar and so forth" are the stages of applying the code words. The nectars are the eight branches of drink, such as grape wine. "The taste of the self of all" is by the power of all of them coming together into a single taste. "The supreme of life" is the fortunate enjoyment of joy.

Again, in order to teach the offering torma made from the substances of the inner elements, the text says, beginning:

Ripe mango and grape,
jackfruit and coconut,

These five great fruits should be taken as substances of accomplishment in a manner free of fault. They are made into pills as substances of sacred commitment, smeared with the three sweet substances and offered as torma; and one should hold them beneath the tongue while doing the recitation.

Again, the fabricated torma: images of the five sentient beings are made and smeared with khar and the like; and images of the five great fruits, tinted with the scent of licorice and the like and the color of turmeric and the like, are well made from tree bark, birch bark and so forth, and offered as the offering torma.

Now, in order to please the family maṇḍalas, and those who dwell in their retinues, with special offering tormas, the passage begins:

Offering food to the buddhas,
meat and beer are wholly abandoned.

To Vairocana one offers a feast of meat and beer free of all fault, with songs, cymbals and so forth. To Heruka one offers images of the flesh and blood of a man who bears the marks. The others are easy to understand.

As for the branch of the rite, it should be applied in every context of torma, such as the fire offering and the maṇḍala rite. This is the explanation of the meaning of the words "mudrā of the torma of all offerings."

Now, sixteenth, from the words "mudrā of empowerment by mudrā," the passage begins:

First, of Vajrasattva:
the thumbs in the cross-legged posture of the sattva,
bend the tips of the two index fingers.

First is empowerment with the jewel and the crown. From the joined vajra palms, the right thumb presses the left, making them like the throne seat of a stūpa. Bending the tips of the two index fingers, one sets them against the middle fingers in the manner of the throne's raised back. The middle fingers are bent at the first joint in the manner of a vase, and the little fingers and ring fingers are crossed to be like the backrest, the halo of light. With its mantra, one touches this to the five places of the head and confers empowerment, and one should bind and release it from the ends of the hanging silk ribbons. Their mantras are recited: ratna māle abhiṣiñca traṃ paṭala abalaṃ bi hūṃ; one should clap the palms of delight three times and say vajra tuṣya hoḥ. After that, one binds the mudrā of the skull cup, does as before, and should say hūṃ abhiṣiñca māṃ.

"Glorious Vajra Heruka, lotus, precious jewel" refers to the skull crown of Vajrasattva; that of Heruka is the very same, arising from the cause of jewels and abiding with the characteristic of the pure lotus.

Again, in order to teach the other family maṇḍalas, the text says, beginning:

Joining the vajra palms evenly,
the two hands, the precious jewel,

Thus, one binds the jewel mudrā of Vairocana and confers empowerment with khaṃ abhiṣiñca māṃ. One binds the mudrā of beauty of Heruka, puts on a wrathful frown, and says hi abhiṣiñca māṃ. One binds the lotus mudrā of Padmanarteśvara and says hrīḥ abhiṣiñca māṃ. One binds the jewel mudrā of Vajrasūrya and says traṃ abhiṣiñca māṃ. And for Paramāśva, one binds any crown according to whatever activity is at hand and says haṃ abhiṣiñca māṃ. The methods of binding these mudrās and their benefits are not explained, because they are easy to understand.

As for the branch of the rite, it is applied on two occasions: meditation on the deity, and conferring empowerment on the disciple. In the first case, one confers empowerment on Vajrasattva abiding in the absorption of the four doors of liberation; there is the arising of the body through the five manifest awakenings; the particulars of that body's color and emblems; the accomplishment of the body's subtle particles through blessing the five places; and the purification of all places. After these, one confers empowerment with the crown of any one of the family maṇḍalas and binds the silk ribbons, whereby the result, the body of the family, is accomplished.

Second, for the sake of conferring empowerment on the disciple, there are: entering the site of the maṇḍala; making the supplication; setting up the platform; placing the disciple on a seat and adorning him; purifying the disciple, visualizing him as the deity of the family, and making offerings; and, after the water empowerment, conferring the crown empowerment. This is the explanation of the meaning of the words "mudrā of empowerment by mudrā."

Now, seventeenth, from the words "mudrā of all vajras": first, in order to teach the basis of binding mudrās, it is said:

Interlacing the fingers with one another
is explained to be the vajra palms.
Clasping all the fingers together
is explained to be the vajra clasp.

First, one who is endowed with the yoga of the family should visualize, on the tips of the five fingers of the right hand and on a sun disc in its palm, the Dharma heart syllables of the six secret families. On the moon disc of the left hand one should visualize the Dharma heart syllables of the six consorts. One meditates on the emanation and gathering of light rays from them, up to the point of their respective hand emblems and subtle bodies; and one imagines that they spread out to perform their respective activities, gather in the family maṇḍalas, and dissolve into their respective bodies. From their dissolving into the respective bodies, their minds stir in the aspect of passion, and father and mother gaze at one another and arouse each other with signs of union. As a sign of that, one should interlace the vajra palms. Again, as a sign of abiding in the absorption of the play of union of the four mudrās, one binds the vajra clasp.

Again, in order to teach their nature, two verses are spoken, beginning:

Having made the vajra clasp,
the index fingers and thumbs into the womb,

The first teaches that, from the secret of the body of the maṇḍala deities, the melting in the two, bhaga and liṅga, gives rise to its great joy. The second teaches that, from the great self-arisen wisdom, the great bliss of mind in which the expanse and wisdom are not two in meaning, the various actions of the vast and boundless accomplishments are achieved, and the accomplishment of undefiled great bliss is achieved.

Now, dividing them in detail: in order to apply them to all the actions of accomplishment of Vajrasattva, two methods of binding the mudrā are taught together with their benefits. One binds the mudrā of the five-pointed vajra, which represents the five kinds of wisdom of the unchanging mind; one exhorts the deities of the maṇḍala and touches it to oneself, whereby one brings under control the host of accomplishments and all actions, vast and boundless, without exception. With that same mudrā one should also perform the dance for the sake of others. The second mudrā is the previous mudrā with the middle fingers formed like a hook; it is placed at one's own heart, and with light rays it summons all desired actions without exception. One invites the wisdom maṇḍala and applies it at the times of accomplishment, of enlightened activity, and of the rite.

For the Buddha Vairocana: from the vajra clasp, the vajra mudrā is turned like a wheel, and this is applied to all actions; and from the vajra mudrā, the palms are made hollow, whereby those accomplishments are sealed in a single instant. For Heruka: from the wrathful vajra with open mouth, the middle fingers are made like a hook, and this makes clear the meaning of the expanse and wisdom. For Padmanarteśvara: one binds the lotus vajra, sets the two thumbs against each other, joins the tips of the two index fingers and bends them, making them like the petals of a lotus. For Vajrasūrya: from the vajra binding, the two middle fingers are bent with corners, like a jewel, and the two little fingers are made like arrows, whereby one brings under control the whole kingdom of the three realms. For Paramāśva: from the vajra binding, one applies it to one's own activity, like a crossed vajra.

Their characteristics, their respective intentions, and the contexts of accomplishment to which they are applied are taught in the fivefold rite and in the rite of the feast gathering, so they are not elaborated at length here.

As for the branch of the rite: the basis and nature of binding mudrās are applied at the beginning of all the action and sacred commitment mudrās; and the characteristics of each particular one should be applied at the times of approach, close approach, and receiving the accomplishment of great accomplishment, and in the context of performing the actions of the minor activities. This is the explanation of the meaning of the words "all the vajra mudrās."

Now, eighteenth, from the words "mudrā of the name together with the Teacher": first, in order to teach the general characteristic of the common meaning, three verses are spoken, beginning:

Joining together the vowels and consonants,
one should tie a knot right at the mouth.

First, a yogin endowed with excellent qualities, in a secluded and adorned place, by way of the three grades of people's understanding, highest, middling and lowest, should meditate on his own suchness by focusing on meditative concentration. Then, in order to accomplish signs by purifying the support, and to gather the accumulations and give protection by way of drawing a diagram, he should engage in the rite of the letter wheel.

As for the drawing: the ground is well prepared, and one draws with colored powders, or draws with paint and brush on cloth, tree bark and the like. On a moon disc there is a wheel having seven tiers of garlands, and in the hub at the center one draws the root heart syllable of any one of the family maṇḍalas, with its head toward the west, standing in its own color. Their rims are encircled with garlands of their respective emblems. At the outermost edge one should write the secret name of the yogin and of whoever is to be protected, and the protection mantra.

On the inner wheel with eight points, beginning from the east, one should set out, without confusing the directions and intermediate directions, the eight letters that are the Dharma heart syllables of the respective goddesses. On the outer tier beyond that, the wheel with sixteen points, one should set out the hundred-syllable mantra, beginning from the first point in the east and continuing until it comes back around to it. Thus, on the first point is oṃ vajrasattva samaya. On the second is manupālaya. On the third is vajrasattva tvenopatiṣṭha. On the fourth is dṛḍho me bha. On the fifth is sutoṣyo me bha. On the sixth is anurakto me bhava. On the seventh is supoṣyo me bhava. On the eighth is sarva siddhi mme. On the ninth is prayaccha. On the tenth is sarva karma su ca me. On the eleventh is cittaṃ śreyaḥ ku. On the twelfth is hūṃ and four ha and hoḥ.

On the thirteenth is bhagavān. On the fourteenth is sarva tathāgata. On the fifteenth is vajra māme muñca. On the sixteenth is vajrī bhava mahā samaya sattva āḥ. These are taught with reference to Vajrasattva; for the other families there are other secret mantras, altered with their own names.

On the sixteen spokes of the wheel one writes the secret mantras of the activities of the respective families, given below, together with their final ornaments. Outside that, on the central enclosure of the four prongs of the crossed vajra, one writes the Dharma heart syllable in accordance with the one at the center; for Vairocana, in particular, it is oṃ. On the four spokes of the crossed vajra one writes the four hūṃ syllables, the heart syllables of the wrathful one Sumbha, together with their phrases. In the southeast is oṃ sumbha ni sumbha hūṃ. In the southwest is gṛhṇa gṛhṇa hūṃ. In the northwest is gṛhṇāpaya gṛhṇāpaya hūṃ. In the northeast is ānaya hoḥ bhagavān vidārājāya hūṃ phaṭ.

On the rim outside that, in the directions and intermediate directions in order, one sets out the sixteen vowels, two in each place. On the rim outside that, one should set out the consonants, one group each in the directions and intermediate directions. All the letters except the central letter should be written with their heads facing east. On the outermost rim one should adorn it with:

oṃ All phenomena arise from causes;
their cause the Tathāgata has taught,
and what their cessation is,
this too the Great Ascetic has taught,

followed by svāhā. For this reason these things are also explained at length in the great root tantra; here the meaning has been condensed into verse and taught for those who hold the collection of many texts. By drawing such a wheel, making offerings to it and meditating on it, the qualities described below will be accomplished. Thus, one adorns it with various colors and with various fine scents such as saffron, and sets out a vast array of offerings; one summons and installs the family maṇḍala and, in the manner of a consecration, makes it nondual.

Now, the meaning of the words: since the vowels and consonants arise as signs representing skillful means and wisdom, they are made into two garlands, even and alike, together with threads of light, and the tips of head and tail should be knotted directly before the mouth, facing east. When meditating on them, they abide at the heart, so the junction of the letters is a knot directly before the mouth; and when practicing with a support, this is because it is also shown facing straight toward the yogin. In this way one should set out all the letters by way of the arrangement of the various letters. These are taught as the common rite, to be understood as the general meaning of the family maṇḍalas by way of the three kinds of persons.

Now, in order to teach the six family maṇḍalas in order, as the particular characteristics, the passage begins:

Having written the name, together with praise,
of Vajrasattva, the glorious deity,

By way of Vajrasattva, two particular kinds of wheel are taught: for the time of protecting others and purifying wrongdoing, and for the time of applying oneself to accomplishment. This is by way of altering the mantras and final ornaments that stand on the sixteen spokes of the wheel. In the same way the wheels of the Buddha Vairocana and the others should be understood; they are not explained, because they are easy to understand.

The particular benefit of writing and reciting these is:

Just as writing brings great merit,
so too does recitation:
the vast accomplishments of action
are swiftly achieved, all without exception.

Those who are unable to meditate and practice just as it is set out apply it to all actions by reciting the mantra as well.

The manner in which one recites the names of the chosen deity and the masters is:

Whatever the exalted deity,
whatever the master may be,
their names
should be spoken together with the word "glorious."

In order to avoid disrespect toward them, one adds the words "glorious feet" with regard to their station and qualities.

Again, for the sake of conferring empowerment merely by way of an image maṇḍala, a feast gathering and these applications, it is said:

Having brought the disciple into the maṇḍala,
and having received the supreme offering fee,
by empowering him with a vajra name,
one prophesies that he will be a tathāgata.

Second, for the sake of receiving the mantra of the chosen deity: in a secluded place one should smear the maṇḍala well, and with powdered stone draw a red eight-petaled lotus resting on a white triangle. On it one sets out the letters of the vowels and consonants: in the center kṣa, with its head facing west. On the eight petals, beginning from the east, one should write the ka group, the ca group, the group of the retroflex ta, the group of the dental ta, the pa group, the ya group, the śa group, and the group of the vowels. Around its edge one arranges the array of offerings, the array of tormas, and all the ornaments; having purified them, one should then bless them.

Then the vajra master should perform the preliminary actions, gather the accumulation of merit, meditate on the protection wheel, perform the samādhi of the ground and support, and by the action of the three visualizations summon and install the wisdom maṇḍalas. Having dissolved them in the form of the mantra so that they are pleased, he should extract the heart mantra of the chosen deity, write it with powdered stone at the edge of the maṇḍala, and have the disciple read it. Then, having caused the blessing of the Sugata and that very form of the mantra to descend into the disciple's mindstream, he should stabilize it with the hundred-syllable mantra and bring the action to completion. After these, having pleased the guests with the torma rite, one should recite the rite of auspiciousness.

Its benefit is:

Through these applications of mudrā,
until the heart of awakening,
one abides fully in the vajra name,
and then one becomes a buddha as well.

This is easy to understand.

As for the branch of the rite, it says: at the times of gathering the accumulations, of protecting and accomplishing, of application to all the actions of the actions of accomplishment, of conferring empowerment, and of receiving the secret mantra, one should undertake this very practice, endowed with the preliminary branches of the rite.

This is the explanation of the meaning of the words "mudrā of the name together with praise."

Now, from the nineteenth, called "the mudrā of signs." First, in order to pay homage by way of the samaya mudrā of bodily signs, it says:

Touching the crown of the head
Is taught as homage;
Touching the forehead
Should be known as the reply to homage.

When the yogins gather in the morning at charnel grounds and other such places, in order to recognize the manner of which door to enter and so on, and the outer and inner signs; in order for the yogins to test one another's samaya at the place of the feast gathering; and so that, at the times of accomplishment and enlightened activity, they abide in the secret signs and are not obstructed by ordinary bodily conduct, all of them, the vajra master together with the disciples, should engage in exactly this manner. At the time of homage, one touches the crown of the head to the ground, rubbing it there. At the time of the reply to homage, one raises the forehead upward with a glow of joy.

Further, to teach by way of all the major and minor limbs of the body, the eyebrows and the rest, it says, "Moving the eyebrows is the sign of the abode of all the buddhas," and so on. Since the right and left eyebrows, distinguished from each other, abide as the essence of the father and mother deities, raising the right one upward is looking to the sky, meaning, "I have obtained the siddhi of the ḍākinīs, which accords with it." Raising the left one means applying oneself to all the siddhis of the three worlds and overpowering them. Turning the left eye is the sign of wisdom, indicating a woman; the right is the sign of method, indicating a man. The left ear means listening to the sweet melody of wisdom's song, and the right, the well-spoken words of method. With the right nostril, by way of method, one asks to stay; with the left, by way of wisdom, one asks for scents and other things. Rubbing the tongue to the right and to the left means the flesh of a caṇḍāla and garlic liquor. Pressing the lower lip with the right canine signifies a man, through the mark of method; the left, through the sign of wisdom, signifies a woman. The throat is the seat of the sound that utters all the sublime Dharma, and it indicates the meaning of all ideas. The heart is the secret treasury, the seat of self-arisen wisdom; so making as if to draw something out from there with the index finger shows the meaning of the Vajra Vehicle, and applies all the phenomena of saṃsāra to the path of great awakening. The navel, being the center of the body, accomplishes the meaning of the Middle Way, free of all extremes, and from the wheel of wisdom it teaches the meaning of the various dharmas and turns that wheel. The erect organ indicates the body of great bliss arisen from the bliss of great self-arisen wisdom. With the right shoulder one urges, in order to summon; with the left one sends away again. Embracing oneself with the right and left upper arms means that all rites are accomplished, and, beyond that, all the lordship of great qualities is accomplished. Clenching the right fist binds whoever it may be; opening the left means, "Release them again!" The right thigh applies to the activity of desire; the left applies to the various dharmas. The left knee is a mere bow, while the right is homage made with joy and faith; and further, right and left together mean, "Bind and release all the tasks to be done!" The left foot presses down in the manner of wisdom, and the right, by method, destroys them. Their benefits are easy to understand. Thus the samaya mudrā of signs is taught by way of the major and minor limbs of the body.

Now, to teach homage and its reply through signs of speech: this sign, "oṃ atipu," is taught as homage, and "pratipu" should be known as the reply to homage. In the maṇḍalas of the individual families, indicating it with each one's own mantra, one should utter the words with respect and reply with delight. These are taught in general; the detailed explanation of their particular meanings is found in the root tantra, and has been summarized and clarified in the rite of the feast gathering.

Now, among the signs of the characteristics of the maṇḍala, first, Vajrasattva:

The being without beginning or end,
Vajrasattva, supreme bliss,

and so on. Glorious Vajrasattva arose in the beginningless and endless time of saṃsāra, and abides as the body of great bliss, the nature and source of all phenomena. Therefore the glorious Paramādya Tantra also says:

The being without beginning or end,
Vajrasattva, supreme bliss,
Supreme awakening mind, chief of chiefs,
The universal nature of Samantabhadra.

Because he possesses wondrous and marvelous qualities, he is called "Wondrous Bliss"; if one utters that name with the word "glorious," one is freed from all sins and accomplishes all happiness.

Now, to teach the goddesses of the maṇḍala in order:

Saṃvara, Wondrous Bliss,
Lamp, and likewise Shishi,

and so on. The sugatas of the four families, the appearance of the four wisdoms that are one's own great qualities, abide as the signs of the four goddesses. Because they are inseparable from them and always abide with them, they are called the four secret goddesses. Because they exhaust the five aggregates and the five afflictions, they are taught in code as the Sealing Lady, the Bewildering Lady, the Devouring Lady and the Life-Increasing Lady. The four mothers of the families, which appear as the objects of wisdom's focus and are characterized as the complete purity of the four kinds of awakening mind, are taught by the code names Buddha Awakening Lady and the rest. The appearances of the conduct of the four immeasurables of skillful means are taught by the code names Horse-Faced Lady and the rest. The four inner offering goddesses and the four outer goddesses are taught as signs indicating the four kinds of awakening mind of the great seal that arise from joy, and the five undefiled aggregates. The signs of the vast marks of the maṇḍala indicate various meanings: the entire assembly of qualities of one's own appearance, arisen from oneself.

Further, the signs of the hand emblems of the four secret goddesses:

Moving the eyebrows is the four signs;
bhaga and liṅga, Wondrous Bliss,

and so on. Shooting the flower arrow out of passion and the glance of moving eyebrows are alike in nature; they become the sign of abiding, through the four joys, in the essence of the awakening mind of bliss. bhaga and liṅga become the sign of generating the great bliss of body and mind. The skull cup is alike in nature to the lotus and shows the meaning of complete purity; with it, even while enjoying the whole world and its beings in saṃsāra, one abides in harmony with that purity. As a sign of having conquered the three realms and being free of pride, there appear the skull cup filled with nectar and the khaṭvāṅga. In order to hold all beings with the nectar of the awakening mind of bliss and accomplish them as the deathless body, the skull cup is shown together with the body of a buddha.

Now, to teach the etymology of maṇḍala:

The maṇḍala is explained as a wheel;
That itself is explained as samaya.

The wheel of the maṇḍala and the wheel of weapons are alike in quality and function: they gather, they cut, they protect, they are swift, and they terrify others. Yet that itself, by way of its difference, abides as an intrinsic quality. Further, the etymology of the feast gathering:

Because all the deities gather,
All the holy persons gather,

and so on. It is taught in terms of the coming together of the four excellences, the seven arrangements and all friends.

Now, from the characteristics of the support, in order to teach in turn the characteristics of the vajra king, the deputy, the ritual assistant and the rest, it says:

Because all the goddesses gather,
All, various, fortunate,

and so on. The vajra king has the aspect of abiding inseparably from glorious Vajrasattva. He has become the source from which all the enjoyments of all the goddesses and all objects of desire arise; he possesses omniscient wisdom, unconfused about all that can be known; and he is always diligent in acting for the benefit of beings.
