The deputy follows his example, clearly knows and carries out all the rites and tasks, and possesses all the branches of the ritual. The karma vajra has mastered a portion of the activities, has diligence and wisdom as companions, and has the fortune of one of the three, superior, middling or lesser. These teach the characteristics of the persons who serve as supports.

Now, to teach the signs of empowerment:

All the buddhas, supreme jewel,
The sublime, unchanging crown,

and so on. As the sign indicating the five kinds of family maṇḍala together with the kingdom of qualities, there is the five-pointed crown made of five jewels; as the sign indicated by the six wisdoms and the six perfections, there is the garland of skulls; and the sign of possessing the sixth, the great Vajradhara, is marked by the buddha crest jewel. Further, as marks indicating the family of the very nature of the other family maṇḍalas, Vairocana and the rest, and the collections of their wisdom, qualities and deeds:

One accomplished in the nature of the buddha,
And likewise one in the nature of the lotus;
All empowerments are the great empowerment:
So it is said, accomplishing every aim.

By showing the crowns of the individual families as stated above, the code of reality is unlocked.

Moreover, the stages of the offering fee:

It is explained as the wealth of riches;
Since the heart too is the best of riches,
The buddhas explain it with the mudrā of signs.

The best of the three kinds of fee, superior, middling and lesser, is to carry out the instructions with faith and heartfelt longing.

Further, the substances for practice in the feast maṇḍala:

Camphor and red sandalwood
Are explained as the mixture and the great blood,

and so on. In the center of the feast maṇḍala one should gather and offer the five nectars, the signs of great passion, and all that serves as conditions for enjoying the six kinds of offering substances. Thereby, at the time of offering, it shows the meaning of reality in its outer, inner and secret modes, indicates the characteristics of each, and delights the maṇḍala they characterize.

Now, to teach the signs of samaya:

Then the oath sworn for accomplishment
Is explained as samaya,

and so on. Having entered the maṇḍala and obtained empowerment, one abides by the stages of the vows just as one has pledged them as oath and samaya, and does not transgress them; and one puts them into practice through knowing the suchness of accomplishment in terms of the meaning of the four goddesses.

Further, the signs of mudrā:

Because even all the tathāgatas
Do not transgress it,

and so on. The samaya mudrās of body, speech and mind are to be meditated on and accomplished by the yogin at all times, and the sugatas too set their seal on them; therefore being joined to the mudrās of body, speech and mind is the supreme samaya. For then the one whose action makes union, mudrā and samaya a single mode joins the manifold to a single essence.

Further, to teach signs by way of offering:

Its close enjoyment
Is the entire three realms without exception,

and so on. Through the practice of visualizing the family maṇḍalas, one meditates on all of appearance and existence, outer and inner, the world and its beings, as the maṇḍala of gods and goddesses, and one should emanate and arrange all the appearances arising from that as hosts of offerings. If one asks what form these take: they are the six kinds of sense pleasures, which become the sign of offering to oneself with one's own enjoyments. Thus the aspects of color and shape are the goddess of form. The various kinds of sound that resound are the goddess of sound. In the manner of spreading fragrance, there is the goddess of scent. Since all tastes transform into rasayana, the elixir, holding that is the goddess of taste. Through experiencing the meaning of both joy and bliss, there is the goddess of touch.

Because it joins undefiled wisdom to the path of liberation, there is the goddess of phenomena. These appear in all the outer elements as well, and because they arise from oneself, by oneself, they are to be presented as the yogin's enjoyments, in whatever way is blissful. That is what is meant.

Now, among the code terms for the sequence of the four yogas, first, in order to teach their defining characteristics, it says:

Joining oneself as the superior deity
is the pledge of one's own offering.
When one offers through subsequent yoga,
it is the supreme bliss of all the yogas.
Through the mudrā of the buddhas' code,
it is explained to be great bliss.

First the defining characteristics are taught in brief. Accomplishing oneself as the causal Vajrasattva through yoga is the yoga of the superior deity; it is the limb of pleasing oneself. Generating the consort through subsequent yoga (anuyoga), and bringing the activities to completion through the light rays of the awakening mind, is the second limb of pleasing. Generating the maṇḍala of utmost yoga (atiyoga), and having its deities carry out the general and particular activities extensively, is the third limb of pleasing. Then, blessing the six sense sources and meditating on vajra body, speech and mind and so forth, bliss becomes supreme: omniscient wisdom and the superknowledges of this very life are accomplished, and one is joined to the body of great bliss. Therefore it is also said that great yoga (mahāyoga) abides as the very nature of the result.

Further, to show its etymology, two verses are spoken, beginning:

Absorption in wisdom alone
is what is called yoga.

First, the five manifest awakenings are joined in the manner of the five wisdoms, and, in the manner of making the five kinds of awakening mind clear, one focuses merely on the single body of skillful means; it is for this that the word "alone" is spoken. The rest is easy to understand. Its meaning is clearly explained in the seventh chapter:

Through the sequence of the four yogas,
having completed the maṇḍala,
by means of the subsequent activities
one should accomplish the maṇḍala.

And again, the root tantra also says:

Having clearly meditated on the maṇḍala
in sequence, according to the divisions of yoga,
from the accumulations of the path that accords with the cause
the fruit of liberation will arise.
By the power of having meditated on yoga,
subsequent yoga and the rest become clear.
The nonconceptual, liberated intelligence, the fruit,
the source from which various qualities arise, is accomplished.

Now, among the code terms for approach and accomplishment, first, two verses are spoken, beginning:

The sign is explained as the accomplishment of joyful conduct;
subsequent yoga is subsequent accomplishment.

These teach, by their distinct individual names, the signs that indicate the stages of approach and accomplishment. First, meditating clearly from the purpose of the meditative absorption up to yoga is accomplishment (sādhana). Likewise, meditating clearly up to subsequent yoga, utmost yoga and great yoga are called subsequent accomplishment, great accomplishment and utmost accomplishment. This applies also to the essence of all the limbs of approach and accomplishment, and in particular it is taught for the sake of persons of lesser intelligence who delight in brevity. It is explained at length in the root tantra as well, and in agreement with it the Glorious Candraguhyatilaka also says:

Accomplishment is the yoga of the deity;
subsequent accomplishment is the goddess's aim;
great accomplishment is the seal of both;
in equal union, the supreme vajra;
close union is the abode of both;
utmost union is sublime bliss.

The meaning of their words is easy to understand.

Further, as for how they connect, two verses are spoken, beginning:

Possessing all, going everywhere,
it is proclaimed the sublime accomplishment.

By way of the four limbs of approach and accomplishment, one is joined to the two kinds of accomplishment, the common and the supreme, and the activities that focus on the appearances of the families and the wisdoms, in the manner of each respective occasion, are accomplished through four modes. From the gathering of the causes, by the gathering of the conditions, all the fruits that are the qualities abiding in oneself are made manifest. Their benefits are not explained, because they are easy to understand.

Now, among the various code terms that are taught, first, the five offerings:

Blood is explained as flowers;
radiance is explained as incense;
the great flesh is the lamp;
ashes are explained as perfume.

That which becomes the sign of passion and makes it manifest is like a flower; through satisfying the mind it is like incense; the flesh of one born seven times becomes the lamp; and because it purifies the body it is like scented ointment.

Again, the code for food and drink, beginning:

Liquor is explained as the coquette;
utter intoxication is honey.

For these, the eight limbs of the path of nectar are the five nectars, adorned with fat, blood and ashes. Since they become the vital essence of the body, they are like nectar. The others have already been explained above and are easy to understand.

Again, to teach the code of the five fruits, it says, beginning:

Mango, grape, jackfruit,
coconut, myrobalan.

These teach the code of the five great fruits, supreme in their distinction: possessing the power of their own nature, possessing the power of combined substances, possessing the power of blessing, and arising from accomplishing them.

Now, among the code terms for pleasing the deities, in order to explain them according to the sequence of Vajrasattva, Vairocana, Sūrya (the Sun), Heruka, Nartteśvara (the Lord of Dance), and Paramāśva, it says, beginning:

Of Vajrasattva, joined with glory,
the song, the cymbals, and the dancing.

These became the code for making offerings with the song and cymbals of splendor, the song and cymbals of utter peace, the song and cymbals of the lord of men, the song and cymbals of the charnel ground, the song and cymbals of passion, and the song and cymbals of great heroism. Further, through the etymology of ḍākinī and the etymology of illusion, in the manner of indicating that each teaches its own meaning, these are designated as the code for the whole maṇḍala of glorious Vajrasattva and the rest.

Now, among the teachings of the meaning of the code by way of various names, first, the maṇḍala of Vajrasattva together with the goddesses. Through the passage beginning:

Union of all the buddhas,
dancing with every illusion,

the enumeration of the names of the principal deity and the retinue, and the mantras of their respective activities, are taught. In connection with this, the characteristics of all the buddhas of the four families are explained: the buddha Vairocana, who is utterly steadfast; Vajradhara, who is thoroughly steadfast; Lokeśvara, who is wholly steadfast; and Ākāśagarbha, who is vastly steadfast. These became the signs of the code in the manner of coming to know, becoming purified, becoming accomplished, and entering and bringing under control.

Again, the particular names of the buddha Vairocana. Through the passage beginning:

aṃ āṃ a ā, all of these explained,
are the mudrā of the buddhas' code,

the mudrās of the body, speech and mind of the principal deity, and the particular names of the qualities and activities of the retinue goddesses, are taught.

Again, the particular names of Heruka. Through sounds such as krodha krodha, a code possessing many verbal roots is taught by way of the five senses and the five objects. Moreover, the yakṣas, Daśagrīva of Laṅkā and the others, express it in the language of the barbarians by way of ten names, ten winds, ten perfections, ten grounds, ten offerings and ten praises. Thus by the sound krodha kṣa of the ten names, in sequence:

You teach like a father;
you are loving like a mother;
you have the bearing of a lion;
you have the gaze of an elephant;
you blaze like fire;
you gather like water;
you are adorned with a garland of qualities;
you are lord of all enlightened activities;
you are the source of precious qualities;
you are the great cloud of truth.

This is spoken in the Secret Wisdom Tantra, and since it is also explained at length in the Tantra of the Empowerment of Vajrapāṇi and the Vajra Array Tantra, it is not elaborated here. Their benefits are easy to understand. The code of the hand emblems and the goddesses has already been explained above.

Again, in order to set the goddesses to their tasks by way of enlightened activity:

Protect, swiftly, glorious Victor!
Crush them utterly, like ashes!

This is the particular activity of the four goddesses of the families. haṃ haṃ hra hūṃ is the mantra that sets the four goddesses to their activities.

Play! Take heed! And likewise,
let them play, let them slay!

This is the particular activity of the four outer goddesses. ma ta ra ṭa phaṭ phaṭ sets them to their tasks. By this the signs of the code of glorious Heruka are taught.

Again, the characteristics of Padmanarteśvara are explained by way of code. The passage beginning:

The king, Lord Avalokiteśvara,
is called the Lord of the World,

explains in code the code that teaches the reality of the principal deity, and the meanings of the nature, names and activities of the retinue goddesses. śuddha and the rest are the code for setting them to their tasks by way of the heart mantras of the eight goddesses.

Further, to teach the meaning of the code of Vajrasūrya, the passage beginning:

Ākāśagarbha, in every direction,
who fulfills every hope,

teaches the characteristics and activities of the principal deity, the mantras that set the goddesses to their tasks, and the code of their names.

Again, the code that indicates the meaning of Paramāśva's play:

Various lotuses, all eyes,
and likewise all the worlds.

This teaches that, through the activities of the four families, the self that accomplishes all worldly and transworldly activities is endowed with a nature possessing the four modes, together with its qualities. The limbs of the rite are taught so that, at the time of performing accomplishment and so forth, all the aims one desires may be known by way of code and signs. This has been the explanation of the meaning of "the mudrā of code."

Now chapter twenty, called "The Divisions of the Wisdom Mudrā," was spoken in order to accomplish the superknowledges of this world and unsurpassed awakening in the next. On the passage beginning:

"One should be attached," it says,
acting as one who knows the guise,

through glorious Vajrasattva, one is attached to the wisdom of great emptiness in all phenomena, and for the sake of oneself and others one accomplishes and abides in the accomplishment that has signs, the deity's body, exactly in accordance with the characteristics of the mudrā; thereby one is joined to all activities through the corresponding result together with enlightened activity. This is with its benefits. Through the maṇḍala of Vairocana, one comes to realize all phenomena of saṃsāra and nirvāṇa as the nature of the wisdom of the dharmadhātu; from that, conventionally, one meditates clearly on one's own maṇḍala, and by the power of accomplishing it, by way of vajra body, speech and mind, one is joined to activities that accord in manner with that very reality. Through glorious Heruka, one joins all phenomena with the mirror-like wisdom, and conventionally that very wisdom is joined to all accomplishments by way of the mudrās of body, speech and mind. Through Padmanarteśvara, one joins all phenomena with the wisdom of equality, and conventionally, through the guise of the deity's body and mudrā, one purifies all the accumulated obscurations and accomplishes all accomplishments. Through Vajrasūrya, with discriminating wisdom one knows the various entities of phenomena without mixing them, makes the hosts of disciples rejoice, and should accomplish all activities such as offering. Through Paramāśva, with the wisdom that accomplishes activities, one destroys all arrogance, such as conceptual thought, and through one's own guise one is joined to the work of enlightened activity. Thus the root tantra says:

For the superknowledges of this life and so forth,
and to accomplish omniscience in all other times,
the modes of great wisdom,
toward all phenomena without exception,
through attachment and the other modes,
purify the maṇḍalas of the families.

And again:

By the power of making clear the rites
of approach and completion of the six family maṇḍalas,
a body in accord with the action of the great seal
will arise in each one's own continuum.
The five superknowledges and the rest
are to be accomplished in one's own support;
by the power of applying knowledge of the guise,
all accomplishments will arise.

The condensed meaning is this. In order that the yogin who has generated the immeasurables for the sake of self and others may deliver all beings, having first vowed to deliver oneself, one who possesses the yoga contemplates the empowerment, gathers the accumulations with regard to the noble ones, gathers the accumulations with regard to sentient beings, meditates on the protection wheel, and undertakes any of the following: the meditative absorption of the ground, the meditative absorption of the support, the meditative absorption of union, the act of focusing on the wisdom maṇḍala, the act of focusing on the maṇḍala in front, the act of focusing on the nondual maṇḍala, and the subsequent yoga. Then, relying on the respective hand emblems of the six families, one should apply the heart of Dharma to the work of accomplishment. Their meaning is explained at length in the root tantra; here it is taught only in condensed form. This has been the explanation of the meaning of "knowing the wisdom mudrā."

Now chapter twenty-one, by way of knowing the consciousness mudrā, is for observing the omens of this world's signs, of whether the signs are accomplished or not accomplished, and so forth. First, bringing down a prasena divination through Vajrasattva:

Vajrasattva, glorious in union,
whatever form and shape he has,

and so forth. Abiding in the yoga of glorious Vajrasattva and relying on one's own mudrā, one recites the heart mantra and the heart of Dharma a hundred thousand times, invites the maṇḍala deities, makes offerings to them, and dissolves them into oneself as nondual. Then, in a place adorned with ornaments, one should prepare a maṇḍala well and adorn it with flowers, bathe a boy or a girl, whichever is suitable, and bless the child, clothed and adorned. Having released the vajra mudrā upon that child, one should bring down the maṇḍala of the family, dissolve it into the child, and please it with offerings. Then, having recited the secret mantra a hundred thousand times, one should ask the child how the omens of the signs appear.

Again, through the buddha Vairocana, with the passage beginning:

Earth, water, fire, wind and space,
and all the three realms, from everything,

one who possesses the yoga of the family should observe the omens of the signs from the five elements in the same way as before. For Heruka, with:

Wholly, all, the self of all,
and all the qualities of desire,

one should examine it in the same way. For Padmanarteśvara, with the passage beginning:

Attached through attachment to the lotus,
opening the various lotuses,

one should examine it in the same way. For Vajrasūrya, with the passage beginning:

Flowers, incense, supreme lamps,
perfume, and in all the offerings,

one should examine it in the same way. For Paramāśva's play, with the passage beginning:

Touched and untouched, the great vajra,
sword, mirror, bow and arrow,

one should examine it in the same way. If one asks what is to be examined, then the limb of the rite in passages such as:

What is to be done and what is not to be done,
what is lost and what is not lost,

should be applied on any occasion when one's work is complete, or for the sake of disciples in an image maṇḍala, or also at the times of accomplishment and of receiving the accomplishments of enlightened activity. This has been the explanation of the meaning of "knowing the consciousness mudrā."

Now chapter twenty-two, on knowing the mudrās of all protections. First the yoga of Vajrasattva is explained, with the words beginning:

The glorious union of Vajrasattva,
endowed with form and shape just as they are,

Thus, one who possesses the yoga of glorious Vajrasattva takes ash from a charnel ground, a temple of the gods, or a shrine of the mātṛkās, whichever is available, blesses it with recitation of one's own mantra, and makes a dot or three finger-stripes on whoever one wishes to protect. In the same way, one who possesses the yoga should protect with water, protect with white mustard seed, protect with the body of the deity as a solitary hero, and protect through the yoga of the elaborate maṇḍala. The condensed meaning of these is as follows. In order to protect, one should first carry out the stages such as gathering the accumulations, together with the protection wheel and union with the wisdom deities; then, placing any of the three kinds of support in front, one blesses it by reciting one's own secret mantra a hundred thousand times; the very person one wishes to protect takes refuge in the Three Jewels; and having blessed that person with the mantra, one should protect him or her. If one lacks those substances, then by one's own meditative absorption, once it has become clear, one should protect through the two yogas, the condensed and the elaborate.

Now, in order to protect by other yogas, the words beginning:

Vajrasattva, glorious in union,
endowed with the union that is the self of all buddhas,

are spoken. Through the yoga of Vairocana one protects with the wheel mudrā; likewise one protects with the mudrā of Heruka, protects with the mudrā of Padmanarteśvara, protects with the mudrā of Vajrasūrya, and protects with the mudrā of Paramāśva's play. The condensed meaning is that, abiding in the yoga of each family, one makes clear the sign of one's own mudrā through the rites of meditation and recitation, and should then place it upon the very person to be protected. As for the limb of the rite, one should apply the four activities according to the circumstances and protect by way of the rite of the family. Their benefits are not explained, because they are easy to understand.

Again, for the yoga of Paramāśva it is explained according to the rite. One requests the wisdom maṇḍala to depart and gathers in the pledge maṇḍala; then one protects by making the body of the lord of the family clear again, and the very one to be protected enters into one's own body or into that of any deity, as an armor and a fastened coat of mail. Adorning that one with a jeweled crown and hanging silk streamers, one should make it firm by clapping the hands joyfully three times. This has been the explanation of the meaning of "the hand emblems and mudrās of all protections."

Now, from chapter twenty-three, called "The Mudrā of Healing All," first the rite for dispelling illness is explained:

Displaying the manner of one who contemplates the Dharma,
and with the two vajra fists,

and so forth. First, in order to dispel fevers and poisons, through one-pointed yoga one acts upon the sick person's body with the vajra-fist mudrā and a peaceful gaze, and casts out the ugly images of the fever and the poison.

Moreover, against cold fevers one protects with the body and with a mass of red light rays, and against hot fevers with one the color of crystal water. Again, in order to turn back illness and remove suffering, one places the one-pointed vajra mudrā between the eyebrows in the manner of a crown protuberance, looks toward the directions and intermediate directions, and should destroy all illness and suffering. To remove a piercing pain, one makes that dry one-pointed vajra mudrā as though drawing it out. To pacify eye disease, one smears the mudrā of the Glorious Accomplished Eye on the eyes. To dispel diseases of the brain, one draws the illnesses toward the mouth with a tight vajra fist, and, possessing the yoga of the deity, drinks them. Its mantra and mudrā are tathāgata and so forth. The condensed meaning is this: having made the yoga of the family clear and blessed one's own body, speech and mind, in order to protect others one recites one's own mantra and should apply it to their tasks by way of the respective mudrās.

Now, in order to protect by mudrās:

With the right hand,
having bound the crown protuberance of the hero,

and so forth. First, with the mudrā of the crown protuberance one protects children; with the mudrā of the serpent's hood one protects against the workings of poison; with the wheel mudrā one frees from all seizing spirits; with the vajra mudrā one accomplishes all activities; and their benefits are taught. The condensed meaning is that, abiding in the yoga, one recites one's own secret mantras ten thousand or a hundred thousand times, and when applying them to the work of protection, one attaches oṃ rakṣa rakṣa hūṃ as the closing mantra and, by way of what has been taught, should protect and bless whoever is to be protected.

Now, to teach the yoga of healing:

Through the glorious union of Vajrasattva,
having mounted all the horses,

and so forth. One who possesses the yoga of glorious Vajrasattva should, by way of mantra, mudrā and meditative absorption, heal horses, heal elephants, heal birds, and heal trees. This has been the explanation of the meaning of "the mudrā of healing all."

Now, from chapter twenty-four, called "The Mudrā of All Weapons," first, to teach the five kinds of weapons:

Hurled by a machine, not hurled,
and likewise hurled by hand,

and so forth. In order to turn back all hostile assailants and make oneself invincible to others, he destroys others through the use of the five weapons. Among them, those hurled by a machine are fire catapults, water catapults, stone catapults and the like. Those not hurled are the sword, the spear, the trident and the like. Those hurled by hand are the discus, the arrow, the stone and the like. Those held back after being hurled are the noose, the corded spear and the like. Those wielded by strength are kicks of the foot, the fist of the hand, and the like.

Now, by way of the mudrās of the ten piercings, the words beginning:

Shape, ease, union,
complete union, drawing, and swiftness,

are spoken: the mudrā of shape, the mudrā of ease, the mudrā of union, the mudrā of complete union, the mudrā of drawing, the mudrā of swiftness, the mudrā of filling, the mudrā of hurling, the mudrā of clarity, the mudrā of pressing down, and the mudrā of variety. The condensed meaning is that, at the time of the protection wheel and so forth, one should undertake the work of the ten piercings by way of the deities of enlightened activity, Heruka, Paramāśva and the others.

Now, in order to perform the work by way of the limb of giving:

Hurled forcefully and hurled evenly,
and likewise hurled like lightning,

and so forth. These teach the mudrā of all means, the mudrā of certain repelling, the mudrā of all seizing, and their benefits; these should be applied as limbs of all the rites above. This has been the explanation of the meaning of "the mudrā of all weapons."

Now, from chapter twenty-five, called "The Mudrā of All Activities," first, that of Vajrasattva:

Within the two ring fingers
one should place the thumbs well,

and so forth. As the self of all the families he applies himself to enlightened activity, and possessing the mudrā that unites all enlightened activities into one through Vajrasattva's vast accumulations of merit and wisdom, he summons and accomplishes all accomplishments and all hosts of activities. To teach those of the other family maṇḍalas as well, the words beginning:

Clench the vajra fist well,
and place one's thumb inside,

are spoken. The meaning is this: through the yoga of Vairocana one accomplishes pacifying activity; through the yoga of glorious Heruka one accomplishes wrathful activity; through the yoga of Padmanarteśvara one accomplishes magnetizing activity; through the yoga of Vajrasūrya one accomplishes enriching activity; and through the yoga of Paramāśva's play one accomplishes various activities. Possessing the respective mantras, mudrās and yogas for these aims, one should undertake one's own enlightened activity. The condensed meaning is that, when undertaking enlightened activities that rely on fire offerings, yantras and mudrās, one first completes the preliminary limbs of the rite and the stages of approach, and then, when applying oneself to each respective activity, one should be equipped with these mudrās. This has been the explanation of the meaning of "the mudrā of all activities."

Now, from chapter twenty-six, called "The Mudrā of Glorious Vajra Trampling," first, from the general characteristics of trampling:

With the postures of the leg stretched out, of pressing down,
and of drawing it in and lifting it,
by the union of right and left,
all forms are to be trampled.

Having become the self of all the families, he abides in the manner of the trampling postures of the four activities, and abides trampling the males and females of all beings with the two feet of skillful means and wisdom.

Again, the manner in which the principal deities of the maṇḍala trample with their feet is spoken in the passage beginning:

Proud through the power of one free of evil,
having trampled glorious Vairocana,

They abide in three ways: as abiding in the aspect of the cause, abiding in the aspect of the path, and abiding in the aspect of the result. In terms of the first, they trample the very afflictions of the disciples of the families, and generate and summon the result that arises from that. In terms of the second, yogins who train in following each family maṇḍala directly purify the continuum of their three doors and summon the accomplishments that accord with each family. Third, by the power of the disciples, they trample with their feet the sugatas who are not different from themselves, summon their consorts, and are shown as if enjoying them.

Again, in order to trample with the feet the causes and results of the defining characteristics and to enjoy the goddesses of the result:

Having trampled awakening and the perfections,
accomplishments, dhāraṇīs and the grounds,
the families and the mudrās,
I enjoy them as I examine them.

This is enjoying the thirty-seven factors of awakening, the ten perfections, perfect accomplishment, the gates of dhāraṇī, the ten grounds, the families and the mudrās. Further, the goddesses of the result are seventy, as the root tantra says:

Joining with the bases of the tenfold awakening,
completing the work of the ten perfections,
practicing the ten mudrās of accomplishment,
entering the mode of the qualities of the ten dhāraṇīs,
perfecting the wisdom of the ten grounds,
the power of the ten activities of the families,
making clear the ten majestic, subjugating mudrās,
one enters the abode of all the goddesses.

He tramples the goddesses with his feet and enjoys them.

Further, in order to trample with the feet the arrogant gods and spirits of the world, the passage beginning:

Proud through the power of one free of evil,
having trampled the supreme Lord,

is spoken. He tramples Maheśvara, Viṣṇu, Brahmā, Kāmadeva, the hosts of mātṛkās, the ḍākinīs, the yakṣas and the others, the eight classes of gods and nāgas, the kings of men and the rest, and enjoys the hosts of their wives.

Further, in order to trample with the feet all phenomena of saṃsāra and nirvāṇa and to enjoy their results, it says:

All the lords, without exception,
of the moving and the unmoving,
I, the yogin, having trampled them,
enjoy them as my own enjoyment.

The condensed meaning is this: at the time of the enlightened activity of those on the path, one should have faith in the treasury of the Victors' intent and train in following it; the clinging thoughts of the powerful worldly gods and others are turned back; and the manner of the result and of enlightened activity is taught together with its qualities. This has been the explanation of the meaning of "the mudrā of glorious vajra action."

Now, from chapter twenty-seven, called "The Mudrā of the Lord of All Accomplishments," in order to teach in accordance with the yoga of the six families, the words beginning:

The fist held at the heart
is explained as the woman's mudrā,

are spoken. Through the yoga of glorious Vajrasattva, for the sake of accomplishment, one places the woman's fist at one's own heart and rests the left hand, with the bell, at the hip; then again, with the mudrā of the right hand, one raises the five-pointed vajra upward and holds it in the manner of praise; by this all women come under one's power and one applies oneself to all activities. Again, through the mudrā of the yoga of Vairocana he makes the lower grounds rise up; through the mudrā of the yoga of glorious Heruka he brings forth the female lords of vajra illusion; through the mudrā of the yoga of Padmanarteśvara he purifies all phenomena and joins the hosts of joy; through the mudrā of the yoga of Vajrasūrya he fulfills all wishes; and through the mudrā of the yoga of Paramāśva he makes one a supreme lord. As for the limb of the yoga, having completed approach, close approach and accomplishment, on the occasion of great accomplishment one should, equipped with the respective mudrās, apply them to the work of accomplishment. This has been the explanation of the meaning of "the mudrā of the lord of all accomplishments."

The pledge of the all-various is to abide in the pledge by possessing the twenty-seven groups of mudrās and the various subtle mudrās that arise from them. This has been the explanation of the meaning of the ninth chapter.

Having taught the distinctions of the view of the phenomena of the ground, the benefits of the path, the means of practicing it, and the means of accomplishing the two kinds of path, then as for the result that arises from them: in order to teach, with regard to this world, the eight accomplishments and so forth that arise from the accomplishments of minor activities and from common rites, and the manner in which the various inferior worldly activities are made into supreme accomplishments, the deeds of the result together with enlightened activity are taught in accordance with the meaning of chapter four, of "the mudrā of glorious vajra action," and of chapter ten.

Here, in order to explain the meaning of what was spoken in the first chapter, by way of union, in two verses beginning:

I myself am all the buddhas,
and I am all the heroes,

from the teaching of the characteristics of the result, the pledge to explain it first is this:

Then this accomplishment
called the mudrā of the all-various,
through the yoga of the all-various,
I shall explain as all-various.

That is: by the very manner in which the six bodies are spontaneously present, from their various emanations and in accordance with how they appear to disciples, the divisions of the all-various mudrā are known; by the power of accomplishment, beings on the path see them appear as the clear results that accord with it; and by applying in all various ways the enlightened activities performed through it, they appear as various for the sake of sentient beings. This is what I shall explain.

In explaining it according to that meaning, first, in order to release the mudrā of the dance method endowed with union, it says:

With brows and eyes, with teeth and lips,
with mouth and body, feet and hands,
dancing in keeping with the attire,
all the mudrās are completely released.

Glorious Vajrasattva, through the dance method of union, enters absorption in various enlightened activities for the sake of beings; through the brows, the eyes and so forth he radiates the light rays of the dance method, and he sends forth the maṇḍalas of mudrā for the sake of beings and releases them.

Again, in order to accomplish in accordance with the meaning of meditating on the maṇḍala, it says, "Vajrasattva, glorious union, joined with all the goddesses," and so forth. That glorious Vajrasattva, never parted from the assembly of goddesses, enjoys them: this is the all-various good fortune. As the aim of having first meditated on the maṇḍala, the accomplishment of bliss will be attained.

Again, these two verses follow on. Their meaning is also taught: they apply as well to meditating on the deity, to inviting the wisdom maṇḍala and requesting it to depart, to gathering in the maṇḍala and releasing the mudrās, and to the rites of protection. The meaning of the Victor's word has the character of many intentions; by practicing and applying them, these powers will be accomplished. Their benefits are taught in a single verse, which is easy to understand.

Again, in order to show the connection by which those essences of the result arise, two verses are spoken, beginning:

Through union equal with all the buddhas,
one becomes equal to all the buddhas.

Of these, first, the characteristic of the view is realized and made clear through union equal with the two buddhas; then, if one practices the dharmas of the path of skillful means that accomplish its meaning, the result in accordance with it is clearly accomplished.

What is that like? It appears in the manner of qualities together with enlightened deeds. Two verses are spoken, beginning:

The body one never tires of beholding
displays itself as the self of all.

The arrayed six bodies of the Sugata, adorned with a mass of precious qualities, the body one never tires of beholding, show themselves to the mind-streams of disciples; they bestow the excellent result, the path of bliss and the various accomplishments, and utter such words as "Excellent!", granting everything up to the supreme accomplishment.

Again:

The rite of worshipping oneself:
with the boundless multitudes of arrays
of all the clouds of offerings
arising for all the senses,
he worships the self of all.

Having gained mastery over all the bodies and wisdoms and become the self of all, the eighty great qualities of a buddha and the three hundred and sixty enlightened activities are complete in his mind-stream, and the clouds of offerings of the sense objects at all times become boundless and various arrays; he enjoys them in the manner of non-duality with himself.

Again, in order to seal, it is taught together with its benefits in three verses, beginning:

The glorious left, with a graceful pose,
of the glorious right hand.

Of these: the attire of the mudrā abiding clearly, in the manner of applying and sealing it to all activities such as the accomplishments, the left hand, the bell mudrā, is placed at the hip, and the right, the lotus fist, is placed at the forehead; raising the gaze of the eyes upward, one should enter the abode of accomplishment. Why is this taught at the stage of the result? In order to enter its abode in a manner that accords with the enlightened deeds. "Eyes like lotuses flung upward" means that, through the complete purity of all dharmas, in the manner of gazing upon all the activities of accomplishment, the mudrās and the answering mudrās are shown.

Now, among the benefits, first, in order to show the characteristic of a scripture that is certain and unchanging:

Through the union of all the buddhas,
ever endowed with immeasurable life,
to the boundless reaches of the expanse of space,
they proclaim the words of truth.

Through the definitive vehicle, the buddhas, the sugatas together with their heirs, having filled these worlds of the great trichiliocosm throughout, with their ever unchanging body, speech and mind expound the various tantras, the words of truth of the resultant Vajra Vehicle, which is the meaning of the Great Vehicle.

Again, there is taught the point that the qualities of the tantra and the maṇḍala are beyond expression. For that a single verse is spoken, which is easy to understand.

Again, it is taught that the result abides as innate, together with its secret characteristic:

Accomplished through power and through nature,
ever accomplished by its essential nature,
accomplished in accordance with each, it grants bliss:
this is the supreme secret, supreme of the supreme.

Because it arises from the power of having gathered the two collections of merit and wisdom, and from the strength of all dharmas abiding primordially as the nature of the result and of that very nature being made clear by its essential nature, whatever one accomplishes follows each result that accords with one's karma, and through accomplishing it the supreme wisdom of bliss arises. Therefore it is the supreme of the supreme meaning of the whole secret vehicle.

Furthermore, the Victor, free of all conceptualization, has no deeds whatsoever: for although he teaches the various tantras and families, activities and maṇḍalas, he is free of the conceptual clinging that grasps at them. Therefore one and a half verses are spoken, beginning:

For the sake of beings the Victor does not at all
perform the purifying and the applying of actions.

It is taught that whoever realizes such a meaning is the very source of all qualities:

Whoever accomplishes bliss through their own bliss
is now made steadfast by the supreme accomplishment.

Through the power of one's own form being clear as granting whatever is wished, the supreme accomplishment becomes stable, and one has always entered the accomplishments of body, speech and mind. By these the meaning of the benefits has been explained.

Now, as it is taught in the final section of the root tantra, because the Lord was pleased with the rejoicing of those who, through the teaching, had gained faith and delight, it says:

When the All-Various had spoken thus,
the Blessed One, the Tathāgata, was ever delighted.

In the manner of himself being sublime to himself, from the maṇḍala of his heart he brought forth the meaning of his intent just as it is, through his glorious throat; he spread it upon the lotus of his tongue and, from between his forty-two excellent teeth, taught it in the voice of Brahmā; and it became his own heart's commitment. Moreover, having entrusted the teaching of the root tantra in two ways, with a great smile of wonder he brought forth the radiance of the retinue, and with "a la la" this heart of the teaching became supreme. Why is that? It is said, "He teaches in the manner of the non-duality of the profound and the vast," and so forth.

After that, the assemblies of the retinue too made offerings, circumambulations and so forth, and offered praise. Therefore it says:

To what glorious Vajrasattva had so well spoken,
they gave praise and became filled with delight.

"The samaya rite of the All-Various Good Fortune" means this: that very buddha, whose nature is the spontaneous presence of all six bodies, abides as the self of saṃsāra and nirvāṇa, as the source and the nature of all dharmas; therefore he is the good fortune of the all-various. By the word śrī it is meant that he is never parted from all the collections of qualities, and that he teaches this very thing to others too by way of samaya.

This concludes the explanation of the meaning of the tenth chapter.

Glorious body, the very self of all the Victors,
supreme being whose existence has no beginning or end,
source from which the many families and the various maṇḍalas arise,
supreme protector, lord of compassion: how marvelous!

Tantra of an ocean of secrets, complete in the essence of intent,
exceedingly hard to fathom, a precious form of light in the realm of darkness,
destroying manifold conceptions and teaching all the rites,
mudrā of great bliss: wondrous and marvelous!

The two minds of the one who grants refuge are like sun rays rising in turn,
a clear and many-faceted jewel dispelling the darkness of the mind;
its many modes of fine explanation are the domain of the learned,
and it is free of contradiction with the explanation: how marvelous!

Those of later times, with many conceptions and endless opinions,
hard to please even with the well-spoken words of the Victors,
may on account of me fall into imputing, denying and disparaging:
may the stains of the obscurations they gather be purified!

Adorned with dustless snow mountains and precious Mount Meru,
by the radiance of the stars, the full moon and the sun,
may they be free of all adverse conditions, enter the excellent path,
and attain the state of a peerless wheel-turning king!

The Explanation of the Meaning of the Supreme Glorious Union with All Buddhas, Ḍākinī Māyā, composed by the great scholar of glorious Oḍḍiyāna, who is like the sun of Jambudvīpa, the accomplished master, the great king Indranāla, is complete.

It was translated and edited by the Indian preceptor Vidyākarasiṃha and the chief editor-translator, the venerable Lhé Rinpoché, and later properly finalized in accordance with the new language standard.

Translator's notes

"The tantra that is symbolized" reads མཚོན་བྱའི for the written མཚོན་ཆའི ("of weapons"), which makes no sense here; the next section of the text discusses the symbolized and the symbolizing (མཚོན་བྱ་མཚོན་བྱེད). The king named སྨེ་ཁྲོ་རྒྱལ་པོ could not be identified with confidence and is translated literally as "Wrathful Impurity". "The one like the day" (ཉིན་མཚུངས) may be a personal name in the lineage; rendered by meaning. "Drop of Magical Display" renders སྒྱུ་འཕྲུལ་ཐིག་ལེ, likely the Māyājāla Tilaka tantra. The twenty-first item reads སྲ་སྲེ་ཡོ་གའི་ལྟས; the sense of སྲ་སྲེ is unclear and is rendered tentatively as "firmness and blending". The six deities listed for the fifth chapter (Vajrasattva, Vairocana, Heruka, Narteśvara, Sūrya, Paramāśva) correspond to the six families of the Sarvabuddhasamāyoga. "The Glorious Sacred Garland" renders དཔལ་ཕྲེང་བ་དམ་པ, a work whose commentary is cited for the classification of tantras; it could not be identified with confidence. "The Seven Chapters" renders རྟོག་པ་བདུན, which may instead refer to the seventh chapter of the tantra. The seven animals and the lamp in the verse are the seven proofs by example announced earlier. In "the blazing jewel are joined" the Tibetan has ཀ་ཏ་སྦྱར་བས, where ཀ་ཏ is unclear; it is rendered by the general sense of joining. "In terms of shape" reads དབྱིབས་ཀྱི་སྒོ་ནས for the written སྒྲོ་ནས, a copyist's slip. The citations given as from རྟོག་པ་བདུན་པ are rendered as the Seven Chapters, as earlier, though the phrase may mean the seventh chapter. "The thirty-three abodes" renders སུམ་ཅུ་རྩ་གསུམ་གྱི་གནས; in this context of the inner winds it seems to refer to a set of thirty-three inner sites or conceptions rather than the Heaven of the Thirty-Three. "Gathered from the ten places into four hundred thousand koṭis" renders bye ba bzhi 'bum literally; the intended count (perhaps of channels or breaths) is unclear. "Kata" (ka ta) at the juncture of Vajradhātvī and the blazing jewel is left untranslated, as its exact sense in this sexual-yogic context is uncertain. In the root verse, "there is no attachment" renders chags med, which the commentary treats as the first of three unobserved states (attachment, freedom from attachment, the middle). "Seal" for rgya in the root verse follows the commentary's gloss as phyag rgya. "The actual buddha and the buddha of realization" renders dngos rtogs kyi sangs rgyas gnyis; the exact pairing intended is uncertain. "Good conception" renders bzang rtog, apparently the purified, deity-oriented conception contrasted with ordinary conceptual thought. The "wild sheep" is the gna' bu (bharal), the commentary's chapter-emblem example, parallel to the soaring garuḍa earlier. śambara abhiṣiñca appears to be offered as a Sanskrit etymology linking "śam" (bliss) with abhiṣeka (empowerment, literally "sprinkling"); the phrase "the reasoning applied to the sounds of the Sanskrit" renders gtan tshigs tshangs pa'i sgra sbyar ba, and its exact sense is uncertain. "The master, the Second Buddha" (slob dpon sangs rgyas gnyis pa) is an epithet whose referent is not identified here. "Hawk" renders bya lag, a bird of prey whose precise species is uncertain. "Bringing about the marks and minor marks where these are lacking and unclear" renders a difficult clause (mtshan dang dpe byad mi ldan pa dang mi gsal bdag grub par mdzad nas) whose wording may be faulty; the sense is uncertain. "Four of the tongue-root class and two unclear ones" (lce rtsa can bzhi, mi gsal gnyis) are the extra letters that bring the consonants to forty; the precise letters intended are not specified. Madana, Jayakara and Madhukara are reconstructed from myos byed, rgyal bar byed pa and sbrang rtsir byed pa, the lords of the three worlds. Paramādya renders dpal mchog dang po. "In this twofold way" renders don gnyis kyi tshul, referring apparently to the pairing of phenomena and their nature just described. The pun in "joined" plays on the Tibetan word for yoga (rnal 'byor) and 'byor pa, "joined, endowed." "Restraining the Elephant" renders the title glang po rab 'gog literally; I could not identify its Sanskrit form with confidence. The examples "garuḍa and its young" (gna' bu) and "garuḍa and the lesser birds" (bya lag) are tentative readings of rare terms. The long-metre verse from the Condensed Chapters is obscure, and its pronoun references (who relies on whom) are uncertain; it is rendered closely. "Chapter" renders rtog pa (Skt. kalpa), which the final sentence glosses. The verse from the Condensed Chapters plays on rtog pa, which means both "chapter" (Skt. kalpa) and "conception"; the pun cannot be fully kept in English. The opening sentence of the second chapter's commentary (dmigs su med pa stong phrag) is terse; it is read as an analogy of aimless jewel-seekers. "Descents" renders dbab pa, the bringing down of blessing or wisdom in practice. In "the first of the sublime," tho ma is read as thog ma, "beginning." The Seven Chapters verse on the postures plays on them: standing (langs pa) as conceptions rising up, sitting as blocking the objects, walking as the arhats' single file, laughing and speaking as brahmins and bodhisattvas. "The arhat who realizes conditions" renders rkyen rtogs dgra bcom, the pratyekabuddha. "Like the wrestler's jewel" alludes to the simile of the wrestler who does not know the jewel lodged in his own brow; kun du chol is read as "lost." The final sentence's "even with only the accumulations gathered through body and speech" renders a terse phrase (lus ngag gi tshogs bsags kyang); in light of the verse ("though one's merit be small") the sense is that slight accumulation does not prevent success. In the verse "what is not attained in ten million eons," bya ba is read as bye ba (ten million), as the commentary itself has it. The peacock simile alludes to the traditional image of the peacock whose plumage grows brighter from eating poison, as bad conduct becomes pure Dharma for the realized yogin. "Seal" in "merely by accomplishing this seal" renders rgya, taken as the mudrā. "No one is to be passed over on account of his state or his entry" renders a terse transition (gnas pa dang 'jug pa'i sgo nas 'da' bar bya ba ma yin); the sense is that no one is excluded from the practice. In the verse "with all the great clouds of offerings," mtshon pa is read as mchod pa, "offering," following the commentary's "clouds of offerings." "Withering" renders 'jums pa among the ritual activities. "Poison" renders དུག as written ("the enjoyments of desire, which are poison"); a reading དྲུག, "the six enjoyments of desire," is also possible. In the final sentence རིགས་དྲུག is taken as the six buddha families, since it stands beside the six bodies and six wisdoms, rather than the six classes of beings. Each of the six root verses here appears to be an abbreviated verse, beginning with a line such as "By the very power of union with glorious Vajrasattva," explained in turn by the commentary for each of the six buddhas. The Lotus Lord in the last verse is taken as an abbreviation of Padmanarteśvara. In the first root verse the text reads "the pledge of suffering"; the translation follows the commentary, which explains this line as "the lovely pledge," taking the reading as a slip for "lovely." The phrase rendered "into exaltation of sinful action" (in the root verse, "by the power of the sinless, into exaltation") is obscure; it seems to mean that the yogin, through love and compassion, turns what would ordinarily be sinful conduct into a cause of elevation. The count of body hairs reads literally "koṭi, four hundred thousand," and its exact figure is uncertain. In the six-family verse, "Lord of the World" renders the epithet the commentary explains as Padmanarteśvara gazing on all beings (the title Lokeśvara). The names of the six grounds at the end are translated from the Tibetan. In the second root-tantra verse the text reads "padmo sbyin" ("Lotus Giver"); in the parallel prose of the preceding passage the corresponding ground is "Lotus Eye" (padma spyan), so the evident reading "Lotus Eye" is translated. The line "the yogin does not act for any purpose" (rnal 'byor pa yis don mi bya) is terse; it appears to describe purposeless, spontaneous conduct, but the precise sense is uncertain. "The two passions" freed from in passion likely refers to attachment to the two partners or the two extremes; the text does not specify. "Buddhas of the provisional sense" (drang ba'i sangs rgyas) apparently denotes the buddhahood of the tenth ground as presented in the Characteristics Vehicle, as opposed to definitive buddhahood; the exact sense is uncertain. Sbyor ba, rjes su sbyor ba, shin tu sbyor ba and sbyor ba chen po are rendered as yoga, anuyoga, atiyoga and mahāyoga, the four phases of approach and accomplishment. The final sentence is terse; it is read as distributing approach and near approach to the accumulation of merit, and accomplishment and great accomplishment to the accumulation of wisdom. Bkra shis brtan ma, "the Auspicious Steadfast One," wife of Viṣṇu, is taken as Lakṣmī (Śrī); dka' zlog grub pa mo as Umā; dga' rab dbang phyug as Kāmadeva and dga' ba mo as his wife Rati. "Great Accomplished Lady of All the Buddhas" and "Supreme Sovereign Lady," consorts of Paramāśva, are translated literally; whether they are one figure or two is unclear. In the first verse on seizing, nyes par ("wrongly") is read as nye bar ("intimately"), as in the parallel verses. "Lam pa'i dbang du" is obscure and read as "bringing under control through the path." "Lce pog" is taken as a kind of incense; uncertain. "Lha mo gnyis" is read as "the second, among the goddesses," i.e. the second rite, for Lakṣmī. Several ritual materials are uncertain: sna ma is taken as jasmine, rams as indigo, and ren de is left untranslated as unidentified; gnod mdzes me tog is taken as "Śaṅkara flowers," a flower named after Śiva, but the identification is uncertain. "The recitation that makes the single root clear" (rtsa gcig) is obscure. "Human emblems" renders na ra'i mtshan ma, uncertain. The final mantra continues into the next piece. The deities of the three rites in verse are not named in the Tibetan except for Brahmāṇī and Indra; the first, with arrows and an army of intoxication, appears to be Kāmadeva, and the second, four-faced and golden, Brahmā. "Lhang tsher" is an unidentified pigment and is left untranslated. In the intermediate-direction line of the second rite, the Tibetan word for "brahmin" stands inside the mantra and is translated. In the opening verse, "the supreme lord and the excellent lord" are taken from the commentary's gloss as Maheśvara and Kāmadeva respectively, and the verse's Mahendra corresponds to the commentary's Indra. The commentary calls Viṣṇu "the teacher of the gods," as the text has it. The "three verses" are cited by their opening and closing lines, which belong to the root tantra and are not quoted in full here. The numbered items (second, fifth, third, fourth) refer to chapters of the root tantra. In the verse "the steadfast one, lord of great sovereignty," the phrase rendered "great sovereignty" is the same word elsewhere used for the god Maheśvara; here, in the light of the "eight sovereignties" that follow, it is read as a description of the attainment rather than the god's name. In the quotation from the Seven Chapters, the Tibetan reads "island" where "narrative setting" is plainly meant; the evident reading has been translated. The names of the eon (Excellent Ornament), the buddha (Glorious Excellent Host) and the monk (Banner of Liberation) could not be identified with confidence and are translated literally; "an arrogant one with a host" may also be a proper name of the being he became. The commentary plays on the word for the "ritual systems" in the verse, which also means "conception," and explains it as the conceptual views to be destroyed. The comparison with sendhapa evidently refers to the Sanskrit word saindhava, a stock example of a word with several meanings. The play on "ḍākinī" as "sky-goer" underlies the five kinds of "going in the sky." The Arrangement of the Secret Meaning of Vajrasattva is taken as the title of a text; it could also be read as a description of the teaching. In the passage on the support for the accumulations, "elements" in the verse is read by the commentary first as the precious metals of which images are made and then, on the profound level, as the inner winds, cakras and faculties. In the description of the non-abiding nirvāṇa, "that does not abide in any extreme" spells out the sense of the standard term. The names of the eon (Hard to Grasp), the buddha (Lion of Supreme Strength), the monk (Patient One) and the being he became (Fierce Mass of Darkness) could not be identified with certainty and are translated literally. "The partial views" renders a term for a tīrthika position that may name a particular school; its identification is uncertain. The figures given for the maṇḍalas add up as the text states: seventy-one, four times one hundred and twenty-eight, and one hundred and thirty make seven hundred and thirteen. The commentary plays on རྟོག་པ, which means both "conception" and "kalpa," a ritual section or chapter of a tantra; the verse and its explanation turn on this double sense. ཡིད་དཔྱོད is rendered as presumptive or conjectural cognition, the technical term for a cognition that is neither direct perception nor valid inference. ལྷ་མ་ཉེས་པར is read as ལྷ་མཉེས་པར, "to please the deities." Here དབང་པོ means the faculties of practitioners, not Indra. The explanation of "Then" (de nas) refers to the Sanskrit word it renders (presumably atha), read with a short sound as "immediately" and with a long sound as an auspicious word. In this passage དབང་པོ means the sense faculties, not Indra. The names of the eon, the buddha, the master and the disciple in the Rudra narrative could not be confidently identified with Sanskrit forms, so they are translated by meaning: Holder of Six, Moon of Heroes, Wheel of Dharma, and the Black Renunciant. The names of the six songs are given as the Tibetan spells them; they are unidentified, and the division of "niridantaka" (possibly "ridantaka" preceded by a particle) is uncertain. The Howling Hell may correspond to the hell usually called Raurava. "Chapter" renders དབུགས་དབྱུང (ucchvāsa). "Twofold manner" renders བཅས་བཅོས་རྣམ་པ་གཉིས, whose precise sense here is uncertain. "Jewel poison" renders དབྱིག་དུག, explained in the commentary by deceiving others in the guise of a jewel. གུ་ལང is rendered Paśupati, an epithet of Maheśvara (Śiva), as the commentary's gloss "Maheśvara in the form of" suggests; the identification is probable but not certain. ཆུ་ལུགས in the final paragraph is read as ཆ་ལུགས, "guise." The garment terms གཡང་གཞི and ཚམ་ངམ are rendered as "flayed hides" and "shawl" by context. "Binds them" renders སྒྲོག་པ, which can also mean "proclaim"; the sense intended in the objection is not certain. "In the manner called seralkha" leaves the obscure term སེ་རལ་ཁ untranslated, as its meaning is uncertain. The names Supreme Victor and Arrogant One Dwelling at the Extreme, the activity goddess Arrow-Holder, and the wrathful deity Vajra Desire-Wrath are translated literally because their Sanskrit forms could not be identified with confidence; "Hayagrīvā the Lofty" is likewise tentative. The "eon endowed with essence" renders བཅུད་ལྡན, a less common name for one of the world-ages. "Chapter" renders དབུགས་དབྱུང་བ (Skt. āśvāsa), here closing a section. "Fifty hands" renders བརྒྱ་ཕྱེད, literally "half a hundred." "The Lasso of Method" is given in translation, since the Sanskrit form of this tantra's title could not be fixed with confidence. "Not separated from the two kinds of passion" follows the text as written (མ་བྲལ་བ). "Chapter" and "brings relief" both render དབུགས་དབྱུང་བ (Skt. āśvāsa), which closes a section as "chapter" and in the next sentence, with Vajrasūrya as agent, appears to carry its literal sense of giving relief. The names the Wealthy One and the Arrogant Miser are translated literally, as their Sanskrit forms could not be identified with confidence. "The unstable eon" renders བསྐལ་པ་མི་གནས་པ, an uncommon eon name. Several items in the root-tantra description are uncertain: the left-hand line ཁུ་ཚུར་སྣ་ཚོགས may mean a fist with a crossed vajra; the figures beneath his feet, rendered "the Triangle Goddess and Śrī" and "the Liberating Goddess and Ratī", are translated literally where they could not be identified with confidence; "the Three-Eyed One" is Śiva. The names the Arrogant One of Various Magic and the eon Great Splendid Light are translated literally. "Liberates" renders བསྒྲལ, the tantric term for ritually slaying and freeing a being's consciousness. The five arts of combat correspond to the classical Indian division of weapons into those released by a device, not released, released by hand, released and retrieved, and wielded by strength. "Gathered their wives to himself" reads ལྔར ("into five") as ངར, since four wives follow and "into five" makes no sense here. The goddess names She Who Reverses Austerities and the Mistress of Delight are translated literally; the latter is evidently Ratī, Kāmadeva's consort, and རུ་མིག་མ is taken as Rukmiṇī. In the Ninth Chapter quotation, "draws forth the three and the six" renders the obscure གསུམ་དྲུག་དྲོངས and is uncertain. The names of the subdued goddesses and of most of the emanated goddesses (for example Dark Lady of the Charnel Ground, Jewel Glory-Stealer, Disciple Lady, Desire Creeper Lady) could not be identified with confidence with Sanskrit forms and are translated literally. In the first of the four mother-goddess clauses the tamer carries no agent marker, so the named goddess is taken as the one tamed. The text pairs mirror-like wisdom with the compassion family, discriminating wisdom with the jewel family and equality with the lotus family, which departs from the usual scheme; it is translated as it stands. Apart from Tārā, Rudra and the eight Heruka goddesses (Gaurī and the rest, whose Sanskrit names the Tibetan transcribes), the names of the emanated and subdued goddesses could not be identified with confidence and are translated literally. The final clause could also be read as "the king of the māras, the Great God, and the hosts of the arrogant ones," treating the Great God as a separate trainee; it has been rendered that way. The word tshogs is used throughout in two senses, "accumulation" (of merit and wisdom) and "feast" (gaṇa); it is rendered by context, and the commentary deliberately links the two. "The Seventh Ritual Section" renders rtog pa bdun pa, apparently a section (kalpa) of the tantra cycle cited as a source; its exact identity is uncertain. The line "and beginning the stages of the feast" appears to be a quoted root-text phrase marking the connection with the previous chapter. Several of the gem names are uncertain: kartika, aśmarāga, spug, andarnyil and niman are given in the forms the Tibetan uses, since their identities could not be fixed with confidence. Among the instruments, "pearl rattles" (mu tig ha ra) and "clappers" (phe rdob pa) are tentative. The two titles cited for the offering and torma mudrās are taken as the names of ritual works; they might also be read as ordinary instructions to know the mudrās. "Blissful Lady" renders e ma bde ba mo, where the exclamation e ma ("how wonderful") appears to be part of the goddess's name; she is evidently the same goddess called "Blissful Lady" in the earlier account of the maṇḍala's origin. The gem aśmarāga is left in the form the Tibetan gives, as its identity is uncertain. The four face colors of the buddha are given as gold, kārtika, indranīla and aśmarāga; kārtika and aśmarāga are transliterated as they stand, since their exact identification as gems or hues is uncertain. In Caurī's description the text reads "praises" (bstod), which makes little sense; it is taken as a slip for "raises" (stod/btegs). For the last goddess the text reads mchu shel, taken as chu shel, "crystal"; the combination with "green" is rendered as "green with the luster of crystal." Several attendant goddesses' names (All-Buddhas-Proclaiming Lady, Supremely Victorious Lady, Irresistible Jewel-Vessel-Holding Lady, All-Buddhas-Holding Lady, Fair-Faced Lady, Great Cloud Lady, Sky-Eye Lady, Hayagrīva Tall Lady, Variegated-Seat-Holding Lady) are translated literally because their Sanskrit forms cannot be confirmed with confidence. Gaurī's "four faces, eight hands" is translated as written. The text reads "four left hands like" (gyon pa bzhin), evidently a slip for "four left hands" (gyon pa bzhi), and is translated so. "The three siddhis" (dngos grub gsum) trampled by Paramāśva's first right hoof is translated as written, though it may conceal a deity name; zhi ba mo is taken as Śāntī and dga' ba ma as Rati, consort of Kāmadeva, which is likely but not certain. The names Beholding Lady, Earth-Holding Lady, Fierce Lotus Mistress, Lotus-Garbed Lady, Stūpa Lady, Bow-and-Arrow Lady, Vajra Khaṭvāṅga Lady, Vajra Sun Lady and Vajra Fire Lady are translated literally because their Sanskrit forms cannot be confirmed. Padmanarteśvara's "two fifties" of hands is rendered as a hundred. The two Sanskrit words glossed in the commentary (gana bindhura, ghana vihara) are given as spelled; they apparently stand for terms such as gaṇa and vihāra in the root text. "The great red" in the quoted verse is taken, with the commentary that follows, as the "great vermilion," a code term for menstrual blood among the five nectars; "vajra water" and "vajra excrement" are likewise code terms for urine and feces. The sixth-verse passage about the brahmins' sacrifices is obscure: bhṛṅgāra (a ceremonial ewer) and dadura (perhaps dhattūra) are tentative readings of words the Tibetan gives in transliteration. "Fourfold glory" renders dpal bzhi 'gyur literally. The ingredients of the mouth perfume are rendered with standard identifications where these are reasonably secure (sug smel as small cardamom, gla sgang as cyperus, seng ldeng as khadira, shing tsha as cinnamon, na le sham as black pepper); kakkola, patra, lodhra and hasakari are left in their Indian forms, and hasakari is unidentified. The "tamāla peg" (ta ma la'i phur pa) among the erotic arts is rendered literally; its precise reference is uncertain. "The precious gau lute" renders rin chen gau rgyud mangs; the sense of gau is uncertain. "The all-following" renders sna tshogs 'brang ba literally, as the commentary glosses it. "The form of a woman" takes dman pa'i dbyibs in the sense of dman (woman); it could also be read as "a lesser form." "In the stream of speech" renders skad kyi rgyud literally; it may be a slip for skad cig, "in an instant." The opening prose glosses the words of the preceding verse ("how wonderful", "supremely fortunate", "play", "lord", "Disciple Lady", "supreme goddesses"); quotation marks follow the verse wording loosely. "Ḍākinī Māyā" renders mkha' 'gro sgyu ma, the ḍākinī-illusion named in the tantra's title (Ḍākinīmāyāśaṃvara). "Nine moods" renders nyams dgu, the nine dramatic sentiments (navarasa) of the heruka. "Bratakara" renders the transliterated Indic word 'bra ta ka ra in the root-tantra quotation; it appears to name a place or inner site of union, but its Sanskrit form cannot be identified with confidence. "The Seventh Chapter" renders rtog pa bdun pa, presumably the seventh kalpa of the root tantra. "In their own form" reads rang nyid for rang gnyid ("own sleep") as written, an evident slip. The instruction on combining the six lines with the mantras is terse; the rendering follows the Tibetan word order as closely as sense allows. "The speaking mudrā" renders smra byed kyi phyag rgya, apparently a hand gesture used to take up the secret substance; its exact form is uncertain. "The great red" (dmar chen po) is a tantric code term for the female generative substance, kept literal here as in the root verse. In the passage on the ḍākinīs, "the hosts of fierce gods and goddesses" renders drag po drag mo'i tshogs, taken here as a general class rather than the name Rudra. The verse beginning "Having entirely abandoned your own nature" is terse; the commentary takes it as rejecting self-torment through austerity in favor of blissful practice. The verse on the four modes of practice is likewise compressed; its lines are rendered following the commentary's four persons (alone, with consort, with the assembly, and the subsequent emanation practice). The opening syllable ཆ before ཚོགས appears to be a stray syllable and has been left untranslated. "The second buddha" refers to the vajra master, as the heading of the section indicates. In the verse, འདོད་དགུ is rendered "every desirable thing," while the commentary reads it as "the nine kinds of desirable enjoyments." In the Vajrasattva verse, རྟོག་པ is taken in the sense of ritual procedure (kalpa), as the commentary's རྟོག་པའི་ཆོ་ག indicates. "The arrangements of the secret meaning" may refer to a specific text; it is translated literally. The phrase rendered "in the east" reads རྭ་ཕྱོགས in the Tibetan; since the following placements are south, west and north, it has been taken as the eastern direction (perhaps a slip for ཤར་ཕྱོགས). Where the text has རྟོགས་པ་ཐམས་ཅད ("all realizations"), it has been read as རྟོག་པ ("ritual procedures"), following the root verse explained in the previous passage. The last line of the second quoted verse, རེ་དང་དམ་པའི་ཚུལ་དུ་དགོངས, is obscure and has been translated literally. ལྷ་ཆེན་པོ་དག in the list of the outer courtyard is plural and is taken here as the class of great gods, not as the single name the Great God. The final section opening the next chapter alludes to a series of stories that cannot be identified with confidence. The names Renbu, Excellent Beauty, Precious Enjoyment, Discernment, Having a Friend and Golden Protector are literal translations of Tibetan names that could not be securely identified with Sanskrit originals; Ajita (Tibetan མ་ཕམ་པ) and Rāhu (སྒྲ་གཅན) are the standard equivalents but the stories referred to are unclear. The simile "nectar that devours poison at the time one's karma has reached its end" is a tentative rendering of an obscure phrase. In the colophon, "the Sixth Chapter" renders རྟོག་པ་དྲུག་པ, consistent with "the Seventh Chapter" elsewhere. The figures in the opening list of examples (the parrot Lord of Speech, the brahmin's daughter Blue One, King Protector of the Land, Garlanded Threefold Beauty, King Refuge Lion) are from stories not identified; their names are translated literally. The hermitage is said to be "five hundred yojanas" (དཔག་ཚད་ལྔ་བརྒྱ) from a village as the text stands; the standard measure for a hermitage is five hundred fathoms (one krośa), so this may be a scribal slip, but it is translated as written. In the verse, Vajrapāṇi and Lokeśvara stand for the families explained in the commentary as Heruka and Padmanarteśvara. In the list of the disciple's preparation, རྒྱས་པའི་དབང་པོ has been read as རྒྱས་པའི་དབང་དུ ("in the extensive form"), parallel to བསྡུས་པའི་དབང་དུ that follows; ཤེས་པར་བྱ་བའི་དོན་དུ has been read as ཤིས་པར ("for the sake of auspiciousness"), as in the parallel list earlier in the text; and དྲི་ཆད as དྲི་ཆབ ("scented water"). "In the manner of lords" renders དབང་པོའི་ཚུལ་དུ, which could also be read as "in the manner of Indra" or of the presiding powers; the sense is uncertain. In "the purpose of this is that its measurements will not shift," the text has ཉིན ("on that day"), read as ཉིད. "The Condensed Rite" (རྟོག་པ་བསྡུས་པ) is translated literally; I have not identified the work with confidence. "The last" reads ཐམས་ནི as ཐ་མས་ནི, as the sequence "the first ... the second ..." requires. "The eight qualities of the origin" renders ཀུན་འབྱུང་གི་ཡོན་ཏན་བརྒྱད literally; the reference is unclear. The final sentence could also be read with Paramāśva as the one who accomplishes the aims of those to be tamed. In the second line of the quoted verse, བསྐལ་པ་བཟང ("good eon") is read as སྐལ་པ་བཟང ("fortunate"), which the commentary on it requires. "The wondrous supreme bliss" renders ཨེ་མ་བདེ་བ་མཆོག, where ཨེ་མ may be an exclamation or part of a goddess's name; it is unclear. Several goddess names are translated literally because I cannot identify their Sanskrit forms with confidence: Buddha Awakening Lady (སངས་རྒྱས་བྱང་ཆུབ་མ), Dharma Wheel Lady (ཆོས་ཀྱི་འཁོར་ལོ་མ), the Mistress of Desire (འདོད་པའི་དབང་མོ), Buddha All-Enjoying Lady (སངས་རྒྱས་ཀུན་སྤྱོད་མ) and the Heroine (དཔའ་མོ). "Segment" renders ལེའུ་ཚེ, apparently the petal-like divisions of the maṇḍala. Several goddess names could not be identified with confidence and are translated literally: the Lady Holding the Inexhaustible Vessel, the Lady Endowed with the Buddha Body, the Lady with the Beautiful Face, the Great Cloud Lady, the Lady with the Eye of Space, the Lady of Clear Vision, the Earth-Holding Lady, the Lady of Various Lotuses, the Lady with the Lotus Garment and the Lady of Great Offering. "The Subtle Lady" (Tibetan phra mo) stands where the parallel Heruka retinue lists Pramohā; it is kept literal. "The great red" and "the great heart" are coded names for the sacramental substances (blood and heart). The western item of the Heruka maṇḍala names only the skull cup; the following "and" is kept as written. "The great worldly gods" here renders a plural class of mighty mundane deities qualified as "the arrogant ones," not the single Great God (Śiva) named elsewhere in the text. The three-banner gesture is the tripatāka hand of Indian dance, and the lotus circling is the dancer's padmāvartaka turn of the wrist. The names of dance movements in the opening list (the eight soarings, the six changes, the limbed, the great word, the strength of the bee, the necklace, the lion's stretch, the play of the seven positions) are technical terms of the Indian dance treatises that could not be identified with confidence and are translated literally. Muṣṭihara is kept as the transliterated term of the Tibetan; it appears to be a drum or hand-stroke rhythm. Glorious Good Fortune renders dpal skal ba bzang po, the title of the mudrā section, translated literally. The illustrative stories of King Horse Ear, the brahmin woman Excellent One, the feast of the magician Eater, and the peacock and the śirīṣa tree could not be identified with confidence; the names are translated literally. In "perfect cause and perfect conditions" the Tibetan reads dngo rkyen, evidently a slip for dang rkyen. In "whatever path is to be accomplished" the text has lam gang ma dag, read as lam gang dag; taken literally it might mean "whatever impure path." Tsegu cloth renders tshe gu, an unidentified fabric of the lower garment. The bramata (bra ma ta) leaf used in the simile for the hands could not be identified; the term is kept as written. The moon "of the sixteenth day" follows the Tibetan, which presumably means the full moon. The phrase introducing the first of the further goddesses (ཨེ་མའོ་བདེ་བ་མོའི་དམ་ཚིག་ཆགས་པ་མོའི་དམ་ཚིག་གི་བརྡས་མཚོན་ཏེ) is compressed; ཨེ་མའོ is taken as the exclamation that opens the root verse, and "Bliss Lady" and "Passionate Lady" as the goddess names, but the exact division is uncertain. The term སེ་རལ་ཁ describing how Vairocanī leans on the khaṭvāṅga is obscure; it is rendered by sense as leaning against it as a support. Several goddess names here (Lady Who Proclaims All Buddhas, Glorious Lady, She Who Holds the Unbearable Vessel, Buddha Lady of Seven Limbs, Fair-Faced Form Lady, Glorious Accomplished Eye) could not be identified with confidence and are translated by meaning. Here རིགས་དྲུག refers to the six buddha families of this maṇḍala, not the six classes of beings. "With the word cakra" renders འཁོར་ལོའི་སྒྲ་དང་ལྡན་པར, apparently meaning that the term is inserted into each mantra. In "holding the skin of Maheśvara stretched out," the text's བརྒྱད ("eight") makes no sense and is read as a form of "spread, stretched out." The description of world-system arrangements uses terms (སྣྲེལ་ཞི, སྤྱིའུ་ཚུགས) rendered as "crosswise" and "upside down" following their usual sense. Vetālī and Śmaśānī (written སྨ་ཤཱ་མ) are both glossed with the same epithet, "Slaying Lady," in the Tibetan. "Glorious Vajra Fierce One" renders དཔལ་རྡོ་རྗེ་དྲག་པོ, whose Sanskrit form is uncertain. The phrase rendered "like an elephant in the state of mating" (གླང་པོ་ཆེ་ཁྱོ་ཤུག་བྱེད་པའི་གནས་སྐབས) is uncertain. "Marked by eight and four" is left as terse as the Tibetan; it probably refers to a form with eight arms and four faces. "Buddha of Life" in the root verse is identified by the commentary itself as Amitāyus. Several goddess names (All-Seeing Lady, Adorned Lady, Lady of Varied Lotuses, Lady of the Great Glorious Lotus, Treasure Lady) are translated by meaning, as their Sanskrit forms could not be identified with confidence. The sentence "By this she arouses the one who dwells in the lotus," explaining the makara banner, is obscure. The text's དག་པོའི་ཚོགས ("hosts of the pure") is read as དྲག་པོའི་ཚོགས ("hosts of the fierce"), which the context requires. The goddess "Accomplished by Three" translates a name I cannot securely identify with a Sanskrit form; she is here the consort of Maheśvara, arisen from his tears. "Triple banner" renders the Tibetan literally; it may stand for a trident-like emblem. The phrase "kindles the fire of anger" reads a verb that literally means "dedicate," which makes no sense here; I have taken the evident sense. "Kāmadeva" renders the Tibetan "divine son, Lord of Supreme Joy," identified by his consort Rati and the flower imagery. The "Tantra of the Array of Speech" is a literal rendering of a title I cannot identify with confidence. Note that the text assigns the peaceful third face to taming Śakra, not Brahmā. The six "equal unions" render samāyoga as names of the six families (Equal Union with All Buddhas, All Space, All Vajras, Various Lotuses, All Kingship, All Actions). This text pairs mirror-like wisdom with Heruka, the wisdom of equality with Padmanarteśvara, and discriminating wisdom with Vajrasūrya; translated as it stands. "The tantra of all actions" for Paramāśva may mean the family or class of all actions. "Wondrous Bliss" renders the Tibetan exclamation "Emaho" plus "bliss," which here appears to name the second of the four retinue goddesses, parallel to Saṃvarā, Vairocanī, and Disciple Lady; the Sanskrit form is uncertain. The "excellent vessel" renders a Sanskrit loanword in Tibetan script, bhāṇḍa, a ritual vessel, likely a skull cup.
