"The four modes beginning with charm" reads the unusual word in the Tibetan as a slip for the similar-looking word for "charm, grace," which fits the description of the countenance. The heading "mudrā of the gaze" at the end of that section reads "knowing" in the Tibetan, evidently a slip for the quotative particle used in the parallel headings. "The fifth and the sixth notes" render terms for the Indian musical notes pañcama and ṣaḍja. The names of the instrument goddesses and of the goddesses Lady of the Wheel of Dharma, Lady Victorious over the Three Worlds, Lady Vine of Desire, Sun and Moon Lady, and Lady Who Reduces to Ash are translated from the Tibetan, as their Sanskrit forms are not certain. "The Great Glorious One" likely refers to Heruka. The nine dances correspond to the nine dramatic sentiments (grace, heroism, compassion, laughter, wrath, terror, repulsiveness, wonder, peace), each paired here with a worldly deity to be tamed. "The crooked-nosed vināyaka" translates ལོག་འདྲེན་སྣ་ཡོན literally; it may refer to Gaṇeśa (Gaṇapati), but this is not certain. སྒྲ་གཅན་འཛིན is rendered Rāhula per the house glossary, though in this list of worldly beings to be tamed the eclipse demon Rāhu may be meant. ཕམ་པ here means losing the contest of signs and mudrās, not a root downfall. "The hide of the Great God" renders ལྷ་ཆེན་པོའི་གཡང་གཞི, the animal hide worn by Heruka, taken from Śiva as a sign of his subjugation. The names of the musician goddesses (Flute Lady, Vīṇā Lady, Clay Drum Lady, Great Drum Lady) are rendered by meaning, as their Sanskrit forms are not given here. The terms in the torma passage (the Graceful Lady, the Young Girl, the Intoxicating, the Lone Hero, Passion, the Crooked One, the Maidservant) are code words of the root tantra, here decoded by the commentator. "Khar" (ཁར) is left as it stands; it appears to be one of these code terms, perhaps for a sweet substance, but its referent is not certain. The "five sentient beings" whose images form the fabricated torma probably stand for the five meats. The second Sanskrit run in the crown mantra is given exactly as spelled. "The fivefold rite" renders ཆོ་ག་ལྔ་པ, where the edition reads ཆོ་ག་ལྔ་p, an evident typing slip; it may also be read as "the fifth rite." The mantra syllables are given exactly as the text spells them. The heading at the end reads "name together with praise" (བསྟོད་པ), where the heading at the start of the section read "name together with the Teacher" (སྟོན་པ); the text is translated as it stands in each place. "Sumbha" renders ཁྲོ་བོ་གནོད་མཛེས, the standard rendering of the wrathful deity Sumbha (Sumbharāja), whose mantra follows. Several of the bodily signs are code language of the feast gathering and their exact referents are uncertain: "asks to stay" for the right nostril, and "the flesh of a caṇḍāla and garlic liquor" for the tongue, which likely name samaya substances under cover words. In the verse naming the goddesses, bde mchog is rendered Saṃvara; it may here be a code name meaning "Supreme Bliss." Shi shi is an unidentified code name, given phonetically. The names of the four secret goddesses (Sealing, Bewildering, Devouring and Life-Increasing Lady) are translated from the Tibetan, as their Sanskrit forms could not be identified with confidence. The deputy (rgyal thebs) and the ritual assistant (las mkhan, later called the karma vajra) are the officiants who assist the vajra king, the presiding master of the feast. In the code verse, camphor and red sandalwood are cover names for the substances of the feast; "the mixture and the great blood" follows the Tibetan literally. In the verse on the family crowns, don kun grub par is read as "accomplishing every aim" rather than as a buddha's name; it may allude to Amoghasiddhi or Sarvārthasiddhi, but the Tibetan does not clearly give a name. The four excellences and seven arrangements of the feast are named without explanation in this passage. The four yogas are yoga, subsequent yoga (anuyoga), utmost yoga (atiyoga) and great yoga (mahāyoga); the commentary pairs them with accomplishment, subsequent accomplishment, great accomplishment and utmost accomplishment in that order, which is kept as written. "The flesh of one born seven times" renders skye ba bdun pa'i shas, the standard code for the great flesh. Only four items are listed under "the five offerings" in this piece; the text is followed as it stands. The fruit names in the verse are transliterated Sanskrit (āmra, panasa, nālikera) mixed with Tibetan, and are rendered as the fruits they name. "Lord of Dance" (gar gyi dbang phyug) is given as Nartteśvara. The tantra titles are rendered in English: ye shes gsang ba'i rgyud as the Secret Wisdom Tantra, phyag na rdo rje dbang bskur ba'i rgyud as the Tantra of the Empowerment of Vajrapāṇi, and rdo rje bkod pa'i rgyud as the Vajra Array Tantra; their exact Sanskrit titles are not identified with confidence here. "Glorious Victor" (rnam rgyal dpal) is taken as an address in the protection verse. In this chapter Heruka is paired with the mirror-like wisdom, as the text states. "Bringing down a prasena divination" renders phra sen dbab pa, the rite of making a deity descend into a child medium (or a mirror) in order to read omens. In the opening, bsgral ba is used in its general sense of delivering beings, not in the wrathful sense. "Trees" renders shing dgas, which is uncertain; the word may name some other class of living thing. The chapter speaks of "the ten piercings" but the list that follows names eleven mudrās; the text is followed as it stands. "The Glorious Accomplished Eye" renders dpal grub spyan, the name of a mudrā. In the passage on the worldly gods the text reads gzhan gyis mnan, "trampled by another"; the sense of the passage is that the deity tramples them himself, and it is translated that way. In "the pledge to explain it first," the text has dbang por, taken as a slip for dang por, "first." "The two buddhas" (sangs rgyas gnyis) is translated as it stands; the commentary does not specify which two are meant (perhaps the buddha of the ground and of the result, or the two buddha-bodies). The phrase rendered "one's own form being clear as granting whatever is wished" (rang nyid kyi rnam pa ci sbyin par gsal ba) is terse and its exact sense is uncertain. The text says the Tathāgata has forty-two teeth, where the standard list of major marks gives forty; translated as it stands. The author's name brgya byin sdong po is rendered Indranāla, the form under which this commentary is usually catalogued; the identification of this Oḍḍiyāna king with other figures named Indra is uncertain. The translator's name lha'i rin po che is given in phonetics as Lhé Rinpoché.
